### dbindex
--idea
A database index is a second copy of one column that is kept sorted so the database can find rows without reading the whole table. Without an index, finding the row with `id = 8,451,902` means scanning millions of rows. With a B-tree index it reads a handful of pages (a page is the fixed-size block, often 8 KB, that a disk reads in one go). Indexes are the single biggest reason a database query goes from seconds to milliseconds.
Two kinds matter. A B-tree index keeps keys in sorted order inside wide pages, so it answers equality, ranges (`age BETWEEN 20 AND 30`) and sorted output. A hash index sends the key through a hash function to a bucket, so it answers only exact matches but does so in one step. Most databases default to B-trees because ranges and ordering are so common.
--build
1. Take the keys of the column and sort them (see Binary search: everything below depends on sorted order). Each page holds up to `B` keys, so a page is a sorted array.
2. Leaf level: cut the sorted keys into pages of `B`. In a real B+ tree each leaf entry also stores the row's location.
3. Next level up: make a smaller list holding the first key of every page below, and cut that into pages of `B` too. These entries are signposts: the child page `i` holds keys from `signpost[i]` up to (not including) `signpost[i+1]`. Repeat until a level fits in a single page: that is the root.
4. Lookup: start at the root page. In the page, run a binary search for the last signpost that is `<= key`; that tells you which child page to read next. Go down one level per step. In the leaf page, binary search for the key itself. The number of pages read equals the number of levels (`levels`).
5. With `B` keys per page the tree has about `log_B(N)` levels. With `B = 500` and a billion rows that is only 4 levels, and the top levels stay in memory, so a lookup costs about one or two disk reads.
6. Hash index: store `key -> row location` in a Hash table. An exact lookup is one probe. There is no order, so no range scans.
7. Real B-trees also handle inserts: a full page splits in two and a new signpost is pushed up (see B-trees). This model is built in one pass from sorted keys, which is how databases bulk-load an index.
--think
- Trade-offs: an index makes reads faster but every insert, update and delete must also update it, and it costs disk space. So index the columns you filter, join or sort by, not everything.
- Why wide pages: a disk or SSD reads a whole page at a time, so the cost to optimise is pages touched, not comparisons. Wide nodes make the tree shallow. A binary tree with 30 levels would mean 30 page reads.
- A composite index on `(a, b)` is sorted by `a` then `b`. It helps queries on `a`, or `a` and `b`, but not on `b` alone (the left-most prefix rule).
- At scale: the leaf level is a linked list in real B+ trees, so a range scan finds the first key and then walks right. Pages are cached in a buffer pool. Write-heavy systems may prefer LSM trees and key-value stores.
- Mistakes in the model: forgetting that the search on an upper level must pick the last signpost that is `<= key` (not the first that is greater), and using a hash index when the query needs a range.
--cost
Lookup O(log_B N) page reads and O(log B) comparisons inside each page. Space is about N keys plus 1/B extra for each level above. A hash index lookup is O(1) on average.
--java
static class BTreeIndex {
    final int B;                                                 // keys per page
    final List<List<int[]>> levels = new ArrayList<>();          // levels.get(0) = leaf pages; the last level is the root page
    final Map<Integer, Integer> hash = new HashMap<>();          // hash index: key -> row position
    int pagesRead = 0;

    BTreeIndex(int[] sortedKeys, int B) {
        this.B = B;
        for (int i = 0; i < sortedKeys.length; i++) hash.put(sortedKeys[i], i);
        int[] cur = sortedKeys;
        while (true) {
            List<int[]> pages = new ArrayList<>();
            int[] firsts = new int[(cur.length + B - 1) / B];
            for (int p = 0; p * B < cur.length; p++) {
                int[] page = Arrays.copyOfRange(cur, p * B, Math.min(cur.length, (p + 1) * B));
                pages.add(page);
                firsts[p] = page[0];                             // signpost for the level above
            }
            levels.add(pages);
            if (pages.size() == 1) break;
            cur = firsts;
        }
    }

    private static int upperBound(int[] a, int key) {            // binary search: first index with a[i] > key
        int lo = 0, hi = a.length;
        while (lo < hi) { int mid = (lo + hi) >>> 1; if (a[mid] <= key) lo = mid + 1; else hi = mid; }
        return lo;
    }

    boolean contains(int key) {                                  // B-tree descent, one page per level
        int page = 0;
        for (int lv = levels.size() - 1; lv >= 0; lv--) {
            int[] keys = levels.get(lv).get(page);
            pagesRead++;
            int idx = upperBound(keys, key) - 1;                 // last entry <= key
            if (lv == 0) return idx >= 0 && keys[idx] == key;
            page = page * B + Math.max(idx, 0);                  // child page number
        }
        return false;
    }

    boolean containsHash(int key) { return hash.containsKey(key); }   // exact match in one probe
}
--py
import bisect

class BTreeIndex:
    def __init__(self, sorted_keys, B=4):
        self.B = B                                  # keys per page
        self.hash = {k: i for i, k in enumerate(sorted_keys)}   # hash index: key -> row position
        self.levels = []                            # levels[0] = leaf pages; the last level is the root page
        self.pages_read = 0
        cur = sorted_keys
        while True:
            pages = [cur[i:i + B] for i in range(0, len(cur), B)]
            self.levels.append(pages)
            if len(pages) == 1:
                break
            cur = [p[0] for p in pages]             # signposts: first key of each page, for the level above

    def contains(self, key):                        # B-tree descent, one page per level
        page = 0
        for lv in range(len(self.levels) - 1, -1, -1):
            keys = self.levels[lv][page]
            self.pages_read += 1
            idx = bisect.bisect_right(keys, key) - 1    # last entry <= key (binary search)
            if lv == 0:
                return idx >= 0 and keys[idx] == key
            page = page * self.B + max(idx, 0)      # child page number

    def contains_hash(self, key):                   # exact match in one probe
        return key in self.hash
--cpp
struct BTreeIndex {
    int B;                                           // keys per page
    vector<vector<vector<int>>> levels;              // levels[0] = leaf pages; the last level is the root page
    unordered_map<int, int> hash;                    // hash index: key -> row position
    int pagesRead = 0;

    BTreeIndex(const vector<int>& sortedKeys, int B) : B(B) {
        for (size_t i = 0; i < sortedKeys.size(); i++) hash[sortedKeys[i]] = i;
        vector<int> cur = sortedKeys;
        while (true) {
            vector<vector<int>> pages;
            vector<int> firsts;
            for (size_t i = 0; i < cur.size(); i += B) {
                pages.emplace_back(cur.begin() + i, cur.begin() + min(cur.size(), i + (size_t)B));
                firsts.push_back(cur[i]);                // signpost for the level above
            }
            levels.push_back(pages);
            if (pages.size() == 1) break;
            cur = firsts;
        }
    }

    bool contains(int key) {                             // B-tree descent, one page per level
        int page = 0;
        for (int lv = (int)levels.size() - 1; lv >= 0; lv--) {
            const vector<int>& keys = levels[lv][page];
            pagesRead++;
            int idx = int(upper_bound(keys.begin(), keys.end(), key) - keys.begin()) - 1;   // last entry <= key
            if (lv == 0) return idx >= 0 && keys[idx] == key;
            page = page * B + max(idx, 0);               // child page number
        }
        return false;
    }

    bool containsHash(int key) const { return hash.count(key) > 0; }   // exact match in one probe
};
--go
import "sort"

type BTreeIndex struct {
	B         int
	levels    [][][]int   // levels[0] = leaf pages; the last level is the root page
	hash      map[int]int // hash index: key -> row position
	pagesRead int
}

func newBTreeIndex(sorted []int, B int) *BTreeIndex {
	t := &BTreeIndex{B: B, hash: map[int]int{}}
	for i, k := range sorted {
		t.hash[k] = i
	}
	cur := sorted
	for {
		var pages [][]int
		var firsts []int
		for i := 0; i < len(cur); i += B {
			pages = append(pages, cur[i:min(i+B, len(cur))])
			firsts = append(firsts, cur[i]) // signpost for the level above
		}
		t.levels = append(t.levels, pages)
		if len(pages) == 1 {
			break
		}
		cur = firsts
	}
	return t
}

func (t *BTreeIndex) contains(key int) bool { // B-tree descent, one page per level
	page := 0
	for lv := len(t.levels) - 1; lv >= 0; lv-- {
		keys := t.levels[lv][page]
		t.pagesRead++
		idx := sort.Search(len(keys), func(i int) bool { return keys[i] > key }) - 1 // last entry <= key
		if lv == 0 {
			return idx >= 0 && keys[idx] == key
		}
		page = page*t.B + max(idx, 0) // child page number
	}
	return false
}

func (t *BTreeIndex) containsHash(key int) bool { // exact match in one probe
	_, ok := t.hash[key]
	return ok
}

### lsm
--idea
A log-structured merge tree (LSM tree) is the storage design behind many key-value stores (LevelDB, RocksDB, Cassandra). Its goal is fast writes. Updating a sorted structure on disk in place needs random writes, which are slow. An LSM tree never edits a file. Writes go into a small sorted table in memory (the memtable). When it is full it is written to disk in one sequential sweep as an immutable sorted file (an SSTable, "sorted string table"). Old files pile up, and a background job merges them.
A read has to look in the memtable first and then in the files from newest to oldest, because the newest value for a key wins. To avoid opening every file, each file has a Bloom filter: a small bit table that says "definitely not here" or "maybe here". A delete writes a special marker called a tombstone, which hides older values until a merge drops both.
--build
1. Memtable: keep recent writes in a sorted in-memory structure. Real engines use a Skip list or a balanced tree, so the contents can be written out in sorted order. A `put` replaces any older value for the key. A `delete` stores a tombstone (`TOMB`).
2. Flush: when the memtable holds `limit` entries, write its keys in sorted order to a new SSTable: arrays `keys` and `vals`. While writing, add every key to the table's Bloom filter, and then clear the memtable. (A real engine first appends each write to a log on disk so a crash does not lose the memtable.)
3. Read `get(k)`: check the memtable. If absent, go through the SSTables newest first. For each one ask its Bloom filter; if it says "no", skip the file without reading it. If it says "maybe", binary search the sorted `keys` (see Binary search). The first hit decides the answer; a tombstone means "deleted".
4. Compaction: many files slow reads down, so merge them. Do a k-way merge with a Heap holding the next unread entry of each file, ordered by key and, for equal keys, newest file first. Pop entries in order, keep the first one seen for each key (the newest), and drop tombstones when merging everything. The result is one sorted file (this uses the idea of merge sort's merge step, extended to k inputs).
5. Real engines organise files into levels so that compaction merges only a few files at a time, and they trade write amplification (data rewritten by merges) against read cost and space.
--think
- Trade-offs: LSM trees make writes cheap (sequential, batched) and reads costlier (several places to look). B-tree based storage (see Database indexes) is the opposite: cheap reads, random-write cost. Pick by workload: logging, time series and write-heavy systems favour LSM.
- Bloom filters have false positives but never false negatives. A false positive costs one wasted lookup, never a wrong answer. With about 10 bits per key the false positive rate is around 1 percent.
- Deletes are not immediate: space comes back only after a compaction removes both the tombstone and the old value.
- At scale: writes are amplified (one value may be rewritten many times by merges), compaction competes with reads for disk, and engines tune this with level sizes and rate limits.
- Mistakes in the model: letting an older file shadow a newer one (scan newest first), dropping a tombstone while an older file still has the value (only safe when merging all files below), and merging with the wrong tie-break (the newest must come first).
--cost
Write O(log m) in a memtable of m entries plus amortised flush and compaction work. Read O(log m + F * (1 + log n)) worst case for F files, but the Bloom filters usually cut that to one file. Compaction of total size N is O(N log F) with the heap.
--java
static final String TOMB = null;                                  // tombstone marker (a deleted key)

static class Bloom {
    final boolean[] bits; final int k = 3;
    Bloom(int m) { bits = new boolean[m]; }
    private int[] spots(String key) {
        long h = 0xcbf29ce484222325L;
        for (int i = 0; i < key.length(); i++) { h ^= key.charAt(i); h *= 0x100000001b3L; }
        h ^= h >>> 29; h *= 0xbf58476d1ce4e5b9L; h ^= h >>> 32;
        int a = (int) (h & 0x7fffffff), b = (int) ((h >>> 32) & 0x7fffffff) | 1;
        int[] r = new int[k];
        for (int i = 0; i < k; i++) r[i] = (int) (((long) a + (long) i * b) % bits.length);
        return r;
    }
    void add(String key) { for (int p : spots(key)) bits[p] = true; }
    boolean mightHave(String key) { for (int p : spots(key)) if (!bits[p]) return false; return true; }
}

static class Table {
    final String[] keys, vals; final Bloom bloom = new Bloom(1024);
    Table(TreeMap<String, String> mem) {
        keys = mem.keySet().toArray(new String[0]);
        vals = mem.values().toArray(new String[0]);
        for (String k : keys) bloom.add(k);
    }
}

static class LSM {
    TreeMap<String, String> mem = new TreeMap<>();                // memtable: sorted
    List<Table> tables = new ArrayList<>();                       // oldest first
    final int limit;
    LSM(int limit) { this.limit = limit; }

    void put(String k, String v) { mem.put(k, v); if (mem.size() >= limit) flush(); }
    void delete(String k) { put(k, TOMB); }
    void flush() { tables.add(new Table(mem)); mem = new TreeMap<>(); }

    String get(String k) {
        if (mem.containsKey(k)) return mem.get(k);
        for (int t = tables.size() - 1; t >= 0; t--) {            // newest first
            Table tb = tables.get(t);
            if (!tb.bloom.mightHave(k)) continue;                 // definitely absent: skip the file
            int i = Arrays.binarySearch(tb.keys, k);
            if (i >= 0) return tb.vals[i];
        }
        return null;
    }

    void compact() {                                              // k-way merge, newest wins, drop tombstones
        PriorityQueue<int[]> pq = new PriorityQueue<>((x, y) -> {
            int c = tables.get(x[0]).keys[x[1]].compareTo(tables.get(y[0]).keys[y[1]]);
            return c != 0 ? c : Integer.compare(y[0], x[0]);      // equal keys: newer table first
        });
        for (int t = 0; t < tables.size(); t++) if (tables.get(t).keys.length > 0) pq.add(new int[]{t, 0});
        TreeMap<String, String> merged = new TreeMap<>();
        String last = null;
        while (!pq.isEmpty()) {
            int[] e = pq.poll();
            Table tb = tables.get(e[0]);
            String k = tb.keys[e[1]];
            if (!k.equals(last)) { last = k; if (tb.vals[e[1]] != TOMB) merged.put(k, tb.vals[e[1]]); }
            if (e[1] + 1 < tb.keys.length) pq.add(new int[]{e[0], e[1] + 1});
        }
        tables = new ArrayList<>();
        if (!merged.isEmpty()) tables.add(new Table(merged));
    }
}
--py
import bisect, heapq

TOMB = None                                           # tombstone marker (a deleted key)

class Bloom:
    def __init__(self, m=1024, k=3):
        self.m, self.k, self.bits = m, k, bytearray(m)

    def _spots(self, key):                            # k positions from two hashes (double hashing)
        h = 0xcbf29ce484222325
        for ch in key:
            h = ((h ^ ord(ch)) * 0x100000001b3) & 0xFFFFFFFFFFFFFFFF
        a, b = h & 0xFFFFFFFF, (h >> 32) | 1
        return [(a + i * b) % self.m for i in range(self.k)]

    def add(self, key):
        for p in self._spots(key): self.bits[p] = 1

    def might_have(self, key):
        return all(self.bits[p] for p in self._spots(key))

class LSM:
    def __init__(self, limit=4):
        self.mem, self.limit = {}, limit              # memtable (sorted when flushed)
        self.tables = []                              # each: (keys, vals, bloom), oldest first

    def put(self, k, v):
        self.mem[k] = v
        if len(self.mem) >= self.limit:
            self.flush()

    def delete(self, k):
        self.put(k, TOMB)

    def flush(self):                                  # write the memtable out as a sorted, immutable table
        keys = sorted(self.mem)
        bloom = Bloom()
        for k in keys: bloom.add(k)
        self.tables.append((keys, [self.mem[k] for k in keys], bloom))
        self.mem = {}

    def get(self, k):
        if k in self.mem:
            return self.mem[k]
        for keys, vals, bloom in reversed(self.tables):     # newest first
            if not bloom.might_have(k):               # definitely absent: skip the file
                continue
            i = bisect.bisect_left(keys, k)
            if i < len(keys) and keys[i] == k:
                return vals[i]
        return None

    def compact(self):                                # k-way merge, newest wins, drop tombstones
        heap = [(t[0][0], -age, age, 0) for age, t in enumerate(self.tables) if t[0]]
        heapq.heapify(heap)                           # equal keys: larger age (newer) pops first
        keys, vals, last = [], [], object()
        while heap:
            k, _, age, pos = heapq.heappop(heap)
            tk, tv, _ = self.tables[age]
            if k != last:
                last = k
                if tv[pos] is not TOMB:
                    keys.append(k); vals.append(tv[pos])
            if pos + 1 < len(tk):
                heapq.heappush(heap, (tk[pos + 1], -age, age, pos + 1))
        bloom = Bloom()
        for k in keys: bloom.add(k)
        self.tables = [(keys, vals, bloom)] if keys else []
--cpp
const string TOMB = "\x01<deleted>";                  // tombstone marker (a deleted key)

struct Bloom {
    vector<char> bits = vector<char>(1024, 0);
    static const int K = 3;
    array<size_t, K> spots(const string& key) const {
        unsigned long long h = 0xcbf29ce484222325ULL;
        for (unsigned char ch : key) { h ^= ch; h *= 0x100000001b3ULL; }
        h ^= h >> 29; h *= 0xbf58476d1ce4e5b9ULL; h ^= h >> 32;
        unsigned long long a = h & 0x7fffffff, b = ((h >> 32) & 0x7fffffff) | 1;
        array<size_t, K> r;
        for (int i = 0; i < K; i++) r[i] = (a + i * b) % bits.size();
        return r;
    }
    void add(const string& key) { for (size_t p : spots(key)) bits[p] = 1; }
    bool mightHave(const string& key) const { for (size_t p : spots(key)) if (!bits[p]) return false; return true; }
};

struct Table {
    vector<string> keys, vals; Bloom bloom;
    explicit Table(const map<string, string>& m) {
        for (auto& kv : m) { keys.push_back(kv.first); vals.push_back(kv.second); bloom.add(kv.first); }
    }
};

struct LSM {
    map<string, string> mem;                            // memtable: sorted
    vector<Table> tables;                               // oldest first
    size_t limit;
    explicit LSM(size_t limit) : limit(limit) {}

    void put(const string& k, const string& v) { mem[k] = v; if (mem.size() >= limit) flush(); }
    void del(const string& k) { put(k, TOMB); }
    void flush() { tables.emplace_back(mem); mem.clear(); }

    string get(const string& k) const {                 // returns TOMB if deleted, "" if never seen
        auto it = mem.find(k);
        if (it != mem.end()) return it->second;
        for (int t = (int)tables.size() - 1; t >= 0; t--) {     // newest first
            const Table& tb = tables[t];
            if (!tb.bloom.mightHave(k)) continue;       // definitely absent: skip the file
            auto pos = lower_bound(tb.keys.begin(), tb.keys.end(), k);
            if (pos != tb.keys.end() && *pos == k) return tb.vals[pos - tb.keys.begin()];
        }
        return "";
    }

    void compact() {                                    // k-way merge, newest wins, drop tombstones
        typedef tuple<string, int, int> E;              // key, -age, position
        priority_queue<E, vector<E>, greater<E>> pq;    // equal keys: smaller -age (newer table) first
        for (int t = 0; t < (int)tables.size(); t++) if (!tables[t].keys.empty()) pq.push({tables[t].keys[0], -t, 0});
        map<string, string> merged;
        string last; bool have = false;
        while (!pq.empty()) {
            auto [k, negAge, pos] = pq.top(); pq.pop();
            const Table& tb = tables[-negAge];
            if (!have || k != last) { last = k; have = true; if (tb.vals[pos] != TOMB) merged[k] = tb.vals[pos]; }
            if (pos + 1 < (int)tb.keys.size()) pq.push({tb.keys[pos + 1], negAge, pos + 1});
        }
        tables.clear();
        if (!merged.empty()) tables.emplace_back(merged);
    }
};
--go
import "sort"

type entry struct {
	v    string
	dead bool // tombstone: a deleted key
}

type bloom struct{ bits []bool }

func (b *bloom) spots(key string) [3]int {
	h := uint64(0xcbf29ce484222325)
	for i := 0; i < len(key); i++ {
		h ^= uint64(key[i])
		h *= 0x100000001b3
	}
	h ^= h >> 29
	h *= 0xbf58476d1ce4e5b9
	h ^= h >> 32
	a, step := h&0x7fffffff, (h>>32&0x7fffffff)|1
	var r [3]int
	for i := range r {
		r[i] = int((a + uint64(i)*step) % uint64(len(b.bits)))
	}
	return r
}
func (b *bloom) add(key string) {
	for _, p := range b.spots(key) {
		b.bits[p] = true
	}
}
func (b *bloom) mightHave(key string) bool {
	for _, p := range b.spots(key) {
		if !b.bits[p] {
			return false
		}
	}
	return true
}

type table struct { // one immutable sorted file plus its Bloom filter
	keys  []string
	vals  []entry
	bloom bloom
}

func newTable(m map[string]entry) *table {
	t := &table{bloom: bloom{make([]bool, 1024)}}
	for k := range m {
		t.keys = append(t.keys, k)
	}
	sort.Strings(t.keys)
	for _, k := range t.keys {
		t.vals = append(t.vals, m[k])
		t.bloom.add(k)
	}
	return t
}

type LSM struct {
	mem    map[string]entry // memtable (sorted when flushed)
	tables []*table         // oldest first
	limit  int
}

func newLSM(limit int) *LSM { return &LSM{mem: map[string]entry{}, limit: limit} }

func (l *LSM) put(k, v string) { l.set(k, entry{v: v}) }
func (l *LSM) del(k string)    { l.set(k, entry{dead: true}) }
func (l *LSM) set(k string, e entry) {
	l.mem[k] = e
	if len(l.mem) >= l.limit {
		l.tables = append(l.tables, newTable(l.mem))
		l.mem = map[string]entry{}
	}
}

func (l *LSM) get(k string) (string, bool) {
	if e, ok := l.mem[k]; ok {
		return e.v, !e.dead
	}
	for t := len(l.tables) - 1; t >= 0; t-- { // newest first
		tb := l.tables[t]
		if !tb.bloom.mightHave(k) { // definitely absent: skip the file
			continue
		}
		if i := sort.SearchStrings(tb.keys, k); i < len(tb.keys) && tb.keys[i] == k {
			return tb.vals[i].v, !tb.vals[i].dead
		}
	}
	return "", false
}

func (l *LSM) compact() { // merge every table, newest wins, drop tombstones
	merged := map[string]entry{}
	for _, tb := range l.tables { // oldest first, so newer entries overwrite
		for i, k := range tb.keys {
			merged[k] = tb.vals[i]
		}
	}
	for k, e := range merged {
		if e.dead {
			delete(merged, k)
		}
	}
	l.tables = nil
	if len(merged) > 0 {
		l.tables = []*table{newTable(merged)}
	}
}

### queryplan
--idea
When you write `SELECT ... FROM a JOIN b JOIN c ...`, the database must choose the order in which to join the tables. The order does not change the answer but can change the cost by factors of thousands. Joining two huge tables first creates a huge intermediate result, while starting with a small, selective pair keeps every intermediate result small. The query planner (optimiser) is the part of the database that searches for the cheap order.
It uses the cost-based approach introduced in IBM's System R: estimate how many rows each step produces, and pick the order with the smallest total. To avoid trying all `n!` orders, it uses dynamic programming over subsets of tables: the best way to join a set of tables is built from the best way to join a smaller set plus one more table. A bitmask (an integer with one bit per table) names each subset.
--build
1. Give each table `i` an estimated row count `card[i]`, and each pair `(i, j)` a selectivity `sel[i][j]`: the fraction of row pairs that survive the join predicate between them (1.0 if there is no predicate, so a cross product). The estimates come from statistics the database keeps about each column.
2. A set of tables is a bitmask `mask`. The estimated size of joining the set is the product of the table sizes times the product of the selectivities of every pair inside the set: `size[mask]`. It depends only on the set, not on the order.
3. `cost[mask]` is the minimum total cost to join the set. A simple cost is the sum of the sizes of all intermediate results produced. For a single table `cost = 0`.
4. Transition (left-deep plans: add one table at a time): `cost[mask] = min over t in mask of cost[mask minus t] + size[mask]`. Remember the choice in `lastTable[mask]`.
5. Process masks in increasing numeric order, because a subset of a mask is always a smaller number. The answer is `cost[full]`, and following `lastTable` backwards gives the join order.
6. Real planners also consider bushy plans (join two joined subsets together), the physical join method for each step (see Join algorithms), and index use, and they prune with the best cost found so far. For more than about a dozen tables they switch to greedy or randomised search because `2^n` grows too fast.
--think
- Signal: a problem where you choose an order or a subset and the cost of a step depends on which elements are already chosen. With `n <= 20` or so, think bitmask DP (see Bitmask DP). It is the same shape as the travelling salesman DP.
- The model is only as good as the estimates. Real planners are often wrong because columns are correlated, and errors multiply through joins. Many bad query plans come from bad row estimates, which is why databases keep histograms and let you refresh statistics.
- Greedy shortcut: always join the cheapest next table. It is fast and sometimes wrong, so it is used as the fallback for big queries (see Greedy). The DP finds the true minimum for the cost model.
- Pruning: if a partial plan is already worse than a known complete plan, drop it (branch and bound). A priority queue can drive a best-first search of the same space.
- Mistakes: computing `size` from the wrong set (it must be for the whole `mask`), forgetting selectivities between the new table and every table already in the set, and iterating masks in an order where a subset has not been done yet.
--cost
Time O(2^n * n^2) for left-deep plans with this size computation, space O(2^n). Exhaustive bushy search is O(3^n).
--java
// card[i]: estimated rows; sel[i][j]: join selectivity (1.0 = no predicate). Returns {cost, order...}
static double[] bestJoinOrder(double[] card, double[][] sel, int[] orderOut) {
    int n = card.length, full = (1 << n) - 1;
    double[] size = new double[1 << n], cost = new double[1 << n];
    int[] last = new int[1 << n];
    Arrays.fill(cost, Double.POSITIVE_INFINITY);
    for (int t = 0; t < n; t++) { size[1 << t] = card[t]; cost[1 << t] = 0; last[1 << t] = t; }
    for (int mask = 1; mask <= full; mask++) {
        if ((mask & (mask - 1)) == 0) continue;                 // single table: already set
        for (int t = 0; t < n; t++) {
            if ((mask >> t & 1) == 0) continue;
            int rest = mask ^ (1 << t);
            double sz = size[rest] * card[t];                   // join table t onto the set `rest`
            for (int j = 0; j < n; j++) if ((rest >> j & 1) != 0) sz *= sel[t][j];
            double c = cost[rest] + sz;
            if (c < cost[mask]) { cost[mask] = c; size[mask] = sz; last[mask] = t; }
        }
    }
    int m = full;
    for (int i = n - 1; i >= 0; i--) { orderOut[i] = last[m]; m ^= 1 << last[m]; }   // walk the choices backwards
    return new double[]{cost[full]};
}
--py
def best_join_order(card, sel):
    """card[i]: estimated rows; sel[i][j]: join selectivity (1.0 = no predicate). Returns (cost, order)."""
    n, full = len(card), (1 << len(card)) - 1
    size = [0.0] * (1 << n)
    cost = [float("inf")] * (1 << n)
    last = [0] * (1 << n)
    for t in range(n):
        size[1 << t], cost[1 << t], last[1 << t] = card[t], 0.0, t
    for mask in range(1, full + 1):
        if mask & (mask - 1) == 0:                   # single table: already set
            continue
        for t in range(n):
            if not mask >> t & 1:
                continue
            rest = mask ^ (1 << t)
            sz = size[rest] * card[t]                # join table t onto the set `rest`
            for j in range(n):
                if rest >> j & 1:
                    sz *= sel[t][j]
            c = cost[rest] + sz                      # cost = sum of intermediate result sizes
            if c < cost[mask]:
                cost[mask], size[mask], last[mask] = c, sz, t
    order, m = [], full
    while m:                                         # walk the choices backwards
        order.append(last[m])
        m ^= 1 << last[m]
    return cost[full], order[::-1]
--cpp
// card[i]: estimated rows; sel[i][j]: join selectivity (1.0 = no predicate). Returns the cost, fills `order`.
double bestJoinOrder(const vector<double>& card, const vector<vector<double>>& sel, vector<int>& order) {
    int n = card.size(), full = (1 << n) - 1;
    vector<double> size(1 << n, 0.0), cost(1 << n, 1e300);
    vector<int> last(1 << n, 0);
    for (int t = 0; t < n; t++) { size[1 << t] = card[t]; cost[1 << t] = 0; last[1 << t] = t; }
    for (int mask = 1; mask <= full; mask++) {
        if ((mask & (mask - 1)) == 0) continue;                 // single table: already set
        for (int t = 0; t < n; t++) {
            if (!(mask >> t & 1)) continue;
            int rest = mask ^ (1 << t);
            double sz = size[rest] * card[t];                   // join table t onto the set `rest`
            for (int j = 0; j < n; j++) if (rest >> j & 1) sz *= sel[t][j];
            double c = cost[rest] + sz;                         // cost = sum of intermediate result sizes
            if (c < cost[mask]) { cost[mask] = c; size[mask] = sz; last[mask] = t; }
        }
    }
    order.clear();
    for (int m = full; m; m ^= 1 << last[m]) order.push_back(last[m]);   // walk the choices backwards
    reverse(order.begin(), order.end());
    return cost[full];
}
--go
import "math"

// card[i]: estimated rows; sel[i][j]: join selectivity (1.0 = no predicate). Returns the cost and the join order.
func bestJoinOrder(card []float64, sel [][]float64) (float64, []int) {
	n := len(card)
	full := 1<<n - 1
	size := make([]float64, 1<<n)
	cost := make([]float64, 1<<n)
	last := make([]int, 1<<n)
	for m := range cost {
		cost[m] = math.Inf(1)
	}
	for t := 0; t < n; t++ {
		size[1<<t], cost[1<<t], last[1<<t] = card[t], 0, t
	}
	for mask := 1; mask <= full; mask++ {
		if mask&(mask-1) == 0 { // single table: already set
			continue
		}
		for t := 0; t < n; t++ {
			if mask>>t&1 == 0 {
				continue
			}
			rest := mask ^ 1<<t
			sz := size[rest] * card[t] // join table t onto the set rest
			for j := 0; j < n; j++ {
				if rest>>j&1 != 0 {
					sz *= sel[t][j]
				}
			}
			if c := cost[rest] + sz; c < cost[mask] {
				cost[mask], size[mask], last[mask] = c, sz, t
			}
		}
	}
	order := make([]int, n)
	for i, m := n-1, full; i >= 0; i-- { // walk the choices backwards
		order[i] = last[m]
		m ^= 1 << last[m]
	}
	return cost[full], order
}

### joins
--idea
A join combines rows of two tables that have equal values in some column, for example every order with the customer who placed it. There are three classic ways to do it, and a database picks one per query. All three produce the same rows; they differ in how much work they do and what they need.
A nested loop join compares every row of one table with every row of the other: simple, works for any condition, but quadratic. A hash join builds a hash table on one table and probes it with each row of the other: linear time, but equality only and extra memory. A sort-merge join sorts both tables by the join column and walks them side by side like the merge step of merge sort: linear after sorting, and great when the data is already sorted or the output must be.
--build
1. Represent each table as a list of `(key, payload)` rows, where `key` is the join column.
2. Nested loop: for every row `a` of `A` and every row `b` of `B`, output the pair if the keys are equal. Variant, the index nested loop: if `B` has an index (here a sorted array, see Binary search), look up each `a.key` with a binary search instead of scanning `B`.
3. Hash join, build phase: scan the smaller table and put its rows in a Hash table keyed by the join column (a list of payloads per key, since keys can repeat).
4. Hash join, probe phase: scan the other table, and for each row look up its key in the hash table and output a pair for every match. If the hash table does not fit in memory, databases first split both tables into partitions by hash, and join each pair of partitions separately (a grace hash join).
5. Sort-merge: sort both tables by key with a merge sort. Keep two pointers `i` and `j`. If `A[i].key < B[j].key` advance `i`, if greater advance `j`. If equal, find the whole run of equal keys in both tables and output every pairing of the two runs, then move both pointers past the runs.
--think
- Choosing: tiny table or no equality condition, use nested loop. A big equality join with enough memory, use hash join. Inputs already sorted (for example read from an index) or the query needs sorted output, use sort-merge. Index nested loop wins when one side is small and the other has an index.
- Duplicates are the common bug in sort-merge: you must pair the full run of equal keys on both sides, not just advance one pointer.
- Hash join cost is dominated by memory: it builds on the smaller side. Choosing which table to build on is part of query planning (see SQL query planning).
- Outer joins (keep unmatched rows) need an extra "matched" flag per row in the hash or merge step.
- At scale: partition both tables by hash so each machine joins a slice, or sort both and merge in parallel.
--cost
Nested loop O(|A| * |B|). Hash join O(|A| + |B|) expected. Sort-merge O(|A| log |A| + |B| log |B|) plus the output size. Index nested loop O(|A| log |B|).
--java
// rows are {key, payload}; results are {payloadA, payloadB}
static List<int[]> nestedLoop(List<int[]> A, List<int[]> B) {
    List<int[]> out = new ArrayList<>();
    for (int[] a : A) for (int[] b : B) if (a[0] == b[0]) out.add(new int[]{a[1], b[1]});
    return out;
}

static List<int[]> hashJoin(List<int[]> A, List<int[]> B) {
    Map<Integer, List<Integer>> table = new HashMap<>();              // build on A
    for (int[] a : A) table.computeIfAbsent(a[0], k -> new ArrayList<>()).add(a[1]);
    List<int[]> out = new ArrayList<>();
    for (int[] b : B)                                                  // probe with B
        for (int pa : table.getOrDefault(b[0], Collections.emptyList())) out.add(new int[]{pa, b[1]});
    return out;
}

static List<int[]> mergeJoin(List<int[]> A, List<int[]> B) {
    List<int[]> a = new ArrayList<>(A), b = new ArrayList<>(B);
    a.sort((x, y) -> Integer.compare(x[0], y[0]));
    b.sort((x, y) -> Integer.compare(x[0], y[0]));
    List<int[]> out = new ArrayList<>();
    int i = 0, j = 0;
    while (i < a.size() && j < b.size()) {
        int ka = a.get(i)[0], kb = b.get(j)[0];
        if (ka < kb) i++;
        else if (ka > kb) j++;
        else {                                                         // equal: pair the whole run on each side
            int i2 = i, j2 = j;
            while (i2 < a.size() && a.get(i2)[0] == ka) i2++;
            while (j2 < b.size() && b.get(j2)[0] == ka) j2++;
            for (int x = i; x < i2; x++) for (int y = j; y < j2; y++) out.add(new int[]{a.get(x)[1], b.get(y)[1]});
            i = i2; j = j2;
        }
    }
    return out;
}
--py
def nested_loop(A, B):                               # rows are (key, payload)
    return [(pa, pb) for ka, pa in A for kb, pb in B if ka == kb]

def hash_join(A, B):
    table = {}
    for k, p in A:                                   # build on A
        table.setdefault(k, []).append(p)
    return [(pa, pb) for k, pb in B for pa in table.get(k, [])]   # probe with B

def merge_join(A, B):
    A, B = sorted(A), sorted(B)                      # sort by key
    out, i, j = [], 0, 0
    while i < len(A) and j < len(B):
        if A[i][0] < B[j][0]:
            i += 1
        elif A[i][0] > B[j][0]:
            j += 1
        else:                                        # equal: pair the whole run on each side
            k, i2, j2 = A[i][0], i, j
            while i2 < len(A) and A[i2][0] == k: i2 += 1
            while j2 < len(B) and B[j2][0] == k: j2 += 1
            out += [(pa, pb) for _, pa in A[i:i2] for _, pb in B[j:j2]]
            i, j = i2, j2
    return out
--cpp
typedef vector<pair<int, int>> Rows;                 // rows are {key, payload}; results are {payloadA, payloadB}

Rows nestedLoop(const Rows& A, const Rows& B) {
    Rows out;
    for (auto& a : A) for (auto& b : B) if (a.first == b.first) out.push_back({a.second, b.second});
    return out;
}

Rows hashJoin(const Rows& A, const Rows& B) {
    unordered_map<int, vector<int>> table;                       // build on A
    for (auto& a : A) table[a.first].push_back(a.second);
    Rows out;
    for (auto& b : B) {                                          // probe with B
        auto it = table.find(b.first);
        if (it != table.end()) for (int pa : it->second) out.push_back({pa, b.second});
    }
    return out;
}

Rows mergeJoin(Rows A, Rows B) {
    sort(A.begin(), A.end()); sort(B.begin(), B.end());          // sort by key
    Rows out;
    size_t i = 0, j = 0;
    while (i < A.size() && j < B.size()) {
        if (A[i].first < B[j].first) i++;
        else if (A[i].first > B[j].first) j++;
        else {                                                   // equal: pair the whole run on each side
            int k = A[i].first;
            size_t i2 = i, j2 = j;
            while (i2 < A.size() && A[i2].first == k) i2++;
            while (j2 < B.size() && B[j2].first == k) j2++;
            for (size_t x = i; x < i2; x++) for (size_t y = j; y < j2; y++) out.push_back({A[x].second, B[y].second});
            i = i2; j = j2;
        }
    }
    return out;
}
--go
import "sort"

// rows are {key, payload}; results are {payloadA, payloadB}
func nestedLoop(A, B [][2]int) [][2]int {
	var out [][2]int
	for _, a := range A {
		for _, b := range B {
			if a[0] == b[0] {
				out = append(out, [2]int{a[1], b[1]})
			}
		}
	}
	return out
}

func hashJoin(A, B [][2]int) [][2]int {
	table := map[int][]int{} // build on A
	for _, a := range A {
		table[a[0]] = append(table[a[0]], a[1])
	}
	var out [][2]int
	for _, b := range B { // probe with B
		for _, pa := range table[b[0]] {
			out = append(out, [2]int{pa, b[1]})
		}
	}
	return out
}

func mergeJoin(A, B [][2]int) [][2]int {
	a := append([][2]int(nil), A...) // sort copies, leave the inputs alone
	b := append([][2]int(nil), B...)
	byKey := func(r [][2]int) { sort.Slice(r, func(i, j int) bool { return r[i][0] < r[j][0] }) }
	byKey(a)
	byKey(b)
	var out [][2]int
	i, j := 0, 0
	for i < len(a) && j < len(b) {
		switch {
		case a[i][0] < b[j][0]:
			i++
		case a[i][0] > b[j][0]:
			j++
		default: // equal: pair the whole run on each side
			k := a[i][0]
			i2, j2 := i, j
			for i2 < len(a) && a[i2][0] == k {
				i2++
			}
			for j2 < len(b) && b[j2][0] == k {
				j2++
			}
			for x := i; x < i2; x++ {
				for y := j; y < j2; y++ {
					out = append(out, [2]int{a[x][1], b[y][1]})
				}
			}
			i, j = i2, j2
		}
	}
	return out
}

### txn
--idea
A transaction is a group of reads and writes that the database must treat as one indivisible unit: all of it takes effect or none. When many transactions run at once, the database must make the result look as if they ran one after another in some order (serializability). It does this with locks: a transaction takes a lock before touching a row and others must wait. Waiting creates a new danger: deadlock, where transaction A waits for B while B waits for A, and neither can ever continue.
Both ideas are graph problems. The wait-for graph has a node per transaction and an edge `A -> B` when A waits for a lock that B holds. A cycle in it is exactly a deadlock, and the database breaks it by aborting one transaction in the cycle. The precedence graph (draw an edge `A -> B` when an operation of A conflicts with and comes before one of B) tells you whether a recorded schedule is equivalent to some serial order: if the graph has no cycle, a topological order of it is that serial order.
--build
1. Deadlock detection: build `waits[t]`, the list of transactions that `t` is waiting for.
2. Run a Depth-first search with three colours per node: unvisited (0), on the current path (1), finished (2) (this is Cycle detection, directed). Keep the current path in a stack.
3. If the search from `u` reaches a node coloured 1, there is a cycle: it is the part of the path from that node to `u`. Return it so the database can pick a victim (often the youngest or cheapest to roll back) and abort it.
4. Serializability check: for a schedule given as a list of operations `(txn, read-or-write, item)`, two operations conflict if they are from different transactions, touch the same item, and at least one is a write.
5. For every conflicting pair where the first comes earlier in the schedule, add the edge `earlierTxn -> laterTxn`.
6. Run a Topological sort (Kahn's algorithm: repeatedly take a node with no incoming edges, then remove its edges). If all transactions are taken, the order is a valid serial order. If some remain, there is a cycle: the schedule is not conflict serializable.
--think
- Signal: "can A and B both wait", "is this interleaving safe", "find a valid order of tasks with conflicts". Wait-for cycles and precedence cycles are the same cycle check in a directed graph.
- Detection versus prevention: databases either detect deadlocks periodically and abort a victim, or prevent them by always locking in a global order, or by rules such as wait-die that decide by transaction age who may wait.
- Two-phase locking (take all locks, then release all) guarantees the schedule is conflict serializable. Multi-version concurrency control (MVCC) lets readers see an older snapshot instead of waiting, so readers never block writers, but writers can still deadlock.
- Mistakes: treating an undirected cycle as a deadlock (direction matters), counting read-read pairs as conflicts, and forgetting to look in every component of the graph (start the search from every unvisited node).
- At scale: the wait-for graph is spread across machines, so distributed databases use timeouts or send probes along wait edges to find cycles.
--cost
Both graph passes are O(V + E). The precedence graph for a schedule of m operations takes O(m^2) to build in this simple form.
--java
// waits.get(t) = transactions that t waits for. Returns the cycle (a deadlock) or null.
static List<Integer> findDeadlock(List<List<Integer>> waits) {
    int n = waits.size();
    int[] color = new int[n];                                    // 0 new, 1 on path, 2 done
    Deque<Integer> path = new ArrayDeque<>();
    for (int s = 0; s < n; s++) if (color[s] == 0) {
        List<Integer> c = dfs(s, waits, color, path);
        if (c != null) return c;
    }
    return null;
}

private static List<Integer> dfs(int u, List<List<Integer>> waits, int[] color, Deque<Integer> path) {
    color[u] = 1; path.addLast(u);
    for (int v : waits.get(u)) {
        if (color[v] == 1) {                                     // back edge: cycle from v to u
            List<Integer> cyc = new ArrayList<>(path);
            return cyc.subList(cyc.indexOf(v), cyc.size());
        }
        if (color[v] == 0) { List<Integer> c = dfs(v, waits, color, path); if (c != null) return c; }
    }
    color[u] = 2; path.removeLast();
    return null;
}

// ops: {txn, isWrite (0/1), item}. Returns a serial order, or null if not conflict serializable.
static List<Integer> serialOrder(int n, int[][] ops) {
    List<List<Integer>> adj = new ArrayList<>();
    for (int i = 0; i < n; i++) adj.add(new ArrayList<>());
    int[] indeg = new int[n];
    boolean[][] seen = new boolean[n][n];
    for (int i = 0; i < ops.length; i++) for (int j = i + 1; j < ops.length; j++) {
        boolean conflict = ops[i][0] != ops[j][0] && ops[i][2] == ops[j][2] && (ops[i][1] == 1 || ops[j][1] == 1);
        if (conflict && !seen[ops[i][0]][ops[j][0]]) {
            seen[ops[i][0]][ops[j][0]] = true; adj.get(ops[i][0]).add(ops[j][0]); indeg[ops[j][0]]++;
        }
    }
    Deque<Integer> q = new ArrayDeque<>();
    for (int t = 0; t < n; t++) if (indeg[t] == 0) q.add(t);
    List<Integer> order = new ArrayList<>();
    while (!q.isEmpty()) {                                       // Kahn's topological sort
        int u = q.poll(); order.add(u);
        for (int v : adj.get(u)) if (--indeg[v] == 0) q.add(v);
    }
    return order.size() == n ? order : null;
}
--py
def find_deadlock(waits):                            # waits[t] = transactions that t waits for
    state, path = [0] * len(waits), []               # 0 new, 1 on path, 2 done

    def dfs(u):
        state[u] = 1; path.append(u)
        for v in waits[u]:
            if state[v] == 1:                        # back edge: cycle from v to u
                return path[path.index(v):]
            if state[v] == 0:
                c = dfs(v)
                if c: return c
        state[u] = 2; path.pop()
        return None

    for s in range(len(waits)):
        if state[s] == 0:
            c = dfs(s)
            if c: return c
    return None

def serial_order(n, ops):                            # ops: (txn, 'r' or 'w', item); None if not serializable
    adj, indeg = [set() for _ in range(n)], [0] * n
    for i, (t1, o1, x1) in enumerate(ops):
        for t2, o2, x2 in ops[i + 1:]:
            if t1 != t2 and x1 == x2 and 'w' in (o1, o2) and t2 not in adj[t1]:
                adj[t1].add(t2); indeg[t2] += 1      # conflicting pair: t1 must come before t2
    queue = [t for t in range(n) if indeg[t] == 0]
    order = []
    while queue:                                     # Kahn's topological sort
        u = queue.pop(0); order.append(u)
        for v in adj[u]:
            indeg[v] -= 1
            if indeg[v] == 0: queue.append(v)
    return order if len(order) == n else None
--cpp
// waits[t] = transactions that t waits for. Returns the cycle (a deadlock) or an empty vector.
vector<int> findDeadlock(const vector<vector<int>>& waits) {
    int n = waits.size();
    vector<int> color(n, 0), path;                        // 0 new, 1 on path, 2 done
    vector<int> cycle;
    function<bool(int)> dfs = [&](int u) {
        color[u] = 1; path.push_back(u);
        for (int v : waits[u]) {
            if (color[v] == 1) {                          // back edge: cycle from v to u
                cycle.assign(find(path.begin(), path.end(), v), path.end());
                return true;
            }
            if (color[v] == 0 && dfs(v)) return true;
        }
        color[u] = 2; path.pop_back();
        return false;
    };
    for (int s = 0; s < n; s++) if (color[s] == 0 && dfs(s)) return cycle;
    return {};
}

struct Op { int txn; char kind; int item; };              // kind: 'r' or 'w'
// Returns a serial order, or an empty vector if the schedule is not conflict serializable.
vector<int> serialOrder(int n, const vector<Op>& ops) {
    vector<set<int>> adj(n);
    vector<int> indeg(n, 0);
    for (size_t i = 0; i < ops.size(); i++) for (size_t j = i + 1; j < ops.size(); j++) {
        bool conflict = ops[i].txn != ops[j].txn && ops[i].item == ops[j].item && (ops[i].kind == 'w' || ops[j].kind == 'w');
        if (conflict && adj[ops[i].txn].insert(ops[j].txn).second) indeg[ops[j].txn]++;   // earlier txn before later txn
    }
    queue<int> q;
    for (int t = 0; t < n; t++) if (indeg[t] == 0) q.push(t);
    vector<int> order;
    while (!q.empty()) {                                  // Kahn's topological sort
        int u = q.front(); q.pop(); order.push_back(u);
        for (int v : adj[u]) if (--indeg[v] == 0) q.push(v);
    }
    return order.size() == (size_t)n ? order : vector<int>();
}
--go
// waits[t] = transactions that t waits for. Returns the cycle (a deadlock) or nil.
func findDeadlock(waits [][]int) []int {
	color := make([]int, len(waits)) // 0 new, 1 on path, 2 done
	var path []int
	var dfs func(u int) []int
	dfs = func(u int) []int {
		color[u] = 1
		path = append(path, u)
		for _, v := range waits[u] {
			if color[v] == 1 { // back edge: cycle from v to u
				for i, x := range path {
					if x == v {
						return append([]int(nil), path[i:]...)
					}
				}
			}
			if color[v] == 0 {
				if c := dfs(v); c != nil {
					return c
				}
			}
		}
		color[u] = 2
		path = path[:len(path)-1]
		return nil
	}
	for s := range waits {
		if color[s] == 0 {
			if c := dfs(s); c != nil {
				return c
			}
		}
	}
	return nil
}

// ops: {txn, isWrite (0/1), item}. Returns a serial order, or nil if not conflict serializable.
func serialOrder(n int, ops [][3]int) []int {
	adj := make([][]int, n)
	indeg := make([]int, n)
	seen := map[[2]int]bool{}
	for i := range ops {
		for j := i + 1; j < len(ops); j++ {
			a, b := ops[i], ops[j]
			conflict := a[0] != b[0] && a[2] == b[2] && (a[1] == 1 || b[1] == 1)
			if conflict && !seen[[2]int{a[0], b[0]}] { // earlier op must stay before the later one
				seen[[2]int{a[0], b[0]}] = true
				adj[a[0]] = append(adj[a[0]], b[0])
				indeg[b[0]]++
			}
		}
	}
	var q, order []int
	for t := 0; t < n; t++ {
		if indeg[t] == 0 {
			q = append(q, t)
		}
	}
	for len(q) > 0 { // Kahn's topological sort
		u := q[0]
		q = q[1:]
		order = append(order, u)
		for _, v := range adj[u] {
			if indeg[v]--; indeg[v] == 0 {
				q = append(q, v)
			}
		}
	}
	if len(order) == n {
		return order
	}
	return nil
}

### colstore
--idea
A row store keeps each row's fields together, which suits "fetch this customer". A column store keeps each column's values together, which suits analytics such as "average price of orders in March", where only two columns are needed out of fifty. Reading just those columns skips most of the data. Columns also compress far better, because a column holds values of one type that are often similar or repeated.
Two simple compressions do much of the work. Run-length encoding (RLE) replaces a run of equal values by `(value, count)`, so a sorted or low-variety column like `country` shrinks enormously. Entropy coding such as Huffman coding then gives common values shorter bit codes. On top of that, a filter like `country = 'IN'` can be answered as a bitset (one bit per row, 1 for a match). Combining filters on several columns is then one AND over machine words, handling 64 rows per instruction.
--build
1. Store each column separately as an array of values. Optionally sort the table by one column so that column forms long runs.
2. Run-length encode a column: walk the values, and when a value equals the previous one add 1 to its count, otherwise start a new `[value, 1]` run.
3. Evaluate `column = v` directly on the runs, without unpacking them: keep a position counter `pos`. For each run `(val, len)`, if `val == v` set bits `pos .. pos+len-1` in a bitset; then add `len` to `pos`. A whole run is skipped by one comparison, however long it is.
4. Combine predicates on different columns with bitwise AND (both conditions) or OR (either). The number of matching rows is the sum of the population counts (number of 1 bits) of the words.
5. Use the bitset to fetch only the matching positions from the other columns you need.
6. A second layer compresses the runs or the raw values with Huffman coding (short codes for frequent symbols) or a dictionary. The model below stops at RLE plus bitsets, which show the main speed idea. Huffman coding is described in its own topic and plugs in as a byte-level step after step 2.
--think
- Trade-offs: columns make scans and aggregates fast and cheap in space, but reading or writing one whole row touches every column file, so inserting a row is costly. This is why column stores batch writes and keep a small row-oriented buffer for fresh data.
- RLE works only if equal values sit next to each other. Sort order matters: databases pick a sort key so the most useful column has long runs, and that choice is part of the table design.
- Bitsets make combining filters cheap: 64 rows per word, and modern CPUs can do more per instruction (SIMD). The cost of a filter becomes about rows divided by 64, not rows.
- At scale: each column is split into blocks with min and max values stored in a header, so blocks that cannot match are skipped without reading them.
- Mistakes: forgetting to advance `pos` for runs that do not match, setting bits one at a time when a whole range could be set, and using a signed integer shift for bit 63.
--cost
Encoding O(n). A filter is O(runs) to build the bitset plus O(n / 64) to combine and count. Space is O(runs) instead of O(n).
--java
static List<int[]> rleEncode(int[] col) {                       // runs as {value, length}
    List<int[]> runs = new ArrayList<>();
    for (int v : col) {
        if (!runs.isEmpty() && runs.get(runs.size() - 1)[0] == v) runs.get(runs.size() - 1)[1]++;
        else runs.add(new int[]{v, 1});
    }
    return runs;
}

static long[] filterEq(List<int[]> runs, int v, int n) {        // bitset of rows where column == v, built from runs
    long[] bits = new long[(n + 63) / 64];
    int pos = 0;
    for (int[] r : runs) {
        if (r[0] == v) for (int p = pos; p < pos + r[1]; p++) bits[p >> 6] |= 1L << (p & 63);
        pos += r[1];                                            // a non-matching run is skipped in one step
    }
    return bits;
}

static int andCount(long[] a, long[] b) {                       // rows matching both filters
    int c = 0;
    for (int i = 0; i < a.length; i++) c += Long.bitCount(a[i] & b[i]);
    return c;
}
--py
def rle_encode(col):                                 # runs as [value, length]
    runs = []
    for v in col:
        if runs and runs[-1][0] == v:
            runs[-1][1] += 1
        else:
            runs.append([v, 1])
    return runs

def filter_eq(runs, v, n):                           # bitset of rows where column == v, built from runs
    bits, pos = [0] * ((n + 63) // 64), 0
    for val, length in runs:
        if val == v:
            for p in range(pos, pos + length):
                bits[p >> 6] |= 1 << (p & 63)
        pos += length                                # a non-matching run is skipped in one step
    return bits

def and_count(a, b):                                 # rows matching both filters
    return sum(bin(x & y).count("1") for x, y in zip(a, b))
--cpp
vector<pair<int, int>> rleEncode(const vector<int>& col) {      // runs as {value, length}
    vector<pair<int, int>> runs;
    for (int v : col) {
        if (!runs.empty() && runs.back().first == v) runs.back().second++;
        else runs.push_back({v, 1});
    }
    return runs;
}

vector<uint64_t> filterEq(const vector<pair<int, int>>& runs, int v, int n) {   // bitset of rows where column == v
    vector<uint64_t> bits((n + 63) / 64, 0);
    int pos = 0;
    for (auto& r : runs) {
        if (r.first == v) for (int p = pos; p < pos + r.second; p++) bits[p >> 6] |= 1ULL << (p & 63);
        pos += r.second;                                         // a non-matching run is skipped in one step
    }
    return bits;
}

int andCount(const vector<uint64_t>& a, const vector<uint64_t>& b) {   // rows matching both filters
    int c = 0;
    for (size_t i = 0; i < a.size(); i++) c += __builtin_popcountll(a[i] & b[i]);
    return c;
}
--go
import "math/bits"

func rleEncode(col []int) [][2]int { // runs as {value, length}
	var runs [][2]int
	for _, v := range col {
		if n := len(runs); n > 0 && runs[n-1][0] == v {
			runs[n-1][1]++
		} else {
			runs = append(runs, [2]int{v, 1})
		}
	}
	return runs
}

func filterEq(runs [][2]int, v, n int) []uint64 { // bitset of rows where column == v, built from runs
	bitset := make([]uint64, (n+63)/64)
	pos := 0
	for _, r := range runs {
		if r[0] == v {
			for p := pos; p < pos+r[1]; p++ {
				bitset[p>>6] |= 1 << (p & 63)
			}
		}
		pos += r[1] // a non-matching run is skipped in one step
	}
	return bitset
}

func andCount(a, b []uint64) int { // rows matching both filters
	c := 0
	for i := range a {
		c += bits.OnesCount64(a[i] & b[i])
	}
	return c
}
### cache
--idea
A cache is a small, fast store that keeps copies of data from a bigger, slower one, so repeated reads skip the slow trip. The same idea appears at every level: the CPU keeps recently used memory lines in on-chip caches, a browser or CDN (content delivery network) keeps web responses, and an application keeps database query results in memory. A hit means the data was in the cache. A miss means it had to be fetched, and then it is stored for next time.
Caches are small, so when one is full it must evict something. The policy is a bet about the future. LRU (least recently used) evicts what has been untouched longest, betting that recent use predicts future use. LFU (least frequently used) evicts what has been used the fewest times, betting that popular things stay popular. Many web caches also give entries a time to live (TTL), after which they count as stale and are refetched, which limits how out of date a copy can be.
--build
1. Keep a hash table from key to entry for O(1) lookup (see Hash tables and counting).
2. Keep the entries in recency order so the oldest can be found and removed in O(1). The classic form is a hash table plus a doubly linked list (see LRU cache and Doubly linked lists): on every use, unlink the entry and move it to the front; to evict, remove the one at the back. The code uses the ready-made ordered maps of each language, which are exactly this pair.
3. `get(key, now)`: if the key is present and not expired (`expires > now`), count a hit, move it to the most recent position, and return the value.
4. Otherwise count a miss, call the `loader` (the slow source) to fetch the value, store it with `expires = now + ttl`, and mark it most recent. This is the "cache-aside" pattern.
5. If the cache now holds more than `capacity` entries, evict the least recently used one.
6. To get LFU behaviour instead, keep a use count per entry and evict the smallest count (see LFU cache for the constant-time design). Track `hits` and `misses` so you can measure the hit rate.
--think
- Signal: a read-heavy workload where some items are asked for again and again, and the source is slow or expensive. The measure of success is the hit rate, and a few percent more can matter a lot because a miss may cost a thousand times more than a hit.
- LRU handles "what I just used I will use again" (temporal locality). It is weak against one big scan, which flushes the whole cache with items never reused. LFU is weak against change in popularity. Real systems blend them (for example, an admission filter in front of an LRU).
- The hard problem is invalidation: when the source changes, cached copies are wrong. TTLs, explicit deletes on write, and versioned keys are the usual answers. Stale data is a design decision, not a bug to hide.
- At scale: shard the cache across machines with Consistent hashing so adding a server loses only part of the cached data. Beware a stampede, where many requests miss the same hot key at once and all hit the source; coalesce them so only one fetches.
- Mistakes: not moving an entry on a hit (this turns LRU into FIFO), counting an expired entry as a hit, and evicting before inserting so the new entry itself can be dropped.
--cost
Each get or put is O(1) expected. Space O(capacity).
--java
static class Cache {
    static class Entry { int value; double expires; Entry(int v, double e) { value = v; expires = e; } }
    final int capacity; final double ttl;
    final java.util.function.Function<Integer, Integer> loader;      // the slow source
    int hits = 0, misses = 0;
    final LinkedHashMap<Integer, Entry> data;                        // hash table + linked list in access order

    Cache(int capacity, double ttl, java.util.function.Function<Integer, Integer> loader) {
        this.capacity = capacity; this.ttl = ttl; this.loader = loader;
        this.data = new LinkedHashMap<Integer, Entry>(16, 0.75f, true) {   // true = order by last access (LRU)
            protected boolean removeEldestEntry(Map.Entry<Integer, Entry> eldest) { return size() > Cache.this.capacity; }
        };
    }

    int get(int key, double now) {
        Entry e = data.get(key);                                     // a hit also moves it to the recent end
        if (e != null && e.expires > now) { hits++; return e.value; }
        misses++;
        int v = loader.apply(key);                                   // cache-aside: fetch from the source
        data.put(key, new Entry(v, now + ttl));                      // eviction happens inside put
        return v;
    }
}
--py
from collections import OrderedDict

class Cache:
    def __init__(self, capacity, ttl, loader):
        self.capacity, self.ttl, self.loader = capacity, ttl, loader   # loader: the slow source
        self.data = OrderedDict()                    # hash table + linked list; the last item is the most recent
        self.hits = self.misses = 0

    def get(self, key, now=0):
        e = self.data.get(key)
        if e is not None and e[1] > now:             # present and not expired
            self.hits += 1
            self.data.move_to_end(key)               # mark as most recently used
            return e[0]
        self.misses += 1
        value = self.loader(key)                     # cache-aside: fetch from the source
        self.data[key] = (value, now + self.ttl)
        self.data.move_to_end(key)
        if len(self.data) > self.capacity:
            self.data.popitem(last=False)            # evict the least recently used
        return value
--cpp
struct Cache {
    struct Entry { int value; double expires; list<int>::iterator pos; };
    size_t capacity; double ttl;
    function<int(int)> loader;                       // the slow source
    int hits = 0, misses = 0;
    list<int> order;                                 // doubly linked list: front = most recent
    unordered_map<int, Entry> data;                  // hash table: key -> entry

    Cache(size_t capacity, double ttl, function<int(int)> loader) : capacity(capacity), ttl(ttl), loader(loader) {}

    int get(int key, double now) {
        auto it = data.find(key);
        if (it != data.end() && it->second.expires > now) {          // present and not expired
            hits++;
            order.splice(order.begin(), order, it->second.pos);      // move to the front in O(1)
            return it->second.value;
        }
        misses++;
        if (it != data.end()) { order.erase(it->second.pos); data.erase(it); }   // drop the stale copy
        int value = loader(key);                                     // cache-aside: fetch from the source
        order.push_front(key);
        data[key] = {value, now + ttl, order.begin()};
        if (data.size() > capacity) {                                // evict the least recently used
            data.erase(order.back());
            order.pop_back();
        }
        return value;
    }
};
--go
import "container/list"

type item struct {
	key, value int
	expires    float64
}

type Cache struct {
	capacity     int
	ttl          float64
	loader       func(int) int // the slow source
	hits, misses int
	data         map[int]*list.Element // hash table: key -> node
	order        *list.List            // linked list, most recently used at the front
}

func newCache(capacity int, ttl float64, loader func(int) int) *Cache {
	return &Cache{capacity: capacity, ttl: ttl, loader: loader, data: map[int]*list.Element{}, order: list.New()}
}

func (c *Cache) get(key int, now float64) int {
	el, ok := c.data[key]
	if ok {
		c.order.MoveToFront(el) // any access makes it the most recent
		if it := el.Value.(*item); it.expires > now {
			c.hits++
			return it.value
		}
	}
	c.misses++
	v := c.loader(key) // cache-aside: fetch from the source
	if ok {            // expired entry: refresh it in place
		*el.Value.(*item) = item{key, v, now + c.ttl}
		return v
	}
	c.data[key] = c.order.PushFront(&item{key, v, now + c.ttl})
	if c.order.Len() > c.capacity { // evict the least recently used
		old := c.order.Remove(c.order.Back()).(*item)
		delete(c.data, old.key)
	}
	return v
}

### loadbal
--idea
A load balancer spreads incoming requests over a group of servers so that none is overloaded and the group handles more traffic than one machine could. The hard part is that it must decide quickly, often without perfect knowledge of how busy each server is. Different policies trade simplicity against fairness.
Three policies cover most of the ground. Hashing the request key (client address, user id) always sends the same key to the same server, which helps caches, and Consistent hashing keeps most assignments stable when servers come and go. "Least connections" sends each request to the server with the fewest active ones, which needs a priority queue to find the minimum fast. "Power of two choices" picks two servers at random and uses the less loaded of the two. That tiny change makes the busiest server's load grow far more slowly than pure random choice, without needing a global view.
--build
1. Keep `load[i]`, the number of active requests on server `i`.
2. Least connections: keep a Heap of pairs `(load, server)` ordered by load. For each request pop the smallest pair, give the request to that server, and push the pair back with `load + 1`. When a request finishes, the load drops and the entry is updated (real balancers use a lazy update or an indexed heap).
3. Random: choose a server uniformly. It needs no state, but by chance some servers get noticeably more than the average.
4. Power of two choices (see Randomization): choose two servers `a` and `b` independently at random and send the request to the one with the smaller `load`.
5. Key hashing: `server = ring.owner(hash(key))` using Consistent hashing, so that adding a server remaps only about `1/n` of the keys.
6. To compare policies, send `m` requests to `n` servers and look at the maximum load. The model below returns it.
--think
- Signal: spreading work over workers, and "pick one of many" where information is costly. Choose by what matters: stickiness (hashing), best balance with a global view (least connections), or good balance with only local views (two choices).
- Why two choices works: if you throw `n` balls into `n` bins at random, the fullest bin gets about `ln n / ln ln n` balls. With the better of two random bins it is only about `ln ln n`. A second sample removes most of the bad luck. This is a famous result, and it is why large systems use it instead of asking every server.
- Least connections assumes a request's cost is similar. If requests vary widely, weight by estimated work or measure response time.
- Health matters as much as balance: skip servers that fail health checks, and ramp new servers up slowly (slow start) so they are not flooded.
- Mistakes: ignoring that many balancers run at once (each sees stale loads, so all pick the same "best" server together, a herd effect; two random choices avoids this), and hashing with a plain `hash % n` so that every server change reshuffles all keys.
--cost
Least connections costs O(log n) per request with a heap. Random and two choices cost O(1). Key hashing costs O(log n) with a ring lookup.
--java
static int leastConnections(int n, int m) {                  // returns the maximum load after m requests
    PriorityQueue<int[]> heap = new PriorityQueue<>((a, b) -> a[0] != b[0] ? a[0] - b[0] : a[1] - b[1]);
    for (int i = 0; i < n; i++) heap.add(new int[]{0, i});    // {load, server}
    int max = 0;
    for (int r = 0; r < m; r++) {
        int[] s = heap.poll();                                // least loaded server
        s[0]++;                                               // give it the request
        max = Math.max(max, s[0]);
        heap.add(s);
    }
    return max;
}

static int twoChoices(int n, int m, Random rnd) {             // power of two choices
    int[] load = new int[n];
    int max = 0;
    for (int r = 0; r < m; r++) {
        int a = rnd.nextInt(n), b = rnd.nextInt(n);
        int pick = load[a] <= load[b] ? a : b;                // the less loaded of two random servers
        max = Math.max(max, ++load[pick]);
    }
    return max;
}

static int randomChoice(int n, int m, Random rnd) {
    int[] load = new int[n];
    int max = 0;
    for (int r = 0; r < m; r++) max = Math.max(max, ++load[rnd.nextInt(n)]);
    return max;
}
--py
import heapq, random

def least_connections(n, m):                        # returns the maximum load after m requests
    heap = [(0, i) for i in range(n)]               # (load, server)
    heapq.heapify(heap)
    load = [0] * n
    for _ in range(m):
        l, i = heapq.heappop(heap)                  # least loaded server
        load[i] = l + 1                             # give it the request
        heapq.heappush(heap, (l + 1, i))
    return max(load)

def two_choices(n, m):                              # power of two choices
    load = [0] * n
    for _ in range(m):
        a, b = random.randrange(n), random.randrange(n)
        load[a if load[a] <= load[b] else b] += 1   # the less loaded of two random servers
    return max(load)

def random_choice(n, m):
    load = [0] * n
    for _ in range(m):
        load[random.randrange(n)] += 1
    return max(load)
--cpp
int leastConnections(int n, int m) {                  // returns the maximum load after m requests
    priority_queue<pair<int, int>, vector<pair<int, int>>, greater<pair<int, int>>> heap;   // {load, server}
    for (int i = 0; i < n; i++) heap.push({0, i});
    int mx = 0;
    for (int r = 0; r < m; r++) {
        auto [l, i] = heap.top(); heap.pop();         // least loaded server
        mx = max(mx, l + 1);                          // give it the request
        heap.push({l + 1, i});
    }
    return mx;
}

int twoChoices(int n, int m, mt19937& rng) {          // power of two choices
    vector<int> load(n, 0);
    for (int r = 0; r < m; r++) {
        int a = rng() % n, b = rng() % n;
        load[load[a] <= load[b] ? a : b]++;           // the less loaded of two random servers
    }
    return *max_element(load.begin(), load.end());
}

int randomChoice(int n, int m, mt19937& rng) {
    vector<int> load(n, 0);
    for (int r = 0; r < m; r++) load[rng() % n]++;
    return *max_element(load.begin(), load.end());
}
--go
import (
	"container/heap"
	"math/rand"
)

type servers [][2]int // {load, server}, a min-heap ordered by load then id

func (s servers) Len() int           { return len(s) }
func (s servers) Less(i, j int) bool { return s[i][0] < s[j][0] || s[i][0] == s[j][0] && s[i][1] < s[j][1] }
func (s servers) Swap(i, j int)      { s[i], s[j] = s[j], s[i] }
func (s *servers) Push(x any)        { *s = append(*s, x.([2]int)) }
func (s *servers) Pop() any          { o := *s; x := o[len(o)-1]; *s = o[:len(o)-1]; return x }

func leastConnections(n, m int) int { // returns the maximum load after m requests
	h := make(servers, n)
	for i := range h {
		h[i] = [2]int{0, i}
	}
	maxLoad := 0
	for r := 0; r < m; r++ {
		h[0][0]++ // the root is the least loaded server: give it the request
		maxLoad = max(maxLoad, h[0][0])
		heap.Fix(&h, 0) // sift the changed root back down
	}
	return maxLoad
}

func twoChoices(n, m int, rnd *rand.Rand) int { // power of two choices
	load := make([]int, n)
	maxLoad := 0
	for r := 0; r < m; r++ {
		a, b := rnd.Intn(n), rnd.Intn(n)
		pick := b
		if load[a] <= load[b] { // the less loaded of two random servers
			pick = a
		}
		load[pick]++
		maxLoad = max(maxLoad, load[pick])
	}
	return maxLoad
}

func randomChoice(n, m int, rnd *rand.Rand) int {
	load := make([]int, n)
	maxLoad := 0
	for r := 0; r < m; r++ {
		i := rnd.Intn(n)
		load[i]++
		maxLoad = max(maxLoad, load[i])
	}
	return maxLoad
}

### sharding
--idea
Sharding (also called partitioning) splits a dataset over many machines, so that no single machine has to store or serve all of it. Each record belongs to one shard, chosen from its key, for example a user id. Reads and writes for that key go straight to the right machine, so capacity grows by adding machines.
The routing rule decides how painful growth is. The obvious rule is `shard = hash(key) % numberOfShards`, but adding one shard changes the divisor and moves almost every key to a new machine, which means copying nearly all the data. Consistent hashing places shards and keys on a circle and gives each key to the next shard clockwise, so adding a shard takes keys from only its neighbours and moves roughly `1/n` of the data.
--build
1. Choose a good 64-bit hash function for keys and shard names (here FNV-1a followed by a bit mixer, so similar names do not land close together).
2. Build the ring: for each shard create many virtual nodes, named `shard#0`, `shard#1`, ... and hash each name to a position. Keep the `(position, shard)` pairs sorted. Many virtual nodes per shard make the load even and spread a failed shard's keys over many others. This is Consistent hashing.
3. Route a key: hash it and find the first ring position that is greater than or equal to the key's hash, using a lower bound binary search on the sorted ring. If there is none, wrap around to the first entry.
4. Compare with the simple rule `shards[hash(key) % len(shards)]`, which uses plain Hash tables-style bucketing.
5. Measure growth: take a sample of keys, route them with the old and the new shard list, and count how many changed owner. That count is the amount of data that must be copied when you add the shard.
6. Real systems also keep a directory of shard locations (often itself replicated), replicate each shard on a few machines, and sometimes use many more shards than machines ("pre-splitting") so that moving whole shards, not keys, rebalances load.
--think
- Signal: data or traffic too big for one machine, and growth over time. Use hash sharding for even spread, range sharding (by key ranges) when you need ordered scans, and a lookup directory when you need full control over placement.
- Hash sharding loses range queries: neighbouring keys are on different shards. Range sharding keeps them together but can create hot spots (all new timestamps hit the last range).
- Hot keys: one very popular key overloads its shard regardless of the scheme. Fix with key splitting, caching or replication of that key.
- Cross-shard operations are the real cost: a transaction or join across shards needs coordination (see Transactions and deadlock detection) or is avoided by choosing the shard key to keep related data together (all of one customer's rows on one shard).
- Mistakes: using `hash % n` and then being surprised by a full reshuffle, too few virtual nodes (uneven load), and a weak hash that clusters.
--cost
Routing O(log V) for V virtual nodes. Adding a shard moves about 1/n of the keys with the ring, versus about (n-1)/n with plain modulo.
--java
static long hash64(String s) {                                       // FNV-1a, then a final mix
    long h = 0xcbf29ce484222325L;
    for (int i = 0; i < s.length(); i++) { h ^= s.charAt(i); h *= 0x100000001b3L; }
    h ^= h >>> 30; h *= 0xbf58476d1ce4e5b9L;
    h ^= h >>> 27; h *= 0x94d049bb133111ebL;
    return h ^ (h >>> 31);
}

static TreeMap<Long, String> buildRing(List<String> shards, int vnodes) {
    TreeMap<Long, String> ring = new TreeMap<>();
    for (String s : shards) for (int i = 0; i < vnodes; i++) ring.put(hash64(s + "#" + i), s);   // virtual nodes
    return ring;
}

static String routeRing(TreeMap<Long, String> ring, String key) {
    Map.Entry<Long, String> e = ring.ceilingEntry(hash64(key));      // first position >= hash (lower bound)
    return (e != null ? e : ring.firstEntry()).getValue();           // wrap around
}

static String routeMod(List<String> shards, String key) {
    return shards.get((int) Long.remainderUnsigned(hash64(key), shards.size()));
}

static double movedFraction(List<String> keys, java.util.function.Function<String, String> before,
                            java.util.function.Function<String, String> after) {
    int moved = 0;
    for (String k : keys) if (!before.apply(k).equals(after.apply(k))) moved++;   // data that must be copied
    return (double) moved / keys.size();
}
--py
import bisect

M64 = 0xFFFFFFFFFFFFFFFF

def hash64(s):                                       # FNV-1a, then a final mix
    h = 0xcbf29ce484222325
    for ch in s:
        h = ((h ^ ord(ch)) * 0x100000001b3) & M64
    h ^= h >> 30; h = (h * 0xbf58476d1ce4e5b9) & M64
    h ^= h >> 27; h = (h * 0x94d049bb133111eb) & M64
    return h ^ (h >> 31)

def build_ring(shards, vnodes=50):
    return sorted((hash64(f"{s}#{i}"), s) for s in shards for i in range(vnodes))   # virtual nodes

def route_ring(ring, key):
    i = bisect.bisect_left(ring, (hash64(key),))     # first position >= hash (lower bound)
    return ring[i % len(ring)][1]                    # wrap around

def route_mod(shards, key):
    return shards[hash64(key) % len(shards)]

def moved_fraction(keys, before, after):             # before/after: functions key -> shard
    return sum(before(k) != after(k) for k in keys) / len(keys)   # data that must be copied
--cpp
typedef unsigned long long u64;

u64 hash64(const string& s) {                         // FNV-1a, then a final mix
    u64 h = 0xcbf29ce484222325ULL;
    for (unsigned char ch : s) { h ^= ch; h *= 0x100000001b3ULL; }
    h ^= h >> 30; h *= 0xbf58476d1ce4e5b9ULL;
    h ^= h >> 27; h *= 0x94d049bb133111ebULL;
    return h ^ (h >> 31);
}

map<u64, string> buildRing(const vector<string>& shards, int vnodes = 50) {
    map<u64, string> ring;
    for (auto& s : shards) for (int i = 0; i < vnodes; i++) ring[hash64(s + "#" + to_string(i))] = s;   // virtual nodes
    return ring;
}

string routeRing(const map<u64, string>& ring, const string& key) {
    auto it = ring.lower_bound(hash64(key));          // first position >= hash
    if (it == ring.end()) it = ring.begin();          // wrap around
    return it->second;
}

string routeMod(const vector<string>& shards, const string& key) { return shards[hash64(key) % shards.size()]; }
