double movedFraction(const vector<string>& keys, function<string(const string&)> before, function<string(const string&)> after) {
    int moved = 0;
    for (auto& k : keys) if (before(k) != after(k)) moved++;   // data that must be copied
    return (double)moved / keys.size();
}
--go
import (
	"fmt"
	"sort"
)

func hash64(s string) uint64 { // FNV-1a, then a final mix
	h := uint64(0xcbf29ce484222325)
	for i := 0; i < len(s); i++ {
		h ^= uint64(s[i])
		h *= 0x100000001b3
	}
	h ^= h >> 30
	h *= 0xbf58476d1ce4e5b9
	h ^= h >> 27
	h *= 0x94d049bb133111eb
	return h ^ h>>31
}

type Ring struct {
	pos   []uint64          // sorted positions on the ring
	owner map[uint64]string // position -> shard
}

func buildRing(shards []string, vnodes int) *Ring {
	r := &Ring{owner: map[uint64]string{}}
	for _, s := range shards {
		for i := 0; i < vnodes; i++ { // virtual nodes
			p := hash64(fmt.Sprintf("%s#%d", s, i))
			r.pos = append(r.pos, p)
			r.owner[p] = s
		}
	}
	sort.Slice(r.pos, func(i, j int) bool { return r.pos[i] < r.pos[j] })
	return r
}

func routeRing(r *Ring, key string) string {
	h := hash64(key)
	i := sort.Search(len(r.pos), func(i int) bool { return r.pos[i] >= h }) // first position >= hash
	if i == len(r.pos) {
		i = 0 // wrap around
	}
	return r.owner[r.pos[i]]
}

func routeMod(shards []string, key string) string {
	return shards[hash64(key)%uint64(len(shards))]
}

func movedFraction(keys []string, before, after func(string) string) float64 {
	moved := 0
	for _, k := range keys {
		if before(k) != after(k) { // data that must be copied
			moved++
		}
	}
	return float64(moved) / float64(len(keys))
}

### merkle
--idea
A Merkle tree is a tree of hashes that summarises a big collection of data in one short value and lets two holders of a copy find exactly where they differ, without sending the data. A hash function turns any chunk of data into a short fixed-size fingerprint, and a different chunk almost certainly gives a different fingerprint.
Split the data into chunks and hash each chunk: those are the leaves. Then hash each pair of neighbouring hashes together to get the parent, and repeat until one hash is left, the root. If two replicas have the same root, they hold the same data. If the roots differ, compare the two children: any child whose hash matches is identical and can be skipped, and you only go down into the children that differ. After about `log n` steps per differing chunk you know which chunks to copy.
--build
1. Pick a hash (here a 64-bit mixer for readability; real systems use a cryptographic hash such as SHA-256, see Cryptographic hashes and passwords, and also String hashing for the idea of fingerprinting text). Define `leafHash(chunk)` and `combine(left, right)`.
2. Split the data into chunks (for example 4 KB blocks or rows) and compute the leaf hashes: this is `levels[0]`.
3. Build the next level up: pair neighbours, `parent = combine(left, right)`. If a level has an odd count, pair the last hash with itself. Continue until a level has one hash. That level is the root. Store all levels (this is a complete Binary tree stored level by level in arrays).
4. To compare two trees with the same shape, start at the root. If the hashes are equal, stop: nothing differs. Otherwise go to the two children of the node and repeat for each. At level 0 a mismatch is a differing chunk: record its index.
5. Count how many hashes were compared. If only a few chunks differ, this is about `d * log n` instead of `n`.
6. A proof that one chunk belongs to a tree is the chunk plus the sibling hashes along its path to the root, about `log n` hashes.
--think
- Signal: two large copies must agree, or you need to verify a piece of data against a trusted short value. Replicated databases (anti-entropy repair in Cassandra and Dynamo-style systems), Git objects, Bitcoin transactions, certificate transparency logs, file download verification.
- The trade-off is hashing cost and tree maintenance versus transfer savings. For a small change you rehash only one path from leaf to root, `log n` hashes.
- Security depends on the hash being collision resistant. With a weak hash an attacker could swap data without changing the root. The mixer in the model is not secure, only illustrative.
- At scale: choose the chunk size to balance tree depth and chunk transfer cost, and keep trees per key range so one write does not rehash everything.
- Mistakes: comparing trees of different shapes (the data must be chunked the same way), forgetting to handle an odd node at a level, hashing `a + b` without a separator so that different splits give the same input (here `combine` is order sensitive and takes two fixed-size values).
--cost
Build O(n). Compare O(d log n) hash comparisons for d differing chunks. A membership proof is O(log n) hashes.
--java
static final long GOLD = 0x9E3779B97F4A7C15L;

static long mix(long x) {                                           // 64-bit bit mixer (not cryptographic)
    x ^= x >>> 30; x *= 0xbf58476d1ce4e5b9L;
    x ^= x >>> 27; x *= 0x94d049bb133111ebL;
    return x ^ (x >>> 31);
}

static long leafHash(String chunk) {
    long h = 0xcbf29ce484222325L;
    for (int i = 0; i < chunk.length(); i++) { h ^= chunk.charAt(i); h *= 0x100000001b3L; }
    return mix(h);
}

static long combine(long a, long b) { return mix(a * GOLD ^ mix(b)); }    // order sensitive

static List<long[]> build(String[] chunks) {                        // levels.get(0) = leaves, last = {root}
    List<long[]> levels = new ArrayList<>();
    long[] cur = new long[chunks.length];
    for (int i = 0; i < chunks.length; i++) cur[i] = leafHash(chunks[i]);
    levels.add(cur);
    while (cur.length > 1) {
        long[] next = new long[(cur.length + 1) / 2];
        for (int i = 0; i < next.length; i++)
            next[i] = combine(cur[2 * i], 2 * i + 1 < cur.length ? cur[2 * i + 1] : cur[2 * i]);   // odd node pairs with itself
        levels.add(next);
        cur = next;
    }
    return levels;
}

// both trees have the same shape. Returns indices of differing chunks; compared[0] counts hash comparisons.
static List<Integer> diff(List<long[]> A, List<long[]> B, int[] compared) {
    List<Integer> out = new ArrayList<>();
    Deque<int[]> stack = new ArrayDeque<>();
    stack.push(new int[]{A.size() - 1, 0});                          // {level, index}: start at the root
    while (!stack.isEmpty()) {
        int[] t = stack.pop();
        compared[0]++;
        if (A.get(t[0])[t[1]] == B.get(t[0])[t[1]]) continue;        // identical subtree: skip it
        if (t[0] == 0) { out.add(t[1]); continue; }                  // a differing chunk
        for (int c = 2 * t[1]; c <= 2 * t[1] + 1 && c < A.get(t[0] - 1).length; c++) stack.push(new int[]{t[0] - 1, c});
    }
    Collections.sort(out);
    return out;
}
--py
M64 = 0xFFFFFFFFFFFFFFFF
GOLD = 0x9E3779B97F4A7C15

def mix(x):                                          # 64-bit bit mixer (not cryptographic)
    x ^= x >> 30; x = (x * 0xbf58476d1ce4e5b9) & M64
    x ^= x >> 27; x = (x * 0x94d049bb133111eb) & M64
    return x ^ (x >> 31)

def leaf_hash(chunk):
    h = 0xcbf29ce484222325
    for ch in chunk:
        h = ((h ^ ord(ch)) * 0x100000001b3) & M64
    return mix(h)

def combine(a, b):                                   # order sensitive
    return mix(((a * GOLD) & M64) ^ mix(b))

def build(chunks):                                   # levels[0] = leaves, levels[-1] = [root]
    cur = [leaf_hash(c) for c in chunks]
    levels = [cur]
    while len(cur) > 1:
        cur = [combine(cur[i], cur[i + 1] if i + 1 < len(cur) else cur[i])   # odd node pairs with itself
               for i in range(0, len(cur), 2)]
        levels.append(cur)
    return levels

def diff(A, B):
    """Both trees have the same shape. Returns (indices of differing chunks, number of hash comparisons)."""
    out, compared, stack = [], 0, [(len(A) - 1, 0)]       # start at the root
    while stack:
        lv, i = stack.pop()
        compared += 1
        if A[lv][i] == B[lv][i]:
            continue                                      # identical subtree: skip it
        if lv == 0:
            out.append(i)                                 # a differing chunk
        else:
            stack += [(lv - 1, c) for c in (2 * i, 2 * i + 1) if c < len(A[lv - 1])]
    return sorted(out), compared
--cpp
typedef unsigned long long u64;
const u64 GOLD = 0x9E3779B97F4A7C15ULL;

u64 mix(u64 x) {                                      // 64-bit bit mixer (not cryptographic)
    x ^= x >> 30; x *= 0xbf58476d1ce4e5b9ULL;
    x ^= x >> 27; x *= 0x94d049bb133111ebULL;
    return x ^ (x >> 31);
}

u64 leafHash(const string& chunk) {
    u64 h = 0xcbf29ce484222325ULL;
    for (unsigned char ch : chunk) { h ^= ch; h *= 0x100000001b3ULL; }
    return mix(h);
}

u64 combine(u64 a, u64 b) { return mix(a * GOLD ^ mix(b)); }   // order sensitive

vector<vector<u64>> build(const vector<string>& chunks) {      // levels[0] = leaves, back() = {root}
    vector<u64> cur;
    for (auto& c : chunks) cur.push_back(leafHash(c));
    vector<vector<u64>> levels = {cur};
    while (cur.size() > 1) {
        vector<u64> next;
        for (size_t i = 0; i < cur.size(); i += 2)
            next.push_back(combine(cur[i], i + 1 < cur.size() ? cur[i + 1] : cur[i]));   // odd node pairs with itself
        levels.push_back(next);
        cur = next;
    }
    return levels;
}

// both trees have the same shape. Returns indices of differing chunks; `compared` counts hash comparisons.
vector<int> diff(const vector<vector<u64>>& A, const vector<vector<u64>>& B, int& compared) {
    vector<int> out;
    vector<pair<int, int>> stack = {{(int)A.size() - 1, 0}};        // {level, index}: start at the root
    compared = 0;
    while (!stack.empty()) {
        auto [lv, i] = stack.back(); stack.pop_back();
        compared++;
        if (A[lv][i] == B[lv][i]) continue;                         // identical subtree: skip it
        if (lv == 0) { out.push_back(i); continue; }                // a differing chunk
        for (int c = 2 * i; c <= 2 * i + 1 && c < (int)A[lv - 1].size(); c++) stack.push_back({lv - 1, c});
    }
    sort(out.begin(), out.end());
    return out;
}
--go
import "sort"

const gold = 0x9E3779B97F4A7C15

func mix(x uint64) uint64 { // 64-bit bit mixer (not cryptographic)
	x ^= x >> 30
	x *= 0xbf58476d1ce4e5b9
	x ^= x >> 27
	x *= 0x94d049bb133111eb
	return x ^ x>>31
}

func leafHash(chunk string) uint64 {
	h := uint64(0xcbf29ce484222325)
	for i := 0; i < len(chunk); i++ {
		h ^= uint64(chunk[i])
		h *= 0x100000001b3
	}
	return mix(h)
}

func combine(a, b uint64) uint64 { return mix(a*gold ^ mix(b)) } // order sensitive

func build(chunks []string) [][]uint64 { // levels[0] = leaves, last = {root}
	cur := make([]uint64, len(chunks))
	for i, c := range chunks {
		cur[i] = leafHash(c)
	}
	levels := [][]uint64{cur}
	for len(cur) > 1 {
		next := make([]uint64, (len(cur)+1)/2)
		for i := range next {
			r := cur[2*i]
			if 2*i+1 < len(cur) { // an odd node pairs with itself
				r = cur[2*i+1]
			}
			next[i] = combine(cur[2*i], r)
		}
		levels = append(levels, next)
		cur = next
	}
	return levels
}

// both trees have the same shape. Returns indices of differing chunks and the hash comparisons made.
func diff(A, B [][]uint64) (out []int, compared int) {
	stack := [][2]int{{len(A) - 1, 0}} // {level, index}: start at the root
	for len(stack) > 0 {
		t := stack[len(stack)-1]
		stack = stack[:len(stack)-1]
		compared++
		if A[t[0]][t[1]] == B[t[0]][t[1]] { // identical subtree: skip it
			continue
		}
		if t[0] == 0 { // a differing chunk
			out = append(out, t[1])
			continue
		}
		for c := 2 * t[1]; c <= 2*t[1]+1 && c < len(A[t[0]-1]); c++ {
			stack = append(stack, [2]int{t[0] - 1, c})
		}
	}
	sort.Ints(out)
	return
}

### dht
--idea
A distributed hash table (DHT) is a lookup service spread over many machines (peers) with no central server. Every peer and every key has an ID, and the peer whose ID is "closest" to a key is responsible for it. BitTorrent's trackerless mode and many peer-to-peer systems use Kademlia, which measures closeness with the XOR of two IDs read as a number.
XOR distance is a neat choice because it is symmetric and has a tree structure: two IDs that share a long common prefix are close. Each peer keeps only a few contacts, grouped in buckets by how many leading bits they share with it. To find the peer closest to a target, you ask the nearest contacts you know, they reply with the nearest contacts they know, and each round gets at least one bit closer. So a network of `n` peers needs about `log n` rounds.
--build
1. Give every peer a random `BITS`-bit ID (here 16 bits). The distance between two IDs `a` and `b` is `a XOR b` (see XOR tricks).
2. Routing table: a peer keeps up to `k` contacts for each bucket. Bucket `i` holds peers whose XOR distance from it has its highest set bit at position `i`, that is, peers that agree with it on all bits above `i` and differ at bit `i`. This is like walking a Trie of IDs: bucket `i` is "the other branch at depth `BITS - i`".
3. `closestKnown(node, target, k)`: from a peer's contacts plus itself, return the `k` with the smallest `XOR target`.
4. Lookup from a starting peer: make a shortlist of the `k` closest peers it knows. Repeatedly take the closest peer in the shortlist that has not been asked yet, ask it for its `k` closest to the target, merge them into the shortlist and keep the best `k`.
5. Stop when every peer in the shortlist has been asked. The closest peer on the list is the answer (the node responsible for the key). Count the asks as hops.
6. A peer joins by looking up its own ID through any known peer, which fills its buckets with the right neighbours, and others learn about it as it talks to them. Data for a key is stored on the `k` closest peers, and re-published periodically because peers leave.
--think
- Signal: you need a shared key-value lookup with no coordinator, huge numbers of nodes, and nodes that come and go. The trade-off is that lookups cost `log n` messages across the network rather than one hop to a central server.
- Compare with Consistent hashing: both place nodes and keys in one ID space and give a key to a nearby node. Consistent hashing as used inside a data centre keeps the whole ring in every node (one hop). A DHT keeps only `log n` contacts per node, and pays for it with several hops.
- Buckets far from the node's ID cover half the space, so they are filled from many candidates. Buckets close by hold few peers. Keeping up to `k` contacts per bucket and preferring long-lived ones makes the table resistant to churn and to attackers flooding it with new IDs.
- Failure and attack: lookups run in parallel (ask several peers at once) so slow or dead peers do not stall them. Sybil attacks, where one attacker makes many IDs, are the main security worry.
- Mistakes: measuring closeness with ordinary subtraction instead of XOR, putting a peer in the wrong bucket (use the highest set bit of the XOR), and stopping the lookup too early, before the closest `k` have all answered.
--cost
A lookup takes O(log n) rounds in a network of n peers, and each peer stores O(k log n) contacts.
--java
// tables.get(node) = contacts of that node. Returns {closestFound, hops}.
static int[] lookup(Map<Integer, List<Integer>> tables, int start, int target, int k) {
    Comparator<Integer> byDist = Comparator.comparingInt(x -> x ^ target);   // XOR distance
    List<Integer> shortlist = closestKnown(tables, start, target, k);
    Set<Integer> asked = new HashSet<>(); asked.add(start);
    int hops = 0;
    while (true) {
        Integer next = null;
        for (int c : shortlist) if (!asked.contains(c)) { next = c; break; }   // closest peer not yet asked
        if (next == null) break;
        asked.add(next); hops++;
        Set<Integer> merged = new HashSet<>(shortlist);
        merged.addAll(closestKnown(tables, next, target, k));
        shortlist = new ArrayList<>(merged);
        shortlist.sort(byDist);
        if (shortlist.size() > k) shortlist = new ArrayList<>(shortlist.subList(0, k));
    }
    return new int[]{shortlist.get(0), hops};
}

static List<Integer> closestKnown(Map<Integer, List<Integer>> tables, int node, int target, int k) {
    List<Integer> c = new ArrayList<>(tables.get(node));
    c.add(node);
    c.sort(Comparator.comparingInt(x -> x ^ target));
    return new ArrayList<>(c.subList(0, Math.min(k, c.size())));
}

static Map<Integer, List<Integer>> buildTables(List<Integer> ids, int bits, int k, Random rnd) {
    Map<Integer, List<Integer>> tables = new HashMap<>();
    for (int a : ids) {
        List<List<Integer>> bucket = new ArrayList<>();
        for (int i = 0; i < bits; i++) bucket.add(new ArrayList<>());
        for (int b : ids) if (b != a) bucket.get(31 - Integer.numberOfLeadingZeros(a ^ b)).add(b);   // highest differing bit
        List<Integer> table = new ArrayList<>();
        for (List<Integer> bk : bucket) { Collections.shuffle(bk, rnd); table.addAll(bk.subList(0, Math.min(k, bk.size()))); }
        tables.put(a, table);
    }
    return tables;
}
--py
import random

def build_tables(ids, bits=16, k=8):
    tables = {}
    for a in ids:
        bucket = [[] for _ in range(bits)]
        for b in ids:
            if b != a:
                bucket[(a ^ b).bit_length() - 1].append(b)     # bucket = highest differing bit
        table = []
        for bk in bucket:
            random.shuffle(bk)
            table += bk[:k]                                    # keep up to k contacts per bucket
        tables[a] = table
    return tables

def closest_known(tables, node, target, k):
    return sorted(tables[node] + [node], key=lambda x: x ^ target)[:k]   # XOR distance

def lookup(tables, start, target, k=8):
    shortlist = closest_known(tables, start, target, k)
    asked, hops = {start}, 0
    while True:
        todo = [c for c in shortlist if c not in asked]        # closest peer not yet asked comes first
        if not todo:
            break
        asked.add(todo[0]); hops += 1
        merged = set(shortlist) | set(closest_known(tables, todo[0], target, k))
        shortlist = sorted(merged, key=lambda x: x ^ target)[:k]
    return shortlist[0], hops
--cpp
typedef unordered_map<int, vector<int>> Tables;

Tables buildTables(const vector<int>& ids, int bits, int k, mt19937& rng) {
    Tables tables;
    for (int a : ids) {
        vector<vector<int>> bucket(bits);
        for (int b : ids) if (b != a) bucket[31 - __builtin_clz(a ^ b)].push_back(b);   // highest differing bit
        for (auto& bk : bucket) {
            shuffle(bk.begin(), bk.end(), rng);
            for (int i = 0; i < (int)bk.size() && i < k; i++) tables[a].push_back(bk[i]);   // up to k per bucket
        }
    }
    return tables;
}

vector<int> closestKnown(const Tables& tables, int node, int target, int k) {
    vector<int> c = tables.at(node);
    c.push_back(node);
    sort(c.begin(), c.end(), [&](int x, int y) { return (x ^ target) < (y ^ target); });   // XOR distance
    if ((int)c.size() > k) c.resize(k);
    return c;
}

pair<int, int> lookup(const Tables& tables, int start, int target, int k = 8) {   // {closest found, hops}
    vector<int> shortlist = closestKnown(tables, start, target, k);
    set<int> asked = {start};
    int hops = 0;
    while (true) {
        int next = -1;
        for (int c : shortlist) if (!asked.count(c)) { next = c; break; }   // closest peer not yet asked
        if (next < 0) break;
        asked.insert(next); hops++;
        set<int> merged(shortlist.begin(), shortlist.end());
        for (int x : closestKnown(tables, next, target, k)) merged.insert(x);
        shortlist.assign(merged.begin(), merged.end());
        sort(shortlist.begin(), shortlist.end(), [&](int x, int y) { return (x ^ target) < (y ^ target); });
        if ((int)shortlist.size() > k) shortlist.resize(k);
    }
    return {shortlist[0], hops};
}
--go
import (
	"math/bits"
	"math/rand"
	"slices"
)

// tables[node] = contacts of that node. Returns the closest id found and the hops used.
func lookup(tables map[int][]int, start, target, k int) (closest, hops int) {
	byDist := func(a, b int) int { return (a ^ target) - (b ^ target) } // XOR distance
	shortlist := closestKnown(tables, start, target, k)
	asked := map[int]bool{start: true}
	for {
		next, found := 0, false
		for _, c := range shortlist {
			if !asked[c] { // closest peer not yet asked
				next, found = c, true
				break
			}
		}
		if !found {
			break
		}
		asked[next] = true
		hops++
		merged := append(slices.Clone(shortlist), closestKnown(tables, next, target, k)...)
		slices.SortFunc(merged, byDist)
		merged = slices.Compact(merged) // equal ids have equal distance, so they sit side by side
		shortlist = merged[:min(k, len(merged))]
	}
	return shortlist[0], hops
}

func closestKnown(tables map[int][]int, node, target, k int) []int {
	c := append(slices.Clone(tables[node]), node)
	slices.SortFunc(c, func(a, b int) int { return (a ^ target) - (b ^ target) })
	return c[:min(k, len(c))]
}

func buildTables(ids []int, nbits, k int, rnd *rand.Rand) map[int][]int {
	tables := map[int][]int{}
	for _, a := range ids {
		bucket := make([][]int, nbits)
		for _, b := range ids {
			if b != a {
				i := bits.Len(uint(a^b)) - 1 // highest differing bit
				bucket[i] = append(bucket[i], b)
			}
		}
		for _, bk := range bucket {
			rnd.Shuffle(len(bk), func(i, j int) { bk[i], bk[j] = bk[j], bk[i] })
			tables[a] = append(tables[a], bk[:min(k, len(bk))]...)
		}
	}
	return tables
}
### gossip
--idea
A gossip protocol spreads information through a group of machines the way a rumour spreads through people. A machine that learns something new tells a few randomly chosen peers each round. Those peers tell a few others, and so on. There is no central coordinator and no machine needs to know the whole group, yet the news reaches everyone quickly and the scheme keeps working when machines fail.
It is fast because the informed group grows exponentially at first: if each informed machine tells 2 others per round, the number of informed machines roughly triples each round while most machines are still uninformed. So the news reaches all `n` machines in about `log n` rounds. Cassandra, Consul and many cluster systems use gossip to spread membership (who is alive) and small bits of state.
--build
1. Model the cluster as a graph (see BFS for the graph search used below): `adj[u]` lists the peers machine `u` can contact. For a fully connected cluster it is everyone else.
2. Keep the set `informed`, starting with the one machine that has the news.
3. One round: every informed machine picks `fanout` peers at random from `adj[u]` (see Randomization) and tells them. Add all the peers reached to a `new` set, and only after the whole round merge it into `informed`, so that news travels one step per round.
4. Count rounds until every machine is informed. Give up after a round limit, which happens if the graph is not connected.
5. As a reference, a BFS from the start machine gives `depth`, the fewest hops needed to reach the farthest machine. No gossip can finish in fewer rounds than that. On a fully connected graph the depth is 1, but gossip needs about `log n` rounds because each machine contacts only a few peers.
6. Real protocols also let machines pull updates from peers (anti-entropy), attach a version number to each update so newer replaces older, and stop spreading old news after a while.
--think
- Signal: spread state to many nodes with no leader, tolerate failures and joins, and accept that all nodes converge after a short delay (eventual consistency). Failure detection works the same way: gossip heartbeat counters and mark a machine dead if its counter stops growing.
- Why random targets: the load is spread evenly, no node is a bottleneck or a single point of failure, and a dead node simply wastes some sends. Each node sends only `fanout` messages per round however big the cluster is.
- Trade-off: some messages are redundant (a node can be told the same thing many times), and there is no guarantee for a given round, only a very high probability within a few rounds more than `log n`.
- Push, pull and push-pull: pushing is fast at the start, pulling is fast at the end when only a few are uninformed. Push-pull does both and finishes in about `log log n` extra rounds.
- Mistakes: updating `informed` while still looping over it (news then travels many hops in one round), not handling a disconnected graph, and expecting an exact round count (it is random; test with a range).
--cost
About O(log n) rounds on a well-connected graph, with O(n * fanout) messages per round at most. No machine sends more than `fanout` messages per round.
--java
static int bfsDepth(List<List<Integer>> adj, int start) {           // reference: fewest hops to the farthest node
    int[] dist = new int[adj.size()];
    Arrays.fill(dist, -1);
    Deque<Integer> q = new ArrayDeque<>();
    dist[start] = 0; q.add(start);
    int depth = 0;
    while (!q.isEmpty()) {
        int u = q.poll();
        for (int v : adj.get(u)) if (dist[v] < 0) { dist[v] = dist[u] + 1; depth = Math.max(depth, dist[v]); q.add(v); }
    }
    return depth;
}

static int gossipRounds(List<List<Integer>> adj, int start, int fanout, Random rnd, int maxRounds) {
    int n = adj.size();
    boolean[] informed = new boolean[n];
    informed[start] = true;
    int count = 1, rounds = 0;
    while (count < n) {
        if (++rounds > maxRounds) return -1;                         // not connected, or unlucky
        List<Integer> fresh = new ArrayList<>();
        for (int u = 0; u < n; u++) {
            if (!informed[u] || adj.get(u).isEmpty()) continue;
            for (int f = 0; f < fanout; f++) fresh.add(adj.get(u).get(rnd.nextInt(adj.get(u).size())));   // tell a random peer
        }
        for (int v : fresh) if (!informed[v]) { informed[v] = true; count++; }   // merge after the round
    }
    return rounds;
}
--py
import random
from collections import deque

def bfs_depth(adj, start):                           # reference: fewest hops to the farthest node
    dist = {start: 0}
    q = deque([start])
    while q:
        u = q.popleft()
        for v in adj[u]:
            if v not in dist:
                dist[v] = dist[u] + 1
                q.append(v)
    return max(dist.values())

def gossip_rounds(adj, start, fanout=2, max_rounds=1000):
    n = len(adj)
    informed = {start}
    rounds = 0
    while len(informed) < n:
        rounds += 1
        if rounds > max_rounds:                      # not connected, or unlucky
            return -1
        fresh = set()
        for u in informed:
            if adj[u]:
                for _ in range(fanout):
                    fresh.add(random.choice(adj[u]))     # tell a random peer
        informed |= fresh                            # merge after the round: news moves one step per round
    return rounds
--cpp
int bfsDepth(const vector<vector<int>>& adj, int start) {            // reference: fewest hops to the farthest node
    vector<int> dist(adj.size(), -1);
    queue<int> q;
    dist[start] = 0; q.push(start);
    int depth = 0;
    while (!q.empty()) {
        int u = q.front(); q.pop();
        for (int v : adj[u]) if (dist[v] < 0) { dist[v] = dist[u] + 1; depth = max(depth, dist[v]); q.push(v); }
    }
    return depth;
}

int gossipRounds(const vector<vector<int>>& adj, int start, int fanout, mt19937& rng, int maxRounds = 1000) {
    int n = adj.size(), count = 1, rounds = 0;
    vector<char> informed(n, 0);
    informed[start] = 1;
    while (count < n) {
        if (++rounds > maxRounds) return -1;                         // not connected, or unlucky
        vector<int> fresh;
        for (int u = 0; u < n; u++) {
            if (!informed[u] || adj[u].empty()) continue;
            for (int f = 0; f < fanout; f++) fresh.push_back(adj[u][rng() % adj[u].size()]);   // tell a random peer
        }
        for (int v : fresh) if (!informed[v]) { informed[v] = 1; count++; }   // merge after the round
    }
    return rounds;
}
--go
import "math/rand"

func bfsDepth(adj [][]int, start int) int { // reference: fewest hops to the farthest node
	dist := make([]int, len(adj))
	for i := range dist {
		dist[i] = -1
	}
	dist[start] = 0
	q, depth := []int{start}, 0
	for len(q) > 0 {
		u := q[0]
		q = q[1:]
		for _, v := range adj[u] {
			if dist[v] < 0 {
				dist[v] = dist[u] + 1
				depth = max(depth, dist[v])
				q = append(q, v)
			}
		}
	}
	return depth
}

func gossipRounds(adj [][]int, start, fanout int, rnd *rand.Rand, maxRounds int) int {
	n := len(adj)
	informed := make([]bool, n)
	informed[start] = true
	count, rounds := 1, 0
	for count < n {
		if rounds++; rounds > maxRounds { // not connected, or unlucky
			return -1
		}
		var fresh []int
		for u := 0; u < n; u++ {
			if !informed[u] || len(adj[u]) == 0 {
				continue
			}
			for f := 0; f < fanout; f++ {
				fresh = append(fresh, adj[u][rnd.Intn(len(adj[u]))]) // tell a random peer
			}
		}
		for _, v := range fresh { // merge after the round
			if !informed[v] {
				informed[v] = true
				count++
			}
		}
	}
	return rounds
}

### ratelimiter
--idea
A rate limiter decides whether a request may go through, so that no client can use more than its fair share and a flood cannot overload a service. API gateways, login pages and message services all use one. The answer is just yes or no (or "retry later"), and it must be cheap, because it runs on every single request.
Several designs exist. The token bucket (see Token bucket) allows bursts up to a capacity and a steady long-run rate. The sliding window log remembers the exact time of each recent request and allows a new one only if fewer than `limit` happened in the last `window` seconds. It is exact but stores up to `limit` timestamps per client. The sliding window counter keeps only two counters, for the current and previous fixed windows, and estimates the count in the sliding window by weighting the previous one. It uses constant memory and is slightly approximate.
--build
1. Choose the rule in words first: "at most `limit` requests in any `window` seconds, per client key". Keep one limiter object per key in a hash map.
2. Sliding window log: keep a queue (a Deque) of the timestamps of accepted requests, oldest at the front.
3. On a request at time `now`: remove from the front every timestamp `<= now - window`, since they have left the window (this is the Sliding window technique: the left edge moves forward). If the queue now holds fewer than `limit` entries, push `now` and accept. Otherwise reject.
4. Sliding window counter: split time into fixed windows of length `window`. Keep `cur` (accepted in the current window) and `prev` (in the one before). At time `now`, with `elapsed = now - windowStart`, estimate the count as `prev * (1 - elapsed / window) + cur`. If the estimate is below `limit`, increment `cur` and accept. When time moves into a new window, `prev` becomes the old `cur` (or 0 if more than one window passed) and `cur` resets.
5. Token bucket (the topic of its own lesson) is the third option, good when bursts are fine.
6. In a cluster the counters live in a shared store (often Redis) or each node limits locally with a share of the total, trading accuracy for speed.
--think
- Signal: "at most N per period". Decide the tolerance for bursts. A fixed window counter is simplest but allows twice the limit at a window boundary (a burst at the end of one window and the start of the next). The sliding versions fix that.
- Memory versus precision: the log is exact but costs O(limit) per client, which is too much for a limit of a million. The counter uses two numbers per client and is usually within a few percent.
- Where to limit: as early as possible (the edge), by a key that matches the abuse (API key, user, IP address, or a mix). Return a retry hint, for example how long until the oldest timestamp leaves the window.
- Failure mode: decide what happens when the limiter's store is down. Failing open keeps the service up and failing closed protects it. Both are valid choices.
- Mistakes: using wall-clock time that can jump (use a monotonic clock), rejected requests being recorded in the log (they should not count against the client), and forgetting that a deque pop must use `<=` or `<` consistently so the window is exactly `window` long.
--cost
Log: O(1) amortised time, O(limit) space per key. Counter: O(1) time and O(1) space per key.
--java
static class SlidingLog {
    final int limit; final double window;
    final Deque<Double> times = new ArrayDeque<>();                  // accepted request times, oldest first
    SlidingLog(int limit, double window) { this.limit = limit; this.window = window; }

    boolean allow(double now) {
        while (!times.isEmpty() && times.peekFirst() <= now - window) times.pollFirst();   // left the window
        if (times.size() < limit) { times.addLast(now); return true; }
        return false;
    }
}

static class SlidingCounter {
    final int limit; final double window;
    double curStart = 0, cur = 0, prev = 0;
    SlidingCounter(int limit, double window) { this.limit = limit; this.window = window; }

    boolean allow(double now) {
        double start = Math.floor(now / window) * window;            // start of the current fixed window
        if (start != curStart) { prev = (start == curStart + window) ? cur : 0; cur = 0; curStart = start; }
        double estimate = prev * (1 - (now - start) / window) + cur; // weighted count over the sliding window
        if (estimate < limit) { cur++; return true; }
        return false;
    }
}
--py
from collections import deque

class SlidingLog:
    def __init__(self, limit, window):
        self.limit, self.window = limit, window
        self.times = deque()                         # accepted request times, oldest first

    def allow(self, now):
        while self.times and self.times[0] <= now - self.window:
            self.times.popleft()                     # left the window
        if len(self.times) < self.limit:
            self.times.append(now)
            return True
        return False

class SlidingCounter:
    def __init__(self, limit, window):
        self.limit, self.window = limit, window
        self.cur_start, self.cur, self.prev = 0.0, 0, 0

    def allow(self, now):
        start = (now // self.window) * self.window   # start of the current fixed window
        if start != self.cur_start:
            self.prev = self.cur if start == self.cur_start + self.window else 0
            self.cur, self.cur_start = 0, start
        estimate = self.prev * (1 - (now - start) / self.window) + self.cur   # weighted count over the sliding window
        if estimate < self.limit:
            self.cur += 1
            return True
        return False
--cpp
struct SlidingLog {
    int limit; double window;
    deque<double> times;                             // accepted request times, oldest first
    SlidingLog(int limit, double window) : limit(limit), window(window) {}

    bool allow(double now) {
        while (!times.empty() && times.front() <= now - window) times.pop_front();   // left the window
        if ((int)times.size() < limit) { times.push_back(now); return true; }
        return false;
    }
};

struct SlidingCounter {
    int limit; double window;
    double curStart = 0, cur = 0, prev = 0;
    SlidingCounter(int limit, double window) : limit(limit), window(window) {}

    bool allow(double now) {
        double start = floor(now / window) * window;                 // start of the current fixed window
        if (start != curStart) { prev = (start == curStart + window) ? cur : 0; cur = 0; curStart = start; }
        double estimate = prev * (1 - (now - start) / window) + cur; // weighted count over the sliding window
        if (estimate < limit) { cur++; return true; }
        return false;
    }
};
--go
import "math"

type SlidingLog struct {
	limit  int
	window float64
	times  []float64 // accepted request times, oldest first
}

func (s *SlidingLog) allow(now float64) bool {
	for len(s.times) > 0 && s.times[0] <= now-s.window { // left the window
		s.times = s.times[1:]
	}
	if len(s.times) < s.limit {
		s.times = append(s.times, now)
		return true
	}
	return false
}

type SlidingCounter struct {
	limit                int
	window               float64
	curStart, cur, prev float64
}

func (s *SlidingCounter) allow(now float64) bool {
	start := math.Floor(now/s.window) * s.window // start of the current fixed window
	if start != s.curStart {
		s.prev = 0
		if start == s.curStart+s.window { // adjacent window: it becomes the previous one
			s.prev = s.cur
		}
		s.cur, s.curStart = 0, start
	}
	estimate := s.prev*(1-(now-start)/s.window) + s.cur // weighted count over the sliding window
	if estimate < float64(s.limit) {
		s.cur++
		return true
	}
	return false
}

### routing
--idea
A router decides, for each packet, which neighbour to forward it to so that it reaches its destination by a good path. Every router builds a routing table: for each destination, the next hop and the cost. Two families of protocols build these tables in different ways, and both are graph shortest-path algorithms in disguise.
Link-state protocols such as OSPF (Open Shortest Path First) have each router learn the whole network map by flooding link descriptions to everybody, and then run Dijkstra on that map from its own position. Distance-vector protocols such as RIP (Routing Information Protocol) never see the map. Each router tells its neighbours "I can reach X at cost c", and each router updates its own table from what its neighbours say. This is the Bellman-Ford algorithm, run in a distributed way.
--build
1. Model the network as a weighted graph: nodes are routers, edges are links with a cost (see Graph representation). Links work both ways, so add each edge in both directions.
2. Link-state (OSPF): build `adj` and run Dijkstra from the router `src`. Use a Heap of `(distance, node)`. When the edge `u -> v` improves `dist[v]`, also set `hop[v]`: the first router on the path, which is `v` itself if `u` is `src`, otherwise `hop[u]`. The forwarding table is `hop[destination]`.
3. Distance-vector (RIP): every router `u` keeps `dist[u][dst]`, its best known cost to each destination, starting at 0 for itself and infinity for the rest.
4. One exchange: for every link `(u, v, w)` and every destination `dst`, if `w + dist[v][dst] < dist[u][dst]`, then router `u` has found a better way through neighbour `v`: update it (and remember `v` as the next hop). This is Bellman-Ford's relaxation step.
5. Repeat the exchange until nothing changes, which happens within `n - 1` rounds for `n` routers, because a shortest path has at most `n - 1` links.
6. Both methods give the same shortest costs. Real networks add areas, link failures (re-flood or re-advertise), and tie-breaking.
--think
- Trade-offs: link-state converges fast and each router has the full map, but needs more memory and flooding traffic. Distance-vector is simple and uses little memory, but is slow to react to bad news ("count to infinity": after a link fails, routers keep feeding each other old routes and the cost grows step by step). RIP caps the cost at 16 to stop that, and uses tricks such as split horizon.
- Signal for the algorithm choice: non-negative costs and one source, use Dijkstra. Many sources, or negative or changing information flowing hop by hop, Bellman-Ford fits.
- Internet scale: no one protocol runs the whole internet. Inside an organisation (OSPF, IS-IS) routers know the whole map. Between organisations BGP uses path vectors and policies, because the rule is who agrees to carry whose traffic, not just the shortest path.
- Mistakes: forgetting links are two-way, a Dijkstra with negative weights (not allowed), updating `hop` incorrectly for the first edge out of the source, and stopping the Bellman-Ford rounds too early.
--cost
Dijkstra with a heap: O(E log V) per router. Distance vector: O(V * E) per full exchange round in this model and up to V - 1 rounds in total.
--java
static final int INF = 1_000_000_000;

// edges: {u, v, w}, undirected. Returns dist, and fills hop[] with the first router on each path.
static int[] ospf(int n, int[][] edges, int src, int[] hop) {
    List<List<int[]>> adj = new ArrayList<>();
    for (int i = 0; i < n; i++) adj.add(new ArrayList<>());
    for (int[] e : edges) { adj.get(e[0]).add(new int[]{e[1], e[2]}); adj.get(e[1]).add(new int[]{e[0], e[2]}); }
    int[] dist = new int[n];
    Arrays.fill(dist, INF); Arrays.fill(hop, -1);
    dist[src] = 0;
    PriorityQueue<int[]> pq = new PriorityQueue<>((a, b) -> a[0] - b[0]);   // {distance, node}
    pq.add(new int[]{0, src});
    while (!pq.isEmpty()) {
        int[] t = pq.poll();
        int u = t[1];
        if (t[0] > dist[u]) continue;                              // stale entry
        for (int[] e : adj.get(u)) {
            if (dist[u] + e[1] < dist[e[0]]) {
                dist[e[0]] = dist[u] + e[1];
                hop[e[0]] = (u == src) ? e[0] : hop[u];            // first router on the path
                pq.add(new int[]{dist[e[0]], e[0]});
            }
        }
    }
    return dist;
}

static int[][] rip(int n, int[][] edges) {                          // distance vector: dist[u][dst]
    int[][] dist = new int[n][n];
    for (int[] row : dist) Arrays.fill(row, INF);
    for (int i = 0; i < n; i++) dist[i][i] = 0;
    for (int round = 0; round < n - 1; round++) {
        boolean changed = false;
        for (int[] e : edges) for (int dir = 0; dir < 2; dir++) {
            int u = dir == 0 ? e[0] : e[1], v = dir == 0 ? e[1] : e[0];
            for (int dst = 0; dst < n; dst++)                       // u hears v's table
                if (dist[v][dst] < INF && e[2] + dist[v][dst] < dist[u][dst]) { dist[u][dst] = e[2] + dist[v][dst]; changed = true; }
        }
        if (!changed) break;
    }
    return dist;
}
--py
import heapq

INF = float("inf")

def ospf(n, edges, src):                             # edges: (u, v, w), undirected
    adj = [[] for _ in range(n)]
    for u, v, w in edges:
        adj[u].append((v, w)); adj[v].append((u, w))
    dist, hop = [INF] * n, [-1] * n                  # hop[v] = first router on the path to v
    dist[src] = 0
    pq = [(0, src)]
    while pq:
        d, u = heapq.heappop(pq)
        if d > dist[u]:
            continue                                 # stale entry
        for v, w in adj[u]:
            if d + w < dist[v]:
                dist[v] = d + w
                hop[v] = v if u == src else hop[u]   # first router on the path
                heapq.heappush(pq, (dist[v], v))
    return dist, hop

def rip(n, edges):                                   # distance vector: dist[u][dst]
    dist = [[0 if i == j else INF for j in range(n)] for i in range(n)]
    for _ in range(n - 1):
        changed = False
        for a, b, w in edges:
            for u, v in ((a, b), (b, a)):
                for dst in range(n):                 # u hears v's table
                    if w + dist[v][dst] < dist[u][dst]:
                        dist[u][dst] = w + dist[v][dst]
                        changed = True
        if not changed:
            break
    return dist
--cpp
const int INF = 1000000000;
struct Edge { int u, v, w; };

// Returns dist, and fills hop[] with the first router on each path (-1 if unreachable).
vector<int> ospf(int n, const vector<Edge>& edges, int src, vector<int>& hop) {
    vector<vector<pair<int, int>>> adj(n);
    for (auto& e : edges) { adj[e.u].push_back({e.v, e.w}); adj[e.v].push_back({e.u, e.w}); }
    vector<int> dist(n, INF);
    hop.assign(n, -1);
    dist[src] = 0;
    priority_queue<pair<int, int>, vector<pair<int, int>>, greater<pair<int, int>>> pq;   // {distance, node}
    pq.push({0, src});
    while (!pq.empty()) {
        auto [d, u] = pq.top(); pq.pop();
        if (d > dist[u]) continue;                                   // stale entry
        for (auto [v, w] : adj[u]) {
            if (d + w < dist[v]) {
                dist[v] = d + w;
                hop[v] = (u == src) ? v : hop[u];                    // first router on the path
                pq.push({dist[v], v});
            }
        }
    }
    return dist;
}

vector<vector<int>> rip(int n, const vector<Edge>& edges) {          // distance vector: dist[u][dst]
    vector<vector<int>> dist(n, vector<int>(n, INF));
    for (int i = 0; i < n; i++) dist[i][i] = 0;
    for (int round = 0; round < n - 1; round++) {
        bool changed = false;
        for (auto& e : edges) for (int dir = 0; dir < 2; dir++) {
            int u = dir == 0 ? e.u : e.v, v = dir == 0 ? e.v : e.u;
            for (int dst = 0; dst < n; dst++)                        // u hears v's table
                if (dist[v][dst] < INF && e.w + dist[v][dst] < dist[u][dst]) { dist[u][dst] = e.w + dist[v][dst]; changed = true; }
        }
        if (!changed) break;
    }
    return dist;
}
--go
import "container/heap"

const INF = 1_000_000_000

type pq [][2]int // {distance, node}, a min-heap on distance

func (p pq) Len() int           { return len(p) }
func (p pq) Less(i, j int) bool { return p[i][0] < p[j][0] }
func (p pq) Swap(i, j int)      { p[i], p[j] = p[j], p[i] }
func (p *pq) Push(x any)        { *p = append(*p, x.([2]int)) }
func (p *pq) Pop() any          { o := *p; x := o[len(o)-1]; *p = o[:len(o)-1]; return x }

// edges: {u, v, w}, undirected. Returns dist and the first router on each path.
func ospf(n int, edges [][3]int, src int) (dist, hop []int) {
	adj := make([][][2]int, n)
	for _, e := range edges {
		adj[e[0]] = append(adj[e[0]], [2]int{e[1], e[2]})
		adj[e[1]] = append(adj[e[1]], [2]int{e[0], e[2]})
	}
	dist, hop = make([]int, n), make([]int, n)
	for i := range dist {
		dist[i], hop[i] = INF, -1
	}
	dist[src] = 0
	h := &pq{{0, src}}
	for h.Len() > 0 {
		t := heap.Pop(h).([2]int)
		u := t[1]
		if t[0] > dist[u] { // stale entry
			continue
		}
		for _, e := range adj[u] {
			if dist[u]+e[1] < dist[e[0]] {
				dist[e[0]] = dist[u] + e[1]
				hop[e[0]] = hop[u] // first router on the path
				if u == src {
					hop[e[0]] = e[0]
				}
				heap.Push(h, [2]int{dist[e[0]], e[0]})
			}
		}
	}
	return
}

func rip(n int, edges [][3]int) [][]int { // distance vector: dist[u][dst]
	dist := make([][]int, n)
	for i := range dist {
		dist[i] = make([]int, n)
		for j := range dist[i] {
			dist[i][j] = INF
		}
		dist[i][i] = 0
	}
	for round := 0; round < n-1; round++ {
		changed := false
		for _, e := range edges {
			for dir := 0; dir < 2; dir++ {
				u, v := e[0], e[1]
				if dir == 1 {
					u, v = v, u
				}
				for dst := 0; dst < n; dst++ { // u hears v's table
					if dist[v][dst] < INF && e[2]+dist[v][dst] < dist[u][dst] {
						dist[u][dst] = e[2] + dist[v][dst]
						changed = true
					}
				}
			}
		}
		if !changed {
			break
		}
	}
	return dist
}

### tcp
--idea
A sliding-window protocol lets a sender keep several packets in flight at once instead of waiting for an acknowledgement (ack) after each one. If the sender had to wait for every ack, a link with a long round-trip delay would sit idle most of the time. With a window of `W` packets, the sender may send up to `W` packets that are not yet acknowledged. Each ack slides the window forward and allows new packets out. TCP, the protocol under most of the internet, is built on this idea, with a window that grows and shrinks to match the network.
Packets can be lost, so the sender must resend them. The receiver sends cumulative acks: "I have everything up to number `x`". In Go-Back-N, the simplest scheme, the receiver accepts only the next packet in order and discards anything that arrives after a gap. If the sender's timer expires, it goes back and resends everything from the first unacknowledged packet. Selective repeat, and real TCP with selective acknowledgements (SACK), buffer out-of-order packets and resend only the missing ones.
--build
1. Number the packets `0 .. total-1`. The sender keeps `base`, the oldest unacknowledged packet, so the window is `base .. base + W - 1`. (Sliding window: both ends of the range move only forward.)
2. The sender stores each packet it has sent but not yet acknowledged in a buffer indexed by `seq % W` (a circular buffer, so memory stays at `W` slots however long the transfer runs), and records the packet numbers in a queue of in-flight packets.
3. Each round, the sender sends every packet in the window. Each packet is lost with probability `loss`.
4. The receiver keeps `expected`, the next packet number it needs. A packet that arrives with `seq == expected` is delivered and `expected` goes up by one. Any other packet (a gap before it) is discarded in Go-Back-N.
5. The cumulative ack is `expected`. The sender removes acknowledged packets from the front of the in-flight queue, and moves `base = expected`. The next round starts at the new base. Packets after a lost one are sent again even if they arrived, which is the price of the simple receiver.
6. Count the packets sent and the rounds used. With no loss, the rounds are `ceil(total / W)`. Real TCP adds congestion control: it grows the window while all goes well and cuts it on loss, to avoid overloading the network.
--think
- Signal: reliable delivery over an unreliable channel, or any "keep the pipe full" problem. The bandwidth-delay product (rate times round-trip time) is how many packets must be in flight to use the link fully, so the window must be at least that large.
- Go-Back-N is simple but wasteful on lossy links, because one loss causes a whole window to be resent. Selective repeat sends only the lost packet but needs a buffer and acks per packet at the receiver. Real TCP uses cumulative acks plus SACK blocks, and fast retransmit (three duplicate acks mean a loss, no need to wait for the timer).
- Window size is a trade-off: too small wastes the link, too large floods the network or the receiver. TCP has two limits: the receiver's advertised window (flow control) and its own estimate of the network's capacity (congestion control).
- The circular buffer works because the window never holds more than `W` packets, so `seq % W` never clashes with another live packet. For the sequence numbers on the wire (which wrap around) the sequence space must be larger than the window, about twice as large for selective repeat.
- Mistakes: advancing `base` on anything other than the cumulative ack, letting the receiver accept out-of-order packets in a protocol that assumes it will not, and sequence numbers that wrap but are compared as if they never did.
--cost
Memory O(W) at the sender. With loss rate p the expected packets sent for a window of W in Go-Back-N grows roughly like total * (1 + p * W) for small p. Throughput with no loss is W packets per round trip.
--java
// Go-Back-N over a lossy channel. Returns {packetsSent, rounds}. Every round is one round trip.
static int[] goBackN(int total, int W, double loss, Random rnd) {
    int[] buf = new int[W];                                         // circular buffer of unacknowledged packets
    Deque<Integer> inflight = new ArrayDeque<>();                   // queue of sent, unacknowledged packets
    int base = 0, expected = 0, sent = 0, rounds = 0;
    while (base < total) {
        rounds++;
        int hi = Math.min(base + W, total);
        inflight.clear();
        for (int seq = base; seq < hi; seq++) {                     // send the whole window
            sent++;
            buf[seq % W] = seq; inflight.addLast(seq);
            boolean lost = rnd.nextDouble() < loss;
            if (!lost && seq == expected) expected++;               // receiver takes only the next in-order packet
        }
        while (!inflight.isEmpty() && inflight.peekFirst() < expected) inflight.pollFirst();   // cumulative ack
        base = expected;                                            // window slides forward
    }
    return new int[]{sent, rounds};
}
--py
import random
from collections import deque

def go_back_n(total, W, loss):                       # returns (packets sent, rounds); one round = one round trip
    buf = [None] * W                                 # circular buffer of unacknowledged packets
    inflight = deque()                               # queue of sent, unacknowledged packets
    base = expected = sent = rounds = 0
    while base < total:
        rounds += 1
        inflight.clear()
        for seq in range(base, min(base + W, total)):    # send the whole window
            sent += 1
            buf[seq % W] = seq
            inflight.append(seq)
            lost = random.random() < loss
            if not lost and seq == expected:
                expected += 1                        # receiver takes only the next in-order packet
        while inflight and inflight[0] < expected:
            inflight.popleft()                       # cumulative ack removes them
        base = expected                              # window slides forward
    return sent, rounds
--cpp
// Go-Back-N over a lossy channel. Returns {packetsSent, rounds}. Every round is one round trip.
pair<int, int> goBackN(int total, int W, double loss, mt19937& rng) {
    vector<int> buf(W, -1);                                         // circular buffer of unacknowledged packets
    deque<int> inflight;                                            // queue of sent, unacknowledged packets
    uniform_real_distribution<double> unit(0.0, 1.0);
    int base = 0, expected = 0, sent = 0, rounds = 0;
    while (base < total) {
        rounds++;
        inflight.clear();
        for (int seq = base; seq < min(base + W, total); seq++) {   // send the whole window
            sent++;
            buf[seq % W] = seq; inflight.push_back(seq);
            bool lost = unit(rng) < loss;
            if (!lost && seq == expected) expected++;               // receiver takes only the next in-order packet
        }
        while (!inflight.empty() && inflight.front() < expected) inflight.pop_front();   // cumulative ack
        base = expected;                                            // window slides forward
    }
    return {sent, rounds};
}
--go
import "math/rand"

// Go-Back-N over a lossy channel. Returns packets sent and rounds. Every round is one round trip.
func goBackN(total, W int, loss float64, rnd *rand.Rand) (sent, rounds int) {
	buf := make([]int, W) // circular buffer of unacknowledged packets
	var inflight []int    // queue of sent, unacknowledged packets
	base, expected := 0, 0
	for base < total {
		rounds++
		hi := min(base+W, total)
		inflight = inflight[:0]
		for seq := base; seq < hi; seq++ { // send the whole window
			sent++
			buf[seq%W] = seq
			inflight = append(inflight, seq)
			lost := rnd.Float64() < loss
			if !lost && seq == expected { // receiver takes only the next in-order packet
				expected++
			}
		}
		for len(inflight) > 0 && inflight[0] < expected { // cumulative ack
			inflight = inflight[1:]
		}
		base = expected // window slides forward
	}
	return
}

### sched
--idea
A scheduler decides which job runs next when more jobs want the CPU (or a worker, or a printer) than it can serve at once. The policy shapes how long jobs wait and how responsive the system feels. Operating systems, batch clusters and web servers all run one.
Two classic policies show the trade-off. Shortest Job First (SJF) always runs the job with the least work next. It minimises the average waiting time (a greedy exchange argument shows that putting a longer job before a shorter one only makes the total wait larger), and it needs a priority queue to pick the smallest ready job fast. Round Robin gives each job a short time slice (the quantum), then sends it to the back of a queue. No job waits long for its first turn, so it feels fair and responsive, at the cost of a worse average finishing time and some switching overhead.
--build
1. Describe each job by its arrival time, its burst (the CPU time it needs), and an id.
2. SJF (non-preemptive, so a running job is never interrupted): sort jobs by arrival. Keep a clock `t` and a min Heap of ready jobs ordered by burst.
3. Loop: add to the heap every job with `arrival <= t`. If the heap is empty, jump `t` to the next arrival. Otherwise pop the job with the smallest burst, run it to completion (`t += burst`), and record its waiting time `start - arrival`.
4. The average waiting time is the sum of waits divided by the number of jobs.
5. Round Robin: put all jobs in a Queue as `(id, remaining)`. Pop the front job, run it for `min(quantum, remaining)` and advance the clock. If work remains, push it to the back; otherwise record its finish time.
6. For deadlines use Earliest Deadline First: the same heap, ordered by deadline instead of length. It is optimal for a single CPU when jobs can be preempted (see Interval scheduling for the greedy proof style).
--think
- Signal: "which one next" with a measurable goal: average wait (SJF), responsiveness (Round Robin), deadlines (EDF), importance (priority queue). The data structure is nearly always a heap or a queue, and the proof is nearly always an exchange argument (see Greedy).
- SJF's weakness: it needs to know the burst in advance (systems estimate it from past behaviour), and long jobs can starve if short ones keep arriving. Aging, raising a waiting job's priority over time, fixes starvation.
- Round Robin's quantum is a trade-off: too small and the cost of switching dominates, too large and it behaves like first come first served.
- Real schedulers (the Linux CFS, Windows) combine ideas: priority classes, a time slice, and fairness accounting, plus per-CPU queues to avoid contention.
- Mistakes: not jumping the clock forward when the ready heap is empty (an infinite loop or wrong waits), ordering the heap ties arbitrarily when you need a deterministic result (break ties by arrival time and id), and computing waiting time as finish minus arrival, which also includes the run time (that is turnaround time).
--cost
SJF O(n log n) with a heap. Round Robin O(total work / quantum) slices, each O(1) with a queue.
--java
// jobs: {arrival, burst}. Returns the order the jobs run; wait[i] is filled with job i's waiting time.
static List<Integer> sjf(int[][] jobs, int[] wait) {
    Integer[] byArrival = new Integer[jobs.length];
    for (int i = 0; i < jobs.length; i++) byArrival[i] = i;
    Arrays.sort(byArrival, (a, b) -> jobs[a][0] - jobs[b][0]);
    PriorityQueue<int[]> ready = new PriorityQueue<>((a, b) -> a[0] != b[0] ? a[0] - b[0] : a[1] != b[1] ? a[1] - b[1] : a[2] - b[2]);
    List<Integer> order = new ArrayList<>();
    int t = 0, i = 0;
    while (i < jobs.length || !ready.isEmpty()) {
        if (ready.isEmpty() && t < jobs[byArrival[i]][0]) t = jobs[byArrival[i]][0];   // idle: jump to the next arrival
        while (i < jobs.length && jobs[byArrival[i]][0] <= t) {
            int j = byArrival[i++];
            ready.add(new int[]{jobs[j][1], jobs[j][0], j});                  // {burst, arrival, id}
        }
        int[] job = ready.poll();                                              // shortest ready job
        order.add(job[2]);
        wait[job[2]] = t - job[1];
        t += job[0];
    }
    return order;
}

static int[] roundRobin(int[] bursts, int quantum) {                          // all jobs arrive at time 0
    int[] finish = new int[bursts.length];
    Deque<int[]> q = new ArrayDeque<>();
    for (int i = 0; i < bursts.length; i++) q.addLast(new int[]{i, bursts[i]});
    int t = 0;
    while (!q.isEmpty()) {
        int[] job = q.pollFirst();
        int run = Math.min(quantum, job[1]);
        t += run;
        if (job[1] > run) q.addLast(new int[]{job[0], job[1] - run});         // back of the queue
        else finish[job[0]] = t;
    }
    return finish;
}
--py
import heapq
from collections import deque

def sjf(jobs):                                       # jobs: (arrival, burst). Returns (order, wait per job)
    order, wait = [], [0] * len(jobs)
    pending = sorted(range(len(jobs)), key=lambda j: jobs[j][0])
    ready, t, i = [], 0, 0                           # ready: heap of (burst, arrival, id)
    while i < len(pending) or ready:
        if not ready and t < jobs[pending[i]][0]:
            t = jobs[pending[i]][0]                  # idle: jump to the next arrival
        while i < len(pending) and jobs[pending[i]][0] <= t:
            j = pending[i]; i += 1
            heapq.heappush(ready, (jobs[j][1], jobs[j][0], j))
        burst, arrival, j = heapq.heappop(ready)     # shortest ready job
        order.append(j)
        wait[j] = t - arrival
        t += burst
    return order, wait

def round_robin(bursts, quantum):                    # all jobs arrive at time 0; returns finish times
    finish, t = [0] * len(bursts), 0
    q = deque(enumerate(bursts))
    while q:
        j, remaining = q.popleft()
        run = min(quantum, remaining)
        t += run
        if remaining > run:
            q.append((j, remaining - run))           # back of the queue
        else:
            finish[j] = t
    return finish
--cpp
// jobs: {arrival, burst}. Returns the order the jobs run; wait[i] is filled with job i's waiting time.
vector<int> sjf(const vector<pair<int, int>>& jobs, vector<int>& wait) {
    int n = jobs.size();
    vector<int> byArrival(n), order;
    iota(byArrival.begin(), byArrival.end(), 0);
    stable_sort(byArrival.begin(), byArrival.end(), [&](int a, int b) { return jobs[a].first < jobs[b].first; });
    typedef tuple<int, int, int> J;                                  // {burst, arrival, id}
    priority_queue<J, vector<J>, greater<J>> ready;
    wait.assign(n, 0);
    int t = 0, i = 0;
    while (i < n || !ready.empty()) {
        if (ready.empty() && t < jobs[byArrival[i]].first) t = jobs[byArrival[i]].first;   // idle: jump to the next arrival
        while (i < n && jobs[byArrival[i]].first <= t) {
            int j = byArrival[i++];
            ready.push({jobs[j].second, jobs[j].first, j});
        }
        auto [burst, arrival, j] = ready.top(); ready.pop();         // shortest ready job
        order.push_back(j);
        wait[j] = t - arrival;
        t += burst;
    }
    return order;
}

vector<int> roundRobin(const vector<int>& bursts, int quantum) {     // all jobs arrive at time 0
    vector<int> finish(bursts.size(), 0);
    queue<pair<int, int>> q;
    for (size_t i = 0; i < bursts.size(); i++) q.push({(int)i, bursts[i]});
    int t = 0;
    while (!q.empty()) {
        auto [j, remaining] = q.front(); q.pop();
        int run = min(quantum, remaining);
        t += run;
        if (remaining > run) q.push({j, remaining - run});           // back of the queue
        else finish[j] = t;
    }
    return finish;
}
--go
import (
	"container/heap"
	"sort"
)

type ready [][3]int // {burst, arrival, id}: a min-heap, shortest burst first

func (r ready) Len() int { return len(r) }
func (r ready) Less(i, j int) bool {
	a, b := r[i], r[j]
	return a[0] < b[0] || a[0] == b[0] && (a[1] < b[1] || a[1] == b[1] && a[2] < b[2])
}
func (r ready) Swap(i, j int) { r[i], r[j] = r[j], r[i] }
func (r *ready) Push(x any)   { *r = append(*r, x.([3]int)) }
func (r *ready) Pop() any     { o := *r; x := o[len(o)-1]; *r = o[:len(o)-1]; return x }

// jobs: {arrival, burst}. Returns the order the jobs run and each job's waiting time.
func sjf(jobs [][2]int) (order, wait []int) {
	byArrival := make([]int, len(jobs))
	for i := range byArrival {
		byArrival[i] = i
	}
	sort.SliceStable(byArrival, func(a, b int) bool { return jobs[byArrival[a]][0] < jobs[byArrival[b]][0] })
	wait = make([]int, len(jobs))
	q := &ready{}
	t, i := 0, 0
	for i < len(jobs) || q.Len() > 0 {
		if q.Len() == 0 && t < jobs[byArrival[i]][0] { // idle: jump to the next arrival
			t = jobs[byArrival[i]][0]
		}
		for i < len(jobs) && jobs[byArrival[i]][0] <= t {
			j := byArrival[i]
			heap.Push(q, [3]int{jobs[j][1], jobs[j][0], j})
			i++
		}
		job := heap.Pop(q).([3]int) // shortest ready job
		order = append(order, job[2])
		wait[job[2]] = t - job[1]
		t += job[0]
	}
	return
}

func roundRobin(bursts []int, quantum int) []int { // all jobs arrive at time 0
	finish := make([]int, len(bursts))
	var q [][2]int // {id, remaining}
	for i, b := range bursts {
		q = append(q, [2]int{i, b})
	}
	t := 0
	for len(q) > 0 {
		job := q[0]
		q = q[1:]
		run := min(quantum, job[1])
		t += run
		if job[1] > run {
			q = append(q, [2]int{job[0], job[1] - run}) // back of the queue
		} else {
			finish[job[0]] = t
		}
	}
	return finish
}
### build
--idea
A build system (make, Bazel, Gradle, npm scripts) turns source files into programs by running steps in the right order, and re-runs only the steps whose inputs changed. Every target (a file to produce) lists what it depends on: `app` needs `main.o` and `util.o`, and `main.o` needs `main.c` and `util.h`. These rules form a directed graph, and a valid build order is a topological order: everything a target needs is built before it.
The same graph tells the tool what to skip. If only `util.h` changed, then `main.o` and `util.o` are out of date (they depend on it), and so is `app` (it depends on them), but nothing else needs to run. So the algorithm is: walk the graph depth first, mark a target as stale if any of its dependencies changed or was rebuilt, and emit the stale ones in post-order. If the graph has a cycle (`a` needs `b` needs `a`) there is no valid order, and the tool reports the cycle.
--build
1. Represent the rules as `deps[target] = [targets or files it needs]`. Files with no entry are plain sources. Also take `dirty`, the set of source files that changed since the last build (a real tool compares file timestamps or content hashes).
2. Do a Depth-first search from each target with three states: not visited, on the current path, finished. This is the same colouring used for Cycle detection, directed.
3. When the search reaches a node that is on the current path, there is a cycle: report the path from that node to here and stop.
4. On finishing a target (post-order), decide if it is `changed`: it is changed if it is dirty itself, or if any of its dependencies is changed. A target that has dependencies and is changed must be rebuilt: append it to `order`.
5. Because a target is appended only after the search has finished all its dependencies, the list `order` is a valid Topological sort of the work to do. Running the commands in that order is correct.
6. Return `order`. Real systems also run independent targets in parallel (all targets whose dependencies are done), cache results by content hash, and keep dependency lists in sync with what each command actually reads.
--think
- Signal: tasks with "must happen after" rules, build only what changed, run in a valid order. Beyond compilers: data pipelines, CI job graphs, spreadsheet-like recalculation (see Spreadsheet recalculation), installing packages (see Package managers and version solving).
- Topological order is not unique. Any order that respects the dependencies works, which is what lets a build run unrelated targets in parallel. Kahn's algorithm (repeatedly take nodes with no unmet dependencies) gives the parallel batches directly.
- Correctness depends on the declared dependencies being complete. A missing edge produces a build that works on one run and fails on another (a race or a stale output). Hermetic tools such as Bazel enforce this by sandboxing.
- Early cutoff: if a rebuilt target produces byte-identical output, targets that depend on it need not rebuild. Hash the output, not the timestamp.
- Mistakes: not detecting cycles (infinite recursion), treating a changed source as something to "build" (it has no recipe), and visiting the same target several times instead of caching its state (exponential time on a diamond-shaped graph).
--cost
O(V + E) for the targets and dependency edges, plus the cost of running the commands.
--java
// deps: target -> its dependencies. dirty: changed source files. Returns targets to rebuild, in a valid order.
static List<String> buildPlan(Map<String, List<String>> deps, Set<String> dirty) {
    Map<String, Integer> state = new HashMap<>();            // 1 = on the path, 2 = done
    Map<String, Boolean> changed = new HashMap<>();
    List<String> order = new ArrayList<>();
    Deque<String> path = new ArrayDeque<>();
    for (String t : deps.keySet()) visit(t, deps, dirty, state, changed, order, path);
    return order;
}

private static boolean visit(String t, Map<String, List<String>> deps, Set<String> dirty, Map<String, Integer> state,
                             Map<String, Boolean> changed, List<String> order, Deque<String> path) {
    int s = state.getOrDefault(t, 0);
    if (s == 2) return changed.get(t);
    if (s == 1) throw new IllegalStateException("cycle through " + t + " (path " + path + ")");
    state.put(t, 1); path.addLast(t);
    boolean ch = dirty.contains(t);
    List<String> ds = deps.getOrDefault(t, Collections.emptyList());
    for (String d : ds) if (visit(d, deps, dirty, state, changed, order, path)) ch = true;   // a changed dependency makes t stale
    path.removeLast(); state.put(t, 2); changed.put(t, ch);
    if (ch && !ds.isEmpty()) order.add(t);                   // post-order: dependencies were added first
    return ch;
}
--py
def build_plan(deps, dirty):
    """deps: target -> list of dependencies. dirty: changed source files. Returns targets to rebuild, in order."""
    state, changed, order, path = {}, {}, [], []           # state: 1 = on the path, 2 = done

    def visit(t):
        if state.get(t) == 2:
            return changed[t]
        if state.get(t) == 1:                              # back edge: a cycle
            raise ValueError("cycle through " + " -> ".join(path[path.index(t):] + [t]))
        state[t] = 1; path.append(t)
        ch = t in dirty
        for d in deps.get(t, []):
            if visit(d):
                ch = True                                  # a changed dependency makes t stale
        path.pop(); state[t] = 2; changed[t] = ch
        if ch and deps.get(t):
            order.append(t)                                # post-order: dependencies were added first
        return ch

    for t in deps:
        visit(t)
    return order
--cpp
// deps: target -> its dependencies. dirty: changed source files. Returns targets to rebuild, in a valid order.
vector<string> buildPlan(const map<string, vector<string>>& deps, const set<string>& dirty) {
    map<string, int> state;                                  // 1 = on the path, 2 = done
    map<string, bool> changed;
    vector<string> order, path;
    function<bool(const string&)> visit = [&](const string& t) {
        int s = state.count(t) ? state[t] : 0;
        if (s == 2) return changed[t];
        if (s == 1) throw runtime_error("cycle through " + t);
        state[t] = 1; path.push_back(t);
        bool ch = dirty.count(t) > 0;
        auto it = deps.find(t);
        if (it != deps.end()) for (auto& d : it->second) if (visit(d)) ch = true;   // a changed dependency makes t stale
        path.pop_back(); state[t] = 2; changed[t] = ch;
        if (ch && it != deps.end() && !it->second.empty()) order.push_back(t);      // post-order
        return ch;
    };
    for (auto& kv : deps) visit(kv.first);
    return order;
}
--go
import "fmt"

// deps: target -> its dependencies. dirty: changed source files. Returns targets to rebuild, in a valid order.
func buildPlan(deps map[string][]string, dirty map[string]bool) ([]string, error) {
	state := map[string]int{} // 1 = on the path, 2 = done
	changed := map[string]bool{}
	var order, path []string
	var visit func(t string) (bool, error)
	visit = func(t string) (bool, error) {
		switch state[t] {
		case 2:
			return changed[t], nil
		case 1:
			return false, fmt.Errorf("cycle through %s (path %v)", t, path)
		}
		state[t] = 1
		path = append(path, t)
		ch := dirty[t]
		for _, d := range deps[t] {
			c, err := visit(d)
			if err != nil {
				return false, err
			}
			ch = ch || c // a changed dependency makes t stale
		}
		path = path[:len(path)-1]
		state[t], changed[t] = 2, ch
		if ch && len(deps[t]) > 0 { // post-order: dependencies were added first
			order = append(order, t)
		}
		return ch, nil
	}
	for t := range deps {
		if _, err := visit(t); err != nil {
			return nil, err
		}
	}
	return order, nil
}

### pkg
--idea
A package manager (pip, npm, cargo, apt) must pick one version of every package you need such that every package's requirements are satisfied together. Your app needs `web >= 2`; `web 2` needs `http >= 1, < 3`; another library needs `http < 2`. Is there a set of versions that works? Finding one is a search problem. In general it is NP-hard (hard to solve quickly in the worst case), so solvers search cleverly rather than check everything.
The core method is backtracking with propagation. Pick a package that is needed but not yet chosen, try its versions from newest to oldest, and for each one narrow the allowed ranges of the packages it depends on. If any range becomes empty, or contradicts a package already chosen, that version is a dead end, so undo and try the next. When all needed packages have a version, you have a solution. Modern solvers (such as PubGrub in Dart and Cargo, or SAT-based solvers) also learn from each conflict so they do not repeat it.
--build
1. Describe the repository: `pkgs[name][version] = {dependency: (lo, hi)}`, meaning that version needs the dependency in the closed version range `lo .. hi`. Versions are integers here.
2. Start with `req`, the ranges required by your project (for example `{"web": (2, 99)}`), and an empty `chosen` map.
3. `go(chosen, req)`: find a name that is in `req` but not in `chosen`. If there is none, return `chosen`: every requirement has a version.
4. Try each available version `v` of that name inside its range, newest first (preferring new versions is the usual policy).
5. For that `v`, go through its dependencies and intersect each range with what `req` already says (`lo = max(lo, lo2)`, `hi = min(hi, hi2)`). This is propagation. If a range becomes empty, or the dependency is already chosen at a version outside the range, this `v` conflicts: skip it.
6. Otherwise recurse with `v` added to `chosen` and the narrowed `req`. If the recursion succeeds, return its answer. If every version fails, return `None`, so the caller backtracks to its previous choice (see Backtracking).
7. The conflict structure is a satisfiability problem. Each package-version is a yes/no variable, and rules like "at most one version of a package" and "if A 2 then B in this range" are clauses. When every rule involves only two choices it is exactly 2-SAT, which is solvable in linear time by the implication graph (see 2-SAT). General ranges are not that simple, hence the search.
--think
- Signal: choose one option per slot, subject to pairwise or range constraints, and the best answer is "any one that works" with a preference for newest. Dependency resolution, course scheduling, configuration, puzzles.
- Propagation is what makes this practical. Narrowing ranges as soon as a choice is made detects a dead end early, before wasting time deeper in the search. Ordering also matters: choosing the most constrained package first (fewest versions) fails fast.
- Without learning, a solver can take exponential time re-exploring the same contradiction. Conflict-driven solvers record "these two choices cannot go together" and prune forever after.
- Policies matter as much as the algorithm: prefer newest versions or the minimal change from the lockfile, allow or forbid several versions of one package side by side (npm allows nested copies, pip does not), and make the result reproducible with a lockfile.
- Mistakes: not checking a new constraint against packages already chosen, mutating shared state without undoing it on backtrack (this model copies `req` for each branch), and error messages that say only "no solution" instead of naming the conflict.
--cost
Worst case exponential in the number of packages. In practice propagation and good ordering make real repositories resolve in milliseconds to seconds.
--java
// pkgs: name -> version -> (dependency -> {lo, hi}). req: name -> {lo, hi}. Returns name -> version, or null.
static Map<String, Integer> solve(Map<String, Map<Integer, Map<String, int[]>>> pkgs, Map<String, int[]> req, Map<String, Integer> chosen) {
    String name = null;
    for (String n : new TreeSet<>(req.keySet())) if (!chosen.containsKey(n)) { name = n; break; }
    if (name == null) return chosen;                                  // every requirement has a version
    int lo = req.get(name)[0], hi = req.get(name)[1];
    List<Integer> versions = new ArrayList<>(pkgs.get(name).keySet());
    versions.sort(Collections.reverseOrder());                        // newest first
    for (int v : versions) {
        if (v < lo || v > hi) continue;
        Map<String, int[]> next = new HashMap<>();
        for (Map.Entry<String, int[]> e : req.entrySet()) next.put(e.getKey(), e.getValue().clone());
        boolean ok = true;
        for (Map.Entry<String, int[]> d : pkgs.get(name).get(v).entrySet()) {
            int[] cur = next.getOrDefault(d.getKey(), new int[]{0, Integer.MAX_VALUE});
            int l = Math.max(cur[0], d.getValue()[0]), h = Math.min(cur[1], d.getValue()[1]);   // propagate: narrow the range
            Integer fixed = chosen.get(d.getKey());
            if (l > h || (fixed != null && (fixed < l || fixed > h))) { ok = false; break; }    // conflict
            next.put(d.getKey(), new int[]{l, h});
        }
        if (!ok) continue;
        Map<String, Integer> c2 = new HashMap<>(chosen);
        c2.put(name, v);
        Map<String, Integer> r = solve(pkgs, next, c2);               // go deeper; null means backtrack
        if (r != null) return r;
    }
    return null;
}
--py
def solve(pkgs, req, chosen=None):
    """pkgs[name][version] = {dep: (lo, hi)}; req: {name: (lo, hi)}. Returns {name: version} or None."""
    chosen = chosen or {}
    todo = sorted(n for n in req if n not in chosen)
    if not todo:
        return chosen                                        # every requirement has a version
    name = todo[0]
    lo, hi = req[name]
    for v in sorted((v for v in pkgs[name] if lo <= v <= hi), reverse=True):   # newest first
        nxt, ok = dict(req), True
        for dep, (l2, h2) in pkgs[name][v].items():
            l, h = nxt.get(dep, (0, float("inf")))
            l, h = max(l, l2), min(h, h2)                    # propagate: narrow the range
            if l > h or (dep in chosen and not l <= chosen[dep] <= h):
                ok = False                                   # conflict: this version is a dead end
                break
            nxt[dep] = (l, h)
        if ok:
            r = solve(pkgs, nxt, {**chosen, name: v})        # go deeper; None means backtrack
            if r is not None:
                return r
    return None
--cpp
typedef map<string, map<int, map<string, pair<int, int>>>> Repo;   // name -> version -> (dep -> {lo, hi})

// req: name -> {lo, hi}. Returns true and fills `chosen` when a full solution exists.
bool solve(const Repo& pkgs, const map<string, pair<int, int>>& req, map<string, int> chosen, map<string, int>& result) {
    string name;
    for (auto& kv : req) if (!chosen.count(kv.first)) { name = kv.first; break; }   // map order = sorted names
    if (name.empty()) { result = chosen; return true; }                              // every requirement has a version
    auto [lo, hi] = req.at(name);
    for (auto it = pkgs.at(name).rbegin(); it != pkgs.at(name).rend(); ++it) {       // newest first
        int v = it->first;
        if (v < lo || v > hi) continue;
        map<string, pair<int, int>> next = req;
        bool ok = true;
