        for (auto& d : it->second) {
            auto cur = next.count(d.first) ? next[d.first] : make_pair(0, INT_MAX);
            int l = max(cur.first, d.second.first), h = min(cur.second, d.second.second);   // propagate: narrow the range
            if (l > h || (chosen.count(d.first) && (chosen[d.first] < l || chosen[d.first] > h))) { ok = false; break; }   // conflict
            next[d.first] = {l, h};
        }
        if (!ok) continue;
        map<string, int> c2 = chosen;
        c2[name] = v;
        if (solve(pkgs, next, c2, result)) return true;                              // go deeper; false means backtrack
    }
    return false;
}
--go
import (
	"maps"
	"math"
	"slices"
)

type rng = [2]int // {lo, hi}, inclusive

// pkgs: name -> version -> (dependency -> range). req: name -> range. Returns name -> version, or nil.
func solve(pkgs map[string]map[int]map[string]rng, req map[string]rng, chosen map[string]int) map[string]int {
	name := ""
	for _, n := range slices.Sorted(maps.Keys(req)) {
		if _, done := chosen[n]; !done {
			name = n
			break
		}
	}
	if name == "" { // every requirement has a version
		return chosen
	}
	versions := slices.Sorted(maps.Keys(pkgs[name]))
	slices.Reverse(versions) // newest first
	for _, v := range versions {
		if v < req[name][0] || v > req[name][1] {
			continue
		}
		next := maps.Clone(req)
		ok := true
		for dep, r := range pkgs[name][v] {
			cur, has := next[dep]
			if !has {
				cur = rng{0, math.MaxInt}
			}
			l, h := max(cur[0], r[0]), min(cur[1], r[1]) // propagate: narrow the range
			if fixed, set := chosen[dep]; l > h || set && (fixed < l || fixed > h) {
				ok = false // conflict
				break
			}
			next[dep] = rng{l, h}
		}
		if !ok {
			continue
		}
		c2 := maps.Clone(chosen)
		c2[name] = v
		if r := solve(pkgs, next, c2); r != nil { // go deeper; nil means backtrack
			return r
		}
	}
	return nil
}

### hashmapimpl
--idea
A hash map stores key-value pairs and finds a key in constant time on average. The trick is to turn the key into an array position. A hash function converts the key into a large number, the number modulo the array size gives a bucket index, and the pair is stored in that bucket. Looking up the key repeats the same calculation and goes straight to the right bucket.
Two things need handling. Different keys can land in the same bucket (a collision). This model uses chaining: each bucket holds a linked list of the pairs that landed there, and a lookup walks that short list. And as the map fills, lists get long. So when the number of pairs exceeds 75 percent of the number of buckets (the load factor limit), the map doubles the bucket array and re-inserts everything. A resize is expensive, but it happens rarely, so the average cost per insert stays constant (amortised analysis).
--build
1. Keep an array `buckets` of heads of linked lists (start with 8), a count `size`, and the rule "resize when `size > 0.75 * capacity`".
2. Index: `index = hash(key) mod capacity` (see Modular arithmetic). Here `hash` multiplies by a large odd constant to scatter nearby integers, then the modulus picks the bucket. Keeping the capacity a prime or mixing the bits well avoids patterns in the keys landing in a few buckets.
3. `get(key)`: walk the chain in `buckets[index]` comparing keys. Return the value or "missing".
4. `put(key, value)`: walk the chain; if the key is there, replace the value. Otherwise insert a new node at the head of the chain (O(1)) and add 1 to `size`. Then, if the load limit is crossed, call `resize`.
5. `remove(key)`: walk the chain keeping the previous node, and unlink the matching node.
6. `resize`: allocate an array of twice the capacity. For every node of every old chain, recompute its index for the new capacity and relink it at the head of its new chain. No node is copied, only relinked.
7. Why the average stays constant (see Amortized analysis): doubling at `n` entries costs `n` moves. The previous doubling happened at `n / 2` entries, so over a run of `n` inserts the total moving work is `n + n/2 + n/4 + ...`, which is less than `2n`. Spread over the inserts, that is under 2 extra moves each.
--think
- Signal: "have I seen this", "count occurrences", "look up by key": use a hash map unless you need order or range queries (then use a tree map, see Ordered sets and maps). Understanding the internals explains the odd cases: why iteration order is arbitrary, why worst case is slow, why keys must not change while stored.
- Worst case: if many keys collide (a bad hash, or an attacker choosing keys that collide), a chain degenerates to a list and operations become linear. Java's HashMap turns long chains into balanced trees, and many languages randomise the hash seed to resist this attack.
- Chaining versus open addressing: open addressing stores everything in the array and probes for the next free slot. It is more cache friendly but needs a lower load factor and careful deletion (tombstones). Chaining is simpler and degrades gracefully.
- The load factor trade-off: a lower limit means shorter chains and faster lookups, but more memory.
- Mistakes: using `hash % capacity` with a negative hash value (the result can be negative in some languages), changing a key's hash after insertion (the entry is then lost), forgetting to recompute the index on resize (the old index is no longer valid), and deleting from a chain without fixing the `size`.
--cost
Average O(1) for get, put and remove given a good hash and the 0.75 load limit. Resize is O(n) but amortises to O(1) per insert. Worst case O(n) per operation.
--java
static class MyMap {
    static class Node { int key, val; Node next; Node(int k, int v, Node n) { key = k; val = v; next = n; } }
    Node[] buckets = new Node[8];
    int size = 0;
    long moves = 0;                                              // counts relinks done by resizing

    private static int index(int key, int cap) {
        long h = (key * 2654435761L) & 0xFFFFFFFFL;              // scatter nearby keys, then reduce modulo the capacity
        return (int) (h % cap);
    }

    Integer get(int key) {
        for (Node n = buckets[index(key, buckets.length)]; n != null; n = n.next) if (n.key == key) return n.val;
        return null;
    }

    void put(int key, int val) {
        int i = index(key, buckets.length);
        for (Node n = buckets[i]; n != null; n = n.next) if (n.key == key) { n.val = val; return; }
        buckets[i] = new Node(key, val, buckets[i]);             // insert at the head of the chain
        if (++size > 0.75 * buckets.length) resize();
    }

    boolean remove(int key) {
        int i = index(key, buckets.length);
        for (Node prev = null, n = buckets[i]; n != null; prev = n, n = n.next) {
            if (n.key == key) { if (prev == null) buckets[i] = n.next; else prev.next = n.next; size--; return true; }
        }
        return false;
    }

    private void resize() {                                      // double the buckets and relink every node
        Node[] old = buckets;
        buckets = new Node[old.length * 2];
        for (Node head : old) {
            for (Node n = head; n != null; ) {
                Node next = n.next;
                int i = index(n.key, buckets.length);
                n.next = buckets[i]; buckets[i] = n;
                moves++;
                n = next;
            }
        }
    }
}
--py
class Node:
    def __init__(self, key, val, nxt):
        self.key, self.val, self.next = key, val, nxt

class MyMap:
    def __init__(self):
        self.buckets = [None] * 8
        self.size = 0
        self.moves = 0                                   # counts relinks done by resizing

    @staticmethod
    def _index(key, cap):
        return ((key * 2654435761) & 0xFFFFFFFF) % cap   # scatter nearby keys, then reduce modulo the capacity

    def get(self, key, default=None):
        n = self.buckets[self._index(key, len(self.buckets))]
        while n:
            if n.key == key:
                return n.val
            n = n.next
        return default

    def put(self, key, val):
        i = self._index(key, len(self.buckets))
        n = self.buckets[i]
        while n:
            if n.key == key:
                n.val = val
                return
            n = n.next
        self.buckets[i] = Node(key, val, self.buckets[i])    # insert at the head of the chain
        self.size += 1
        if self.size > 0.75 * len(self.buckets):
            self._resize()

    def remove(self, key):
        i = self._index(key, len(self.buckets))
        prev, n = None, self.buckets[i]
        while n:
            if n.key == key:
                if prev: prev.next = n.next
                else: self.buckets[i] = n.next
                self.size -= 1
                return True
            prev, n = n, n.next
        return False

    def _resize(self):                                   # double the buckets and relink every node
        old, self.buckets = self.buckets, [None] * (2 * len(self.buckets))
        for n in old:
            while n:
                nxt = n.next
                i = self._index(n.key, len(self.buckets))
                n.next, self.buckets[i] = self.buckets[i], n
                self.moves += 1
                n = nxt
--cpp
struct MyMap {
    struct Node { int key, val; Node* next; };
    vector<Node*> buckets = vector<Node*>(8, nullptr);
    int size = 0;
    long long moves = 0;                                         // counts relinks done by resizing

    static size_t index(int key, size_t cap) {
        unsigned long long h = ((unsigned long long)(unsigned)key * 2654435761ULL) & 0xFFFFFFFFULL;   // scatter, then reduce
        return h % cap;
    }
    ~MyMap() { for (Node* head : buckets) while (head) { Node* n = head->next; delete head; head = n; } }

    bool get(int key, int& out) const {
        for (Node* n = buckets[index(key, buckets.size())]; n; n = n->next) if (n->key == key) { out = n->val; return true; }
        return false;
    }

    void put(int key, int val) {
        size_t i = index(key, buckets.size());
        for (Node* n = buckets[i]; n; n = n->next) if (n->key == key) { n->val = val; return; }
        buckets[i] = new Node{key, val, buckets[i]};             // insert at the head of the chain
        if (++size > 0.75 * buckets.size()) resize();
    }

    bool remove(int key) {
        size_t i = index(key, buckets.size());
        for (Node *prev = nullptr, *n = buckets[i]; n; prev = n, n = n->next) {
            if (n->key == key) {
                if (prev) prev->next = n->next; else buckets[i] = n->next;
                delete n; size--;
                return true;
            }
        }
        return false;
    }

    void resize() {                                              // double the buckets and relink every node
        vector<Node*> old(buckets.size() * 2, nullptr);
        old.swap(buckets);                                       // `old` now holds the old bucket array
        for (Node* head : old) {
            for (Node* n = head; n; ) {
                Node* next = n->next;
                size_t i = index(n->key, buckets.size());
                n->next = buckets[i]; buckets[i] = n;
                moves++;
                n = next;
            }
        }
    }
};
--go
type node struct {
	key, val int
	next     *node
}

type MyMap struct {
	buckets []*node
	size    int
	moves   int // counts relinks done by resizing
}

func newMyMap() *MyMap { return &MyMap{buckets: make([]*node, 8)} }

func index(key, capacity int) int {
	h := uint32(key) * 2654435761 // scatter nearby keys (wraps mod 2^32), then reduce modulo the capacity
	return int(h % uint32(capacity))
}

func (m *MyMap) get(key int) (int, bool) {
	for n := m.buckets[index(key, len(m.buckets))]; n != nil; n = n.next {
		if n.key == key {
			return n.val, true
		}
	}
	return 0, false
}

func (m *MyMap) put(key, val int) {
	i := index(key, len(m.buckets))
	for n := m.buckets[i]; n != nil; n = n.next {
		if n.key == key {
			n.val = val
			return
		}
	}
	m.buckets[i] = &node{key, val, m.buckets[i]} // insert at the head of the chain
	if m.size++; float64(m.size) > 0.75*float64(len(m.buckets)) {
		m.resize()
	}
}

func (m *MyMap) remove(key int) bool {
	i := index(key, len(m.buckets))
	var prev *node
	for n := m.buckets[i]; n != nil; prev, n = n, n.next {
		if n.key == key {
			if prev == nil {
				m.buckets[i] = n.next
			} else {
				prev.next = n.next
			}
			m.size--
			return true
		}
	}
	return false
}

func (m *MyMap) resize() { // double the buckets and relink every node
	old := m.buckets
	m.buckets = make([]*node, len(old)*2)
	for _, n := range old {
		for n != nil {
			next := n.next
			i := index(n.key, len(m.buckets))
			n.next, m.buckets[i] = m.buckets[i], n
			m.moves++
			n = next
		}
	}
}

### gc
--idea
Garbage collection (GC) frees the memory of objects a program can no longer reach, so the programmer does not have to free it by hand. The key idea is reachability. Think of memory as a graph: every object is a node, and a reference from one object to another is an edge. A few special starting points, the roots (local variables, global variables, the stack), are known to be in use. Any object you can reach by following references from a root is alive. Anything else can never be used again, so it is garbage, even if garbage objects point to each other in a cycle.
The basic algorithm, mark and sweep, has two phases. Mark: do a graph search from the roots and flag every object you visit. Sweep: scan all of memory and free every object that is not flagged. A second family, copying collectors, does a breadth-first search from the roots and copies each live object into a fresh area, leaving the garbage behind. Then the old area is discarded whole.
--build
1. Model the heap as `heap[i] = list of objects that object i refers to`, with ids `0 .. n-1`. `roots` is the list of objects referenced from outside the heap.
2. Mark phase: use a stack of objects to visit, started with the roots, and a `marked` array. Pop an object. If it is already marked, skip it (this is how cycles are handled, the same visited check as in DFS). Otherwise mark it and push everything it refers to.
3. Sweep phase: every object that is not marked is garbage. Return the list of freed ids (a real collector puts the memory on a free list).
4. Copying collection (Cheney's algorithm, uses BFS and a queue): keep `order`, the list of copied objects, and a table `forward[old] = new position`. Copy each root: give it the next new position. Then scan the copied objects in order with a pointer `scan`. For each, copy every object it refers to (reuse the forwarding entry if it was already copied), and write the object with its references renamed to the new positions.
5. When `scan` catches up with the end of `order`, everything reachable has been copied, in breadth-first order. The result is a compact heap with no holes. Cost depends only on the live objects, not on how much garbage there was.
6. Real collectors add generations (most objects die young, so collect a small young area often), incremental or concurrent marking so the program does not pause for long, and write barriers to track references that appear while marking.
--think
- Signal: "which objects are still in use", "what can be reached from these starting points". It is the same graph search as anywhere else, and the cycle handling (marking visited nodes) is what makes it work where reference counting fails. Pure reference counting cannot free a cycle of objects that point to each other.
- Mark and sweep versus copying: mark and sweep does not move objects (good when moving is hard, but memory gets fragmented) and costs time proportional to the whole heap during the sweep. Copying compacts memory and costs time proportional only to live data, but needs twice the space and must update every pointer.
- Generational collection exploits the "weak generational hypothesis": most objects die very young. The young area is small and mostly garbage, so collecting it is fast with a copying collector.
- Pauses: a simple collector stops the whole program ("stop the world"). Modern collectors (G1, ZGC, Go's collector) mark concurrently while the program runs, using barriers to stay correct.
- Mistakes: forgetting a root (the collector then frees an object that is still in use, a crash), marking recursively on a long linked list (stack overflow, use an explicit stack), and updating pointers during copying without the forwarding table (an object gets copied twice).
--cost
Mark is O(live objects and their references), sweep is O(heap size). Copying is O(live objects) and needs space for the copy.
--java
// heap.get(i) = objects referenced by i. Returns the ids of garbage objects (mark and sweep).
static List<Integer> markSweep(List<List<Integer>> heap, List<Integer> roots) {
    boolean[] marked = new boolean[heap.size()];
    Deque<Integer> stack = new ArrayDeque<>(roots);
    while (!stack.isEmpty()) {                                    // mark: DFS from the roots
        int u = stack.pop();
        if (marked[u]) continue;
        marked[u] = true;
        for (int v : heap.get(u)) stack.push(v);
    }
    List<Integer> freed = new ArrayList<>();
    for (int i = 0; i < marked.length; i++) if (!marked[i]) freed.add(i);   // sweep
    return freed;
}

// Cheney copying collection. Returns the new heap (compact, BFS order); newRoots is filled with the roots' new ids.
static List<List<Integer>> copyCollect(List<List<Integer>> heap, List<Integer> roots, List<Integer> newRoots) {
    Map<Integer, Integer> forward = new HashMap<>();               // old id -> new id
    List<Integer> order = new ArrayList<>();                       // old ids in copy order (the queue)
    for (int r : roots) newRoots.add(copy(r, forward, order));
    List<List<Integer>> to = new ArrayList<>();
    for (int scan = 0; scan < order.size(); scan++) {              // scan pointer chases the end of the queue
        List<Integer> refs = new ArrayList<>();
        for (int c : heap.get(order.get(scan))) refs.add(copy(c, forward, order));
        to.add(refs);
    }
    return to;
}

private static int copy(int obj, Map<Integer, Integer> forward, List<Integer> order) {
    Integer f = forward.get(obj);
    if (f != null) return f;                                       // already copied: reuse the forwarding entry
    forward.put(obj, order.size());
    order.add(obj);
    return order.size() - 1;
}
--py
from collections import deque

def mark_sweep(heap, roots):                         # heap[i] = objects referenced by i. Returns garbage ids
    marked = [False] * len(heap)
    stack = list(roots)
    while stack:                                     # mark: DFS from the roots
        u = stack.pop()
        if marked[u]:
            continue
        marked[u] = True
        stack.extend(heap[u])
    return [i for i, m in enumerate(marked) if not m]    # sweep

def copy_collect(heap, roots):                       # Cheney copying collection: returns (new heap, new roots)
    forward, order = {}, []                          # forward: old id -> new id; order: the queue of copied objects

    def copy(obj):
        if obj not in forward:                       # already copied: reuse the forwarding entry
            forward[obj] = len(order)
            order.append(obj)
        return forward[obj]

    new_roots = [copy(r) for r in roots]
    to, scan = [], 0
    while scan < len(order):                         # scan pointer chases the end of the queue
        to.append([copy(c) for c in heap[order[scan]]])
        scan += 1
    return to, new_roots
--cpp
// heap[i] = objects referenced by i. Returns the ids of garbage objects (mark and sweep).
vector<int> markSweep(const vector<vector<int>>& heap, const vector<int>& roots) {
    vector<char> marked(heap.size(), 0);
    vector<int> stack(roots.begin(), roots.end());
    while (!stack.empty()) {                                       // mark: DFS from the roots
        int u = stack.back(); stack.pop_back();
        if (marked[u]) continue;
        marked[u] = 1;
        for (int v : heap[u]) stack.push_back(v);
    }
    vector<int> freed;
    for (size_t i = 0; i < marked.size(); i++) if (!marked[i]) freed.push_back(i);   // sweep
    return freed;
}

// Cheney copying collection. Returns the new heap (compact, BFS order); newRoots gets the roots' new ids.
vector<vector<int>> copyCollect(const vector<vector<int>>& heap, const vector<int>& roots, vector<int>& newRoots) {
    unordered_map<int, int> forward;                               // old id -> new id
    vector<int> order;                                             // old ids in copy order (the queue)
    auto copy = [&](int obj) {
        auto it = forward.find(obj);
        if (it != forward.end()) return it->second;                // already copied: reuse the forwarding entry
        forward[obj] = order.size();
        order.push_back(obj);
        return (int)order.size() - 1;
    };
    for (int r : roots) newRoots.push_back(copy(r));
    vector<vector<int>> to;
    for (size_t scan = 0; scan < order.size(); scan++) {           // scan pointer chases the end of the queue
        vector<int> refs;
        for (int c : heap[order[scan]]) refs.push_back(copy(c));
        to.push_back(refs);
    }
    return to;
}
--go
// heap[i] = objects referenced by i. Returns the ids of garbage objects (mark and sweep).
func markSweep(heap [][]int, roots []int) []int {
	marked := make([]bool, len(heap))
	stack := append([]int(nil), roots...)
	for len(stack) > 0 { // mark: DFS from the roots
		u := stack[len(stack)-1]
		stack = stack[:len(stack)-1]
		if marked[u] {
			continue
		}
		marked[u] = true
		stack = append(stack, heap[u]...)
	}
	var freed []int
	for i, m := range marked { // sweep
		if !m {
			freed = append(freed, i)
		}
	}
	return freed
}

// Cheney copying collection. Returns the new heap (compact, BFS order) and the roots' new ids.
func copyCollect(heap [][]int, roots []int) (to [][]int, newRoots []int) {
	forward := map[int]int{} // old id -> new id
	var order []int          // old ids in copy order (the queue)
	copyObj := func(obj int) int {
		if f, ok := forward[obj]; ok { // already copied: reuse the forwarding entry
			return f
		}
		forward[obj] = len(order)
		order = append(order, obj)
		return len(order) - 1
	}
	for _, r := range roots {
		newRoots = append(newRoots, copyObj(r))
	}
	for scan := 0; scan < len(order); scan++ { // scan pointer chases the end of the queue
		var refs []int
		for _, c := range heap[order[scan]] {
			refs = append(refs, copyObj(c))
		}
		to = append(to, refs)
	}
	return
}

### paging
--idea
Virtual memory lets every program believe it has a large private memory, while the machine has much less physical memory (RAM). Memory is cut into fixed-size pages (commonly 4 KB). Only some pages are in RAM at a time, in slots called frames. When a program touches a page that is not in RAM, a page fault occurs: the operating system loads the page from disk, which is thousands of times slower than RAM. If all frames are full it must first choose a page to evict.
The page replacement policy decides which page to evict, and a good policy means far fewer faults. The ideal (evict the page whose next use is farthest away) needs to see the future, so real systems approximate it. LRU, evicting the least recently used page, bets that the past predicts the future. Pure LRU is expensive to track on every memory access, so most operating systems use the Clock algorithm, a cheap approximation of LRU.
--build
1. The input is a reference string: the list of page numbers the program touches, in order. The number of frames is `frames`. A fault happens when the page is not currently in a frame.
2. LRU with a hash table plus an ordered list (see LRU cache): keep the pages in order of last use. On a hit, move the page to the most recent end. On a fault, if all frames are full, remove the least recent page, then insert the new page as most recent.
3. Clock: arrange the frames in a circle (a Queue that wraps around) with a "reference bit" for each page, set to 1 by the hardware whenever the page is used, and a hand that points at a frame.
4. On a hit, set the page's reference bit to 1. Nothing else changes, so a hit is nearly free.
5. On a fault, move the hand: while the page under the hand has its bit set to 1, clear the bit (a second chance) and move on. The first page found with bit 0 is the victim. Replace it with the new page (bit set to 1) and move the hand past it.
6. Count faults. The hash table `where` maps a page to its frame in the Clock version, so a hit check is O(1) (see Hash tables and counting).
--think
- Signal: a fixed-size fast store and a slower backing store, and you must pick what to evict. It is the same problem as every cache (see CPU, web and database caches). The measure is the fault (miss) rate, because each fault costs a disk read.
- FIFO is simplest (evict the oldest loaded page) but can behave strangely: with FIFO, giving a program more frames can sometimes cause more faults (Belady's anomaly). LRU and Clock do not have that problem.
- Clock keeps one bit per page instead of full ordering, and the work happens only on faults. Real kernels use improved variants (two lists of active and inactive pages, and extra bits for modified pages, since evicting a modified page means writing it to disk first).
- Thrashing: if the pages a program needs at once (its working set) do not fit in memory, it faults constantly and does almost no work. The fix is more memory or fewer running programs.
- Mistakes: not marking a page as used on a hit (Clock then degenerates to FIFO), not updating the `where` table when evicting, and counting the initial loading of empty frames as evictions (they are faults, but they evict nothing).
--cost
LRU: O(1) per reference with a hash table and linked list. Clock: O(1) per hit, and O(frames) in the worst case per fault, O(1) amortised.
--java
static int lruFaults(int[] refs, int frames) {
    LinkedHashMap<Integer, Boolean> mem = new LinkedHashMap<>(16, 0.75f, true);   // access order: eldest = least recently used
    int faults = 0;
    for (int p : refs) {
        if (mem.containsKey(p)) { mem.get(p); continue; }          // hit: get() marks it most recent
        faults++;
        if (mem.size() == frames) { Integer eldest = mem.keySet().iterator().next(); mem.remove(eldest); }   // evict LRU
        mem.put(p, true);
    }
    return faults;
}

static int clockFaults(int[] refs, int frames) {
    int[] page = new int[frames];
    boolean[] ref = new boolean[frames];
    Arrays.fill(page, -1);
    Map<Integer, Integer> where = new HashMap<>();                // page -> frame
    int hand = 0, faults = 0;
    for (int p : refs) {
        Integer f = where.get(p);
        if (f != null) { ref[f] = true; continue; }               // hit: set the reference bit
        faults++;
        while (page[hand] != -1 && ref[hand]) { ref[hand] = false; hand = (hand + 1) % frames; }   // second chance
        if (page[hand] != -1) where.remove(page[hand]);           // evict the victim
        page[hand] = p; ref[hand] = true; where.put(p, hand);
        hand = (hand + 1) % frames;
    }
    return faults;
}
--py
from collections import OrderedDict

def lru_faults(refs, frames):
    mem, faults = OrderedDict(), 0                   # least recently used page is first
    for p in refs:
        if p in mem:
            mem.move_to_end(p)                       # hit: mark as most recent
            continue
        faults += 1
        if len(mem) == frames:
            mem.popitem(last=False)                  # evict the least recently used
        mem[p] = True
    return faults

def clock_faults(refs, frames):
    page, ref, where = [None] * frames, [False] * frames, {}    # where: page -> frame
    hand = faults = 0
    for p in refs:
        if p in where:
            ref[where[p]] = True                     # hit: set the reference bit
            continue
        faults += 1
        while page[hand] is not None and ref[hand]:
            ref[hand] = False                        # second chance
            hand = (hand + 1) % frames
        if page[hand] is not None:
            del where[page[hand]]                    # evict the victim
        page[hand], ref[hand], where[p] = p, True, hand
        hand = (hand + 1) % frames
    return faults
--cpp
int lruFaults(const vector<int>& refs, int frames) {
    list<int> order;                                  // front = most recently used
    unordered_map<int, list<int>::iterator> where;    // page -> position in the list
    int faults = 0;
    for (int p : refs) {
        auto it = where.find(p);
        if (it != where.end()) { order.splice(order.begin(), order, it->second); continue; }   // hit: move to front
        faults++;
        if ((int)order.size() == frames) { where.erase(order.back()); order.pop_back(); }       // evict the LRU page
        order.push_front(p);
        where[p] = order.begin();
    }
    return faults;
}

int clockFaults(const vector<int>& refs, int frames) {
    vector<int> page(frames, -1);
    vector<char> ref(frames, 0);
    unordered_map<int, int> where;                    // page -> frame
    int hand = 0, faults = 0;
    for (int p : refs) {
        auto it = where.find(p);
        if (it != where.end()) { ref[it->second] = 1; continue; }   // hit: set the reference bit
        faults++;
        while (page[hand] != -1 && ref[hand]) { ref[hand] = 0; hand = (hand + 1) % frames; }   // second chance
        if (page[hand] != -1) where.erase(page[hand]);              // evict the victim
        page[hand] = p; ref[hand] = 1; where[p] = hand;
        hand = (hand + 1) % frames;
    }
    return faults;
}
--go
import "slices"

func lruFaults(refs []int, frames int) int {
	var mem []int // least recently used first
	faults := 0
	for _, p := range refs {
		if i := slices.Index(mem, p); i >= 0 {
			mem = slices.Delete(mem, i, i+1) // hit: pull it out, it goes back at the end
		} else {
			faults++
			if len(mem) == frames {
				mem = mem[1:] // evict the LRU page
			}
		}
		mem = append(mem, p) // most recent at the end
	}
	return faults
}

func clockFaults(refs []int, frames int) int {
	page := make([]int, frames)
	ref := make([]bool, frames)
	for i := range page {
		page[i] = -1
	}
	where := map[int]int{} // page -> frame
	hand, faults := 0, 0
	for _, p := range refs {
		if f, ok := where[p]; ok {
			ref[f] = true // hit: set the reference bit
			continue
		}
		faults++
		for page[hand] != -1 && ref[hand] { // second chance
			ref[hand] = false
			hand = (hand + 1) % frames
		}
		if page[hand] != -1 {
			delete(where, page[hand]) // evict the victim
		}
		page[hand], ref[hand], where[p] = p, true, hand
		hand = (hand + 1) % frames
	}
	return faults
}
### filesys
--idea
A file system turns a raw disk, which is just a long row of numbered blocks, into named files in folders. It has to answer three questions: where is the file called `/home/a/notes.txt` (find it by name), which blocks hold its bytes (find its data), and which blocks are still free (find space for new data). Each question has a neat data structure behind it.
Folders form a tree: a directory maps names to entries, and an entry is a file or another directory, so a path is found by walking the tree one name at a time. Free space is tracked with a bitset, one bit per block, where 0 means free. To store a file, the system looks for a run of free blocks. Directories with very many entries are indexed with a B-tree (ext4's HTree, NTFS, XFS and Btrfs do this), so that finding a name does not scan the whole folder.
--build
1. Disk: `nblocks` blocks, and a bitset `used` with one bit per block (see Bitsets). Initially all bits are 0.
2. Allocate `n` blocks: scan the bitset from the start counting a run of consecutive free bits. When the run reaches `n`, set those bits to 1 and return `(start, n)`, an extent (a start block and a length). This is "first fit". If no run is long enough, report that the disk is full or fragmented.
3. Directory tree: a directory is a map from name to entry, and an entry is either another directory or a file record `(size, start, n)` (see Binary trees for the tree idea). The root is an empty directory.
4. `mkdir(path)` and `create(path, size)`: split the path at `/`, walk down through directories (a missing one is an error), and add the entry in the last directory. For a file, compute `n = ceil(size / BLOCK)` and allocate an extent first.
5. `lookup(path)`: walk the same way and return the entry, or nothing.
6. `remove(path)`: unlink the entry and clear its extent's bits so the blocks become free again.
7. Real file systems store files as several extents or trees of blocks (inodes), keep a journal so a crash cannot leave the structures half updated, and use a B-tree for big directories so lookup is `log n` instead of a scan.
--think
- Signal: hierarchical names, allocation of space in units, "find a free run". The same pair, a tree for names and a bitmap for space, appears in memory allocators and databases.
- Fragmentation: first fit leaves small holes over time. Files may be split into several extents (so no contiguous run is needed), or the system defragments. SSDs care less about contiguity than spinning disks.
- Why a B-tree for directories: a flat list makes lookups linear in the number of entries, which is awful for a folder with millions of files. A B-tree keeps names sorted in wide pages (see B-trees and Database indexes), so a lookup reads a few pages and a listing comes out in order.
- Crash safety is a major design axis: journaling file systems write the intended change to a log first, and copy-on-write file systems never overwrite live data.
- Mistakes: leaking blocks when removal fails halfway, treating a path with a trailing slash differently, an off-by-one when rounding a size up to whole blocks (use `(size + BLOCK - 1) / BLOCK`), and forgetting that a zero-length file needs no blocks.
--cost
Allocation O(nblocks) per scan with a plain bitset (word-level tricks speed it up). Path lookup O(depth) with hash or tree directories.
--java
static class FileSystem {
    static final int BLOCK = 4096;
    final BitSet used = new BitSet();                      // free-space bitset: 1 = block in use
    final int nblocks;
    final Map<String, Object> root = new HashMap<>();      // a directory maps names to entries
    FileSystem(int nblocks) { this.nblocks = nblocks; }

    private int[] alloc(int n) {                           // first fit: a run of n free blocks
        int run = 0;
        for (int i = 0; i < nblocks; i++) {
            run = used.get(i) ? 0 : run + 1;
            if (run == n) { used.set(i - n + 1, i + 1); return new int[]{i - n + 1, n}; }
        }
        return null;
    }

    @SuppressWarnings("unchecked")
    private Map<String, Object> dir(String[] parts) {      // walk to the directory holding the last name
        Map<String, Object> d = root;
        for (int i = 0; i < parts.length - 1; i++) {
            Object e = d.get(parts[i]);
            if (!(e instanceof Map)) return null;
            d = (Map<String, Object>) e;
        }
        return d;
    }

    boolean mkdir(String path) {
        String[] p = path.replaceAll("^/+", "").split("/");
        Map<String, Object> d = dir(p);
        if (d == null || d.containsKey(p[p.length - 1])) return false;
        d.put(p[p.length - 1], new HashMap<String, Object>());
        return true;
    }

    boolean create(String path, int size) {
        String[] p = path.replaceAll("^/+", "").split("/");
        Map<String, Object> d = dir(p);
        if (d == null || d.containsKey(p[p.length - 1])) return false;
        int n = (size + BLOCK - 1) / BLOCK;                // whole blocks, rounded up
        int[] extent = n == 0 ? new int[]{0, 0} : alloc(n);
        if (extent == null) return false;                  // disk full or too fragmented
        d.put(p[p.length - 1], extent);                    // a file entry is its extent {start, n}
        return true;
    }

    int[] lookup(String path) {
        String[] p = path.replaceAll("^/+", "").split("/");
        Map<String, Object> d = dir(p);
        Object e = d == null ? null : d.get(p[p.length - 1]);
        return e instanceof int[] ? (int[]) e : null;
    }

    boolean remove(String path) {
        String[] p = path.replaceAll("^/+", "").split("/");
        Map<String, Object> d = dir(p);
        Object e = d == null ? null : d.get(p[p.length - 1]);
        if (!(e instanceof int[])) return false;
        int[] x = (int[]) e;
        used.clear(x[0], x[0] + x[1]);                     // blocks become free again
        d.remove(p[p.length - 1]);
        return true;
    }
}
--py
BLOCK = 4096

class FileSystem:
    def __init__(self, nblocks):
        self.nblocks = nblocks
        self.used = 0                                   # free-space bitset as an integer: bit i = 1 means block i is in use
        self.root = {}                                  # a directory maps names to entries; a file entry is (start, n)

    def _alloc(self, n):                                # first fit: a run of n free blocks
        run = 0
        for i in range(self.nblocks):
            run = 0 if self.used >> i & 1 else run + 1
            if run == n:
                start = i - n + 1
                self.used |= ((1 << n) - 1) << start
                return (start, n)
        return None

    def _dir(self, parts):                              # walk to the directory holding the last name
        d = self.root
        for name in parts[:-1]:
            d = d.get(name)
            if not isinstance(d, dict):
                return None
        return d

    def mkdir(self, path):
        parts = [p for p in path.split("/") if p]
        d = self._dir(parts)
        if d is None or parts[-1] in d:
            return False
        d[parts[-1]] = {}
        return True

    def create(self, path, size):
        parts = [p for p in path.split("/") if p]
        d = self._dir(parts)
        if d is None or parts[-1] in d:
            return False
        n = (size + BLOCK - 1) // BLOCK                 # whole blocks, rounded up
        extent = (0, 0) if n == 0 else self._alloc(n)
        if extent is None:
            return False                                # disk full or too fragmented
        d[parts[-1]] = extent
        return True

    def lookup(self, path):
        parts = [p for p in path.split("/") if p]
        d = self._dir(parts)
        e = d.get(parts[-1]) if d is not None else None
        return e if isinstance(e, tuple) else None

    def remove(self, path):
        parts = [p for p in path.split("/") if p]
        d = self._dir(parts)
        e = d.get(parts[-1]) if d is not None else None
        if not isinstance(e, tuple):
            return False
        self.used &= ~(((1 << e[1]) - 1) << e[0])       # blocks become free again
        del d[parts[-1]]
        return True
--cpp
struct FileSystem {
    static const int BLOCK = 4096;
    struct Dir;
    struct Entry { bool isDir; int start, n; shared_ptr<Dir> dir; };     // a file is an extent {start, n}
    struct Dir { map<string, Entry> items; };
    int nblocks;
    vector<bool> used;                                                   // free-space bitset: true = block in use
    shared_ptr<Dir> root = make_shared<Dir>();
    explicit FileSystem(int nblocks) : nblocks(nblocks), used(nblocks, false) {}

    static vector<string> split(const string& path) {
        vector<string> parts; string cur; stringstream ss(path);
        while (getline(ss, cur, '/')) if (!cur.empty()) parts.push_back(cur);
        return parts;
    }
    int alloc(int n) {                                                   // first fit: a run of n free blocks
        int run = 0;
        for (int i = 0; i < nblocks; i++) {
            run = used[i] ? 0 : run + 1;
            if (run == n) { for (int j = i - n + 1; j <= i; j++) used[j] = true; return i - n + 1; }
        }
        return -1;
    }
    shared_ptr<Dir> walk(const vector<string>& parts) {                  // directory holding the last name
        shared_ptr<Dir> d = root;
        for (size_t i = 0; i + 1 < parts.size(); i++) {
            auto it = d->items.find(parts[i]);
            if (it == d->items.end() || !it->second.isDir) return nullptr;
            d = it->second.dir;
        }
        return d;
    }
    bool mkdir(const string& path) {
        auto p = split(path); auto d = walk(p);
        if (!d || d->items.count(p.back())) return false;
        d->items[p.back()] = {true, 0, 0, make_shared<Dir>()};
        return true;
    }
    bool create(const string& path, int size) {
        auto p = split(path); auto d = walk(p);
        if (!d || d->items.count(p.back())) return false;
        int n = (size + BLOCK - 1) / BLOCK, start = 0;                   // whole blocks, rounded up
        if (n > 0 && (start = alloc(n)) < 0) return false;               // disk full or too fragmented
        d->items[p.back()] = {false, start, n, nullptr};
        return true;
    }
    bool lookup(const string& path, int& start, int& n) {
        auto p = split(path); auto d = walk(p);
        if (!d || !d->items.count(p.back()) || d->items[p.back()].isDir) return false;
        start = d->items[p.back()].start; n = d->items[p.back()].n;
        return true;
    }
    bool remove(const string& path) {
        auto p = split(path); auto d = walk(p);
        if (!d || !d->items.count(p.back()) || d->items[p.back()].isDir) return false;
        Entry e = d->items[p.back()];
        for (int j = e.start; j < e.start + e.n; j++) used[j] = false;   // blocks become free again
        d->items.erase(p.back());
        return true;
    }
};
--go
import "strings"

const block = 4096

type extent [2]int      // a file is a run of blocks {start, n}
type dir map[string]any // a directory maps names to a dir or an extent

type FileSystem struct {
	used []bool // free-space map: true = block in use
	root dir
}

func newFS(nblocks int) *FileSystem { return &FileSystem{used: make([]bool, nblocks), root: dir{}} }

func (fs *FileSystem) alloc(n int) (extent, bool) { // first fit: a run of n free blocks
	run := 0
	for i, u := range fs.used {
		if u {
			run = 0
		} else {
			run++
		}
		if run == n {
			for j := i - n + 1; j <= i; j++ {
				fs.used[j] = true
			}
			return extent{i - n + 1, n}, true
		}
	}
	return extent{}, false
}

func (fs *FileSystem) walk(path string) (dir, string) { // the directory holding the last name
	parts := strings.Split(strings.TrimLeft(path, "/"), "/")
	d := fs.root
	for _, p := range parts[:len(parts)-1] {
		sub, ok := d[p].(dir)
		if !ok {
			return nil, ""
		}
		d = sub
	}
	return d, parts[len(parts)-1]
}

func (fs *FileSystem) mkdir(path string) bool {
	d, name := fs.walk(path)
	if _, exists := d[name]; d == nil || exists {
		return false
	}
	d[name] = dir{}
	return true
}

func (fs *FileSystem) create(path string, size int) bool {
	d, name := fs.walk(path)
	if _, exists := d[name]; d == nil || exists {
		return false
	}
	e, ok := extent{}, true
	if n := (size + block - 1) / block; n > 0 { // whole blocks, rounded up
		e, ok = fs.alloc(n)
	}
	if !ok { // disk full or too fragmented
		return false
	}
	d[name] = e
	return true
}

func (fs *FileSystem) lookup(path string) (extent, bool) {
	d, name := fs.walk(path)
	e, ok := d[name].(extent)
	return e, ok
}

func (fs *FileSystem) remove(path string) bool {
	e, ok := fs.lookup(path)
	if !ok {
		return false
	}
	for i := e[0]; i < e[0]+e[1]; i++ { // blocks become free again
		fs.used[i] = false
	}
	d, name := fs.walk(path)
	delete(d, name)
	return true
}

### compiler
--idea
A compiler translates a program from one language into another form a machine can run. Even a tiny one shows the classic stages. The lexer (tokeniser) cuts the text into tokens: numbers, operators, brackets. The parser checks the token order against the grammar and builds a tree that shows structure, for example that `2 + 3 * 4` means `2 + (3 * 4)`. The code generator walks the tree and emits instructions. Real compilers add optimisation and register allocation in between.
Each stage uses a standard algorithm. The lexer is a finite automaton: it reads one character at a time and its state ("inside a number") decides what to do. The parser uses recursion (or an explicit stack) to match nested brackets and operator precedence. Code for an expression tree comes from a post-order traversal: do the left, then the right, then the operator, which is exactly the order a stack machine needs. Register allocation, deciding which values live in which CPU registers, is graph colouring.
--build
1. Lexer: scan the text with a state machine. If the character is a digit, stay in the "number" state and keep appending digits. A space ends the token. Operators and brackets are one-character tokens. Output a list of tokens (see Parsing and tokenizing and Finite automata matching).
2. Parser (recursive descent, one function per precedence level): `expr` parses `term` then, while the next token is `+` or `-`, parses another `term` and builds a node. `term` does the same for `*` and `/` over `factor`. `factor` is a number, or `(` `expr` `)` which calls `expr` again. Precedence comes from the call depth, and the loops give left-to-right grouping (`8-2-3` is `(8-2)-3`).
3. The result is a binary tree: a leaf holds a number, an inner node holds an operator and two children (see Binary trees).
4. Code generation: a post-order traversal of the tree (Tree traversals) emits `push v` for a leaf, and after both children emits the operator instruction. For `2+3*4` it gives `push 2, push 3, push 4, mul, add`.
5. Run the code on a stack machine: `push` puts a value on the stack (see Stacks), an operator pops two values, computes, and pushes the result. The answer is the one value left.
6. Register allocation: build an interference graph where each value is a node, and two values are joined if they are alive at the same time. Give each node a "colour" (a register) so joined nodes differ. Greedy colouring in order of decreasing degree usually works. If more than `k` colours are needed, some values must be spilled to memory (see Graph coloring).
--think
- Signal: structured text that must be understood, not just matched: calculators, config files, query languages, programming languages. Regular expressions are enough for tokens, but nesting (brackets) needs a parser. A grammar and a recursive descent parser are the quickest way to a correct one.
- Precedence and associativity are the usual bugs. Handle each precedence level in its own function, and use a loop (not recursion on the right) for left-associative operators.
- Intermediate representations: real compilers do not go from tree to machine code directly. They lower to a graph-based IR where optimisations (constant folding, dead code removal, inlining) are easier, and then pick instructions and registers.
- Graph colouring is NP-hard in general, so compilers use heuristics (Chaitin-Briggs) or linear-scan allocation for speed, as in JIT compilers.
- Mistakes: integer division semantics (truncation toward zero in C and Java, floor in Python), not handling an unexpected token with a clear error, and recursion depth on deeply nested input.
--cost
Lexing and parsing O(n) for an input of n characters. Code generation and running O(nodes). Greedy colouring O(V + E).
--java
static List<String> lex(String src) {                              // finite automaton: digits stay in the "number" state
    List<String> toks = new ArrayList<>();
    int i = 0;
    while (i < src.length()) {
        char c = src.charAt(i);
        if (Character.isDigit(c)) {
            int j = i;
            while (j < src.length() && Character.isDigit(src.charAt(j))) j++;
            toks.add(src.substring(i, j)); i = j;
        } else { if (c != ' ') toks.add(String.valueOf(c)); i++; }
    }
    return toks;
}

static class Parser {                                              // recursive descent: expr -> term -> factor
    final List<String> toks; int pos = 0; final List<String> code = new ArrayList<>();
    Parser(List<String> toks) { this.toks = toks; }
    String peek() { return pos < toks.size() ? toks.get(pos) : ""; }

    void expr() {
        term();
        while (peek().equals("+") || peek().equals("-")) { String op = toks.get(pos++); term(); code.add(op); }   // post-order: operator after operands
    }
    void term() {
        factor();
        while (peek().equals("*") || peek().equals("/")) { String op = toks.get(pos++); factor(); code.add(op); }
    }
    void factor() {
        String t = toks.get(pos++);
        if (t.equals("(")) { expr(); pos++; }                      // skip ")"
        else code.add("push " + t);
    }
}

static int run(List<String> code) {                                // stack machine
    Deque<Integer> st = new ArrayDeque<>();
    for (String ins : code) {
        if (ins.startsWith("push ")) { st.push(Integer.parseInt(ins.substring(5))); continue; }
        int b = st.pop(), a = st.pop();
        st.push(ins.equals("+") ? a + b : ins.equals("-") ? a - b : ins.equals("*") ? a * b : a / b);
    }
    return st.pop();
}

static int[] colorRegisters(List<List<Integer>> adj, int k) {      // greedy graph colouring; null if a value must spill
    int n = adj.size();
    Integer[] order = new Integer[n];
    for (int i = 0; i < n; i++) order[i] = i;
    Arrays.sort(order, (a, b) -> adj.get(b).size() - adj.get(a).size());   // busiest first
    int[] color = new int[n];
    Arrays.fill(color, -1);
    for (int v : order) {
        boolean[] taken = new boolean[k];
        for (int u : adj.get(v)) if (color[u] >= 0) taken[color[u]] = true;
        int c = 0;
        while (c < k && taken[c]) c++;
        if (c == k) return null;
        color[v] = c;
    }
    return color;
}
// usage: Parser p = new Parser(lex("2+3*(4-1)")); p.expr(); run(p.code) == 11
--py
def lex(src):                                        # finite automaton: digits stay in the "number" state
    toks, i = [], 0
    while i < len(src):
        c = src[i]
        if c.isdigit():
            j = i
            while j < len(src) and src[j].isdigit():
                j += 1
            toks.append(src[i:j]); i = j
        else:
            if c != " ":
                toks.append(c)
            i += 1
    return toks

def compile_expr(src):                               # recursive descent; returns stack-machine code
    toks, pos, code = lex(src), [0], []

    def peek():
        return toks[pos[0]] if pos[0] < len(toks) else ""

    def take():
        pos[0] += 1
        return toks[pos[0] - 1]

    def expr():
        term()
        while peek() in ("+", "-"):
            op = take(); term(); code.append(op)     # post-order: operator after its operands

    def term():
        factor()
        while peek() in ("*", "/"):
            op = take(); factor(); code.append(op)

    def factor():
        t = take()
        if t == "(":
            expr(); take()                           # skip ")"
        else:
            code.append("push " + t)

    expr()
    return code

def run(code):                                       # stack machine
    st = []
    for ins in code:
        if ins.startswith("push "):
            st.append(int(ins[5:])); continue
        b, a = st.pop(), st.pop()
        st.append({"+": a + b, "-": a - b, "*": a * b, "/": int(a / b)}[ins])   # division truncates toward zero
    return st.pop()

def color_registers(adj, k):                         # greedy graph colouring; None if a value must spill
    color = {}
    for v in sorted(range(len(adj)), key=lambda v: -len(adj[v])):    # busiest first
        taken = {color[u] for u in adj[v] if u in color}
        c = next((c for c in range(k) if c not in taken), None)
        if c is None:
            return None
        color[v] = c
    return [color[v] for v in range(len(adj))]
--cpp
vector<string> lexer(const string& src) {                      // finite automaton: digits stay in the "number" state
    vector<string> toks;
    size_t i = 0;
    while (i < src.size()) {
        if (isdigit((unsigned char)src[i])) {
            size_t j = i;
            while (j < src.size() && isdigit((unsigned char)src[j])) j++;
            toks.push_back(src.substr(i, j - i)); i = j;
        } else { if (src[i] != ' ') toks.push_back(string(1, src[i])); i++; }
    }
    return toks;
}

struct Parser {                                                 // recursive descent: expr -> term -> factor
    vector<string> toks; size_t pos = 0; vector<string> code;
    explicit Parser(const string& src) : toks(lexer(src)) {}
    string peek() { return pos < toks.size() ? toks[pos] : ""; }
    void expr() {
        term();
        while (peek() == "+" || peek() == "-") { string op = toks[pos++]; term(); code.push_back(op); }   // post-order
    }
    void term() {
        factor();
        while (peek() == "*" || peek() == "/") { string op = toks[pos++]; factor(); code.push_back(op); }
    }
    void factor() {
        string t = toks[pos++];
        if (t == "(") { expr(); pos++; }                        // skip ")"
        else code.push_back("push " + t);
    }
};

int run(const vector<string>& code) {                           // stack machine
    vector<int> st;
    for (auto& ins : code) {
        if (ins.rfind("push ", 0) == 0) { st.push_back(stoi(ins.substr(5))); continue; }
        int b = st.back(); st.pop_back();
        int a = st.back(); st.pop_back();
        st.push_back(ins == "+" ? a + b : ins == "-" ? a - b : ins == "*" ? a * b : a / b);
    }
    return st.back();
}

vector<int> colorRegisters(const vector<vector<int>>& adj, int k) {   // greedy graph colouring; empty if a value must spill
    int n = adj.size();
    vector<int> order(n), color(n, -1);
    iota(order.begin(), order.end(), 0);
    sort(order.begin(), order.end(), [&](int a, int b) { return adj[a].size() > adj[b].size(); });   // busiest first
    for (int v : order) {
        vector<char> taken(k, 0);
        for (int u : adj[v]) if (color[u] >= 0) taken[color[u]] = 1;
        int c = 0;
        while (c < k && taken[c]) c++;
        if (c == k) return {};
        color[v] = c;
    }
    return color;
}
--go
import (
	"sort"
	"strconv"
	"strings"
)

func lex(src string) []string { // finite automaton: digits stay in the "number" state
	var toks []string
	for i := 0; i < len(src); {
		if c := src[i]; c >= '0' && c <= '9' {
			j := i
			for j < len(src) && src[j] >= '0' && src[j] <= '9' {
				j++
			}
			toks = append(toks, src[i:j])
			i = j
		} else {
			if c != ' ' {
				toks = append(toks, string(c))
			}
			i++
		}
	}
	return toks
}

type Parser struct { // recursive descent: expr -> term -> factor
	toks []string
	pos  int
	code []string
}

func (p *Parser) peek() string {
	if p.pos < len(p.toks) {
		return p.toks[p.pos]
	}
	return ""
}

func (p *Parser) binary(next func(), ops string) {
	next()
	for t := p.peek(); t != "" && strings.Contains(ops, t); t = p.peek() {
		p.pos++
		next()
		p.code = append(p.code, t) // post-order: operator after operands
	}
}
func (p *Parser) expr() { p.binary(p.term, "+-") }
func (p *Parser) term() { p.binary(p.factor, "*/") }
func (p *Parser) factor() {
	t := p.toks[p.pos]
	p.pos++
	if t == "(" {
		p.expr()
		p.pos++ // skip ")"
	} else {
		p.code = append(p.code, "push "+t)
	}
}

func run(code []string) int { // stack machine
	var st []int
	for _, ins := range code {
		if n, ok := strings.CutPrefix(ins, "push "); ok {
			v, _ := strconv.Atoi(n)
			st = append(st, v)
			continue
		}
		a, b := st[len(st)-2], st[len(st)-1]
		st = st[:len(st)-2]
		switch ins {
		case "+":
			a += b
		case "-":
			a -= b
		case "*":
			a *= b
		default:
			a /= b
		}
		st = append(st, a)
	}
	return st[0]
}

func colorRegisters(adj [][]int, k int) []int { // greedy graph colouring; nil if a value must spill
	order := make([]int, len(adj))
	color := make([]int, len(adj))
	for i := range order {
		order[i], color[i] = i, -1
	}
	sort.SliceStable(order, func(a, b int) bool { return len(adj[order[a]]) > len(adj[order[b]]) }) // busiest first
	for _, v := range order {
		taken := make([]bool, k)
		for _, u := range adj[v] {
			if color[u] >= 0 {
				taken[color[u]] = true
			}
		}
		c := 0
		for c < k && taken[c] {
			c++
		}
		if c == k {
			return nil
		}
		color[v] = c
	}
	return color
}

// usage: p := &Parser{toks: lex("2+3*(4-1)")}; p.expr(); run(p.code) == 11

### regex
--idea
A regular expression (regex) engine decides whether text matches a pattern such as `a.*b`. Two designs exist. A backtracking engine (Perl, Python's `re`, Java, JavaScript) tries the alternatives one at a time, and when a choice fails it goes back and tries the next. It is simple and supports extras like back-references, but some patterns make it take exponential time. An automaton engine (grep in some modes, RE2, Rust's regex) turns the pattern into a finite automaton and follows all the choices at once, which takes time proportional to the text, never exponential.
The model supports characters, the wildcard `.` and the star `x*` (zero or more of `x`). The automaton method is Thompson's: the pattern is a row of positions, and the set of positions we could be in after reading each character is tracked together. Moving through the text updates the set once per character, so it needs only one pass. This set of states is what a DFA (deterministic finite automaton) built lazily would cache.
--build
1. Parse the pattern into tokens `(char, star)`: a character or `.`, and whether it is followed by `*`. A pattern with `m` tokens has states `0 .. m`, where state `i` means "the first `i` tokens are matched" and state `m` means "all matched".
2. Backtracking matcher `match(p, t)` (see Backtracking and Wildcard and regex matching): if the pattern is used up, succeed only if the text is used up. Let `first` say the next character fits token `p`. For a starred token, either skip it (`match(p+1, t)`) or, if `first`, consume one character and stay (`match(p, t+1)`). Otherwise require `first` and go to `match(p+1, t+1)`.
3. Automaton matcher: keep `cur`, a set of states. Start with state 0 and take its closure: from any state at a starred token you may also skip it for free, so add the next state (a free move, an epsilon transition).
4. For each text character `ch`: build `nxt`. For each state `i` in `cur` with token `i` matching `ch` (same char, or `.`): add `i` if the token is starred (it can match more), else `i + 1`. Then take the closure of `nxt` and make it the new `cur`.
5. At the end of the text the match succeeds if state `m` is in `cur`.
6. Both methods answer "does the whole text match". Real engines also search for a match anywhere, return the matched part, and compile bigger syntax (alternation `|`, groups, classes, counted repeats) into the same kind of state graph.
--think
- Signal: pattern matching with repetition and alternatives. For fixed strings use a simple search (see KMP). For simple patterns like wildcards, a DP over (pattern position, text position) also works and is the same idea as the state set.
- Backtracking can explode: `(a*)*b` against `aaaaaaaaaaaaaaaaaaaaaaaaaaac` tries exponentially many ways to split the `a`s before failing. This is a real source of outages, called ReDoS (regular expression denial of service). The state-set method runs in time linear in the text however nasty the pattern.
- What backtracking buys: features beyond regular languages (back-references like `(\w+) \1`) and easier capture groups. Pick the automaton for untrusted patterns or input, backtracking for rich features on trusted input.
- A DFA caches the state sets: each distinct set becomes one DFA state, built on demand. After the cache warms up, matching is one table lookup per character.
- Mistakes: forgetting the closure after the start and after every step (a leading starred token would then be mishandled), keeping a starred token's own state out of `nxt`, and confusing "matches the whole text" with "matches somewhere in the text".
--cost
Backtracking: exponential in the worst case. State sets: O(text length * pattern length) time, O(pattern length) space.
--java
static boolean matchBacktrack(String p, int pi, String t, int ti) {
    if (pi == p.length()) return ti == t.length();
    boolean first = ti < t.length() && (p.charAt(pi) == '.' || p.charAt(pi) == t.charAt(ti));
    if (pi + 1 < p.length() && p.charAt(pi + 1) == '*')           // star: skip the token, or use one character and stay
        return matchBacktrack(p, pi + 2, t, ti) || (first && matchBacktrack(p, pi, t, ti + 1));
    return first && matchBacktrack(p, pi + 1, t, ti + 1);
}

static boolean matchNfa(String p, String t) {                     // simulate all choices at once
    List<Character> ch = new ArrayList<>(); List<Boolean> star = new ArrayList<>();
    for (int i = 0; i < p.length(); i++) {
        boolean s = i + 1 < p.length() && p.charAt(i + 1) == '*';
        ch.add(p.charAt(i)); star.add(s);
        if (s) i++;
    }
    int m = ch.size();
    boolean[] cur = new boolean[m + 1];
    cur[0] = true;
    closure(cur, star);
    for (int k = 0; k < t.length(); k++) {
        boolean[] nxt = new boolean[m + 1];
        for (int i = 0; i < m; i++)
            if (cur[i] && (ch.get(i) == '.' || ch.get(i) == t.charAt(k))) nxt[star.get(i) ? i : i + 1] = true;
        closure(nxt, star);
        cur = nxt;
    }
    return cur[m];
}

private static void closure(boolean[] s, List<Boolean> star) {   // free moves: skip a starred token
    for (int i = 0; i < star.size(); i++) if (s[i] && star.get(i)) s[i + 1] = true;
}
--py
def match_backtrack(p, t):
    if not p:
        return not t
    first = bool(t) and p[0] in (t[0], ".")
    if len(p) >= 2 and p[1] == "*":                  # star: skip the token, or use one character and stay
        return match_backtrack(p[2:], t) or (first and match_backtrack(p, t[1:]))
    return first and match_backtrack(p[1:], t[1:])

def match_nfa(p, t):                                 # simulate all choices at once
    toks, i = [], 0
    while i < len(p):
        star = i + 1 < len(p) and p[i + 1] == "*"
        toks.append((p[i], star))
        i += 2 if star else 1
    m = len(toks)

    def closure(s):                                  # free moves: skip a starred token
        for i in range(m):
            if i in s and toks[i][1]:
                s.add(i + 1)
        return s

    cur = closure({0})
    for ch in t:
        nxt = set()
        for i in cur:
            if i < m and toks[i][0] in (ch, "."):
                nxt.add(i if toks[i][1] else i + 1)  # a starred token stays, a plain one moves on
        cur = closure(nxt)
    return m in cur
--cpp
bool matchBacktrack(const string& p, size_t pi, const string& t, size_t ti) {
    if (pi == p.size()) return ti == t.size();
    bool first = ti < t.size() && (p[pi] == '.' || p[pi] == t[ti]);
    if (pi + 1 < p.size() && p[pi + 1] == '*')                   // star: skip the token, or use one character and stay
        return matchBacktrack(p, pi + 2, t, ti) || (first && matchBacktrack(p, pi, t, ti + 1));
    return first && matchBacktrack(p, pi + 1, t, ti + 1);
}

bool matchNfa(const string& p, const string& t) {               // simulate all choices at once
    vector<char> ch; vector<bool> star;
    for (size_t i = 0; i < p.size(); i++) {
        bool s = i + 1 < p.size() && p[i + 1] == '*';
        ch.push_back(p[i]); star.push_back(s);
        if (s) i++;
    }
    int m = ch.size();
    auto closure = [&](vector<char>& s) { for (int i = 0; i < m; i++) if (s[i] && star[i]) s[i + 1] = 1; };   // free moves
    vector<char> cur(m + 1, 0);
    cur[0] = 1; closure(cur);
    for (char c : t) {
        vector<char> nxt(m + 1, 0);
        for (int i = 0; i < m; i++)
            if (cur[i] && (ch[i] == '.' || ch[i] == c)) nxt[star[i] ? i : i + 1] = 1;
        closure(nxt);
        cur = nxt;
    }
    return cur[m];
}
--go
func matchBacktrack(p string, pi int, t string, ti int) bool {
	if pi == len(p) {
		return ti == len(t)
	}
	first := ti < len(t) && (p[pi] == '.' || p[pi] == t[ti])
	if pi+1 < len(p) && p[pi+1] == '*' { // star: skip the token, or use one character and stay
		return matchBacktrack(p, pi+2, t, ti) || first && matchBacktrack(p, pi, t, ti+1)
	}
	return first && matchBacktrack(p, pi+1, t, ti+1)
}

func matchNfa(p, t string) bool { // simulate all choices at once
	var ch []byte
	var star []bool
	for i := 0; i < len(p); i++ {
		s := i+1 < len(p) && p[i+1] == '*'
		ch, star = append(ch, p[i]), append(star, s)
		if s {
			i++
		}
	}
	m := len(ch)
	cur := make([]bool, m+1) // cur[i]: the automaton may be before token i
	cur[0] = true
	closure(cur, star)
	for k := 0; k < len(t); k++ {
		nxt := make([]bool, m+1)
		for i := 0; i < m; i++ {
			if cur[i] && (ch[i] == '.' || ch[i] == t[k]) {
				if star[i] {
					nxt[i] = true // a starred token can eat more
				} else {
					nxt[i+1] = true
				}
			}
		}
		closure(nxt, star)
		cur = nxt
	}
	return cur[m]
}

func closure(s, star []bool) { // free moves: skip a starred token
	for i := range star {
		if s[i] && star[i] {
			s[i+1] = true
		}
	}
}
### diff
--idea
A diff tool shows how two versions of a text differ: which lines were removed, which were added, and which stayed. It is behind `git diff`, code review screens and merge tools. The natural way to define "the smallest set of changes" is to find the longest common subsequence (LCS) of the two files: the longest list of lines that appear in both, in the same order. Everything in the old file outside it is a deletion, and everything in the new file outside it is an insertion.
The LCS comes from dynamic programming. Let `dp[i][j]` be the LCS length of the old lines from `i` onward and the new lines from `j` onward. If the two lines match, take them and add 1. If not, drop one line from either side and take the better result. Filling the table from the end and then walking from the start gives the edit script. Comparing whole lines is made fast by hashing each line to a number first.
--build
1. Hash each line (see String hashing) to a 32-bit number, and compare numbers instead of strings. On a hash match confirm with a real string comparison so a collision can never produce a wrong diff.
2. Fill the table (see Two-sequence DP): for `i` from `n` down to 0 and `j` from `m` down to 0, `dp[i][j] = 0` at either end. If `a[i] == b[j]` then `dp[i][j] = 1 + dp[i+1][j+1]`. Otherwise `dp[i][j] = max(dp[i+1][j], dp[i][j+1])`.
3. Walk from `(0, 0)`. If the lines match, output them unchanged (` `) and step diagonally. Else if `dp[i+1][j] >= dp[i][j+1]` output a deletion of `a[i]` (`-`) and move down, otherwise output an insertion of `b[j]` (`+`) and move right.
4. When one file runs out, output the rest of the other as deletions or insertions.
5. The kept lines form an LCS. The number of changes is `n + m - 2 * LCS`. This is the edit distance if only insertions and deletions are allowed. With substitutions also allowed it is the classic Edit distance.
6. Real diff programs use faster algorithms (Myers' O(ND) algorithm, patience diff for readable code diffs) and trim common start and end lines first, but they find the same kind of answer.
--think
- Signal: "what changed between these two sequences". Version control, file sync, spell correction (edit distance on characters), DNA comparison, plagiarism checks.
- The table costs O(n * m) memory, which is too much for big files. Production tools use the Myers algorithm, which is fast when files are similar (cost grows with the number of differences `D`), and Hirschberg's trick to recover the script in linear space.
- Several different diffs can be equally short. Tools add rules to make them readable, for example preferring to keep blank lines and braces together. Patience diff anchors on lines that occur exactly once.
- A three-way merge (see Git internals and merge base) diffs each branch against the common ancestor and combines the two edit scripts, flagging overlapping changes as conflicts.
- Mistakes: indexing the table with the lengths swapped, forgetting the base row and column of zeros, and trusting the hash alone.
--cost
Time and space O(n * m) for the DP table, or O(n * m) time and O(n + m) space with Hirschberg. Myers is O((n + m) * D).
--java
static int lineHash(String s) {                                   // FNV-1a, 32 bit
    int h = 0x811c9dc5;
    for (int i = 0; i < s.length(); i++) { h ^= s.charAt(i); h *= 16777619; }
    return h;
}

static List<String> diff(String[] a, String[] b) {
    int n = a.length, m = b.length;
    int[] ha = new int[n], hb = new int[m];
    for (int i = 0; i < n; i++) ha[i] = lineHash(a[i]);
    for (int j = 0; j < m; j++) hb[j] = lineHash(b[j]);
    int[][] dp = new int[n + 1][m + 1];                           // LCS length of a[i..] and b[j..]
    for (int i = n - 1; i >= 0; i--) for (int j = m - 1; j >= 0; j--)
        dp[i][j] = (ha[i] == hb[j] && a[i].equals(b[j])) ? 1 + dp[i + 1][j + 1] : Math.max(dp[i + 1][j], dp[i][j + 1]);
    List<String> out = new ArrayList<>();
    int i = 0, j = 0;
    while (i < n && j < m) {
        if (ha[i] == hb[j] && a[i].equals(b[j])) { out.add("  " + a[i]); i++; j++; }       // kept line
        else if (dp[i + 1][j] >= dp[i][j + 1]) out.add("- " + a[i++]);                       // deletion
        else out.add("+ " + b[j++]);                                                         // insertion
    }
    while (i < n) out.add("- " + a[i++]);
    while (j < m) out.add("+ " + b[j++]);
    return out;
}
--py
def line_hash(s):                                    # FNV-1a, 32 bit
    h = 0x811c9dc5
    for ch in s:
        h = ((h ^ ord(ch)) * 16777619) & 0xFFFFFFFF
    return h

def diff(a, b):
    n, m = len(a), len(b)
    ha, hb = [line_hash(x) for x in a], [line_hash(x) for x in b]
    same = lambda i, j: ha[i] == hb[j] and a[i] == b[j]      # hash first, then confirm
    dp = [[0] * (m + 1) for _ in range(n + 1)]               # LCS length of a[i:] and b[j:]
    for i in range(n - 1, -1, -1):
        for j in range(m - 1, -1, -1):
            dp[i][j] = 1 + dp[i + 1][j + 1] if same(i, j) else max(dp[i + 1][j], dp[i][j + 1])
    out, i, j = [], 0, 0
    while i < n and j < m:
        if same(i, j):
            out.append("  " + a[i]); i += 1; j += 1         # kept line
        elif dp[i + 1][j] >= dp[i][j + 1]:
            out.append("- " + a[i]); i += 1                  # deletion
        else:
            out.append("+ " + b[j]); j += 1                  # insertion
    out += ["- " + x for x in a[i:]] + ["+ " + x for x in b[j:]]
    return out
--cpp
unsigned lineHash(const string& s) {                                  // FNV-1a, 32 bit
    unsigned h = 0x811c9dc5u;
    for (unsigned char ch : s) { h ^= ch; h *= 16777619u; }
    return h;
}

vector<string> diff(const vector<string>& a, const vector<string>& b) {
    int n = a.size(), m = b.size();
    vector<unsigned> ha(n), hb(m);
    for (int i = 0; i < n; i++) ha[i] = lineHash(a[i]);
    for (int j = 0; j < m; j++) hb[j] = lineHash(b[j]);
    auto same = [&](int i, int j) { return ha[i] == hb[j] && a[i] == b[j]; };   // hash first, then confirm
    vector<vector<int>> dp(n + 1, vector<int>(m + 1, 0));                      // LCS length of a[i..] and b[j..]
    for (int i = n - 1; i >= 0; i--) for (int j = m - 1; j >= 0; j--)
        dp[i][j] = same(i, j) ? 1 + dp[i + 1][j + 1] : max(dp[i + 1][j], dp[i][j + 1]);
    vector<string> out;
    int i = 0, j = 0;
    while (i < n && j < m) {
        if (same(i, j)) { out.push_back("  " + a[i]); i++; j++; }            // kept line
        else if (dp[i + 1][j] >= dp[i][j + 1]) out.push_back("- " + a[i++]);  // deletion
        else out.push_back("+ " + b[j++]);                                    // insertion
    }
    while (i < n) out.push_back("- " + a[i++]);
    while (j < m) out.push_back("+ " + b[j++]);
    return out;
}
--go
func lineHash(s string) uint32 { // FNV-1a, 32 bit
	h := uint32(0x811c9dc5)
	for i := 0; i < len(s); i++ {
		h ^= uint32(s[i])
		h *= 16777619
	}
	return h
}

func diff(a, b []string) []string {
	n, m := len(a), len(b)
	ha, hb := make([]uint32, n), make([]uint32, m)
	for i := range a {
		ha[i] = lineHash(a[i])
	}
	for j := range b {
		hb[j] = lineHash(b[j])
	}
	same := func(i, j int) bool { return ha[i] == hb[j] && a[i] == b[j] } // cheap hash test first
	dp := make([][]int, n+1)                                              // LCS length of a[i:] and b[j:]
	for i := range dp {
		dp[i] = make([]int, m+1)
	}
	for i := n - 1; i >= 0; i-- {
		for j := m - 1; j >= 0; j-- {
			if same(i, j) {
				dp[i][j] = 1 + dp[i+1][j+1]
			} else {
				dp[i][j] = max(dp[i+1][j], dp[i][j+1])
			}
		}
	}
	var out []string
	i, j := 0, 0
	for i < n && j < m {
		switch {
		case same(i, j): // kept line
			out = append(out, "  "+a[i])
			i, j = i+1, j+1
		case dp[i+1][j] >= dp[i][j+1]: // deletion
			out = append(out, "- "+a[i])
			i++
		default: // insertion
			out = append(out, "+ "+b[j])
			j++
		}
	}
	for ; i < n; i++ {
		out = append(out, "- "+a[i])
	}
	for ; j < m; j++ {
		out = append(out, "+ "+b[j])
	}
	return out
}

### git
--idea
Git stores a project's history as a graph of commits. Each commit records a snapshot, a message and the id of its parent commit (or parents, for a merge). The id is a hash of the commit's content, including its parents' ids, so an id identifies its whole history: change anything in the past and every later id changes. The graph has edges from child to parent and no cycles, so it is a directed acyclic graph (DAG).
Many everyday operations are graph algorithms on this DAG. `git log` lists commits so that children come before parents (a topological order). To merge two branches, Git needs their merge base, the most recent commit that both branches contain, which is a lowest common ancestor. The merge compares each branch against that base to see what each side changed.
--build
1. Id: hash the commit's parent ids plus its message to get a fixed-size id (real Git hashes the full object with SHA-1 or SHA-256, see String hashing and Cryptographic hashes and passwords). The model uses a 64-bit mixer.
2. Store `commits[id] = (parents, message)`. A new commit lists its parents, so the graph only ever grows at the tip and the parents always exist before their children.
3. `ancestors(id)`: depth first or breadth first search following parent links (see DFS), returning the set of every commit reachable, including `id` itself.
4. Merge base of `a` and `b`: take `common = ancestors(a) & ancestors(b)`. These are all common ancestors. The best ones are those that are not an ancestor of another common ancestor, and there may be more than one for criss-cross merges. Return one of them, or all.
5. Log order: restrict to the commits reachable from the head, count for each commit how many of its children are in that set, and run Kahn's topological sort starting from the head: output a commit only after all its children have been output. This guarantees children before parents (a Topological sort).
6. Real Git speeds the merge-base step up with generation numbers and commit-graph files, and uses binary lifting-style jumps in some tools (see LCA and binary lifting for the tree version of the same problem).
--think
- Signal: "relationship between versions", "what do these two branches share", "order events that depend on earlier ones". Version histories, build caches keyed by content hash, blockchains, and package lock files.
- In a tree there is one lowest common ancestor. In a DAG there can be several best common ancestors, so tools take one (the default) or merge the candidates (the recursive strategy).
- The id scheme is a Merkle structure: the hash covers the parents, so history cannot be rewritten without changing ids (see Merkle trees). That is why rebasing creates new commits.
- Because commits are immutable and identified by content, two repositories can sync by exchanging the ids they have and sending the missing commits, much like comparing hash trees.
- Mistakes: following edges from parent to child (they are stored child to parent), forgetting that a merge commit has two parents, and computing the log by timestamp instead of by graph order (clocks differ between machines).
--cost
Ancestors and log: O(V + E) for the commits and parent links reachable. The naive merge base is O(V + E) per query.
--java
static long mix(long x) { x ^= x >>> 30; x *= 0xbf58476d1ce4e5b9L; x ^= x >>> 27; x *= 0x94d049bb133111ebL; return x ^ (x >>> 31); }

static class Repo {
    final Map<String, List<String>> parents = new HashMap<>();
    final Map<String, String> message = new HashMap<>();

    String commit(List<String> ps, String msg) {                          // id = hash of parents and message
        long h = 0xcbf29ce484222325L;
        for (String p : ps) for (int i = 0; i < p.length(); i++) h = mix(h ^ p.charAt(i));
        for (int i = 0; i < msg.length(); i++) h = mix(h ^ msg.charAt(i));
        String id = Long.toHexString(h);
        parents.put(id, ps); message.put(id, msg);
        return id;
    }

    Set<String> ancestors(String id) {                                    // every commit reachable via parent links
        Set<String> seen = new HashSet<>();
        Deque<String> stack = new ArrayDeque<>();
        stack.push(id);
        while (!stack.isEmpty()) {
            String c = stack.pop();
            if (seen.add(c)) for (String p : parents.get(c)) stack.push(p);
        }
        return seen;
    }

    String mergeBase(String a, String b) {                                // a best common ancestor
        Set<String> common = ancestors(a);
        common.retainAll(ancestors(b));
        Set<String> shadowed = new HashSet<>();                           // ancestors of other common commits
        for (String c : common) for (String p : parents.get(c)) shadowed.addAll(ancestors(p));
        List<String> best = new ArrayList<>();
        for (String c : common) if (!shadowed.contains(c)) best.add(c);
        Collections.sort(best);
        return best.isEmpty() ? null : best.get(0);
    }

    List<String> log(String head) {                                       // children before parents
        Set<String> reach = ancestors(head);
        Map<String, Integer> kids = new HashMap<>();
        for (String c : reach) kids.put(c, 0);
        for (String c : reach) for (String p : parents.get(c)) kids.merge(p, 1, Integer::sum);
        Deque<String> ready = new ArrayDeque<>();
        ready.add(head);
        List<String> out = new ArrayList<>();
        while (!ready.isEmpty()) {                                        // Kahn's topological sort
            String c = ready.poll(); out.add(c);
            for (String p : parents.get(c)) if (kids.merge(p, -1, Integer::sum) == 0) ready.add(p);
        }
        return out;
    }
}
--py
M64 = 0xFFFFFFFFFFFFFFFF

def mix(x):
    x ^= x >> 30; x = (x * 0xbf58476d1ce4e5b9) & M64
    x ^= x >> 27; x = (x * 0x94d049bb133111eb) & M64
    return x ^ (x >> 31)

class Repo:
    def __init__(self):
        self.parents, self.message = {}, {}

    def commit(self, parents, msg):                  # id = hash of parents and message
        h = 0xcbf29ce484222325
        for ch in "".join(parents) + msg:
            h = mix(h ^ ord(ch))
        cid = format(h, "x")
        self.parents[cid], self.message[cid] = list(parents), msg
        return cid

    def ancestors(self, cid):                        # every commit reachable via parent links
        seen, stack = set(), [cid]
        while stack:
            c = stack.pop()
            if c not in seen:
