    return (h ^ (h >> 29)) % P

def shingles(text, k=3):                             # set of hashed k-word shingles
    w = text.lower().split()
    out = {shingle_hash(" ".join(w[i:i + k])) for i in range(len(w) - k + 1)}
    return out or {shingle_hash(" ".join(w))}

def minhash(shs, n=128):                             # n hash functions h_j(x) = (a*x + b) mod P
    sig = []
    for j in range(n):
        a, b = 1 + (j * 1103515245 + 12345) % (P - 1), (j * 69069 + 1) % P
        sig.append(min((a * x + b) % P for x in shs))
    return sig

def similarity(a, b):                                # fraction of equal positions
    return sum(x == y for x, y in zip(a, b)) / len(a)

def candidates(sigs, bands):                         # LSH: documents sharing any whole band
    rows, pairs = len(sigs[0]) // bands, set()
    for b in range(bands):
        buckets = {}
        for d, sig in enumerate(sigs):
            buckets.setdefault(tuple(sig[b * rows:(b + 1) * rows]), []).append(d)
        for ds in buckets.values():
            pairs.update((ds[i], ds[j]) for i in range(len(ds)) for j in range(i + 1, len(ds)))
    return pairs
--cpp
typedef unsigned long long u64;
const u64 P = 2147483647ULL;                                         // a prime; a*x stays below 2^62

u64 shingleHash(const string& s) {
    u64 h = 0xcbf29ce484222325ULL;
    for (unsigned char ch : s) { h ^= ch; h *= 0x100000001b3ULL; }
    return (h ^ (h >> 29)) % P;
}

set<u64> shingles(const string& text, int k = 3) {                   // set of hashed k-word shingles
    vector<string> w; stringstream ss(text); string x;
    while (ss >> x) { transform(x.begin(), x.end(), x.begin(), ::tolower); w.push_back(x); }
    set<u64> out;
    for (size_t i = 0; i + k <= w.size(); i++) {
        string sh = w[i];
        for (int j = 1; j < k; j++) sh += " " + w[i + j];
        out.insert(shingleHash(sh));
    }
    if (out.empty()) { string sh = w.empty() ? "" : w[0]; for (size_t j = 1; j < w.size(); j++) sh += " " + w[j]; out.insert(shingleHash(sh)); }
    return out;
}

vector<u64> minhash(const set<u64>& shs, int n = 128) {              // n hash functions h_j(x) = (a*x + b) mod P
    vector<u64> sig(n);
    for (int j = 0; j < n; j++) {
        u64 a = 1 + (j * 1103515245ULL + 12345ULL) % (P - 1), b = (j * 69069ULL + 1) % P, best = ~0ULL;
        for (u64 v : shs) best = min(best, (a * v + b) % P);
        sig[j] = best;
    }
    return sig;
}

double similarity(const vector<u64>& a, const vector<u64>& b) {      // fraction of equal positions
    int same = 0;
    for (size_t j = 0; j < a.size(); j++) same += a[j] == b[j];
    return (double)same / a.size();
}

set<pair<int, int>> candidates(const vector<vector<u64>>& sigs, int bands) {   // LSH: documents sharing any whole band
    int rows = sigs[0].size() / bands;
    set<pair<int, int>> pairs;
    for (int b = 0; b < bands; b++) {
        map<vector<u64>, vector<int>> buckets;
        for (size_t d = 0; d < sigs.size(); d++)
            buckets[vector<u64>(sigs[d].begin() + b * rows, sigs[d].begin() + (b + 1) * rows)].push_back(d);
        for (auto& kv : buckets)
            for (size_t i = 0; i < kv.second.size(); i++) for (size_t j = i + 1; j < kv.second.size(); j++)
                pairs.insert({kv.second[i], kv.second[j]});
    }
    return pairs;
}
--go
import (
	"fmt"
	"math"
	"slices"
	"strings"
)

const P = 2147483647 // a prime; a*x stays below 2^62

func shingleHash(s string) uint64 {
	h := uint64(0xcbf29ce484222325)
	for i := 0; i < len(s); i++ {
		h ^= uint64(s[i])
		h *= 0x100000001b3
	}
	return (h ^ h>>29) % P
}

func shingles(text string, k int) map[uint64]bool { // set of hashed k-word shingles
	w := strings.Fields(strings.ToLower(text))
	out := map[uint64]bool{}
	for i := 0; i+k <= len(w); i++ {
		out[shingleHash(strings.Join(w[i:i+k], " "))] = true
	}
	if len(out) == 0 { // text shorter than k words: one shingle for all of it
		out[shingleHash(strings.Join(w, " "))] = true
	}
	return out
}

func minhash(shs map[uint64]bool, n int) []uint64 { // n hash functions h_j(x) = (a*x + b) mod P
	sig := make([]uint64, n)
	for j := range sig {
		a := 1 + (uint64(j)*1103515245+12345)%(P-1)
		b := (uint64(j)*69069 + 1) % P
		best := uint64(math.MaxUint64)
		for x := range shs {
			best = min(best, (a*x+b)%P)
		}
		sig[j] = best
	}
	return sig
}

func similarity(a, b []uint64) float64 { // fraction of equal positions
	same := 0
	for j := range a {
		if a[j] == b[j] {
			same++
		}
	}
	return float64(same) / float64(len(a))
}

func candidates(sigs [][]uint64, bands int) [][2]int { // LSH: documents sharing any whole band
	rows := len(sigs[0]) / bands
	seen := map[[2]int]bool{}
	for b := 0; b < bands; b++ {
		buckets := map[string][]int{}
		for d, sig := range sigs {
			key := fmt.Sprint(sig[b*rows : (b+1)*rows])
			buckets[key] = append(buckets[key], d)
		}
		for _, ds := range buckets {
			for i := range ds {
				for j := i + 1; j < len(ds); j++ {
					seen[[2]int{ds[i], ds[j]}] = true
				}
			}
		}
	}
	pairs := make([][2]int, 0, len(seen))
	for p := range seen {
		pairs = append(pairs, p)
	}
	slices.SortFunc(pairs, func(a, b [2]int) int { return a[0]*1000000 + a[1] - b[0]*1000000 - b[1] })
	return pairs
}

### analytics
--idea
Streaming analytics answers questions about a flood of events (clicks, log lines, transactions) that is too big to store or even to scan twice. Memory is small and fixed, each event is seen once, and answers can be a little approximate. Three questions come up all the time: how often has each item appeared, how many different items have appeared, and what is the typical (median) value.
Each has a small, clever structure. A count-min sketch estimates the frequency of any item using a small table of counters and several hash functions; it can overestimate but never underestimate. HyperLogLog estimates the number of distinct items using the fact that among `n` random hashes, the longest run of leading zero bits is about `log2 n`. A running median uses two heaps, one holding the smaller half and one the larger half, so the median is always at the top of one of them. A uniform sample of the stream (see Reservoir sampling) covers everything else.
--build
1. Count-min sketch: a table of `depth` rows and `width` counters, and one hash function per row. `add(key)`: in every row `r`, add 1 to `table[r][hash_r(key) mod width]`. `estimate(key)`: take the minimum of those `depth` counters. Other keys may have collided into the same counters and added to them, so every counter is at least the true count, and the minimum is the least inflated one.
2. HyperLogLog with `m = 2^p` registers: hash each item to 64 well-mixed bits. The top `p` bits choose a register. In the remaining bits, count the leading zeros and add 1 (the rank). Keep the maximum rank seen in each register. A rare long run of zeros in one register is evidence of many items, and averaging over `m` registers smooths the luck.
3. Estimate: `E = alpha * m^2 / sum(2^-register)` with `alpha = 0.7213 / (1 + 1.079 / m)` (a harmonic mean). If `E` is small and some registers are still zero, use linear counting instead, `m * ln(m / zeros)`, which is more accurate for small counts.
4. Running median: keep `low`, a max-heap with the smaller half, and `high`, a min-heap with the larger half. To add `x`, push it to `low`, move the largest of `low` to `high`, and if `high` is now bigger than `low`, move its smallest back. The median is the top of `low` (odd count) or the average of both tops (even count). See Running median and Heaps and priority queues.
5. Hashing: all three need a hash that spreads bits evenly (see Hash tables and counting), so the model finishes every hash with a bit mixer.
6. Real systems merge sketches from many machines: count-min tables add cell by cell, and HyperLogLog registers take the cell by cell maximum.
--think
- Signal: "top items", "unique visitors", "percentiles" over data you cannot hold in memory. The trade is exact answers for tiny memory: a HyperLogLog with 1024 registers (about 1 KB with small registers) counts billions of distinct items with about 3 percent error.
- Error knobs: for count-min the error is about `total / width` and the failure chance shrinks exponentially with `depth`. For HyperLogLog the relative error is about `1.04 / sqrt(m)`.
- Count-min finds heavy hitters (frequent items) well and rare items badly, because small counts drown in the collision noise. Combine it with a small heap of the current top candidates to list the heavy hitters, not only to estimate their counts.
- Exact running median needs all data in the heaps. For huge streams use quantile sketches (t-digest, GK, KLL) that give approximate percentiles in small memory.
- Mistakes: using one hash function for all rows of count-min (rows must be independent), using the low bits and the high bits of the same hash for two jobs in HyperLogLog without mixing, and forgetting the small-range correction.
--cost
Count-min: O(depth) per update and per query, O(width * depth) memory. HyperLogLog: O(1) per item, O(m) memory. Running median: O(log n) per item, O(n) memory.
--java
static long mix(long x) { x ^= x >>> 30; x *= 0xbf58476d1ce4e5b9L; x ^= x >>> 27; x *= 0x94d049bb133111ebL; return x ^ (x >>> 31); }
static long hash64(String s) { long h = 0xcbf29ce484222325L; for (int i = 0; i < s.length(); i++) { h ^= s.charAt(i); h *= 0x100000001b3L; } return mix(h); }

static class CountMin {
    final long[][] table; final int width;
    CountMin(int width, int depth) { this.width = width; table = new long[depth][width]; }
    private int col(String key, int row) { return (int) Long.remainderUnsigned(mix(hash64(key) + row * 0x9E3779B97F4A7C15L), width); }
    void add(String key) { for (int r = 0; r < table.length; r++) table[r][col(key, r)]++; }
    long estimate(String key) {                                              // never below the true count
        long best = Long.MAX_VALUE;
        for (int r = 0; r < table.length; r++) best = Math.min(best, table[r][col(key, r)]);
        return best;
    }
}

static class HyperLogLog {
    final int p, m; final int[] reg;
    HyperLogLog(int p) { this.p = p; m = 1 << p; reg = new int[m]; }
    void add(String key) {
        long x = hash64(key);
        int idx = (int) (x >>> (64 - p));                                    // top p bits choose a register
        long w = x << p;                                                     // the remaining bits
        int rank = Math.min(Long.numberOfLeadingZeros(w), 64 - p) + 1;       // position of the first 1 bit
        reg[idx] = Math.max(reg[idx], rank);
    }
    double estimate() {
        double sum = 0; int zeros = 0;
        for (int v : reg) { sum += Math.pow(2, -v); if (v == 0) zeros++; }
        double e = (0.7213 / (1 + 1.079 / m)) * m * m / sum;
        return (e <= 2.5 * m && zeros > 0) ? m * Math.log((double) m / zeros) : e;     // small range: linear counting
    }
}

static class RunningMedian {
    final PriorityQueue<Integer> low = new PriorityQueue<>(Collections.reverseOrder()), high = new PriorityQueue<>();
    void add(int x) {
        low.add(x); high.add(low.poll());                                    // route through both heaps
        if (high.size() > low.size()) low.add(high.poll());                  // keep low the same size or one larger
    }
    double median() { return low.size() > high.size() ? low.peek() : (low.peek() + high.peek()) / 2.0; }
}
--py
import heapq, math

M64 = 0xFFFFFFFFFFFFFFFF

def mix(x):
    x ^= x >> 30; x = (x * 0xbf58476d1ce4e5b9) & M64
    x ^= x >> 27; x = (x * 0x94d049bb133111eb) & M64
    return x ^ (x >> 31)

def hash64(s):
    h = 0xcbf29ce484222325
    for ch in s:
        h = ((h ^ ord(ch)) * 0x100000001b3) & M64
    return mix(h)

class CountMin:
    def __init__(self, width, depth):
        self.width, self.table = width, [[0] * width for _ in range(depth)]

    def _col(self, key, row):                        # an independent hash per row
        return mix((hash64(key) + row * 0x9E3779B97F4A7C15) & M64) % self.width

    def add(self, key):
        for r, row in enumerate(self.table):
            row[self._col(key, r)] += 1

    def estimate(self, key):                         # never below the true count
        return min(row[self._col(key, r)] for r, row in enumerate(self.table))

class HyperLogLog:
    def __init__(self, p=10):
        self.p, self.m = p, 1 << p
        self.reg = [0] * self.m

    def add(self, key):
        x = hash64(key)
        idx = x >> (64 - self.p)                     # top p bits choose a register
        w = (x << self.p) & M64                      # the remaining bits
        rank = min(64 - w.bit_length(), 64 - self.p) + 1     # position of the first 1 bit
        self.reg[idx] = max(self.reg[idx], rank)

    def estimate(self):
        m, zeros = self.m, self.reg.count(0)
        e = (0.7213 / (1 + 1.079 / m)) * m * m / sum(2.0 ** -v for v in self.reg)
        return m * math.log(m / zeros) if e <= 2.5 * m and zeros else e      # small range: linear counting

class RunningMedian:
    def __init__(self):
        self.low, self.high = [], []                 # low: max-heap (stored negated), high: min-heap

    def add(self, x):
        heapq.heappush(self.high, -heapq.heappushpop(self.low, -x))      # route through both heaps
        if len(self.high) > len(self.low):
            heapq.heappush(self.low, -heapq.heappop(self.high))          # keep low the same size or one larger

    def median(self):
        if len(self.low) > len(self.high):
            return -self.low[0]
        return (-self.low[0] + self.high[0]) / 2
--cpp
typedef unsigned long long u64;
u64 mix(u64 x) { x ^= x >> 30; x *= 0xbf58476d1ce4e5b9ULL; x ^= x >> 27; x *= 0x94d049bb133111ebULL; return x ^ (x >> 31); }
u64 hash64(const string& s) { u64 h = 0xcbf29ce484222325ULL; for (unsigned char c : s) { h ^= c; h *= 0x100000001b3ULL; } return mix(h); }

struct CountMin {
    vector<vector<long long>> table; size_t width;
    CountMin(size_t width, size_t depth) : table(depth, vector<long long>(width, 0)), width(width) {}
    size_t col(const string& key, size_t row) const { return mix(hash64(key) + row * 0x9E3779B97F4A7C15ULL) % width; }
    void add(const string& key) { for (size_t r = 0; r < table.size(); r++) table[r][col(key, r)]++; }
    long long estimate(const string& key) const {                       // never below the true count
        long long best = LLONG_MAX;
        for (size_t r = 0; r < table.size(); r++) best = min(best, table[r][col(key, r)]);
        return best;
    }
};

struct HyperLogLog {
    int p, m; vector<int> reg;
    explicit HyperLogLog(int p = 10) : p(p), m(1 << p), reg(1 << p, 0) {}
    void add(const string& key) {
        u64 x = hash64(key);
        int idx = x >> (64 - p);                                          // top p bits choose a register
        u64 w = x << p;                                                   // the remaining bits
        int rank = (w == 0 ? 64 - p : min(__builtin_clzll(w), 64 - p)) + 1;   // position of the first 1 bit
        reg[idx] = max(reg[idx], rank);
    }
    double estimate() const {
        double sum = 0; int zeros = 0;
        for (int v : reg) { sum += pow(2.0, -v); zeros += v == 0; }
        double e = (0.7213 / (1 + 1.079 / m)) * m * m / sum;
        return (e <= 2.5 * m && zeros) ? m * log((double)m / zeros) : e;  // small range: linear counting
    }
};

struct RunningMedian {
    priority_queue<int> low;                                              // max-heap: the smaller half
    priority_queue<int, vector<int>, greater<int>> high;                  // min-heap: the larger half
    void add(int x) {
        low.push(x); high.push(low.top()); low.pop();                     // route through both heaps
        if (high.size() > low.size()) { low.push(high.top()); high.pop(); }   // keep low the same size or one larger
    }
    double median() const { return low.size() > high.size() ? low.top() : (low.top() + high.top()) / 2.0; }
};
--go
import (
	"container/heap"
	"math"
	"math/bits"
)

func mix(x uint64) uint64 {
	x ^= x >> 30
	x *= 0xbf58476d1ce4e5b9
	x ^= x >> 27
	x *= 0x94d049bb133111eb
	return x ^ x>>31
}

func hash64(s string) uint64 {
	h := uint64(0xcbf29ce484222325)
	for i := 0; i < len(s); i++ {
		h ^= uint64(s[i])
		h *= 0x100000001b3
	}
	return mix(h)
}

type CountMin struct{ table [][]int64 }

func newCountMin(width, depth int) *CountMin {
	t := make([][]int64, depth)
	for r := range t {
		t[r] = make([]int64, width)
	}
	return &CountMin{t}
}

func (c *CountMin) col(key string, row int) int {
	return int(mix(hash64(key)+uint64(row)*0x9E3779B97F4A7C15) % uint64(len(c.table[row])))
}
func (c *CountMin) add(key string) {
	for r := range c.table {
		c.table[r][c.col(key, r)]++
	}
}
func (c *CountMin) estimate(key string) int64 { // never below the true count
	best := int64(math.MaxInt64)
	for r := range c.table {
		best = min(best, c.table[r][c.col(key, r)])
	}
	return best
}

type HyperLogLog struct {
	p, m int
	reg  []int
}

func newHLL(p int) *HyperLogLog { return &HyperLogLog{p, 1 << p, make([]int, 1<<p)} }

func (h *HyperLogLog) add(key string) {
	x := hash64(key)
	idx := int(x >> (64 - h.p))                          // top p bits choose a register
	rank := min(bits.LeadingZeros64(x<<h.p), 64-h.p) + 1 // position of the first 1 bit in the rest
	h.reg[idx] = max(h.reg[idx], rank)
}

func (h *HyperLogLog) estimate() float64 {
	sum, zeros, m := 0.0, 0, float64(h.m)
	for _, v := range h.reg {
		sum += math.Pow(2, -float64(v))
		if v == 0 {
			zeros++
		}
	}
	e := 0.7213 / (1 + 1.079/m) * m * m / sum
	if e <= 2.5*m && zeros > 0 { // small range: linear counting
		return m * math.Log(m/float64(zeros))
	}
	return e
}

type minHeap []int

func (h minHeap) Len() int           { return len(h) }
func (h minHeap) Less(i, j int) bool { return h[i] < h[j] }
func (h minHeap) Swap(i, j int)      { h[i], h[j] = h[j], h[i] }
func (h *minHeap) Push(x any)        { *h = append(*h, x.(int)) }
func (h *minHeap) Pop() any          { o := *h; x := o[len(o)-1]; *h = o[:len(o)-1]; return x }

type RunningMedian struct{ low, high minHeap } // low stores negated values, so it acts as a max-heap

func (r *RunningMedian) add(x int) {
	heap.Push(&r.low, -x)
	heap.Push(&r.high, -heap.Pop(&r.low).(int)) // route through both heaps
	if r.high.Len() > r.low.Len() {             // keep low the same size or one larger
		heap.Push(&r.low, -heap.Pop(&r.high).(int))
	}
}

func (r *RunningMedian) median() float64 {
	if r.low.Len() > r.high.Len() {
		return float64(-r.low[0])
	}
	return float64(-r.low[0]+r.high[0]) / 2
}

### abuse
--idea
Spam and abuse detection often starts with a simple question: how many times has this account, address or phrase done something in the last few minutes, and is that too many? Billions of keys and a tight time budget rule out a table of exact counters per key. Small sketches solve this: a few thousand counters can track any number of keys, at the price of slightly inflated counts.
Two sketches do the work here. A count-min sketch counts events per key, and a pair of them covering the current and the previous time window gives a sliding rate, the same weighting trick as a sliding window counter. A Bloom filter holds a block list of known bad keys (addresses, hashes of known spam text) and answers "definitely not blocked" or "probably blocked" in constant time and tiny space. Together they flag what is blocked or going too fast, with fixed memory.
--build
1. Count-min sketch (see Count-min sketch): `depth` rows of `width` counters and one hash per row. `add(key)` increments one counter per row. `estimate(key)` is the minimum over the rows. It never underestimates, so an abuser is never missed, though a busy innocent key may be slightly overcounted.
2. Time windows (see Sliding window): keep two sketches, `cur` for the window that contains now and `prev` for the one before. Windows are fixed intervals of `window` seconds. When time moves into a new window, `prev` becomes `cur` (if exactly one window passed, otherwise it is cleared) and `cur` is cleared.
3. On an event for `key` at time `now`: first rotate if needed, then `cur.add(key)`.
4. Rate estimate: `elapsed = now - windowStart`, `rate = prev.estimate(key) * (1 - elapsed / window) + cur.estimate(key)`. Weighting the previous window by the part of it that still lies inside the sliding window gives a smooth count without a hard reset.
5. Flag the event if `rate > limit`.
6. Block list: a Bloom filter of bad keys. `isBlocked(key)` checks that all `k` hashed bits are set. A key found there is rejected before any counting.
7. Memory stays constant however many keys exist. When the counters are shared by many keys, raise `width` to keep the overcounting small. Real systems combine such counters with machine-learned classifiers and human review.
--think
- Signal: per-key rates over a window with a huge key space, and a limit on what you can store. Login attempts per account, messages per sender, requests per address, repeated content.
- Error direction matters: count-min only overestimates and Bloom only gives false positives, so legitimate users can occasionally be flagged. Use sketches as a cheap first filter and add a precise check, or a captcha, before punishing anyone.
- Fixed windows have a boundary problem (a burst at the end of one window and the start of the next escapes both). The weighted two-window estimate fixes most of it. For exact limits per key see Rate limiting.
- Attackers adapt: they spread events over many keys, rotate addresses, or craft collisions if the hash is known. Use a secret seed for the hash functions, combine signals (address, account, device, content), and keep the thresholds adjustable.
- Mistakes: reusing the same hash for every row (the rows then collide together), not clearing the old window (counts grow forever), and treating a Bloom filter match as proof.
--cost
O(depth + k) per event, memory O(width * depth) for the two sketches plus the Bloom filter, independent of the number of keys.
--java
static long mix(long x) { x ^= x >>> 30; x *= 0xbf58476d1ce4e5b9L; x ^= x >>> 27; x *= 0x94d049bb133111ebL; return x ^ (x >>> 31); }
static long hash64(String s) { long h = 0xcbf29ce484222325L; for (int i = 0; i < s.length(); i++) { h ^= s.charAt(i); h *= 0x100000001b3L; } return mix(h); }

static class CountMin {
    final long[][] t; final int width;
    CountMin(int width, int depth) { this.width = width; t = new long[depth][width]; }
    int col(String k, int r) { return (int) Long.remainderUnsigned(mix(hash64(k) + r * 0x9E3779B97F4A7C15L), width); }
    void add(String k) { for (int r = 0; r < t.length; r++) t[r][col(k, r)]++; }
    long estimate(String k) { long b = Long.MAX_VALUE; for (int r = 0; r < t.length; r++) b = Math.min(b, t[r][col(k, r)]); return b; }
}

static class Bloom {
    final boolean[] bits; final int k = 3;
    Bloom(int m) { bits = new boolean[m]; }
    int spot(String s, int i) { long h = hash64(s); long h1 = h & 0x7fffffffL, h2 = ((h >>> 32) & 0x7fffffffL) | 1; return (int) ((h1 + i * h2) % bits.length); }
    void add(String s) { for (int i = 0; i < k; i++) bits[spot(s, i)] = true; }
    boolean mightHave(String s) { for (int i = 0; i < k; i++) if (!bits[spot(s, i)]) return false; return true; }
}

static class AbuseDetector {
    final double window, limit; final int width, depth;
    CountMin cur, prev; double curStart = 0;
    final Bloom blocked = new Bloom(1 << 14);
    AbuseDetector(double window, double limit, int width, int depth) {
        this.window = window; this.limit = limit; this.width = width; this.depth = depth;
        cur = new CountMin(width, depth); prev = new CountMin(width, depth);
    }
    void block(String key) { blocked.add(key); }

    boolean hit(String key, double now) {                                   // true = flag this event
        if (blocked.mightHave(key)) return true;
        double start = Math.floor(now / window) * window;
        if (start != curStart) { prev = (start == curStart + window) ? cur : new CountMin(width, depth); cur = new CountMin(width, depth); curStart = start; }
        cur.add(key);
        double rate = prev.estimate(key) * (1 - (now - start) / window) + cur.estimate(key);   // weighted sliding count
        return rate > limit;
    }
}
--py
M64 = 0xFFFFFFFFFFFFFFFF

def mix(x):
    x ^= x >> 30; x = (x * 0xbf58476d1ce4e5b9) & M64
    x ^= x >> 27; x = (x * 0x94d049bb133111eb) & M64
    return x ^ (x >> 31)

def hash64(s):
    h = 0xcbf29ce484222325
    for ch in s:
        h = ((h ^ ord(ch)) * 0x100000001b3) & M64
    return mix(h)

class CountMin:
    def __init__(self, width, depth):
        self.width, self.t = width, [[0] * width for _ in range(depth)]

    def col(self, k, r):
        return mix((hash64(k) + r * 0x9E3779B97F4A7C15) & M64) % self.width

    def add(self, k):
        for r, row in enumerate(self.t): row[self.col(k, r)] += 1

    def estimate(self, k):
        return min(row[self.col(k, r)] for r, row in enumerate(self.t))

class Bloom:
    def __init__(self, m=1 << 14, k=3):
        self.m, self.k, self.bits = m, k, bytearray(m)

    def spots(self, s):
        h = hash64(s)
        h1, h2 = h & 0x7fffffff, ((h >> 32) & 0x7fffffff) | 1
        return [(h1 + i * h2) % self.m for i in range(self.k)]

    def add(self, s):
        for p in self.spots(s): self.bits[p] = 1

    def might_have(self, s):
        return all(self.bits[p] for p in self.spots(s))

class AbuseDetector:
    def __init__(self, window, limit, width=1024, depth=4):
        self.window, self.limit, self.width, self.depth = window, limit, width, depth
        self.cur, self.prev, self.cur_start = CountMin(width, depth), CountMin(width, depth), 0.0
        self.blocked = Bloom()

    def block(self, key):
        self.blocked.add(key)

    def hit(self, key, now):                         # True = flag this event
        if self.blocked.might_have(key):
            return True
        start = (now // self.window) * self.window
        if start != self.cur_start:                  # rotate the windows
            self.prev = self.cur if start == self.cur_start + self.window else CountMin(self.width, self.depth)
            self.cur, self.cur_start = CountMin(self.width, self.depth), start
        self.cur.add(key)
        rate = self.prev.estimate(key) * (1 - (now - start) / self.window) + self.cur.estimate(key)   # weighted sliding count
        return rate > self.limit
--cpp
typedef unsigned long long u64;
u64 mix(u64 x) { x ^= x >> 30; x *= 0xbf58476d1ce4e5b9ULL; x ^= x >> 27; x *= 0x94d049bb133111ebULL; return x ^ (x >> 31); }
u64 hash64(const string& s) { u64 h = 0xcbf29ce484222325ULL; for (unsigned char c : s) { h ^= c; h *= 0x100000001b3ULL; } return mix(h); }

struct CountMin {
    vector<vector<long long>> t; size_t width;
    CountMin(size_t width, size_t depth) : t(depth, vector<long long>(width, 0)), width(width) {}
    size_t col(const string& k, size_t r) const { return mix(hash64(k) + r * 0x9E3779B97F4A7C15ULL) % width; }
    void add(const string& k) { for (size_t r = 0; r < t.size(); r++) t[r][col(k, r)]++; }
    long long estimate(const string& k) const { long long b = LLONG_MAX; for (size_t r = 0; r < t.size(); r++) b = min(b, t[r][col(k, r)]); return b; }
};

struct Bloom {
    vector<char> bits = vector<char>(1 << 14, 0);
    size_t spot(const string& s, int i) const { u64 h = hash64(s); u64 h1 = h & 0x7fffffff, h2 = ((h >> 32) & 0x7fffffff) | 1; return (h1 + i * h2) % bits.size(); }
    void add(const string& s) { for (int i = 0; i < 3; i++) bits[spot(s, i)] = 1; }
    bool mightHave(const string& s) const { for (int i = 0; i < 3; i++) if (!bits[spot(s, i)]) return false; return true; }
};

struct AbuseDetector {
    double window, limit, curStart = 0; size_t width, depth;
    CountMin cur, prev; Bloom blocked;
    AbuseDetector(double window, double limit, size_t width = 1024, size_t depth = 4)
        : window(window), limit(limit), width(width), depth(depth), cur(width, depth), prev(width, depth) {}
    void block(const string& key) { blocked.add(key); }

    bool hit(const string& key, double now) {                              // true = flag this event
        if (blocked.mightHave(key)) return true;
        double start = floor(now / window) * window;
        if (start != curStart) {                                           // rotate the windows
            prev = (start == curStart + window) ? cur : CountMin(width, depth);
            cur = CountMin(width, depth); curStart = start;
        }
        cur.add(key);
        double rate = prev.estimate(key) * (1 - (now - start) / window) + cur.estimate(key);   // weighted sliding count
        return rate > limit;
    }
};
--go
import "math"

func mix(x uint64) uint64 {
	x ^= x >> 30
	x *= 0xbf58476d1ce4e5b9
	x ^= x >> 27
	x *= 0x94d049bb133111eb
	return x ^ x>>31
}

func hash64(s string) uint64 {
	h := uint64(0xcbf29ce484222325)
	for i := 0; i < len(s); i++ {
		h ^= uint64(s[i])
		h *= 0x100000001b3
	}
	return mix(h)
}

type CountMin struct{ t [][]int64 }

func newCountMin(width, depth int) *CountMin {
	t := make([][]int64, depth)
	for r := range t {
		t[r] = make([]int64, width)
	}
	return &CountMin{t}
}
func (c *CountMin) col(k string, r int) int {
	return int(mix(hash64(k)+uint64(r)*0x9E3779B97F4A7C15) % uint64(len(c.t[r])))
}
func (c *CountMin) add(k string) {
	for r := range c.t {
		c.t[r][c.col(k, r)]++
	}
}
func (c *CountMin) estimate(k string) int64 {
	b := int64(math.MaxInt64)
	for r := range c.t {
		b = min(b, c.t[r][c.col(k, r)])
	}
	return b
}

type Bloom struct{ bits []bool }

func (b *Bloom) spot(s string, i int) int {
	h := hash64(s)
	h1, h2 := h&0x7fffffff, (h>>32&0x7fffffff)|1
	return int((h1 + uint64(i)*h2) % uint64(len(b.bits)))
}
func (b *Bloom) add(s string) {
	for i := 0; i < 3; i++ {
		b.bits[b.spot(s, i)] = true
	}
}
func (b *Bloom) mightHave(s string) bool {
	for i := 0; i < 3; i++ {
		if !b.bits[b.spot(s, i)] {
			return false
		}
	}
	return true
}

type AbuseDetector struct {
	window, limit float64
	width, depth  int
	cur, prev     *CountMin
	curStart      float64
	blocked       *Bloom
}

func newAbuseDetector(window, limit float64, width, depth int) *AbuseDetector {
	return &AbuseDetector{window: window, limit: limit, width: width, depth: depth,
		cur: newCountMin(width, depth), prev: newCountMin(width, depth), blocked: &Bloom{make([]bool, 1<<14)}}
}

func (d *AbuseDetector) block(key string) { d.blocked.add(key) }

func (d *AbuseDetector) hit(key string, now float64) bool { // true = flag this event
	if d.blocked.mightHave(key) {
		return true
	}
	start := math.Floor(now/d.window) * d.window
	if start != d.curStart {
		if start == d.curStart+d.window { // adjacent window: it becomes the previous one
			d.prev = d.cur
		} else {
			d.prev = newCountMin(d.width, d.depth)
		}
		d.cur, d.curStart = newCountMin(d.width, d.depth), start
	}
	d.cur.add(key)
	rate := float64(d.prev.estimate(key))*(1-(now-start)/d.window) + float64(d.cur.estimate(key)) // weighted sliding count
	return rate > d.limit
}

### compress
--idea
File compression makes data smaller by removing redundancy, with no loss: the original comes back exactly. Zip and gzip use the Deflate method, which combines two ideas. First, LZ77 replaces repeated stretches with pointers ("copy 5 characters from 20 back"). Second, Huffman coding gives frequent symbols short bit codes and rare ones long codes, so the stream of literals and pointers takes fewer bits.
bzip2 takes a different route. The Burrows-Wheeler transform (BWT) rearranges the text so that characters appearing in similar contexts end up next to each other, which makes long runs of equal letters. The rearrangement is fully reversible and does not shrink anything by itself. It prepares the data so that simple run-length and Huffman stages compress it well.
--build
1. LZ77 pass (see LZ77 compression): scan the text and turn it into tokens, either a literal character or a match `(offset, length)` with length at least 3. This model uses a plain search in the last 4096 characters.
2. Turn the tokens into symbols for one alphabet: a literal is its character code `0..255`, and a match is the symbol `256 + length`. Keep the match offsets in a separate list (Deflate also Huffman-codes the offsets).
3. Count how often each symbol occurs. Build the Huffman tree (see Huffman coding): put every symbol in a min-heap by frequency, repeatedly pop the two rarest and push a new node with the summed frequency and the two as children. Reading left as 0 and right as 1 from the root gives each symbol's code. A symbol alone gets the code `0`.
4. Encode: replace each symbol by its code and join the bits. Store the code table (or just the code lengths) and the offsets next to the bits.
5. Decode: read bits until they match a code in the table, which gives a symbol. A literal is appended to the output, a match copies `length` characters from `offset` back, one at a time.
6. BWT (the bzip2 idea): append a unique end marker to the text and list all its rotations in sorted order. The last character of each sorted rotation, read top to bottom, is the transform. To invert it, repeatedly prepend the transform column to a table and sort the rows. After `n` rounds, the row that ends with the marker is the original text.
7. bzip2 then applies move-to-front coding and run-length encoding to the BWT output, and Huffman coding last. See Burrows-Wheeler transform for the fast way to build it with a suffix array.
--think
- Signal: data with repeats (LZ) and uneven symbol frequencies (Huffman). Text, code, logs and structured data both repeat and skew. Random or already compressed data gains nothing, and no method can compress all inputs (a counting argument).
- The pairing makes sense: LZ77 removes long-range repeats and Huffman removes the skew that remains. Modern formats such as Zstandard swap Huffman for faster or stronger entropy coders (finite state entropy, arithmetic coding) and use better match finders.
- Speed versus ratio: a longer search for the best match, a larger window, and bigger blocks all raise the ratio and cost time and memory. gzip levels 1 to 9 move along this scale. BWT-based tools usually compress text better than Deflate and run slower.
- The decoder is always simple. All the intelligence is in the encoder, and the format is fixed by what the decoder can read.
- Mistakes: decoding a match by copying a block at once (overlapping matches need character by character copying), forgetting that Huffman codes must be prefix-free (no code is the start of another, which the tree guarantees), and the single-symbol alphabet case.
--cost
LZ77 with this naive search is O(n * window). Huffman coding is O(n + s log s) for s distinct symbols. The naive BWT here is O(n^2 log n), suffix-array based versions are O(n log n) or better.
--java
static List<int[]> lzTokens(String s) {                                  // {offset, length, char}; length 0 = literal
    List<int[]> out = new ArrayList<>();
    int i = 0, n = s.length();
    while (i < n) {
        int bestLen = 0, bestOff = 0;
        for (int j = Math.max(0, i - 4096); j < i; j++) {
            int l = 0;
            while (i + l < n && s.charAt(j + l) == s.charAt(i + l)) l++;
            if (l > bestLen) { bestLen = l; bestOff = i - j; }
        }
        if (bestLen >= 3) { out.add(new int[]{bestOff, bestLen, 0}); i += bestLen; }
        else { out.add(new int[]{0, 0, s.charAt(i)}); i++; }
    }
    return out;
}

static class HNode { int sym, freq; HNode l, r; HNode(int s, int f) { sym = s; freq = f; } }

static Map<Integer, String> huffmanCodes(Map<Integer, Integer> freq) {
    PriorityQueue<HNode> pq = new PriorityQueue<>((a, b) -> a.freq != b.freq ? a.freq - b.freq : a.sym - b.sym);
    for (Map.Entry<Integer, Integer> e : freq.entrySet()) pq.add(new HNode(e.getKey(), e.getValue()));
    while (pq.size() > 1) {                                              // merge the two rarest
        HNode a = pq.poll(), b = pq.poll(), p = new HNode(Math.min(a.sym, b.sym), a.freq + b.freq);
        p.l = a; p.r = b; pq.add(p);
    }
    Map<Integer, String> codes = new HashMap<>();
    walk(pq.poll(), "", codes);
    return codes;
}

private static void walk(HNode n, String code, Map<Integer, String> codes) {
    if (n.l == null) { codes.put(n.sym, code.isEmpty() ? "0" : code); return; }
    walk(n.l, code + "0", codes); walk(n.r, code + "1", codes);
}

// returns the bit string; offsets receives the match offsets; codes receives the code table
static String deflateLike(String s, List<Integer> offsets, Map<Integer, String> codes) {
    List<Integer> syms = new ArrayList<>();
    Map<Integer, Integer> freq = new HashMap<>();
    for (int[] t : lzTokens(s)) {
        int sym = t[1] == 0 ? t[2] : 256 + t[1];
        if (t[1] > 0) offsets.add(t[0]);
        syms.add(sym); freq.merge(sym, 1, Integer::sum);
    }
    if (syms.isEmpty()) return "";
    codes.putAll(huffmanCodes(freq));
    StringBuilder bits = new StringBuilder();
    for (int sym : syms) bits.append(codes.get(sym));
    return bits.toString();
}

static String inflateLike(String bits, List<Integer> offsets, Map<Integer, String> codes) {
    Map<String, Integer> rev = new HashMap<>();
    for (Map.Entry<Integer, String> e : codes.entrySet()) rev.put(e.getValue(), e.getKey());
    StringBuilder out = new StringBuilder(), cur = new StringBuilder();
    int oi = 0;
    for (char b : bits.toCharArray()) {
        cur.append(b);
        Integer sym = rev.get(cur.toString());
        if (sym == null) continue;                                      // prefix-free: the first match is the symbol
        cur.setLength(0);
        if (sym < 256) out.append((char) (int) sym);
        else { int off = offsets.get(oi++); for (int k = 0; k < sym - 256; k++) out.append(out.charAt(out.length() - off)); }
    }
    return out.toString();
}

static String bwt(String s) {                                           // s must not contain '\0', the end marker
    String t = s + "\0";
    String[] rot = new String[t.length()];
    for (int i = 0; i < t.length(); i++) rot[i] = t.substring(i) + t.substring(0, i);
    Arrays.sort(rot);
    StringBuilder last = new StringBuilder();
    for (String r : rot) last.append(r.charAt(r.length() - 1));
    return last.toString();
}

static String ibwt(String last) {
    int n = last.length();
    String[] table = new String[n];
    Arrays.fill(table, "");
    for (int k = 0; k < n; k++) {
        for (int i = 0; i < n; i++) table[i] = last.charAt(i) + table[i];   // prepend the column
        Arrays.sort(table);
    }
    for (String row : table) if (row.endsWith("\0")) return row.substring(0, n - 1);
    return "";
}
--py
import heapq
from collections import Counter

def lz_tokens(s):                                    # (offset, length, char); length 0 = literal
    out, i = [], 0
    while i < len(s):
        best_len = best_off = 0
        for j in range(max(0, i - 4096), i):
            l = 0
            while i + l < len(s) and s[j + l] == s[i + l]:
                l += 1
            if l > best_len:
                best_len, best_off = l, i - j
        if best_len >= 3:
            out.append((best_off, best_len, "")); i += best_len
        else:
            out.append((0, 0, s[i])); i += 1
    return out

def huffman_codes(freq):                             # symbol -> bit string
    heap = [(f, sym, sym, None, None) for sym, f in freq.items()]   # (freq, tie-break, symbol, left, right)
    heapq.heapify(heap)
    while len(heap) > 1:                             # merge the two rarest
        a, b = heapq.heappop(heap), heapq.heappop(heap)
        heapq.heappush(heap, (a[0] + b[0], min(a[1], b[1]), None, a, b))
    codes = {}

    def walk(node, code):
        if node[3] is None:
            codes[node[2]] = code or "0"
        else:
            walk(node[3], code + "0"); walk(node[4], code + "1")

    walk(heap[0], "")
    return codes

def deflate_like(s):                                 # returns (bit string, match offsets, code table)
    syms, offsets = [], []
    for off, length, ch in lz_tokens(s):
        if length:
            syms.append(256 + length); offsets.append(off)
        else:
            syms.append(ord(ch))
    if not syms:
        return "", [], {}
    codes = huffman_codes(Counter(syms))
    return "".join(codes[x] for x in syms), offsets, codes

def inflate_like(bits, offsets, codes):
    rev, out, cur, oi = {c: s for s, c in codes.items()}, [], "", 0
    for b in bits:
        cur += b
        if cur in rev:                               # prefix-free: the first match is the symbol
            sym, cur = rev[cur], ""
            if sym < 256:
                out.append(chr(sym))
            else:
                off = offsets[oi]; oi += 1
                for _ in range(sym - 256):
                    out.append(out[-off])
    return "".join(out)

def bwt(s):                                          # s must not contain "\0", the end marker
    t = s + "\0"
    return "".join(r[-1] for r in sorted(t[i:] + t[:i] for i in range(len(t))))

def ibwt(last):
    table = [""] * len(last)
    for _ in range(len(last)):
        table = sorted(c + row for c, row in zip(last, table))     # prepend the column, then sort
    return next(r for r in table if r.endswith("\0"))[:-1]
--cpp
vector<array<int, 3>> lzTokens(const string& s) {                       // {offset, length, char}; length 0 = literal
    vector<array<int, 3>> out;
    int i = 0, n = s.size();
    while (i < n) {
        int bestLen = 0, bestOff = 0;
        for (int j = max(0, i - 4096); j < i; j++) {
            int l = 0;
            while (i + l < n && s[j + l] == s[i + l]) l++;
            if (l > bestLen) { bestLen = l; bestOff = i - j; }
        }
        if (bestLen >= 3) { out.push_back({bestOff, bestLen, 0}); i += bestLen; }
        else { out.push_back({0, 0, (unsigned char)s[i]}); i++; }
    }
    return out;
}

struct HNode { int sym, freq; HNode *l = nullptr, *r = nullptr; };

map<int, string> huffmanCodes(const map<int, int>& freq) {
    auto cmp = [](HNode* a, HNode* b) { return a->freq != b->freq ? a->freq > b->freq : a->sym > b->sym; };
    priority_queue<HNode*, vector<HNode*>, decltype(cmp)> pq(cmp);
    for (auto& kv : freq) pq.push(new HNode{kv.first, kv.second});
    while (pq.size() > 1) {                                             // merge the two rarest
        HNode *a = pq.top(); pq.pop(); HNode *b = pq.top(); pq.pop();
        HNode* p = new HNode{min(a->sym, b->sym), a->freq + b->freq};
        p->l = a; p->r = b; pq.push(p);
    }
    map<int, string> codes;
    function<void(HNode*, string)> walk = [&](HNode* n, string code) {
        if (!n->l) { codes[n->sym] = code.empty() ? "0" : code; return; }
        walk(n->l, code + "0"); walk(n->r, code + "1");
    };
    walk(pq.top(), "");                                                 // nodes are leaked for brevity
    return codes;
}

// returns the bit string; offsets receives the match offsets; codes receives the code table
string deflateLike(const string& s, vector<int>& offsets, map<int, string>& codes) {
    vector<int> syms; map<int, int> freq;
    for (auto& t : lzTokens(s)) {
        int sym = t[1] == 0 ? t[2] : 256 + t[1];
        if (t[1] > 0) offsets.push_back(t[0]);
        syms.push_back(sym); freq[sym]++;
    }
    if (syms.empty()) return "";
    codes = huffmanCodes(freq);
    string bits;
    for (int sym : syms) bits += codes[sym];
    return bits;
}

string inflateLike(const string& bits, const vector<int>& offsets, const map<int, string>& codes) {
    map<string, int> rev;
    for (auto& kv : codes) rev[kv.second] = kv.first;
    string out, cur; size_t oi = 0;
    for (char b : bits) {
        cur += b;
        auto it = rev.find(cur);
        if (it == rev.end()) continue;                                  // prefix-free: the first match is the symbol
        int sym = it->second; cur.clear();
        if (sym < 256) out += (char)sym;
        else { int off = offsets[oi++]; for (int k = 0; k < sym - 256; k++) out += out[out.size() - off]; }
    }
    return out;
}

string bwt(const string& s) {                                           // s must not contain '\0', the end marker
    string t = s + string(1, '\0');
    vector<string> rot;
    for (size_t i = 0; i < t.size(); i++) rot.push_back(t.substr(i) + t.substr(0, i));
    sort(rot.begin(), rot.end());
    string last;
    for (auto& r : rot) last += r.back();
    return last;
}

string ibwt(const string& last) {
    size_t n = last.size();
    vector<string> table(n);
    for (size_t k = 0; k < n; k++) {
        for (size_t i = 0; i < n; i++) table[i] = last[i] + table[i];   // prepend the column
        sort(table.begin(), table.end());
    }
    for (auto& row : table) if (row.back() == '\0') return row.substr(0, n - 1);
    return "";
}
--go
import (
	"container/heap"
	"sort"
	"strings"
)

func lzTokens(s string) [][3]int { // {offset, length, char}; length 0 = literal
	var out [][3]int
	for i := 0; i < len(s); {
		bestLen, bestOff := 0, 0
		for j := max(0, i-4096); j < i; j++ {
			l := 0
			for i+l < len(s) && s[j+l] == s[i+l] {
				l++
			}
			if l > bestLen {
				bestLen, bestOff = l, i-j
			}
		}
		if bestLen >= 3 {
			out = append(out, [3]int{bestOff, bestLen, 0})
			i += bestLen
		} else {
			out = append(out, [3]int{0, 0, int(s[i])})
			i++
		}
	}
	return out
}

type hnode struct {
	sym, freq int
	l, r      *hnode
}
type hheap []*hnode // min-heap: rarest first, ties by symbol

func (h hheap) Len() int { return len(h) }
func (h hheap) Less(i, j int) bool {
	return h[i].freq < h[j].freq || h[i].freq == h[j].freq && h[i].sym < h[j].sym
}
func (h hheap) Swap(i, j int) { h[i], h[j] = h[j], h[i] }
func (h *hheap) Push(x any)   { *h = append(*h, x.(*hnode)) }
func (h *hheap) Pop() any     { o := *h; x := o[len(o)-1]; *h = o[:len(o)-1]; return x }

func huffmanCodes(freq map[int]int) map[int]string {
	h := &hheap{}
	for sym, f := range freq {
		heap.Push(h, &hnode{sym: sym, freq: f})
	}
	for h.Len() > 1 { // merge the two rarest
		a, b := heap.Pop(h).(*hnode), heap.Pop(h).(*hnode)
		heap.Push(h, &hnode{sym: min(a.sym, b.sym), freq: a.freq + b.freq, l: a, r: b})
	}
	codes := map[int]string{}
	var walk func(n *hnode, code string)
	walk = func(n *hnode, code string) {
		if n.l == nil {
			if code == "" {
				code = "0"
			}
			codes[n.sym] = code
			return
		}
		walk(n.l, code+"0")
		walk(n.r, code+"1")
	}
	walk(heap.Pop(h).(*hnode), "")
	return codes
}

// returns the bit string, the match offsets and the code table
func deflateLike(s string) (string, []int, map[int]string) {
	var syms, offsets []int
	freq := map[int]int{}
	for _, t := range lzTokens(s) {
		sym := t[2]
		if t[1] > 0 {
			sym = 256 + t[1]
			offsets = append(offsets, t[0])
		}
		syms = append(syms, sym)
		freq[sym]++
	}
	if len(syms) == 0 {
		return "", nil, nil
	}
	codes := huffmanCodes(freq)
	var bits strings.Builder
	for _, sym := range syms {
		bits.WriteString(codes[sym])
	}
	return bits.String(), offsets, codes
}

func inflateLike(bits string, offsets []int, codes map[int]string) string {
	rev := map[string]int{}
	for sym, c := range codes {
		rev[c] = sym
	}
	var out []byte
	cur, oi := "", 0
	for _, b := range bits {
		cur += string(b)
		sym, ok := rev[cur]
		if !ok { // prefix-free: the first match is the symbol
			continue
		}
		cur = ""
		if sym < 256 {
			out = append(out, byte(sym))
		} else {
			off := offsets[oi]
			oi++
			for k := 0; k < sym-256; k++ {
				out = append(out, out[len(out)-off]) // may overlap itself, so copy one byte at a time
			}
		}
	}
	return string(out)
}

func bwt(s string) string { // s must not contain "\x00", the end marker
	t := s + "\x00"
	rot := make([]string, len(t))
	for i := range t {
		rot[i] = t[i:] + t[:i]
	}
	sort.Strings(rot)
	last := make([]byte, len(t))
	for i, r := range rot {
		last[i] = r[len(r)-1]
	}
	return string(last)
}

func ibwt(last string) string {
	n := len(last)
	table := make([]string, n)
	for k := 0; k < n; k++ {
		for i := range table {
			table[i] = string(last[i]) + table[i] // prepend the column
		}
		sort.Strings(table)
	}
	for _, row := range table {
		if strings.HasSuffix(row, "\x00") {
			return row[:n-1]
		}
	}
	return ""
}
### codecs
--idea
Image, audio and video codecs make media small by throwing away what people cannot easily see or hear, and then storing what is left compactly. JPEG is the classic case. It cuts the picture into 8 by 8 blocks. For each block it computes a frequency transform, which rewrites the 64 pixel values as 64 numbers that say how much of each pattern, from smooth to fine detail, the block contains. Natural images are mostly smooth, so most of the fine-detail numbers are tiny.
Then comes the lossy step, quantisation: divide each number by a step size and round to a whole number. Small high-frequency numbers become zero, and the eye barely notices. The result is mostly zeros, which run-length encoding stores as "skip 5 zeros, then the value 3". A Huffman coder then gives short codes to the common pairs. Decoding runs backwards: undo the entropy coding, multiply by the step sizes, and apply the inverse transform. Audio codecs such as MP3 and AAC work the same way on short windows of sound, with a model of what the ear can hear.
--build
1. Cut the signal into blocks of `N = 8` samples (a real image codec uses 8 by 8 pixel blocks; the idea is the same in one dimension, and a 2D transform applies it to the rows and then the columns).
2. Transform: the DCT (discrete cosine transform) gives coefficient `X[k] = c_k * sum over n of x[n] * cos(pi / N * (n + 1/2) * k)` with `c_0 = sqrt(1/N)` and `c_k = sqrt(2/N)` otherwise. `X[0]` is the average level, higher `k` are faster wiggles. The sum written out costs O(N^2) per block. Fast versions compute it with the FFT (see FFT and convolution), in O(N log N).
3. Quantise: choose a step `q[k]` for each coefficient, larger for high frequencies. Store `round(X[k] / q[k])` as an integer. This is the only step that loses information. Bigger steps mean smaller files and lower quality.
4. Run-length encode the integers (see Run-length encoding): write pairs `(zeros before, value)` for the nonzero values and stop after the last one. A block that is mostly smooth becomes two or three pairs.
5. Entropy code the pairs with Huffman coding (see Huffman coding), giving frequent pairs short bit codes. The model leaves out this last step to keep the focus on the transform and quantisation.
6. Decode: expand the pairs back to the integers, multiply by `q[k]`, and apply the inverse DCT, `x[n] = sum over k of c_k * X[k] * cos(pi / N * (n + 1/2) * k)`.
7. Video adds prediction between frames: store only the motion and the small differences from a previous frame, then code those differences as above.
--think
- Signal: media where exact values do not matter to the audience. The two big levers are the transform (compacting the energy into a few numbers) and the perceptual model (deciding what can be dropped).
- Why a transform helps: the DCT is orthonormal, so it keeps the total energy. Smooth blocks put almost all of it in a few low coefficients, and dropping many small ones adds little error. The same signal as raw samples has no zeros to drop.
- Block artefacts: separate blocks quantised on their own show visible seams at high compression. Newer codecs use deblocking filters, overlapping transforms (the MDCT in audio), or wavelets.
- Lossless versus lossy: PNG, FLAC and ZIP use no quantisation, only prediction and entropy coding. Use lossy only for final delivery and keep the original, because every re-encode loses more.
- Mistakes: rounding that differs between encoder and decoder (use one rule, here floor of the value plus one half), forgetting the normalising constants so the inverse is off by a scale, and not clamping decoded pixels to the valid range.
--cost
The direct DCT of a block is O(N^2), or O(N log N) with the FFT form. Quantisation and run-length coding are O(N). The compressed size depends on the content and the step sizes.
--java
static double[] dct(double[] x) {                                         // orthonormal DCT-II
    int n = x.length;
    double[] out = new double[n];
    for (int k = 0; k < n; k++) {
        double s = 0;
        for (int i = 0; i < n; i++) s += x[i] * Math.cos(Math.PI / n * (i + 0.5) * k);
        out[k] = s * Math.sqrt((k == 0 ? 1.0 : 2.0) / n);
    }
    return out;
}

static double[] idct(double[] X) {                                        // the inverse transform
    int n = X.length;
    double[] out = new double[n];
    for (int i = 0; i < n; i++) {
        double s = 0;
        for (int k = 0; k < n; k++) s += Math.sqrt((k == 0 ? 1.0 : 2.0) / n) * X[k] * Math.cos(Math.PI / n * (i + 0.5) * k);
        out[i] = s;
    }
    return out;
}

static List<int[]> encodeBlock(double[] x, int[] q) {                     // pairs {zeros before, value}
    double[] X = dct(x);
    List<int[]> pairs = new ArrayList<>();
    int zeros = 0;
    for (int k = 0; k < X.length; k++) {
        int v = (int) Math.floor(X[k] / q[k] + 0.5);                      // quantise: the only lossy step
        if (v == 0) zeros++; else { pairs.add(new int[]{zeros, v}); zeros = 0; }
    }
    return pairs;                                                          // trailing zeros are implied
}

static double[] decodeBlock(List<int[]> pairs, int[] q) {
    double[] X = new double[q.length];
    int k = 0;
    for (int[] p : pairs) { k += p[0]; X[k] = p[1] * q[k]; k++; }          // expand runs, dequantise
    return idct(X);
}
--py
import math

def dct(x):                                          # orthonormal DCT-II
    n = len(x)
    return [math.sqrt((1 if k == 0 else 2) / n) * sum(x[i] * math.cos(math.pi / n * (i + 0.5) * k) for i in range(n))
            for k in range(n)]

def idct(X):                                         # the inverse transform
    n = len(X)
    return [sum(math.sqrt((1 if k == 0 else 2) / n) * X[k] * math.cos(math.pi / n * (i + 0.5) * k) for k in range(n))
            for i in range(n)]

def encode_block(x, q):                              # pairs (zeros before, value)
    pairs, zeros = [], 0
    for X, step in zip(dct(x), q):
        v = math.floor(X / step + 0.5)               # quantise: the only lossy step
        if v == 0:
            zeros += 1
        else:
            pairs.append((zeros, v)); zeros = 0
    return pairs                                     # trailing zeros are implied

def decode_block(pairs, q):
    X, k = [0.0] * len(q), 0
    for zeros, v in pairs:
        k += zeros
        X[k] = v * q[k]                              # expand runs, dequantise
        k += 1
    return idct(X)
--cpp
vector<double> dct(const vector<double>& x) {                              // orthonormal DCT-II
    int n = x.size();
    vector<double> out(n);
    for (int k = 0; k < n; k++) {
        double s = 0;
        for (int i = 0; i < n; i++) s += x[i] * cos(M_PI / n * (i + 0.5) * k);
        out[k] = s * sqrt((k == 0 ? 1.0 : 2.0) / n);
    }
    return out;
}

vector<double> idct(const vector<double>& X) {                             // the inverse transform
    int n = X.size();
    vector<double> out(n);
    for (int i = 0; i < n; i++) {
        double s = 0;
        for (int k = 0; k < n; k++) s += sqrt((k == 0 ? 1.0 : 2.0) / n) * X[k] * cos(M_PI / n * (i + 0.5) * k);
        out[i] = s;
    }
    return out;
}

vector<pair<int, int>> encodeBlock(const vector<double>& x, const vector<int>& q) {   // pairs {zeros before, value}
    vector<double> X = dct(x);
    vector<pair<int, int>> pairs;
    int zeros = 0;
    for (size_t k = 0; k < X.size(); k++) {
        int v = (int)floor(X[k] / q[k] + 0.5);                             // quantise: the only lossy step
        if (v == 0) zeros++; else { pairs.push_back({zeros, v}); zeros = 0; }
    }
    return pairs;                                                          // trailing zeros are implied
}

vector<double> decodeBlock(const vector<pair<int, int>>& pairs, const vector<int>& q) {
    vector<double> X(q.size(), 0.0);
    size_t k = 0;
    for (auto& p : pairs) { k += p.first; X[k] = p.second * q[k]; k++; }   // expand runs, dequantise
    return idct(X);
}
--go
import "math"

func dct(x []float64) []float64 { // orthonormal DCT-II
	n := len(x)
	out := make([]float64, n)
	for k := range out {
		s := 0.0
		for i, v := range x {
			s += v * math.Cos(math.Pi/float64(n)*(float64(i)+0.5)*float64(k))
		}
		out[k] = s * scale(k, n)
	}
	return out
}

func scale(k, n int) float64 {
	if k == 0 {
		return math.Sqrt(1 / float64(n))
	}
	return math.Sqrt(2 / float64(n))
}

func idct(X []float64) []float64 { // the inverse transform
	n := len(X)
	out := make([]float64, n)
	for i := range out {
		for k, v := range X {
			out[i] += scale(k, n) * v * math.Cos(math.Pi/float64(n)*(float64(i)+0.5)*float64(k))
		}
	}
	return out
}

func encodeBlock(x []float64, q []int) [][2]int { // pairs {zeros before, value}
	var pairs [][2]int
	zeros := 0
	for k, c := range dct(x) {
		v := int(math.Floor(c/float64(q[k]) + 0.5)) // quantise: the only lossy step
		if v == 0 {
			zeros++
		} else {
			pairs = append(pairs, [2]int{zeros, v})
			zeros = 0
		}
	}
	return pairs // trailing zeros are implied
}

func decodeBlock(pairs [][2]int, q []int) []float64 {
	X := make([]float64, len(q))
	k := 0
	for _, p := range pairs { // expand runs, dequantise
		k += p[0]
		X[k] = float64(p[1] * q[k])
		k++
	}
	return idct(X)
}

### genome
--idea
A sequencing machine reads a person's DNA as millions of short pieces (reads) of about 100 to 150 letters. To make sense of them, an aligner finds where in a reference genome of three billion letters each read belongs. Reads contain errors and real differences from the reference, so the match is approximate, and the search must be fast enough to place billions of reads.
Aligners solve this in two stages. First, an index of the genome finds exact matches of a short seed instantly. The index is built from the Burrows-Wheeler transform of the genome together with a suffix array, and is called an FM-index. It counts and locates every occurrence of a string by walking the string backwards, using time proportional to the string's length, not the genome's. Second, around each seed hit the aligner checks the whole read with edit distance, a dynamic programme that counts the fewest substitutions, insertions and deletions. The pigeonhole principle ties the stages together: if a read has at most `k` errors and is cut into `k + 1` pieces, at least one piece has no error and will be found as an exact seed.
--build
1. Genome text: append a unique end marker `$` that sorts before every letter. Let `n` be the length with the marker.
2. Suffix array `sa`: the starting positions of all suffixes sorted alphabetically (see Suffix array). The naive sort here is fine for a model, real tools use fast construction.
3. BWT (see Burrows-Wheeler transform): `bwt[i] = text[sa[i] - 1]`, the character just before each sorted suffix (the last character of each sorted rotation).
4. Two small tables make the BWT searchable. `C[c]` is the number of characters in the text smaller than `c`. `occ[c][i]` is how many times `c` appears in `bwt[0 .. i-1]`.
5. Backward search for a pattern: keep a range `[lo, hi)` of rows, start with all rows `[0, n)`. Process the pattern from its last character to its first. For character `c`: `lo = C[c] + occ[c][lo]` and `hi = C[c] + occ[c][hi]`. If the range becomes empty, the pattern does not occur. Otherwise the range holds exactly the rows of the suffixes that start with the processed part of the pattern.
6. Locate: the start positions of the matches are `sa[lo .. hi-1]`.
7. Approximate alignment: allow `k` errors, cut the read into `k + 1` pieces, and find each piece exactly. For every hit, the read should start `piece offset` characters earlier. Take a window of the genome around that spot (padded by `k` on both sides) and compute the semi-global edit distance: `dp[0][j] = 0` (the read may start anywhere in the window), `dp[i][0] = i`, the usual three-way minimum, and take the best value in the last row. Keep the hits with a distance of at most `k`.
--think
- Signal: finding a short string in a huge fixed text again and again, exactly or with a few errors. Build one index, query it a billion times. The same index also supports counting occurrences and compression (the text can be stored as its BWT in compressed form).
- Why backward search works: sorting suffixes places all suffixes with a common start in one contiguous block. Adding one character at the front maps that block to another contiguous block, and `C` plus `occ` compute exactly where (this is the "LF mapping").
- Memory: a plain suffix array needs 4 bytes or more per base, so tools sample it (store every 32nd entry and walk back using the BWT) and compress the BWT. The human genome index then fits in a few gigabytes.
- Edit distance is quadratic, so it is only run in a small window around candidate hits. Real aligners add banded alignment, scoring that prefers a few long gaps, quality scores, and paired reads.
- Mistakes: forgetting the end marker (the transform is then not invertible and the suffix order is ambiguous), using inclusive instead of exclusive ends for the range, and not padding the window, so a read with an insertion near its start is cut off.
--cost
Building the naive index is O(n^2 log n) time, real constructions are O(n) or O(n log n). Backward search is O(pattern length). Alignment of one candidate is O(read length * window length).
--java
static class FM {
    final String text; final int[] sa, C = new int[128]; final int[][] occ = new int[128][]; final int n;
    FM(String genome) {
        text = genome + "$"; n = text.length();
        Integer[] idx = new Integer[n];
        for (int i = 0; i < n; i++) idx[i] = i;
        Arrays.sort(idx, (a, b) -> text.substring(a).compareTo(text.substring(b)));         // suffix array (naive)
        sa = new int[n];
        for (int i = 0; i < n; i++) sa[i] = idx[i];
        int[] count = new int[128];
        for (int c = 0; c < 128; c++) occ[c] = new int[n + 1];
        for (int i = 0; i < n; i++) {
            char b = text.charAt((sa[i] + n - 1) % n);                                      // BWT character
            for (int c = 0; c < 128; c++) occ[c][i + 1] = occ[c][i] + (c == b ? 1 : 0);
        }
        for (int i = 0; i < n; i++) count[text.charAt(i)]++;
        for (int c = 1; c < 128; c++) C[c] = C[c - 1] + count[c - 1];                       // characters smaller than c
    }

    int[] range(String pat) {                                                               // backward search: rows [lo, hi)
        int lo = 0, hi = n;
        for (int i = pat.length() - 1; i >= 0 && lo < hi; i--) {
            char c = pat.charAt(i);
            lo = C[c] + occ[c][lo]; hi = C[c] + occ[c][hi];
        }
        return new int[]{lo, Math.max(lo, hi)};
    }

    List<Integer> locate(String pat) {
        int[] r = range(pat);
        List<Integer> out = new ArrayList<>();
        for (int i = r[0]; i < r[1]; i++) out.add(sa[i]);
        Collections.sort(out);
        return out;
    }
}

static int semiGlobal(String read, String window) {                                         // read may align anywhere in the window
    int m = read.length(), w = window.length();
    int[] prev = new int[w + 1], cur = new int[w + 1];                                      // row 0: all zeros
    for (int i = 1; i <= m; i++) {
        cur[0] = i;
        for (int j = 1; j <= w; j++)
            cur[j] = Math.min(Math.min(prev[j] + 1, cur[j - 1] + 1), prev[j - 1] + (read.charAt(i - 1) == window.charAt(j - 1) ? 0 : 1));
        int[] t = prev; prev = cur; cur = t;
    }
    int best = Integer.MAX_VALUE;
    for (int v : prev) best = Math.min(best, v);
    return best;
}

static int[] align(FM fm, String read, int k) {                                             // {distance, window start} or null
    int pieces = k + 1, len = read.length() / pieces;
    int[] best = null;
    for (int p = 0; p < pieces; p++) {                                                      // pigeonhole: one piece is exact
        int off = p * len;
        String seed = read.substring(off, p == pieces - 1 ? read.length() : off + len);
        for (int pos : fm.locate(seed)) {
            int start = Math.max(0, pos - off - k), end = Math.min(fm.n - 1, pos - off + read.length() + k);
            int d = semiGlobal(read, fm.text.substring(start, end));
            if (d <= k && (best == null || d < best[0])) best = new int[]{d, start};
        }
    }
    return best;
}
--py
def build_fm(genome):
    text = genome + "$"
    n = len(text)
    sa = sorted(range(n), key=lambda i: text[i:])                      # suffix array (naive)
    bwt = [text[i - 1] for i in sa]                                    # character before each sorted suffix
    chars = sorted(set(text))
    C, total = {}, 0
    for c in chars:                                                    # C[c] = number of characters smaller than c
        C[c] = total; total += text.count(c)
    occ = {c: [0] * (n + 1) for c in chars}                            # occ[c][i] = count of c in bwt[:i]
    for i, b in enumerate(bwt):
        for c in chars:
            occ[c][i + 1] = occ[c][i] + (c == b)
    return {"text": text, "sa": sa, "C": C, "occ": occ, "n": n}

def fm_locate(fm, pat):                                                # start positions of every exact match
    lo, hi = 0, fm["n"]
    for ch in reversed(pat):                                           # backward search
        if ch not in fm["C"]:
            return []
        lo = fm["C"][ch] + fm["occ"][ch][lo]
        hi = fm["C"][ch] + fm["occ"][ch][hi]
        if lo >= hi:
            return []
    return sorted(fm["sa"][lo:hi])

def semi_global(read, window):                                         # read may align anywhere in the window
    prev = [0] * (len(window) + 1)
    for i in range(1, len(read) + 1):
        cur = [i]
        for j in range(1, len(window) + 1):
            cur.append(min(prev[j] + 1, cur[j - 1] + 1, prev[j - 1] + (read[i - 1] != window[j - 1])))
        prev = cur
    return min(prev)

def align(fm, read, k):                                                # (distance, window start) or None
    pieces = k + 1
    size, best = len(read) // pieces, None
    for p in range(pieces):                                            # pigeonhole: one piece is exact
        off = p * size
        seed = read[off:off + size] if p < pieces - 1 else read[off:]
        for pos in fm_locate(fm, seed):
            start, end = max(0, pos - off - k), min(fm["n"] - 1, pos - off + len(read) + k)
            d = semi_global(read, fm["text"][start:end])
            if d <= k and (best is None or d < best[0]):
                best = (d, start)
    return best
--cpp
struct FM {
    string text; int n; vector<int> sa; int C[128]; vector<vector<int>> occ;
    explicit FM(const string& genome) : text(genome + "$"), n(genome.size() + 1), occ(128, vector<int>(genome.size() + 2, 0)) {
        sa.resize(n);
        iota(sa.begin(), sa.end(), 0);
        sort(sa.begin(), sa.end(), [&](int a, int b) { return text.compare(a, string::npos, text, b, string::npos) < 0; });   // suffix array (naive)
        int count[128] = {0};
        for (int i = 0; i < n; i++) {
            char b = text[(sa[i] + n - 1) % n];                                    // BWT character
            for (int c = 0; c < 128; c++) occ[c][i + 1] = occ[c][i] + (c == b);
            count[(int)text[i]]++;
        }
        C[0] = 0;
        for (int c = 1; c < 128; c++) C[c] = C[c - 1] + count[c - 1];             // characters smaller than c
    }

    vector<int> locate(const string& pat) const {                                  // backward search, then read the suffix array
        int lo = 0, hi = n;
        for (int i = (int)pat.size() - 1; i >= 0 && lo < hi; i--) {
            int c = pat[i];
            lo = C[c] + occ[c][lo]; hi = C[c] + occ[c][hi];
        }
        vector<int> out;
        for (int i = lo; i < hi; i++) out.push_back(sa[i]);
        sort(out.begin(), out.end());
        return out;
    }
};

int semiGlobal(const string& read, const string& window) {                        // read may align anywhere in the window
    int m = read.size(), w = window.size();
    vector<int> prev(w + 1, 0), cur(w + 1);
    for (int i = 1; i <= m; i++) {
        cur[0] = i;
        for (int j = 1; j <= w; j++)
            cur[j] = min({prev[j] + 1, cur[j - 1] + 1, prev[j - 1] + (read[i - 1] != window[j - 1])});
        swap(prev, cur);
    }
    return *min_element(prev.begin(), prev.end());
}

pair<int, int> align(const FM& fm, const string& read, int k) {                    // {distance, window start}, or {-1, -1}
    int pieces = k + 1, size = read.size() / pieces;
    pair<int, int> best = {-1, -1};
    for (int p = 0; p < pieces; p++) {                                             // pigeonhole: one piece is exact
        int off = p * size;
        string seed = p < pieces - 1 ? read.substr(off, size) : read.substr(off);
        for (int pos : fm.locate(seed)) {
            int start = max(0, pos - off - k), end = min(fm.n - 1, pos - off + (int)read.size() + k);
            int d = semiGlobal(read, fm.text.substr(start, end - start));
            if (d <= k && (best.first < 0 || d < best.first)) best = {d, start};
        }
    }
    return best;
}
--go
import (
	"slices"
	"sort"
)

type FM struct {
	text string
	sa   []int      // suffix array
	C    [128]int   // C[c] = number of characters smaller than c
	occ  [128][]int // occ[c][i] = copies of c among the first i BWT characters
	n    int
}

func newFM(genome string) *FM {
	f := &FM{text: genome + "$"}
	f.n = len(f.text)
	f.sa = make([]int, f.n)
	for i := range f.sa {
		f.sa[i] = i
	}
	sort.Slice(f.sa, func(a, b int) bool { return f.text[f.sa[a]:] < f.text[f.sa[b]:] }) // suffix array (naive)
	for c := range f.occ {
		f.occ[c] = make([]int, f.n+1)
	}
	var count [128]int
	for i := 0; i < f.n; i++ {
		b := f.text[(f.sa[i]+f.n-1)%f.n] // BWT character
		for c := range f.occ {
			f.occ[c][i+1] = f.occ[c][i]
			if int(b) == c {
				f.occ[c][i+1]++
			}
		}
		count[f.text[i]]++
	}
	for c := 1; c < 128; c++ {
		f.C[c] = f.C[c-1] + count[c-1]
	}
	return f
}

func (f *FM) rng(pat string) (int, int) { // backward search: rows [lo, hi)
	lo, hi := 0, f.n
	for i := len(pat) - 1; i >= 0 && lo < hi; i-- {
		c := pat[i]
		lo, hi = f.C[c]+f.occ[c][lo], f.C[c]+f.occ[c][hi]
	}
	return lo, max(lo, hi)
}

func (f *FM) locate(pat string) []int {
	lo, hi := f.rng(pat)
	out := append([]int(nil), f.sa[lo:hi]...)
	sort.Ints(out)
	return out
}

func semiGlobal(read, window string) int { // read may align anywhere in the window
	prev, cur := make([]int, len(window)+1), make([]int, len(window)+1) // row 0: all zeros
	for i := 1; i <= len(read); i++ {
		cur[0] = i
		for j := 1; j <= len(window); j++ {
			sub := prev[j-1]
			if read[i-1] != window[j-1] {
				sub++
			}
			cur[j] = min(prev[j]+1, cur[j-1]+1, sub)
		}
		prev, cur = cur, prev
	}
	return slices.Min(prev)
}

func align(fm *FM, read string, k int) (dist, start int, ok bool) { // best placement within k edits
	pieces := k + 1
	size := len(read) / pieces
	for p := 0; p < pieces; p++ { // pigeonhole: one piece is exact
		off, end := p*size, p*size+size
		if p == pieces-1 {
			end = len(read)
		}
		for _, pos := range fm.locate(read[off:end]) {
			s, e := max(0, pos-off-k), min(fm.n-1, pos-off+len(read)+k)
			if d := semiGlobal(read, fm.text[s:e]); d <= k && (!ok || d < dist) {
				dist, start, ok = d, s, true
			}
		}
	}
	return
}

### ecc
--idea
Error correcting codes let a receiver repair damaged data without asking for it again, which matters for deep space probes, memory chips, QR codes, discs and mobile networks. A checksum such as a CRC only detects that something went wrong. A correcting code adds just enough extra bits, arranged cleverly, to say where the damage is.
The Hamming(7,4) code is the smallest classic example. It turns 4 data bits into 7 bits by adding 3 parity bits. Each parity bit is the XOR (exclusive or) of a chosen subset of positions. After a transmission, recompute the three parities: together they form the syndrome, a 3-bit number, and for a single flipped bit the syndrome is exactly that bit's position. So the receiver flips the bit at that position and the error is fixed. The arithmetic is done in GF(2), the field with only 0 and 1, where addition is XOR. Linear codes like this one are described by a parity-check matrix `H`, and solving for unknown (erased) bits is Gaussian elimination over GF(2).
--build
1. Number the 7 positions 1 to 7. Positions 1, 2 and 4 (the powers of two) are parity bits. Positions 3, 5, 6 and 7 hold the data bits.
2. Each parity bit `p` at position `2^r` covers every position whose number has bit `r` set. So position 1 covers 1, 3, 5, 7, position 2 covers 2, 3, 6, 7, and position 4 covers 4, 5, 6, 7 (see XOR tricks and Bit manipulation).
3. Encode: place the data bits, then set each parity bit so that the XOR of all the positions it covers is 0.
4. Decode: compute the syndrome as the XOR of the position numbers of all the bits that are 1. If the word is valid, the syndrome is 0. If exactly one bit flipped, the syndrome equals that bit's position, so flip it back. Read the data from positions 3, 5, 6 and 7.
5. This is the parity-check matrix view. Row `r` of `H` is a bitmask with bit `i - 1` set when position `i` has bit `r` set. A valid word satisfies `H * word = 0` over GF(2), that is, the parity of `row AND word` is 0 for every row.
6. Erasures (bits known to be lost, not just wrong): write one equation per row of `H` with the lost bits as unknowns, and the parity of the known bits as the right-hand side. Solve this small system with Gaussian elimination over GF(2): find a row with the unknown, swap it up, and XOR it into the other rows that contain that unknown (see Gauss over GF(2)). If every unknown gets a pivot, the answer is unique. Hamming(7,4) repairs any 2 erasures.
7. Stronger codes (Reed-Solomon, LDPC, turbo codes) follow the same plan with more parity and larger fields.
--think
- Signal: data crossing a noisy channel or sitting in unreliable storage, where retransmission is slow or impossible. The trade-off is overhead: Hamming(7,4) spends 3 extra bits on 4 (75 percent overhead), larger codes spend far less per data bit.
- Detection and correction need different distances. A code with minimum distance `d` (the fewest positions in which two valid words differ) detects `d - 1` errors and corrects `(d - 1) / 2`. Hamming(7,4) has `d = 3`: it corrects one error, or detects two. Adding an overall parity bit gives `d = 4`: correct one, detect two.
- ECC memory uses SECDED codes (single error correct, double error detect) over 64-bit words. RAID 5 is a parity code across disks: the XOR of the other disks restores one lost disk. QR codes and CDs use Reed-Solomon, which treats data as polynomial values and corrects bursts.
- Combine with a CRC: the correcting code repairs what it can, and a CRC on the result confirms the data is right (see Checksums and CRC).
- Mistakes: numbering positions from 0 (the syndrome trick needs positions from 1), assuming two errors can be corrected (the decoder then confidently "fixes" a third bit and returns wrong data), and a Gaussian elimination that forgets to swap rows when the pivot cell is 0.
--cost
Encode and decode of one Hamming word are O(1). Gaussian elimination over GF(2) for `m` equations and `u` unknowns costs O(m * u) row operations, each one XOR of bitmasks.
--java
static int[] hammingEncode(int[] d) {                                     // d: 4 data bits -> 7-bit code, index 0 = position 1
    int[] c = new int[8];                                                 // use positions 1..7
    c[3] = d[0]; c[5] = d[1]; c[6] = d[2]; c[7] = d[3];
    c[1] = c[3] ^ c[5] ^ c[7];                                            // covers positions with bit 0 set
    c[2] = c[3] ^ c[6] ^ c[7];                                            // covers positions with bit 1 set
    c[4] = c[5] ^ c[6] ^ c[7];                                            // covers positions with bit 2 set
    return Arrays.copyOfRange(c, 1, 8);
}

static int[] hammingDecode(int[] code) {                                  // corrects one flipped bit
    int[] c = new int[8];
    System.arraycopy(code, 0, c, 1, 7);
    int syndrome = 0;
    for (int i = 1; i <= 7; i++) if (c[i] == 1) syndrome ^= i;            // XOR of the positions holding a 1
    if (syndrome != 0) c[syndrome] ^= 1;                                  // the syndrome names the wrong position
    return new int[]{c[3], c[5], c[6], c[7]};
}

// H rows are bitmasks over n bits; word has the known bits set, erased positions are listed in `erased`.
// Returns the repaired word, or -1 if the erasures cannot be solved uniquely.
static long recoverErasures(long[] H, long word, int[] erased) {
    long erasedMask = 0;
    for (int e : erased) erasedMask |= 1L << e;
    int u = erased.length;
    long[] rows = new long[H.length];                                     // low u bits: coefficients; bit u: right-hand side
    for (int r = 0; r < H.length; r++) {
        for (int j = 0; j < u; j++) if ((H[r] >> erased[j] & 1) == 1) rows[r] |= 1L << j;
        if (Long.bitCount(H[r] & word & ~erasedMask) % 2 == 1) rows[r] |= 1L << u;
    }
    int rank = 0;
    for (int col = 0; col < u; col++) {                                   // Gaussian elimination over GF(2)
        int piv = -1;
        for (int r = rank; r < rows.length; r++) if ((rows[r] >> col & 1) == 1) { piv = r; break; }
        if (piv < 0) return -1;
        long t = rows[piv]; rows[piv] = rows[rank]; rows[rank] = t;
        for (int r = 0; r < rows.length; r++) if (r != rank && (rows[r] >> col & 1) == 1) rows[r] ^= rows[rank];
        rank++;
    }
    for (int j = 0; j < u; j++) if ((rows[j] >> u & 1) == 1) word |= 1L << erased[j];
    return word;
}
--py
def hamming_encode(d):                               # d: 4 data bits -> 7 bits, index 0 = position 1
    c = [0] * 8                                      # use positions 1..7
    c[3], c[5], c[6], c[7] = d
    c[1] = c[3] ^ c[5] ^ c[7]                        # covers positions with bit 0 set
    c[2] = c[3] ^ c[6] ^ c[7]                        # covers positions with bit 1 set
    c[4] = c[5] ^ c[6] ^ c[7]                        # covers positions with bit 2 set
    return c[1:]

def hamming_decode(code):                            # corrects one flipped bit
    c = [0] + list(code)
    syndrome = 0
    for i in range(1, 8):
        if c[i]:
            syndrome ^= i                            # XOR of the positions holding a 1
    if syndrome:
        c[syndrome] ^= 1                             # the syndrome names the wrong position
    return [c[3], c[5], c[6], c[7]]

def recover_erasures(H, word, erased):
    """H rows are bitmasks over the bits; word has the known bits set. Returns the repaired word or None."""
    u = len(erased)
    mask = sum(1 << e for e in erased)
    rows = []                                        # low u bits: coefficients; bit u: right-hand side
    for h in H:
        row = sum(1 << j for j, e in enumerate(erased) if h >> e & 1)
        if bin(h & word & ~mask).count("1") % 2:
            row |= 1 << u
        rows.append(row)
    rank = 0
    for col in range(u):                             # Gaussian elimination over GF(2)
        piv = next((r for r in range(rank, len(rows)) if rows[r] >> col & 1), None)
        if piv is None:
