Vector search finds the items most similar to a query when every item is a list of numbers, an embedding, produced by a model so that similar things (sentences, images, products) land close together. Comparing the query with every stored vector is exact but too slow for millions of items. Approximate methods trade a small chance of missing the true best match for a very large speed-up.
The inverted file index (IVF) does it with clustering. Use k-means to split the vectors into `nlist` cells, each with a centre. Store each vector in the list of its nearest centre. To answer a query, find the few centres nearest to it, and scan only the vectors in those cells. Because the true nearest neighbours are usually in the same cell as the query or the one next to it, most of the data is never touched. The number of cells scanned (`nprobe`) is the dial between speed and accuracy.
--build
1. Pick `nlist` starting centres, for example evenly spaced vectors from the data.
2. Run a few rounds of k-means (see k-means clustering): assign every vector to its nearest centre by squared distance, then move each centre to the mean of its vectors. Stop after a fixed number of rounds.
3. Build the inverted lists: `lists[c]` holds the ids of the vectors assigned to centre `c`.
4. Search `(query, k, nprobe)`: compute the squared distance to every centre (when there are very many centres, use a k-d tree to find the nearest ones, see k-d tree). Take the `nprobe` closest centres.
5. For every vector in those lists, compute the distance to the query and keep the `k` closest with a max-heap of size `k` (see k-nearest neighbours): push each candidate and drop the farthest when the heap exceeds `k`.
6. Return the heap contents from nearest to farthest.
7. Exact search is the same code with `nprobe = nlist`. Measure quality as recall: the share of the true top `k` that the approximate search returned.
8. Real systems also compress vectors (product quantisation, which stores each vector as a few small codes) to fit billions in memory, or use graph indexes such as HNSW, which walk a network of near neighbours.
--think
- Signal: nearest neighbour lookups over many items in many dimensions (hundreds). Semantic search, recommendations (see Recommendation systems), duplicate detection, retrieval for language models.
- Trees like the k-d tree work well in a few dimensions and degrade in hundreds, because almost all points are about equally far away (the curse of dimensionality). Clustering and graph methods hold up better, and approximate answers are usually good enough for these applications.
- Tuning: more cells make each list shorter but need a larger `nprobe` to keep recall. A common rule is `nlist` near `sqrt(n)`. Raise `nprobe` until recall is acceptable for your latency.
- Boundary problem: a query near the edge of a cell may have neighbours in the next cell, which is exactly why `nprobe > 1` helps.
- Distance choice: for normalised embeddings, cosine similarity and the dot product give the same ranking as Euclidean distance. Be consistent between indexing and querying.
- Mistakes: indexing with one distance and querying with another, a centre with an empty cell (keep its old position), and judging approximate search without measuring recall against the exact answer.
--cost
Build: O(rounds * n * nlist * dim). Search: O(nlist * dim + (n * nprobe / nlist) * dim) for evenly sized cells, instead of O(n * dim) for the exact scan.
--java
static double dist2(double[] a, double[] b) { double s = 0; for (int i = 0; i < a.length; i++) s += (a[i] - b[i]) * (a[i] - b[i]); return s; }

static class IVF {
    double[][] centres; List<List<Integer>> lists = new ArrayList<>();

    IVF(double[][] vecs, int nlist, int rounds) {
        centres = new double[nlist][];
        for (int c = 0; c < nlist; c++) centres[c] = vecs[c * vecs.length / nlist].clone();   // evenly spaced starting centres
        for (int r = 0; r < rounds; r++) {                                 // k-means
            assign(vecs);
            for (int c = 0; c < nlist; c++) {
                if (lists.get(c).isEmpty()) continue;                      // keep the old centre
                double[] m = new double[vecs[0].length];
                for (int id : lists.get(c)) for (int d = 0; d < m.length; d++) m[d] += vecs[id][d] / lists.get(c).size();
                centres[c] = m;
            }
        }
        assign(vecs);
    }

    private void assign(double[][] vecs) {
        lists = new ArrayList<>();
        for (int c = 0; c < centres.length; c++) lists.add(new ArrayList<>());
        for (int i = 0; i < vecs.length; i++) lists.get(nearest(vecs[i])).add(i);
    }

    private int nearest(double[] v) {
        int best = 0;
        for (int c = 1; c < centres.length; c++) if (dist2(v, centres[c]) < dist2(v, centres[best])) best = c;
        return best;
    }

    List<Integer> search(double[][] vecs, double[] q, int k, int nprobe) {
        Integer[] cells = new Integer[centres.length];
        for (int c = 0; c < cells.length; c++) cells[c] = c;
        Arrays.sort(cells, Comparator.comparingDouble(c -> dist2(q, centres[c])));          // nearest centres first
        PriorityQueue<double[]> heap = new PriorityQueue<>((a, b) -> Double.compare(b[0], a[0]));   // farthest on top
        for (int p = 0; p < Math.min(nprobe, cells.length); p++)
            for (int id : lists.get(cells[p])) { heap.add(new double[]{dist2(q, vecs[id]), id}); if (heap.size() > k) heap.poll(); }
        LinkedList<Integer> out = new LinkedList<>();
        while (!heap.isEmpty()) out.addFirst((int) heap.poll()[1]);
        return out;
    }
}
--py
import heapq

def dist2(a, b):
    return sum((x - y) ** 2 for x, y in zip(a, b))

class IVF:
    def __init__(self, vecs, nlist, rounds=10):
        self.centres = [list(vecs[c * len(vecs) // nlist]) for c in range(nlist)]   # evenly spaced starting centres
        for _ in range(rounds):                      # k-means
            self._assign(vecs)
            for c, ids in enumerate(self.lists):
                if ids:                              # keep the old centre if the cell is empty
                    self.centres[c] = [sum(vecs[i][d] for i in ids) / len(ids) for d in range(len(vecs[0]))]
        self._assign(vecs)

    def _assign(self, vecs):                         # inverted lists: centre -> ids of its vectors
        self.lists = [[] for _ in self.centres]
        for i, v in enumerate(vecs):
            self.lists[min(range(len(self.centres)), key=lambda c: dist2(v, self.centres[c]))].append(i)

    def search(self, vecs, q, k, nprobe):
        cells = sorted(range(len(self.centres)), key=lambda c: dist2(q, self.centres[c]))[:nprobe]   # nearest centres first
        heap = []                                    # max-heap of the k best (distances negated)
        for c in cells:
            for i in self.lists[c]:
                heapq.heappush(heap, (-dist2(q, vecs[i]), -i))
                if len(heap) > k:
                    heapq.heappop(heap)              # drop the farthest
        return [-i for _, i in sorted(heap, reverse=True)]
--cpp
double dist2(const vector<double>& a, const vector<double>& b) { double s = 0; for (size_t i = 0; i < a.size(); i++) s += (a[i] - b[i]) * (a[i] - b[i]); return s; }

struct IVF {
    vector<vector<double>> centres;
    vector<vector<int>> lists;                                              // centre -> ids of its vectors

    int nearest(const vector<double>& v) const {
        int best = 0;
        for (size_t c = 1; c < centres.size(); c++) if (dist2(v, centres[c]) < dist2(v, centres[best])) best = c;
        return best;
    }
    void assign(const vector<vector<double>>& vecs) {
        lists.assign(centres.size(), {});
        for (size_t i = 0; i < vecs.size(); i++) lists[nearest(vecs[i])].push_back(i);
    }
    IVF(const vector<vector<double>>& vecs, int nlist, int rounds = 10) {
        for (int c = 0; c < nlist; c++) centres.push_back(vecs[(size_t)c * vecs.size() / nlist]);   // evenly spaced starting centres
        for (int r = 0; r < rounds; r++) {                                  // k-means
            assign(vecs);
            for (int c = 0; c < nlist; c++) {
                if (lists[c].empty()) continue;                             // keep the old centre
                vector<double> m(vecs[0].size(), 0.0);
                for (int id : lists[c]) for (size_t d = 0; d < m.size(); d++) m[d] += vecs[id][d] / lists[c].size();
                centres[c] = m;
            }
        }
        assign(vecs);
    }

    vector<int> search(const vector<vector<double>>& vecs, const vector<double>& q, int k, int nprobe) const {
        vector<int> cells(centres.size());
        iota(cells.begin(), cells.end(), 0);
        sort(cells.begin(), cells.end(), [&](int a, int b) { return dist2(q, centres[a]) < dist2(q, centres[b]); });   // nearest centres first
        priority_queue<pair<double, int>> heap;                             // farthest on top
        for (int p = 0; p < min(nprobe, (int)cells.size()); p++)
            for (int id : lists[cells[p]]) { heap.push({dist2(q, vecs[id]), id}); if ((int)heap.size() > k) heap.pop(); }
        vector<int> out;
        while (!heap.empty()) { out.insert(out.begin(), heap.top().second); heap.pop(); }
        return out;
    }
};
--go
import "sort"

func dist2(a, b []float64) float64 {
	s := 0.0
	for i := range a {
		s += (a[i] - b[i]) * (a[i] - b[i])
	}
	return s
}

type IVF struct { // inverted file index: one list of vector ids per k-means cell
	centres [][]float64
	lists   [][]int
}

func newIVF(vecs [][]float64, nlist, rounds int) *IVF {
	ix := &IVF{centres: make([][]float64, nlist)}
	for c := range ix.centres {
		ix.centres[c] = append([]float64(nil), vecs[c*len(vecs)/nlist]...) // evenly spaced starting centres
	}
	for r := 0; r < rounds; r++ { // k-means
		ix.assign(vecs)
		for c, ids := range ix.lists {
			if len(ids) == 0 { // keep the old centre
				continue
			}
			m := make([]float64, len(vecs[0]))
			for _, id := range ids {
				for d := range m {
					m[d] += vecs[id][d] / float64(len(ids))
				}
			}
			ix.centres[c] = m
		}
	}
	ix.assign(vecs)
	return ix
}

func (ix *IVF) assign(vecs [][]float64) {
	ix.lists = make([][]int, len(ix.centres))
	for i, v := range vecs {
		c := ix.nearest(v)
		ix.lists[c] = append(ix.lists[c], i)
	}
}

func (ix *IVF) nearest(v []float64) int {
	best := 0
	for c := 1; c < len(ix.centres); c++ {
		if dist2(v, ix.centres[c]) < dist2(v, ix.centres[best]) {
			best = c
		}
	}
	return best
}

func (ix *IVF) search(vecs [][]float64, q []float64, k, nprobe int) []int {
	cells := make([]int, len(ix.centres))
	for c := range cells {
		cells[c] = c
	}
	sort.Slice(cells, func(a, b int) bool { return dist2(q, ix.centres[cells[a]]) < dist2(q, ix.centres[cells[b]]) }) // nearest centres first
	var cand []int
	for _, c := range cells[:min(nprobe, len(cells))] { // scan only the probed cells
		cand = append(cand, ix.lists[c]...)
	}
	sort.Slice(cand, func(a, b int) bool { return dist2(q, vecs[cand[a]]) < dist2(q, vecs[cand[b]]) })
	return cand[:min(k, len(cand))] // a size-k heap would avoid sorting every candidate
}
### viterbi
--idea
Speech recognition has to turn a stream of sound measurements into words, though the same sound can come from different words and the sounds are noisy. A hidden Markov model (HMM) describes the situation: there is a hidden sequence of states (the phonemes or words actually spoken) that we cannot see, and at each step the hidden state produces an observation (a sound) that we can see, with some probability. Moving from one hidden state to the next also has probabilities, because some sounds tend to follow others.
Given the observations, the question is: which hidden sequence most probably produced them? Trying every sequence is hopeless, since `n` states over `T` steps gives `n^T` paths. The Viterbi algorithm solves it by dynamic programming over states. The best path ending in state `s` at time `t` must extend the best path ending at some state `r` at time `t - 1`. So it keeps only the best score per state per time, plus a pointer to where each came from, and recovers the whole path by following the pointers backwards.
--build
1. Describe the model: `start[s]` the chance of beginning in state `s`, `trans[r][s]` the chance of moving from `r` to `s`, `emit[s][o]` the chance that state `s` produces observation `o`.
2. Use logarithms: probabilities of long sequences multiply into tiny numbers that underflow, so add logs instead. The log of 0 is minus infinity, meaning "impossible".
3. State table (see State machine DP and Probability DP): `score[t][s]` = the best log probability of any path that explains the first `t + 1` observations and ends in state `s`. At `t = 0`: `score[0][s] = log start[s] + log emit[s][obs[0]]`.
4. Transition: `score[t][s] = max over r of (score[t-1][r] + log trans[r][s]) + log emit[s][obs[t]]`. Remember the best `r` in `back[t][s]`.
5. Finish: the best final state is the one with the highest `score[T-1][s]`. That score is the log probability of the best path.
6. Backtrack: from the best final state, follow `back[t][s]` for `t = T-1` down to 1, collecting states, then reverse the list.
7. Real speech systems use huge state graphs (words, phonemes and their sub-states), score observations with a neural network, and prune: at each step only the states within a margin of the best are kept (beam pruning), since keeping all would be too slow.
--think
- Signal: "most likely hidden sequence" given noisy observations. Speech and handwriting recognition, part-of-speech tagging, decoding errors on a noisy channel (the original use, in satellite links and mobile phones), gene finding, and snapping noisy GPS points to roads (map matching).
- It is the same DP as shortest path on a layered graph: each (time, state) is a node, transitions are edges, and the sum of negative log probabilities is the length. Seeing it that way also explains why a max is used and not a sum. The related forward algorithm uses a sum to compute the total probability of the observations.
- Always compute in logs, or rescale each step. Plain products underflow to 0 after a few hundred steps.
- Learning the model (the probabilities) is a separate problem, solved with the Baum-Welch algorithm or with neural networks. Viterbi only does the decoding.
- Mistakes: taking the log of 0 without handling it, forgetting the emission at step 0, mixing up `trans[r][s]` and `trans[s][r]`, and tie-breaking inconsistently, which can produce different (equally likely) paths.
--cost
O(T * n^2) time for `T` observations and `n` states, O(T * n) memory for the back pointers.
--java
static double lg(double x) { return x == 0 ? Double.NEGATIVE_INFINITY : Math.log(x); }

// returns the best path; logProb[0] receives its log probability
static int[] viterbi(double[] start, double[][] trans, double[][] emit, int[] obs, double[] logProb) {
    int n = start.length, T = obs.length;
    double[][] score = new double[T][n];
    int[][] back = new int[T][n];
    for (int s = 0; s < n; s++) score[0][s] = lg(start[s]) + lg(emit[s][obs[0]]);
    for (int t = 1; t < T; t++) for (int s = 0; s < n; s++) {
        double best = Double.NEGATIVE_INFINITY; int arg = 0;
        for (int r = 0; r < n; r++) {
            double v = score[t - 1][r] + lg(trans[r][s]);                       // best path to r, then step r -> s
            if (v > best) { best = v; arg = r; }
        }
        score[t][s] = best + lg(emit[s][obs[t]]);
        back[t][s] = arg;                                                      // where the best path came from
    }
    int last = 0;
    for (int s = 1; s < n; s++) if (score[T - 1][s] > score[T - 1][last]) last = s;
    logProb[0] = score[T - 1][last];
    int[] path = new int[T];
    for (int t = T - 1; t >= 0; t--) { path[t] = last; if (t > 0) last = back[t][last]; }   // follow the pointers backwards
    return path;
}
--py
import math

def lg(x):
    return -math.inf if x == 0 else math.log(x)

def viterbi(start, trans, emit, obs):                # returns (best path, its log probability)
    n, T = len(start), len(obs)
    score = [[lg(start[s]) + lg(emit[s][obs[0]]) for s in range(n)]]
    back = [[0] * n]
    for t in range(1, T):
        row, brow = [], []
        for s in range(n):
            # best path to r, then the step r -> s
            r = max(range(n), key=lambda r: score[t - 1][r] + lg(trans[r][s]))
            row.append(score[t - 1][r] + lg(trans[r][s]) + lg(emit[s][obs[t]]))
            brow.append(r)                           # where the best path came from
        score.append(row); back.append(brow)
    last = max(range(n), key=lambda s: score[T - 1][s])
    best, path = score[T - 1][last], [last]
    for t in range(T - 1, 0, -1):                    # follow the pointers backwards
        last = back[t][last]
        path.append(last)
    return path[::-1], best
--cpp
double lg(double x) { return x == 0 ? -INFINITY : log(x); }

// returns the best path; logProb receives its log probability
vector<int> viterbi(const vector<double>& start, const vector<vector<double>>& trans, const vector<vector<double>>& emit, const vector<int>& obs, double& logProb) {
    int n = start.size(), T = obs.size();
    vector<vector<double>> score(T, vector<double>(n));
    vector<vector<int>> back(T, vector<int>(n, 0));
    for (int s = 0; s < n; s++) score[0][s] = lg(start[s]) + lg(emit[s][obs[0]]);
    for (int t = 1; t < T; t++) for (int s = 0; s < n; s++) {
        double best = -INFINITY; int arg = 0;
        for (int r = 0; r < n; r++) {
            double v = score[t - 1][r] + lg(trans[r][s]);                       // best path to r, then step r -> s
            if (v > best) { best = v; arg = r; }
        }
        score[t][s] = best + lg(emit[s][obs[t]]);
        back[t][s] = arg;                                                      // where the best path came from
    }
    int last = max_element(score[T - 1].begin(), score[T - 1].end()) - score[T - 1].begin();
    logProb = score[T - 1][last];
    vector<int> path(T);
    for (int t = T - 1; t >= 0; t--) { path[t] = last; if (t > 0) last = back[t][last]; }   // follow the pointers backwards
    return path;
}
--go
import "math"

// returns the best path and its log probability (math.Log(0) is -Inf, which is what we want for impossible steps)
func viterbi(start []float64, trans, emit [][]float64, obs []int) ([]int, float64) {
	n, T := len(start), len(obs)
	score := make([][]float64, T)
	back := make([][]int, T)
	for t := range score {
		score[t], back[t] = make([]float64, n), make([]int, n)
	}
	for s := 0; s < n; s++ {
		score[0][s] = math.Log(start[s]) + math.Log(emit[s][obs[0]])
	}
	for t := 1; t < T; t++ {
		for s := 0; s < n; s++ {
			best, arg := math.Inf(-1), 0
			for r := 0; r < n; r++ {
				if v := score[t-1][r] + math.Log(trans[r][s]); v > best { // best path to r, then step r -> s
					best, arg = v, r
				}
			}
			score[t][s] = best + math.Log(emit[s][obs[t]])
			back[t][s] = arg // where the best path came from
		}
	}
	last := 0
	for s := 1; s < n; s++ {
		if score[T-1][s] > score[T-1][last] {
			last = s
		}
	}
	logProb := score[T-1][last]
	path := make([]int, T)
	for t := T - 1; t >= 0; t-- { // follow the pointers backwards
		path[t] = last
		if t > 0 {
			last = back[t][last]
		}
	}
	return path, logProb
}

### llm
--idea
A large language model generates text one token (a word or piece of a word) at a time. At each step the model, given everything so far, produces a score for every token in its vocabulary, which is turned into a probability distribution over what comes next. The text-generation part is not the network itself. It is the decoding procedure that picks a token from that distribution, appends it, and asks again.
The choice of procedure shapes the output. Always taking the single most likely token (greedy) is repetitive and can miss a better overall sentence, because a locally best word can lead to a poor continuation. Top-k sampling keeps only the `k` most likely tokens and draws at random among them, in proportion to their probabilities, which gives variety without picking absurd words. A temperature divides the scores before the probabilities are computed: low temperature sharpens the distribution (safer), high flattens it (more surprising). Beam search keeps the best `w` partial sentences at each step and extends all of them, so it can find a sequence with a higher total probability than greedy.
--build
1. Model interface: given the last token, `P[prev][next]` is the probability of each next token. This model is a table (a bigram model); in a real LLM it is a huge neural network that sees the whole context. The decoding code is the same either way.
2. Temperature: replace each probability `p` by `p^(1/T)` and renormalise so they sum to 1. `T < 1` sharpens, `T > 1` flattens, and `T` near 0 becomes greedy.
3. Top-k selection: keep only the `k` most probable tokens with a min-heap of size `k` (see Top-k with a heap): push each `(probability, token)` and pop the smallest when the heap grows beyond `k`.
4. Sample: renormalise the survivors, draw a uniform random number in [0, 1), and walk through the survivors adding up probabilities until the running total passes the draw (see Randomization). Return that token.
5. Generate: start from a token, repeatedly sample the next token and append it.
6. Beam search: keep up to `w` candidate sequences with their total log probability (sum of logs, to avoid underflow). For each step, extend every candidate by each possible next token, and keep the `w` best extended candidates with a heap or a sort. After the last step the best candidate is the answer. Width 1 is greedy search. Larger widths search more, at proportionally more cost.
7. Real systems also stop at an end token, add penalties for repetition, cache the work done on earlier tokens, and combine top-k with a probability cut-off (top-p).
--think
- Signal: pick an output from a probability distribution where quality depends on the whole sequence. Translation and speech recognition favour beam search (there is one right answer to find). Creative writing and chat favour sampling (variety is wanted).
- Greedy and beam are deterministic and tend to loops or bland text on open-ended tasks. Sampling with moderate temperature and top-k or top-p reads as more natural.
- Beam search maximises the probability of the whole sequence, but a longer sequence has a lower probability, so real implementations normalise by length or add a length reward.
- Cost is dominated by the model, not by decoding: each new token needs a model call. Caching intermediate results (the key-value cache) makes the cost per token constant instead of growing with the length so far.
- Mistakes: sampling from unnormalised probabilities after cutting to top-k (renormalise), summing probabilities instead of log probabilities in beam search, and applying temperature to probabilities in the wrong direction.
--cost
Top-k selection O(V log k) for a vocabulary of V tokens. Beam search O(steps * w * V) model-table lookups. The model call per step dominates in real systems.
--java
static double[] withTemperature(double[] p, double temp) {                      // p^(1/T), renormalised
    double[] q = new double[p.length];
    double sum = 0;
    for (int i = 0; i < p.length; i++) { q[i] = Math.pow(p[i], 1.0 / temp); sum += q[i]; }
    for (int i = 0; i < q.length; i++) q[i] /= sum;
    return q;
}

static int topKSample(double[] probs, int k, double temp, Random rnd) {
    double[] p = withTemperature(probs, temp);
    PriorityQueue<double[]> heap = new PriorityQueue<>((a, b) -> Double.compare(a[0], b[0]));   // smallest on top
    for (int t = 0; t < p.length; t++) { heap.add(new double[]{p[t], t}); if (heap.size() > k) heap.poll(); }   // keep the best k
    double total = 0;
    for (double[] e : heap) total += e[0];
    double draw = rnd.nextDouble() * total, run = 0;                             // renormalise by scaling the draw
    List<double[]> kept = new ArrayList<>(heap);
    kept.sort((a, b) -> Double.compare(b[0], a[0]));
    for (double[] e : kept) { run += e[0]; if (draw < run) return (int) e[1]; }
    return (int) kept.get(0)[1];
}

// P[prev][next]. Returns the best sequence of `steps` tokens after `start`.
static List<Integer> beamSearch(double[][] P, int start, int width, int steps) {
    List<Object[]> beam = new ArrayList<>();                                     // {log probability, token list}
    beam.add(new Object[]{0.0, new ArrayList<>(List.of(start))});
    for (int s = 0; s < steps; s++) {
        List<Object[]> next = new ArrayList<>();
        for (Object[] b : beam) {
            @SuppressWarnings("unchecked") List<Integer> seq = (List<Integer>) b[1];
            int last = seq.get(seq.size() - 1);
            for (int t = 0; t < P[last].length; t++) {
                if (P[last][t] == 0) continue;
                List<Integer> ext = new ArrayList<>(seq); ext.add(t);
                next.add(new Object[]{(double) b[0] + Math.log(P[last][t]), ext});   // add log probabilities
            }
        }
        next.sort((a, b) -> Double.compare((double) b[0], (double) a[0]));
        beam = new ArrayList<>(next.subList(0, Math.min(width, next.size())));   // keep the best `width`
    }
    @SuppressWarnings("unchecked") List<Integer> best = (List<Integer>) beam.get(0)[1];
    return best.subList(1, best.size());
}
--py
import heapq, math, random

def with_temperature(p, temp):                       # p^(1/T), renormalised
    q = [x ** (1 / temp) for x in p]
    s = sum(q)
    return [x / s for x in q]

def top_k_sample(probs, k, temp=1.0):
    p = with_temperature(probs, temp)
    best = heapq.nlargest(k, ((x, t) for t, x in enumerate(p)))      # the k most probable tokens (a heap inside)
    total = sum(x for x, _ in best)
    draw, run = random.random() * total, 0.0         # renormalise by scaling the draw
    for x, t in best:
        run += x
        if draw < run:
            return t
    return best[0][1]

def beam_search(P, start, width, steps):             # P[prev][next]; best sequence of `steps` tokens after start
    beam = [(0.0, [start])]                          # (log probability, tokens)
    for _ in range(steps):
        cand = [(lp + math.log(P[seq[-1]][t]), seq + [t])            # add log probabilities
                for lp, seq in beam for t in range(len(P[seq[-1]])) if P[seq[-1]][t] > 0]
        beam = heapq.nlargest(width, cand, key=lambda c: c[0])       # keep the best `width`
    return beam[0][1][1:]
--cpp
vector<double> withTemperature(const vector<double>& p, double temp) {          // p^(1/T), renormalised
    vector<double> q(p.size());
    double sum = 0;
    for (size_t i = 0; i < p.size(); i++) { q[i] = pow(p[i], 1.0 / temp); sum += q[i]; }
    for (double& x : q) x /= sum;
    return q;
}

int topKSample(const vector<double>& probs, int k, double temp, mt19937& rng) {
    vector<double> p = withTemperature(probs, temp);
    priority_queue<pair<double, int>, vector<pair<double, int>>, greater<pair<double, int>>> heap;   // smallest on top
    for (size_t t = 0; t < p.size(); t++) { heap.push({p[t], (int)t}); if ((int)heap.size() > k) heap.pop(); }   // keep the best k
    vector<pair<double, int>> kept;
    double total = 0;
    while (!heap.empty()) { kept.push_back(heap.top()); total += heap.top().first; heap.pop(); }
    reverse(kept.begin(), kept.end());               // most probable first
    double draw = uniform_real_distribution<double>(0.0, 1.0)(rng) * total, run = 0;   // renormalise by scaling the draw
    for (auto& e : kept) { run += e.first; if (draw < run) return e.second; }
    return kept[0].second;
}

// P[prev][next]. Returns the best sequence of `steps` tokens after `start`.
vector<int> beamSearch(const vector<vector<double>>& P, int start, int width, int steps) {
    typedef pair<double, vector<int>> Cand;          // {log probability, tokens}
    vector<Cand> beam = {{0.0, {start}}};
    for (int s = 0; s < steps; s++) {
        vector<Cand> next;
        for (auto& b : beam) {
            int last = b.second.back();
            for (size_t t = 0; t < P[last].size(); t++) {
                if (P[last][t] == 0) continue;
                Cand c = {b.first + log(P[last][t]), b.second};      // add log probabilities
                c.second.push_back(t);
                next.push_back(c);
            }
        }
        sort(next.begin(), next.end(), [](const Cand& a, const Cand& b) { return a.first > b.first; });
        if ((int)next.size() > width) next.resize(width);            // keep the best `width`
        beam = next;
    }
    return vector<int>(beam[0].second.begin() + 1, beam[0].second.end());
}
--go
import (
	"math"
	"math/rand"
	"sort"
)

func withTemperature(p []float64, temp float64) []float64 { // p^(1/T), renormalised
	q := make([]float64, len(p))
	sum := 0.0
	for i, v := range p {
		q[i] = math.Pow(v, 1/temp)
		sum += q[i]
	}
	for i := range q {
		q[i] /= sum
	}
	return q
}

func topKSample(probs []float64, k int, temp float64, rnd *rand.Rand) int {
	p := withTemperature(probs, temp)
	ids := make([]int, len(p))
	for i := range ids {
		ids[i] = i
	}
	sort.SliceStable(ids, func(a, b int) bool { return p[ids[a]] > p[ids[b]] }) // best first
	ids = ids[:min(k, len(ids))]                                                // keep the best k
	total := 0.0
	for _, t := range ids {
		total += p[t]
	}
	draw, run := rnd.Float64()*total, 0.0 // renormalise by scaling the draw
	for _, t := range ids {
		if run += p[t]; draw < run {
			return t
		}
	}
	return ids[0]
}

type beamEntry struct {
	logProb float64
	seq     []int
}

// P[prev][next]. Returns the best sequence of steps tokens after start.
func beamSearch(P [][]float64, start, width, steps int) []int {
	beam := []beamEntry{{0, []int{start}}}
	for s := 0; s < steps; s++ {
		var next []beamEntry
		for _, b := range beam {
			last := b.seq[len(b.seq)-1]
			for t, pr := range P[last] {
				if pr == 0 {
					continue
				}
				ext := append(append([]int(nil), b.seq...), t)
				next = append(next, beamEntry{b.logProb + math.Log(pr), ext}) // add log probabilities
			}
		}
		sort.SliceStable(next, func(a, b int) bool { return next[a].logProb > next[b].logProb })
		beam = next[:min(width, len(next))] // keep the best width
	}
	return beam[0].seq[1:]
}

### tokenizer
--idea
A tokeniser cuts text into the pieces a language model reads. Whole words give a vocabulary too large (and unseen words fail), single letters give sequences far too long. Byte-pair encoding (BPE) finds a middle way learned from data: start with single characters, then repeatedly merge the pair of neighbouring symbols that occurs most often into a new symbol. Common words end up as one token, rare words are split into a few familiar pieces, and nothing is ever unknown because single characters remain as a fallback.
Training is a loop of counting and merging. Count every adjacent pair of symbols across the training words (weighted by how often each word occurs), merge the most frequent pair everywhere, and record the merge. After `m` merges the vocabulary is the characters plus `m` new symbols. To tokenise new text, replay the recorded merges in the same order on each word. GPT-style models use a byte-level variant of exactly this procedure.
--build
1. Count words: build a hash table from each word to how often it occurs (see Hash tables and counting). Represent each distinct word as a list of single-character symbols with that count, called the vocabulary `vocab`.
2. Count pairs: for every word in `vocab`, for each adjacent pair of symbols, add the word's count to that pair's total.
3. Choose the best pair: the pair with the highest total. Break ties the same way every time (here, the smaller pair in alphabetical order), so training is repeatable. Real implementations keep the pair counts in a priority queue (see Heaps and priority queues) and update them incrementally, instead of recounting everything each round.
4. Merge: in every word, replace each occurrence of the pair `(a, b)` with one new symbol `a + b`, scanning left to right. Append `(a, b)` to the list `merges`.
5. Repeat steps 2 to 4 for the chosen number of merges, or until no pair occurs twice.
6. Encode a new word: start from its characters. For each merge in the recorded order, scan and merge adjacent `(a, b)` pairs. The order matters, it is what reproduces training. The final symbols are the tokens.
7. Real tokenisers store the final symbols in a vocabulary map from token to id, and a trie or a hash map is used to find the longest known token at a position. They also split text into chunks first (words, numbers, punctuation) so merges never cross those boundaries.
--think
- Signal: turning text into a sequence of units for a model or an index, with a limit on vocabulary size. The vocabulary size is a design trade-off: a bigger vocabulary gives shorter sequences (cheaper to process) but more parameters and rarer tokens that are learned poorly.
- Why subwords: they share meaning (`un`, `happi`, `ness`), handle new and misspelled words and many languages, and avoid an unknown-word token. They are also why models handle some words (rare names, numbers, code) worse: those get split into awkward pieces.
- Variants: WordPiece (used by BERT) chooses merges by a likelihood score rather than raw frequency, and the Unigram model starts large and prunes. Byte-level BPE starts from the 256 byte values so any text, in any script, can be encoded.
- Training is deterministic given the data and tie-break rules, and tokenisation must be identical at training and at use. Changing a tokeniser means retraining the model.
- Mistakes: merging overlapping pairs wrongly (in `aaa` merge `(a,a)` left to right once, giving `aa` and `a`), applying merges in a different order when encoding than in training, and letting merges cross word boundaries.
--cost
Training with recounting is O(merges * total symbols). With incremental pair counts and a heap it is much faster. Encoding one word is O(merges * word length) in this simple form.
--java
// words: the training corpus (repeats count). Returns the merges in order, each as {a, b}.
static List<String[]> trainBpe(List<String> words, int numMerges) {
    Map<List<String>, Integer> vocab = new HashMap<>();
    for (String w : words) {
        List<String> syms = new ArrayList<>();
        for (char c : w.toCharArray()) syms.add(String.valueOf(c));
        vocab.merge(syms, 1, Integer::sum);                                   // word -> count
    }
    List<String[]> merges = new ArrayList<>();
    for (int m = 0; m < numMerges; m++) {
        Map<String, Integer> pairs = new HashMap<>();                         // "a\0b" -> total count
        for (Map.Entry<List<String>, Integer> e : vocab.entrySet())
            for (int i = 0; i + 1 < e.getKey().size(); i++) pairs.merge(e.getKey().get(i) + "\0" + e.getKey().get(i + 1), e.getValue(), Integer::sum);
        String best = null; int bc = 1;
        for (Map.Entry<String, Integer> p : pairs.entrySet())
            if (p.getValue() > bc || (p.getValue() == bc && best != null && p.getKey().compareTo(best) < 0)) { best = p.getKey(); bc = p.getValue(); }
        if (best == null) break;                                              // nothing occurs twice
        String[] ab = best.split("\0");
        merges.add(ab);
        Map<List<String>, Integer> next = new HashMap<>();
        for (Map.Entry<List<String>, Integer> e : vocab.entrySet()) next.merge(mergePair(e.getKey(), ab[0], ab[1]), e.getValue(), Integer::sum);
        vocab = next;
    }
    return merges;
}

static List<String> mergePair(List<String> syms, String a, String b) {      // left to right, no overlap
    List<String> out = new ArrayList<>();
    for (int i = 0; i < syms.size(); i++) {
        if (i + 1 < syms.size() && syms.get(i).equals(a) && syms.get(i + 1).equals(b)) { out.add(a + b); i++; }
        else out.add(syms.get(i));
    }
    return out;
}

static List<String> encodeBpe(String word, List<String[]> merges) {         // replay the merges in training order
    List<String> syms = new ArrayList<>();
    for (char c : word.toCharArray()) syms.add(String.valueOf(c));
    for (String[] ab : merges) syms = mergePair(syms, ab[0], ab[1]);
    return syms;
}
--py
from collections import Counter

def merge_pair(syms, a, b):                          # left to right, no overlap
    out, i = [], 0
    while i < len(syms):
        if i + 1 < len(syms) and syms[i] == a and syms[i + 1] == b:
            out.append(a + b); i += 2
        else:
            out.append(syms[i]); i += 1
    return out

def train_bpe(words, num_merges):                    # words: the training corpus (repeats count)
    vocab = Counter(tuple(w) for w in words)         # word as a tuple of symbols -> count
    merges = []
    for _ in range(num_merges):
        pairs = Counter()
        for syms, n in vocab.items():
            for pair in zip(syms, syms[1:]):
                pairs[pair] += n
        if not pairs:
            break
        top = max(pairs.values())
        if top < 2:
            break                                    # nothing occurs twice
        a, b = min(p for p, c in pairs.items() if c == top)          # repeatable tie-break
        merges.append((a, b))
        new = Counter()
        for syms, n in vocab.items():
            new[tuple(merge_pair(syms, a, b))] += n
        vocab = new
    return merges

def encode_bpe(word, merges):                        # replay the merges in training order
    syms = list(word)
    for a, b in merges:
        syms = merge_pair(syms, a, b)
    return syms
--cpp
typedef vector<string> Syms;

Syms mergePair(const Syms& syms, const string& a, const string& b) {          // left to right, no overlap
    Syms out;
    for (size_t i = 0; i < syms.size(); i++) {
        if (i + 1 < syms.size() && syms[i] == a && syms[i + 1] == b) { out.push_back(a + b); i++; }
        else out.push_back(syms[i]);
    }
    return out;
}

// words: the training corpus (repeats count). Returns the merges in order.
vector<pair<string, string>> trainBpe(const vector<string>& words, int numMerges) {
    map<Syms, int> vocab;
    for (auto& w : words) { Syms s; for (char c : w) s.push_back(string(1, c)); vocab[s]++; }   // word -> count
    vector<pair<string, string>> merges;
    for (int m = 0; m < numMerges; m++) {
        map<pair<string, string>, int> pairs;
        for (auto& e : vocab) for (size_t i = 0; i + 1 < e.first.size(); i++) pairs[{e.first[i], e.first[i + 1]}] += e.second;
        pair<string, string> best; int bc = 1; bool found = false;
        for (auto& p : pairs) if (p.second > bc) { best = p.first; bc = p.second; found = true; }   // map order = repeatable tie-break
        if (!found) break;                                                    // nothing occurs twice
        merges.push_back(best);
        map<Syms, int> next;
        for (auto& e : vocab) next[mergePair(e.first, best.first, best.second)] += e.second;
        vocab = next;
    }
    return merges;
}

Syms encodeBpe(const string& word, const vector<pair<string, string>>& merges) {   // replay the merges in training order
    Syms syms;
    for (char c : word) syms.push_back(string(1, c));
    for (auto& ab : merges) syms = mergePair(syms, ab.first, ab.second);
    return syms;
}
--go
import "strings"

// words: the training corpus (repeats count). Returns the merges in order, each as {a, b}.
func trainBpe(words []string, numMerges int) [][2]string {
	vocab := map[string]int{} // word as symbols joined by spaces -> count
	for _, w := range words {
		syms := []string{}
		for _, c := range w {
			syms = append(syms, string(c))
		}
		vocab[strings.Join(syms, " ")]++
	}
	var merges [][2]string
	for m := 0; m < numMerges; m++ {
		pairs := map[[2]string]int{} // adjacent symbol pair -> total count
		for w, n := range vocab {
			s := strings.Split(w, " ")
			for i := 0; i+1 < len(s); i++ {
				pairs[[2]string{s[i], s[i+1]}] += n
			}
		}
		var best [2]string
		bc := 1
		for p, c := range pairs {
			if c > bc || c == bc && bc > 1 && (p[0] < best[0] || p[0] == best[0] && p[1] < best[1]) {
				best, bc = p, c
			}
		}
		if bc == 1 { // nothing occurs twice
			break
		}
		merges = append(merges, best)
		next := map[string]int{}
		for w, n := range vocab {
			next[strings.Join(mergePair(strings.Split(w, " "), best[0], best[1]), " ")] += n
		}
		vocab = next
	}
	return merges
}

func mergePair(syms []string, a, b string) []string { // left to right, no overlap
	var out []string
	for i := 0; i < len(syms); i++ {
		if i+1 < len(syms) && syms[i] == a && syms[i+1] == b {
			out = append(out, a+b)
			i++
		} else {
			out = append(out, syms[i])
		}
	}
	return out
}

func encodeBpe(word string, merges [][2]string) []string { // replay the merges in training order
	var syms []string
	for _, c := range word {
		syms = append(syms, string(c))
	}
	for _, ab := range merges {
		syms = mergePair(syms, ab[0], ab[1])
	}
	return syms
}

### maps
--idea
A maps app does two jobs. It finds the fastest route between two places on a road network, and it finds the nearest things to a point (the closest road to your GPS position, the nearest petrol station). Both are search problems on structured data, and each has a classic algorithm.
Roads form a weighted graph: intersections are nodes and road segments are edges with a travel cost. Dijkstra's algorithm finds the cheapest route but explores in all directions, so it wastes work on roads leading away from the goal. A* search adds a guess of the remaining distance to the goal, the heuristic, and explores the most promising nodes first. For roads, the straight-line distance never overestimates the real distance, so A* still finds the cheapest route while exploring far fewer nodes. Bidirectional search runs from both ends and stops when the two searches meet, which again explores far less. Snapping a GPS point to the nearest intersection is a nearest neighbour query, answered quickly by a k-d tree.
--build
1. Graph (see Dijkstra for the standard form): `adj[u]` lists `(v, cost)`. Each node has coordinates `(x, y)`. Every edge cost must be at least the straight-line distance between its ends, which is what makes the heuristic safe.
2. A* (see A* search): keep `g[u]`, the cost of the best known path from the start to `u`, and a Heap of `(f, u)` with `f = g[u] + h(u)`, where `h(u)` is the straight-line distance from `u` to the goal.
3. Pop the node with the smallest `f`. If it is the goal, the path is found. If the entry is stale (a better `g` was found later), skip it. For each edge `(u, v, w)`: if `g[u] + w < g[v]`, update `g[v]`, remember `parent[v] = u`, and push `v` with its new `f`.
4. Rebuild the path by following `parent` pointers from the goal back to the start.
5. Bidirectional search (see Bidirectional search): in practice run Dijkstra or A* from the start on the graph and from the goal on the reversed graph, alternating, and stop when the two frontiers touch. Production routers go further and precompute shortcuts (contraction hierarchies) so a cross-country route takes milliseconds.
6. Nearest intersection (see k-d tree): build a tree over the points. At depth `d` split the points at the median of coordinate `d mod 2` (x, then y, then x ...), the median becomes the node, and the two halves are its children.
7. Nearest query: go down toward the query point, then on the way back up check each node. Look into the far side of a split only if the splitting line is closer to the query than the best point found so far (that is what prunes most of the tree).
--think
- Signal: shortest path with a known goal and a sensible estimate of distance, or "nearest point" queries. A* is Dijkstra with a bias. With `h = 0` it is exactly Dijkstra, and a better `h` means fewer nodes explored.
- The heuristic must be admissible, never larger than the true remaining cost, or A* may return a path that is not the cheapest. Real road networks use travel time, so the straight-line distance divided by the maximum speed is used.
- Real systems: map data is stored in tiles, routes use precomputed hierarchies, live traffic changes the edge costs, and results are cached. Turn costs and one-way streets are modelled by splitting nodes or edges.
- k-d trees are efficient in low dimensions like 2D. For huge point sets, spatial databases use R-trees or grids (see Spatial databases and GIS).
- Mistakes: an inadmissible heuristic, stopping A* when the goal is first pushed instead of when it is popped, comparing floating point costs with `==`, and forgetting the pruning test in the k-d tree (which turns the search into a full scan).
--cost
A* worst case is that of Dijkstra, O(E log V), and in practice explores a small part of the graph. A k-d tree builds in O(n log n) and answers a nearest query in O(log n) on average.
--java
// adj.get(u) = list of {v, cost}; cost must be >= the straight-line distance. Returns {cost, path...} or null.
static double[] astar(List<List<double[]>> adj, double[][] xy, int s, int t) {
    int n = adj.size();
    double[] g = new double[n]; int[] parent = new int[n];
    Arrays.fill(g, Double.POSITIVE_INFINITY); Arrays.fill(parent, -1);
    g[s] = 0;
    PriorityQueue<double[]> pq = new PriorityQueue<>((a, b) -> Double.compare(a[0], b[0]));   // {f, node, g at push time}
    pq.add(new double[]{Math.hypot(xy[s][0] - xy[t][0], xy[s][1] - xy[t][1]), s, 0});
    while (!pq.isEmpty()) {
        double[] top = pq.poll();
        int u = (int) top[1];
        if (top[2] > g[u]) continue;                                           // stale entry
        if (u == t) break;                                                     // popped the goal: its cost is final
        for (double[] e : adj.get(u)) {
            int v = (int) e[0];
            if (g[u] + e[1] < g[v]) {
                g[v] = g[u] + e[1]; parent[v] = u;
                double h = Math.hypot(xy[v][0] - xy[t][0], xy[v][1] - xy[t][1]);        // straight-line estimate
                pq.add(new double[]{g[v] + h, v, g[v]});
            }
        }
    }
    if (g[t] == Double.POSITIVE_INFINITY) return null;
    LinkedList<Integer> path = new LinkedList<>();
    for (int v = t; v != -1; v = parent[v]) path.addFirst(v);
    double[] out = new double[path.size() + 1];
    out[0] = g[t];
    int i = 1;
    for (int v : path) out[i++] = v;
    return out;
}

static class KD {
    final double[][] pts; final int[] left, right; int root;
    KD(double[][] pts) { this.pts = pts; left = new int[pts.length]; right = new int[pts.length]; List<Integer> ids = new ArrayList<>(); for (int i = 0; i < pts.length; i++) ids.add(i); root = build(ids, 0); }

    private int build(List<Integer> ids, int depth) {
        if (ids.isEmpty()) return -1;
        final int axis = depth % 2;
        ids.sort(Comparator.comparingDouble(i -> pts[i][axis]));
        int mid = ids.size() / 2, node = ids.get(mid);                          // the median splits the points
        left[node] = build(new ArrayList<>(ids.subList(0, mid)), depth + 1);
        right[node] = build(new ArrayList<>(ids.subList(mid + 1, ids.size())), depth + 1);
        return node;
    }

    int nearest(double[] q) { return search(root, q, 0, -1); }

    private int search(int node, double[] q, int depth, int best) {
        if (node < 0) return best;
        if (best < 0 || d2(pts[node], q) < d2(pts[best], q)) best = node;
        int axis = depth % 2;
        double diff = q[axis] - pts[node][axis];
        int near = diff < 0 ? left[node] : right[node], far = diff < 0 ? right[node] : left[node];
        best = search(near, q, depth + 1, best);
        if (diff * diff < d2(pts[best], q)) best = search(far, q, depth + 1, best);      // the splitting line is closer than the best point
        return best;
    }
    private static double d2(double[] a, double[] b) { return (a[0] - b[0]) * (a[0] - b[0]) + (a[1] - b[1]) * (a[1] - b[1]); }
}
--py
import heapq, math

def astar(adj, xy, s, t):                            # adj[u] = [(v, cost)], cost >= straight-line distance
    g, parent = {s: 0.0}, {s: None}
    h = lambda u: math.dist(xy[u], xy[t])            # straight-line estimate
    pq = [(h(s), s, 0.0)]                            # (f, node, g at push time)
    while pq:
        f, u, gu = heapq.heappop(pq)
        if gu > g[u]:
            continue                                 # stale entry
        if u == t:
            break                                    # popped the goal: its cost is final
        for v, w in adj[u]:
            if gu + w < g.get(v, math.inf):
                g[v], parent[v] = gu + w, u
                heapq.heappush(pq, (g[v] + h(v), v, g[v]))
    if t not in g:
        return None
    path, v = [], t
    while v is not None:
        path.append(v); v = parent[v]
    return g[t], path[::-1]

class KD:
    def __init__(self, pts):
        self.pts = pts
        self.root = self._build(list(range(len(pts))), 0)

    def _build(self, ids, depth):                    # node = (point id, left, right)
        if not ids:
            return None
        ids.sort(key=lambda i: self.pts[i][depth % 2])
        mid = len(ids) // 2                          # the median splits the points
        return (ids[mid], self._build(ids[:mid], depth + 1), self._build(ids[mid + 1:], depth + 1))

    def nearest(self, q):
        best = [None, math.inf]

        def search(node, depth):
            if node is None:
                return
            i, left, right = node
            d = math.dist(self.pts[i], q)
            if d < best[1]:
                best[0], best[1] = i, d
            diff = q[depth % 2] - self.pts[i][depth % 2]
            near, far = (left, right) if diff < 0 else (right, left)
            search(near, depth + 1)
            if abs(diff) < best[1]:                  # the splitting line is closer than the best point
                search(far, depth + 1)

        search(self.root, 0)
        return best[0]
--cpp
// adj[u] = list of {v, cost}; cost must be >= the straight-line distance. Returns the cost and fills path (empty if unreachable).
double astar(const vector<vector<pair<int, double>>>& adj, const vector<pair<double, double>>& xy, int s, int t, vector<int>& path) {
    int n = adj.size();
    vector<double> g(n, 1e300); vector<int> parent(n, -1);
    auto h = [&](int u) { return hypot(xy[u].first - xy[t].first, xy[u].second - xy[t].second); };   // straight-line estimate
    typedef tuple<double, int, double> E;                                    // {f, node, g at push time}
    priority_queue<E, vector<E>, greater<E>> pq;
    g[s] = 0; pq.push({h(s), s, 0.0});
    while (!pq.empty()) {
        auto [f, u, gu] = pq.top(); pq.pop();
        if (gu > g[u]) continue;                                             // stale entry
        if (u == t) break;                                                   // popped the goal: its cost is final
        for (auto [v, w] : adj[u]) if (gu + w < g[v]) { g[v] = gu + w; parent[v] = u; pq.push({g[v] + h(v), v, g[v]}); }
    }
    path.clear();
    if (g[t] >= 1e299) return -1;
    for (int v = t; v != -1; v = parent[v]) path.push_back(v);
    reverse(path.begin(), path.end());
    return g[t];
}

struct KD {
    const vector<array<double, 2>>& pts; vector<int> left, right; int root;
    explicit KD(const vector<array<double, 2>>& pts) : pts(pts), left(pts.size(), -1), right(pts.size(), -1) {
        vector<int> ids(pts.size());
        iota(ids.begin(), ids.end(), 0);
        root = build(ids, 0);
    }
    int build(vector<int> ids, int depth) {
        if (ids.empty()) return -1;
        int axis = depth % 2;
        sort(ids.begin(), ids.end(), [&](int a, int b) { return pts[a][axis] < pts[b][axis]; });
        int mid = ids.size() / 2, node = ids[mid];                           // the median splits the points
        left[node] = build(vector<int>(ids.begin(), ids.begin() + mid), depth + 1);
        right[node] = build(vector<int>(ids.begin() + mid + 1, ids.end()), depth + 1);
        return node;
    }
    static double d2(const array<double, 2>& a, const array<double, 2>& b) { return (a[0] - b[0]) * (a[0] - b[0]) + (a[1] - b[1]) * (a[1] - b[1]); }
    int search(int node, const array<double, 2>& q, int depth, int best) const {
        if (node < 0) return best;
        if (best < 0 || d2(pts[node], q) < d2(pts[best], q)) best = node;
        int axis = depth % 2;
        double diff = q[axis] - pts[node][axis];
        int nearSide = diff < 0 ? left[node] : right[node], farSide = diff < 0 ? right[node] : left[node];
        best = search(nearSide, q, depth + 1, best);
        if (diff * diff < d2(pts[best], q)) best = search(farSide, q, depth + 1, best);   // the splitting line is closer than the best point
        return best;
    }
    int nearest(const array<double, 2>& q) const { return search(root, q, 0, -1); }
};
--go
import (
	"container/heap"
	"math"
	"sort"
)

type edge struct {
	to   int
	cost float64
}
type item struct {
	f, g float64
	node int
}              // f = g + estimate
type pq []item // min-heap on f

func (q pq) Len() int           { return len(q) }
func (q pq) Less(i, j int) bool { return q[i].f < q[j].f }
func (q pq) Swap(i, j int)      { q[i], q[j] = q[j], q[i] }
func (q *pq) Push(x any)        { *q = append(*q, x.(item)) }
func (q *pq) Pop() any          { o := *q; x := o[len(o)-1]; *q = o[:len(o)-1]; return x }

// cost must be >= the straight-line distance. Returns the cost and the path, or ok=false.
func astar(adj [][]edge, xy [][2]float64, s, t int) (cost float64, path []int, ok bool) {
	g, parent := make([]float64, len(adj)), make([]int, len(adj))
	for i := range g {
		g[i], parent[i] = math.Inf(1), -1
	}
	est := func(v int) float64 { return math.Hypot(xy[v][0]-xy[t][0], xy[v][1]-xy[t][1]) } // straight-line estimate
	g[s] = 0
	q := &pq{{est(s), 0, s}}
	for q.Len() > 0 {
		top := heap.Pop(q).(item)
		u := top.node
		if top.g > g[u] { // stale entry
			continue
		}
		if u == t { // popped the goal: its cost is final
			break
		}
		for _, e := range adj[u] {
			if g[u]+e.cost < g[e.to] {
				g[e.to], parent[e.to] = g[u]+e.cost, u
				heap.Push(q, item{g[e.to] + est(e.to), g[e.to], e.to})
			}
		}
	}
	if math.IsInf(g[t], 1) {
		return 0, nil, false
	}
	for v := t; v != -1; v = parent[v] {
		path = append([]int{v}, path...)
	}
	return g[t], path, true
}

type KD struct {
	pts         [][2]float64
	left, right []int
	root        int
}

func newKD(pts [][2]float64) *KD {
	k := &KD{pts: pts, left: make([]int, len(pts)), right: make([]int, len(pts))}
	ids := make([]int, len(pts))
	for i := range ids {
		ids[i] = i
	}
	k.root = k.build(ids, 0)
	return k
}

func (k *KD) build(ids []int, depth int) int {
	if len(ids) == 0 {
		return -1
	}
	axis := depth % 2
	sort.Slice(ids, func(a, b int) bool { return k.pts[ids[a]][axis] < k.pts[ids[b]][axis] })
	mid := len(ids) / 2 // the median splits the points
	node := ids[mid]
	k.left[node] = k.build(append([]int(nil), ids[:mid]...), depth+1)
	k.right[node] = k.build(append([]int(nil), ids[mid+1:]...), depth+1)
	return node
}

func d2(a, b [2]float64) float64 { return (a[0]-b[0])*(a[0]-b[0]) + (a[1]-b[1])*(a[1]-b[1]) }

func (k *KD) nearest(q [2]float64) int { return k.search(k.root, q, 0, -1) }

func (k *KD) search(node int, q [2]float64, depth, best int) int {
	if node < 0 {
		return best
	}
	if best < 0 || d2(k.pts[node], q) < d2(k.pts[best], q) {
		best = node
	}
	axis := depth % 2
	diff := q[axis] - k.pts[node][axis]
	near, far := k.right[node], k.left[node]
	if diff < 0 {
		near, far = far, near
	}
	best = k.search(near, q, depth+1, best)
	if diff*diff < d2(k.pts[best], q) { // the splitting line is closer than the best point
		best = k.search(far, q, depth+1, best)
	}
	return best
}
### gameai
--idea
A game engine's computer opponent chooses its move by looking ahead. It imagines each move it could make, each reply the opponent could make, and so on, building a game tree, then picks the move that leads to the best outcome assuming the opponent also plays well. That is minimax. Looking at every branch is far too slow for real games, so engines use two additions: alpha-beta pruning, which skips branches that cannot change the decision, and sampling, where random games played from a position estimate how good it is.
Tic-tac-toe is small enough to search completely and shows all the parts. The negamax form of minimax uses the fact that a position's value for one player is minus its value for the other, so one function serves both sides. Alpha-beta pruning works much better when promising moves are tried first, so the model tries the centre, then corners, before edges. For games too big to search, such as Go, engines replace the exact value of an unfinished position with the win rate of many random playouts (Monte Carlo methods), and combine this with a search tree (Monte Carlo tree search).
--build
1. Board: 9 cells holding 0 (empty), 1 or 2 (the two players). `winner(board)` checks the 8 lines (3 rows, 3 columns, 2 diagonals) for three equal non-empty cells.
2. Negamax value `value(board, player, alpha, beta)` is the best result for `player` who is to move: `+1` win, `0` draw, `-1` loss. If the previous player has just completed a line, return `-1`. If the board is full, return `0`.
3. Otherwise try every empty cell in a good order (centre, corners, edges). For a move, the value is `-value(newBoard, otherPlayer, -beta, -alpha)`: the opponent's best result negated, with the window flipped (see Minimax).
4. Alpha-beta (see Alpha-beta pruning): keep `best`, raise `alpha = max(alpha, value)`, and stop the loop as soon as `alpha >= beta`. The remaining moves cannot change the answer, because the opponent already has a better option elsewhere.
5. Choosing the move: run the same loop at the top, returning the move with the highest value. Also count the positions visited to see the effect of pruning.
6. Sampling for big games: for each legal move, play `n` random games to the end (each side choosing random legal moves) and count the wins for the player. Pick the move with the best win rate. This is the simplest Monte Carlo method. Monte Carlo tree search improves on it by spending more playouts on moves that look good (see Monte Carlo tree search).
7. Real engines add: iterative deepening (search depth 1, 2, 3 and use earlier results to order moves), a transposition table (a hash table of positions already evaluated), an evaluation function for unfinished positions, and opening books.
--think
- Signal: two-player, turn-based, perfect-information games, or any planning against an adversary. Exact search wins when the tree is small or the evaluation function is good (chess engines). Sampling wins when branching is huge and no good evaluation exists (Go), and when there is chance.
- Move ordering is the main tuning lever of alpha-beta. With best-first ordering the node count drops from `b^d` to about `b^(d/2)`. A full tic-tac-toe search visits about 550,000 positions, with ordering and pruning only a few thousand.
- Use a transposition table: the same position can be reached by many move orders. Hashing the board (Zobrist hashing, using XOR of random numbers per piece and cell) makes the lookup cheap.
- Terminal checks come first: test for a win before generating moves, or the search plays on after the game is over.
- Mistakes: not negating the value when returning from the opponent, not flipping and negating the alpha-beta window, and using strict `>` instead of `>=` for the cutoff, which still works but prunes less.
--cost
Minimax visits O(b^d) positions for branching factor b and depth d. Alpha-beta with good ordering visits about O(b^(d/2)). Flat Monte Carlo costs moves * playouts * game length.
--java
static final int[][] LINES = {{0,1,2},{3,4,5},{6,7,8},{0,3,6},{1,4,7},{2,5,8},{0,4,8},{2,4,6}};
static final int[] ORDER = {4, 0, 2, 6, 8, 1, 3, 5, 7};                 // centre, corners, edges: try likely-best moves first
static long nodes = 0;

static int winner(int[] b) {
    for (int[] l : LINES) if (b[l[0]] != 0 && b[l[0]] == b[l[1]] && b[l[1]] == b[l[2]]) return b[l[0]];
    return 0;
}

static int value(int[] b, int player, int alpha, int beta) {            // negamax with alpha-beta
    nodes++;
    if (winner(b) != 0) return -1;                                       // the previous player completed a line
    int best = -2; boolean moved = false;
    for (int m : ORDER) {
        if (b[m] != 0) continue;
        moved = true;
        b[m] = player;
        int v = -value(b, 3 - player, -beta, -alpha);                    // opponent's best result, negated
        b[m] = 0;
        best = Math.max(best, v);
        alpha = Math.max(alpha, v);
        if (alpha >= beta) break;                                        // cutoff: the opponent has a better option elsewhere
    }
    return moved ? best : 0;                                             // full board: a draw
}

static int bestMove(int[] b, int player) {
    int bestM = -1, bestV = -2;
    for (int m : ORDER) {
        if (b[m] != 0) continue;
        b[m] = player;
        int v = -value(b, 3 - player, -1, 1);
        b[m] = 0;
        if (v > bestV) { bestV = v; bestM = m; }
    }
    return bestM;
}

static int playout(int[] b, int player, Random rnd) {                    // random game to the end: winner, or 0 for a draw
    int[] c = b.clone();
    while (winner(c) == 0) {
        List<Integer> free = new ArrayList<>();
        for (int i = 0; i < 9; i++) if (c[i] == 0) free.add(i);
        if (free.isEmpty()) return 0;
        c[free.get(rnd.nextInt(free.size()))] = player;
        player = 3 - player;
    }
    return winner(c);
}

static int monteCarloMove(int[] b, int player, int n, Random rnd) {     // flat Monte Carlo: best win rate over n playouts
    int bestM = -1; double bestRate = -1;
    for (int m = 0; m < 9; m++) {
        if (b[m] != 0) continue;
        b[m] = player;
        int score = 0;
        for (int i = 0; i < n; i++) { int w = playout(b, 3 - player, rnd); score += w == player ? 2 : w == 0 ? 1 : 0; }
        b[m] = 0;
        if (score > bestRate) { bestRate = score; bestM = m; }
    }
    return bestM;
}
--py
import random

LINES = [(0,1,2),(3,4,5),(6,7,8),(0,3,6),(1,4,7),(2,5,8),(0,4,8),(2,4,6)]
ORDER = [4, 0, 2, 6, 8, 1, 3, 5, 7]                 # centre, corners, edges: try likely-best moves first
nodes = 0

def winner(b):
    for x, y, z in LINES:
        if b[x] and b[x] == b[y] == b[z]:
            return b[x]
    return 0

def value(b, player, alpha=-1, beta=1):             # negamax with alpha-beta
    global nodes
    nodes += 1
    if winner(b):
        return -1                                    # the previous player completed a line
    best, moved = -2, False
    for m in ORDER:
        if b[m]:
            continue
        moved = True
        b[m] = player
        v = -value(b, 3 - player, -beta, -alpha)     # opponent's best result, negated
        b[m] = 0
        best = max(best, v)
        alpha = max(alpha, v)
        if alpha >= beta:
            break                                    # cutoff: the opponent has a better option elsewhere
    return best if moved else 0                      # full board: a draw

def best_move(b, player):
    best_m, best_v = None, -2
    for m in ORDER:
        if b[m]:
            continue
        b[m] = player
        v = -value(b, 3 - player, -1, 1)
        b[m] = 0
        if v > best_v:
            best_m, best_v = m, v
    return best_m

def playout(b, player):                              # random game to the end: winner, or 0 for a draw
    c = list(b)
    while not winner(c):
        free = [i for i in range(9) if not c[i]]
        if not free:
            return 0
        c[random.choice(free)] = player
        player = 3 - player
    return winner(c)

def monte_carlo_move(b, player, n=200):             # flat Monte Carlo: best win rate over n playouts
    best_m, best_score = None, -1
    for m in range(9):
        if b[m]:
            continue
        b[m] = player
        score = sum({player: 2, 0: 1}.get(playout(b, 3 - player), 0) for _ in range(n))
        b[m] = 0
        if score > best_score:
            best_m, best_score = m, score
    return best_m
--cpp
const int LINES[8][3] = {{0,1,2},{3,4,5},{6,7,8},{0,3,6},{1,4,7},{2,5,8},{0,4,8},{2,4,6}};
const int ORDER[9] = {4, 0, 2, 6, 8, 1, 3, 5, 7};                       // centre, corners, edges: try likely-best moves first
long long nodes = 0;

int winner(const array<int, 9>& b) {
    for (auto& l : LINES) if (b[l[0]] && b[l[0]] == b[l[1]] && b[l[1]] == b[l[2]]) return b[l[0]];
    return 0;
}

int value(array<int, 9>& b, int player, int alpha = -1, int beta = 1) { // negamax with alpha-beta
    nodes++;
    if (winner(b)) return -1;                                           // the previous player completed a line
    int best = -2; bool moved = false;
    for (int m : ORDER) {
        if (b[m]) continue;
        moved = true;
        b[m] = player;
        int v = -value(b, 3 - player, -beta, -alpha);                   // opponent's best result, negated
        b[m] = 0;
        best = max(best, v);
        alpha = max(alpha, v);
        if (alpha >= beta) break;                                       // cutoff: the opponent has a better option elsewhere
    }
    return moved ? best : 0;                                            // full board: a draw
}

int bestMove(array<int, 9> b, int player) {
    int bestM = -1, bestV = -2;
    for (int m : ORDER) {
        if (b[m]) continue;
        b[m] = player;
        int v = -value(b, 3 - player, -1, 1);
        b[m] = 0;
        if (v > bestV) { bestV = v; bestM = m; }
    }
    return bestM;
}

int playout(array<int, 9> c, int player, mt19937& rng) {                // random game to the end: winner, or 0 for a draw
    while (!winner(c)) {
        vector<int> freeCells;
        for (int i = 0; i < 9; i++) if (!c[i]) freeCells.push_back(i);
        if (freeCells.empty()) return 0;
        c[freeCells[rng() % freeCells.size()]] = player;
        player = 3 - player;
    }
    return winner(c);
}

int monteCarloMove(array<int, 9> b, int player, int n, mt19937& rng) { // flat Monte Carlo: best win rate over n playouts
    int bestM = -1, bestScore = -1;
    for (int m = 0; m < 9; m++) {
        if (b[m]) continue;
        b[m] = player;
        int score = 0;
        for (int i = 0; i < n; i++) { int w = playout(b, 3 - player, rng); score += w == player ? 2 : w == 0 ? 1 : 0; }
        b[m] = 0;
        if (score > bestScore) { bestScore = score; bestM = m; }
    }
    return bestM;
}
--go
import (
	"math/rand"
	"slices"
)

var LINES = [8][3]int{{0, 1, 2}, {3, 4, 5}, {6, 7, 8}, {0, 3, 6}, {1, 4, 7}, {2, 5, 8}, {0, 4, 8}, {2, 4, 6}}
var ORDER = []int{4, 0, 2, 6, 8, 1, 3, 5, 7} // centre, corners, edges: try likely-best moves first
var nodes int

func winner(b []int) int {
	for _, l := range LINES {
		if b[l[0]] != 0 && b[l[0]] == b[l[1]] && b[l[1]] == b[l[2]] {
			return b[l[0]]
		}
	}
	return 0
}

func value(b []int, player, alpha, beta int) int { // negamax with alpha-beta
	nodes++
	if winner(b) != 0 { // the previous player completed a line
		return -1
	}
	best, moved := -2, false
	for _, m := range ORDER {
		if b[m] != 0 {
			continue
		}
		moved = true
		b[m] = player
		v := -value(b, 3-player, -beta, -alpha) // opponent's best result, negated
		b[m] = 0
		best = max(best, v)
		alpha = max(alpha, v)
		if alpha >= beta { // cutoff: the opponent has a better option elsewhere
			break
		}
	}
	if !moved { // full board: a draw
		return 0
	}
	return best
}

func bestMove(b []int, player int) int {
	bestM, bestV := -1, -2
	for _, m := range ORDER {
		if b[m] != 0 {
			continue
		}
		b[m] = player
		v := -value(b, 3-player, -1, 1)
		b[m] = 0
		if v > bestV {
			bestV, bestM = v, m
		}
	}
	return bestM
}

func playout(b []int, player int, rnd *rand.Rand) int { // random game to the end: winner, or 0 for a draw
	c := slices.Clone(b)
	for winner(c) == 0 {
		var free []int
		for i, v := range c {
			if v == 0 {
				free = append(free, i)
			}
		}
		if len(free) == 0 {
			return 0
		}
		c[free[rnd.Intn(len(free))]] = player
		player = 3 - player
	}
	return winner(c)
}

func monteCarloMove(b []int, player, n int, rnd *rand.Rand) int { // flat Monte Carlo: best win rate over n playouts
	bestM, bestScore := -1, -1
	for m := 0; m < 9; m++ {
		if b[m] != 0 {
			continue
		}
		b[m] = player
		score := 0
		for i := 0; i < n; i++ {
			switch w := playout(b, 3-player, rnd); w {
			case player:
				score += 2
			case 0:
				score++
			}
		}
		b[m] = 0
		if score > bestScore {
			bestScore, bestM = score, m
		}
	}
	return bestM
}

### robotics
--idea
A robot, a delivery drone or a game character has to get from here to there without hitting things. Planning on a grid is the simplest version: the map is cut into cells, each either free or blocked, and a path is a chain of free neighbouring cells. Three algorithms combine to do it well.
First, flood fill checks cheaply whether the goal can be reached at all, before spending effort on a search. Second, A* finds the shortest path, guided by a heuristic that pulls the search toward the goal. Third, safety: a shortest path often grazes walls, which is risky for a real robot with sensor noise. A clearance map, the distance of every free cell to the nearest obstacle, computed by a breadth-first search that starts from all obstacles at once, lets the planner charge extra for cells near walls. The ridge of cells equidistant from two obstacles is the Voronoi diagram of the obstacles, the safest route through the space. Planning along it, or just penalising low clearance, gives paths that keep their distance.
--build
1. Grid: `grid[r][c]` is 0 for free, 1 for blocked. Moves go to the 4 neighbours (up, down, left, right).
2. Reachability with flood fill (see Flood fill): from the start, visit free neighbours with a queue and mark them. If the goal is not marked, no path exists. Stop there.
3. Clearance by multi-source BFS: put every obstacle cell in the queue with distance 0, then expand the BFS outward, giving each free cell the number of steps to the nearest obstacle. This is one BFS for the whole map, not one per cell (see Multi-source BFS and Voronoi diagrams: each cell gets the label of its nearest obstacle, and the borders between labels are the Voronoi edges).
4. Cost of a step into cell `v`: `1 + weight / clear[v]`. With `weight = 0` all steps cost 1 and the planner gives the shortest path. A larger weight makes the planner prefer open space, at the price of longer paths.
5. A* (see A* search): a Heap ordered by `f = g + h`, where `g` is the cost so far and `h` is the Manhattan distance (rows plus columns) to the goal. Every step costs at least 1, so `h` never overestimates and A* stays optimal.
6. Pop the cell with the smallest `f`, skip stale entries, stop at the goal, and for each free neighbour update `g` and the parent if the new cost is lower.
7. Follow the parents back from the goal to get the path. Real planners also smooth the path, use 8 directions or continuous space (sampling methods like RRT), and replan as sensors reveal new obstacles (D* Lite).
--think
- Signal: shortest path on a grid or map with obstacles, where you know where the goal is. A* is the default. If there are many goals or you need distances to everything, run BFS or Dijkstra without a heuristic.
- The heuristic must not overestimate. Manhattan distance is right for 4-direction moves with step cost at least 1, and straight-line distance for 8 directions or continuous space. A weak heuristic only slows the search, an overestimating one can break optimality.
- Safety versus length is a policy choice. A robot has size, so a practical approach is to inflate obstacles by the robot's radius (cells with clearance below the radius become blocked), and then penalise closeness on top of that.
- The clearance map costs one BFS and can be reused for every query, as long as the map does not change.
- Mistakes: dividing by a clearance of 0 (only free cells are planned, and a free cell has clearance 1 or more), using a heuristic in different units from the step cost, and not checking reachability, so that A* searches the entire map before failing.
--cost
Flood fill and clearance O(cells). A* O(cells log cells) worst case and usually much less.
--java
static int[][] clearance(int[][] grid) {                                  // multi-source BFS from every obstacle
    int R = grid.length, C = grid[0].length;
    int[][] d = new int[R][C];
    for (int[] row : d) Arrays.fill(row, -1);
    Deque<int[]> q = new ArrayDeque<>();
    for (int r = 0; r < R; r++) for (int c = 0; c < C; c++) if (grid[r][c] == 1) { d[r][c] = 0; q.add(new int[]{r, c}); }
    int[] dr = {1, -1, 0, 0}, dc = {0, 0, 1, -1};
    while (!q.isEmpty()) {
        int[] u = q.poll();
        for (int k = 0; k < 4; k++) {
            int r = u[0] + dr[k], c = u[1] + dc[k];
            if (r >= 0 && r < R && c >= 0 && c < C && d[r][c] < 0) { d[r][c] = d[u[0]][u[1]] + 1; q.add(new int[]{r, c}); }
        }
    }
    return d;                                                              // -1 everywhere if there are no obstacles
}

static boolean reachable(int[][] grid, int[] s, int[] t) {                // flood fill
    int R = grid.length, C = grid[0].length;
    boolean[][] seen = new boolean[R][C];
    Deque<int[]> q = new ArrayDeque<>();
    q.add(s); seen[s[0]][s[1]] = true;
    int[] dr = {1, -1, 0, 0}, dc = {0, 0, 1, -1};
    while (!q.isEmpty()) {
        int[] u = q.poll();
        for (int k = 0; k < 4; k++) {
            int r = u[0] + dr[k], c = u[1] + dc[k];
            if (r >= 0 && r < R && c >= 0 && c < C && grid[r][c] == 0 && !seen[r][c]) { seen[r][c] = true; q.add(new int[]{r, c}); }
        }
    }
    return seen[t[0]][t[1]];
}

// returns {cost, path as r,c pairs...} or null
static double[] plan(int[][] grid, int[] s, int[] t, double weight) {
    if (grid[s[0]][s[1]] == 1 || grid[t[0]][t[1]] == 1 || !reachable(grid, s, t)) return null;
    int R = grid.length, C = grid[0].length;
    int[][] clear = clearance(grid);
    double[][] g = new double[R][C];
    for (double[] row : g) Arrays.fill(row, Double.POSITIVE_INFINITY);
    int[][] parent = new int[R][C];
    for (int[] row : parent) Arrays.fill(row, -1);
    g[s[0]][s[1]] = 0;
    PriorityQueue<double[]> pq = new PriorityQueue<>((a, b) -> Double.compare(a[0], b[0]));    // {f, r, c, g}
    pq.add(new double[]{Math.abs(s[0] - t[0]) + Math.abs(s[1] - t[1]), s[0], s[1], 0});
    int[] dr = {1, -1, 0, 0}, dc = {0, 0, 1, -1};
    while (!pq.isEmpty()) {
        double[] e = pq.poll();
        int r = (int) e[1], c = (int) e[2];
        if (e[3] > g[r][c]) continue;                                      // stale entry
        if (r == t[0] && c == t[1]) break;
        for (int k = 0; k < 4; k++) {
            int nr = r + dr[k], nc = c + dc[k];
            if (nr < 0 || nr >= R || nc < 0 || nc >= C || grid[nr][nc] == 1) continue;
            double ng = g[r][c] + 1 + (clear[nr][nc] > 0 ? weight / clear[nr][nc] : 0);   // penalise cells near obstacles
            if (ng < g[nr][nc]) {
                g[nr][nc] = ng; parent[nr][nc] = r * C + c;
                pq.add(new double[]{ng + Math.abs(nr - t[0]) + Math.abs(nc - t[1]), nr, nc, ng});
            }
        }
    }
    LinkedList<Integer> cells = new LinkedList<>();
    for (int v = t[0] * C + t[1]; v != -1; v = parent[v / C][v % C]) cells.addFirst(v);
    double[] out = new double[1 + 2 * cells.size()];
    out[0] = g[t[0]][t[1]];
    int i = 1;
    for (int v : cells) { out[i++] = v / C; out[i++] = v % C; }
    return out;
}
--py
import heapq
from collections import deque

DIRS = ((1, 0), (-1, 0), (0, 1), (0, -1))

def clearance(grid):                                 # multi-source BFS from every obstacle
    R, C = len(grid), len(grid[0])
    d = [[-1] * C for _ in range(R)]
    q = deque()
    for r in range(R):
        for c in range(C):
            if grid[r][c] == 1:
                d[r][c] = 0
                q.append((r, c))
    while q:
        r, c = q.popleft()
        for dr, dc in DIRS:
            nr, nc = r + dr, c + dc
            if 0 <= nr < R and 0 <= nc < C and d[nr][nc] < 0:
                d[nr][nc] = d[r][c] + 1
                q.append((nr, nc))
    return d                                         # -1 everywhere if there are no obstacles

def reachable(grid, s, t):                           # flood fill
    R, C = len(grid), len(grid[0])
    seen, q = {s}, deque([s])
    while q:
        r, c = q.popleft()
        for dr, dc in DIRS:
            nr, nc = r + dr, c + dc
            if 0 <= nr < R and 0 <= nc < C and grid[nr][nc] == 0 and (nr, nc) not in seen:
                seen.add((nr, nc)); q.append((nr, nc))
    return t in seen

def plan(grid, s, t, weight=0.0):                    # returns (cost, path) or None
    if grid[s[0]][s[1]] or grid[t[0]][t[1]] or not reachable(grid, s, t):
        return None
    R, C = len(grid), len(grid[0])
    clear = clearance(grid)
    h = lambda r, c: abs(r - t[0]) + abs(c - t[1])   # Manhattan distance never overestimates
    g, parent = {s: 0.0}, {s: None}
    pq = [(h(*s), s, 0.0)]
    while pq:
        f, (r, c), gu = heapq.heappop(pq)
        if gu > g[(r, c)]:
            continue                                 # stale entry
        if (r, c) == t:
            break
        for dr, dc in DIRS:
            nr, nc = r + dr, c + dc
            if 0 <= nr < R and 0 <= nc < C and not grid[nr][nc]:
                ng = gu + 1 + (weight / clear[nr][nc] if clear[nr][nc] > 0 else 0)   # penalise cells near obstacles
                if ng < g.get((nr, nc), float("inf")):
                    g[(nr, nc)], parent[(nr, nc)] = ng, (r, c)
                    heapq.heappush(pq, (ng + h(nr, nc), (nr, nc), ng))
    path, v = [], t
    while v is not None:
        path.append(v); v = parent[v]
    return g[t], path[::-1]
--cpp
typedef vector<vector<int>> Grid;
const int DR[4] = {1, -1, 0, 0}, DC[4] = {0, 0, 1, -1};

vector<vector<int>> clearance(const Grid& grid) {                      // multi-source BFS from every obstacle
    int R = grid.size(), C = grid[0].size();
    vector<vector<int>> d(R, vector<int>(C, -1));
    queue<pair<int, int>> q;
    for (int r = 0; r < R; r++) for (int c = 0; c < C; c++) if (grid[r][c] == 1) { d[r][c] = 0; q.push({r, c}); }
    while (!q.empty()) {
        auto [r, c] = q.front(); q.pop();
        for (int k = 0; k < 4; k++) {
            int nr = r + DR[k], nc = c + DC[k];
            if (nr >= 0 && nr < R && nc >= 0 && nc < C && d[nr][nc] < 0) { d[nr][nc] = d[r][c] + 1; q.push({nr, nc}); }
        }
    }
    return d;                                                          // -1 everywhere if there are no obstacles
}

bool reachable(const Grid& grid, pair<int, int> s, pair<int, int> t) { // flood fill
    int R = grid.size(), C = grid[0].size();
    vector<vector<char>> seen(R, vector<char>(C, 0));
    queue<pair<int, int>> q;
    q.push(s); seen[s.first][s.second] = 1;
    while (!q.empty()) {
        auto [r, c] = q.front(); q.pop();
        for (int k = 0; k < 4; k++) {
            int nr = r + DR[k], nc = c + DC[k];
            if (nr >= 0 && nr < R && nc >= 0 && nc < C && grid[nr][nc] == 0 && !seen[nr][nc]) { seen[nr][nc] = 1; q.push({nr, nc}); }
        }
    }
    return seen[t.first][t.second];
}

// returns the cost and fills path; returns -1 if there is no path
double plan(const Grid& grid, pair<int, int> s, pair<int, int> t, double weight, vector<pair<int, int>>& path) {
    path.clear();
