    if (grid[s.first][s.second] || grid[t.first][t.second] || !reachable(grid, s, t)) return -1;
    int R = grid.size(), C = grid[0].size();
    auto clear = clearance(grid);
    auto h = [&](int r, int c) { return abs(r - t.first) + abs(c - t.second); };   // Manhattan distance never overestimates
    vector<vector<double>> g(R, vector<double>(C, 1e300));
    vector<vector<int>> parent(R, vector<int>(C, -1));
    typedef tuple<double, int, int, double> E;                          // {f, r, c, g}
    priority_queue<E, vector<E>, greater<E>> pq;
    g[s.first][s.second] = 0; pq.push({(double)h(s.first, s.second), s.first, s.second, 0.0});
    while (!pq.empty()) {
        auto [f, r, c, gu] = pq.top(); pq.pop();
        if (gu > g[r][c]) continue;                                     // stale entry
        if (r == t.first && c == t.second) break;
        for (int k = 0; k < 4; k++) {
            int nr = r + DR[k], nc = c + DC[k];
            if (nr < 0 || nr >= R || nc < 0 || nc >= C || grid[nr][nc]) continue;
            double ng = gu + 1 + (clear[nr][nc] > 0 ? weight / clear[nr][nc] : 0);   // penalise cells near obstacles
            if (ng < g[nr][nc]) { g[nr][nc] = ng; parent[nr][nc] = r * C + c; pq.push({ng + h(nr, nc), nr, nc, ng}); }
        }
    }
    for (int v = t.first * C + t.second; v != -1; v = parent[v / C][v % C]) path.push_back({v / C, v % C});
    reverse(path.begin(), path.end());
    return g[t.first][t.second];
}
--go
import (
	"container/heap"
	"math"
)

var dirs = [4][2]int{{1, 0}, {-1, 0}, {0, 1}, {0, -1}}

func clearance(grid [][]int) [][]int { // multi-source BFS from every obstacle
	R, C := len(grid), len(grid[0])
	d := make([][]int, R)
	var q [][2]int
	for r := range d {
		d[r] = make([]int, C)
		for c := range d[r] {
			d[r][c] = -1
			if grid[r][c] == 1 {
				d[r][c] = 0
				q = append(q, [2]int{r, c})
			}
		}
	}
	for len(q) > 0 {
		u := q[0]
		q = q[1:]
		for _, dd := range dirs {
			r, c := u[0]+dd[0], u[1]+dd[1]
			if r >= 0 && r < R && c >= 0 && c < C && d[r][c] < 0 {
				d[r][c] = d[u[0]][u[1]] + 1
				q = append(q, [2]int{r, c})
			}
		}
	}
	return d // -1 everywhere if there are no obstacles
}

func reachable(grid [][]int, s, t [2]int) bool { // flood fill
	R, C := len(grid), len(grid[0])
	seen := make([][]bool, R)
	for r := range seen {
		seen[r] = make([]bool, C)
	}
	q := [][2]int{s}
	seen[s[0]][s[1]] = true
	for len(q) > 0 {
		u := q[0]
		q = q[1:]
		for _, dd := range dirs {
			r, c := u[0]+dd[0], u[1]+dd[1]
			if r >= 0 && r < R && c >= 0 && c < C && grid[r][c] == 0 && !seen[r][c] {
				seen[r][c] = true
				q = append(q, [2]int{r, c})
			}
		}
	}
	return seen[t[0]][t[1]]
}

type cell struct {
	f, g float64
	r, c int
}
type pq []cell // min-heap on f

func (q pq) Len() int           { return len(q) }
func (q pq) Less(i, j int) bool { return q[i].f < q[j].f }
func (q pq) Swap(i, j int)      { q[i], q[j] = q[j], q[i] }
func (q *pq) Push(x any)        { *q = append(*q, x.(cell)) }
func (q *pq) Pop() any          { o := *q; x := o[len(o)-1]; *q = o[:len(o)-1]; return x }

// A* on the grid with a penalty for cells near obstacles. Returns the cost and the path, or ok=false.
func plan(grid [][]int, s, t [2]int, weight float64) (cost float64, path [][2]int, ok bool) {
	if grid[s[0]][s[1]] == 1 || grid[t[0]][t[1]] == 1 || !reachable(grid, s, t) {
		return 0, nil, false
	}
	R, C := len(grid), len(grid[0])
	clear := clearance(grid)
	g := make([][]float64, R)
	parent := make([][]int, R)
	for r := range g {
		g[r], parent[r] = make([]float64, C), make([]int, C)
		for c := range g[r] {
			g[r][c], parent[r][c] = math.Inf(1), -1
		}
	}
	manhattan := func(r, c int) float64 { return math.Abs(float64(r-t[0])) + math.Abs(float64(c-t[1])) }
	g[s[0]][s[1]] = 0
	q := &pq{{manhattan(s[0], s[1]), 0, s[0], s[1]}}
	for q.Len() > 0 {
		e := heap.Pop(q).(cell)
		if e.g > g[e.r][e.c] { // stale entry
			continue
		}
		if e.r == t[0] && e.c == t[1] {
			break
		}
		for _, dd := range dirs {
			nr, nc := e.r+dd[0], e.c+dd[1]
			if nr < 0 || nr >= R || nc < 0 || nc >= C || grid[nr][nc] == 1 {
				continue
			}
			ng := g[e.r][e.c] + 1
			if clear[nr][nc] > 0 { // penalise cells near obstacles
				ng += weight / float64(clear[nr][nc])
			}
			if ng < g[nr][nc] {
				g[nr][nc], parent[nr][nc] = ng, e.r*C+e.c
				heap.Push(q, cell{ng + manhattan(nr, nc), ng, nr, nc})
			}
		}
	}
	for v := t[0]*C + t[1]; v != -1; v = parent[v/C][v%C] {
		path = append([][2]int{{v / C, v % C}}, path...)
	}
	return g[t[0]][t[1]], path, true
}

### graphics
--idea
A game or simulator must decide, many times per second, which objects touch each other and which are visible. Checking every pair of objects is quadratic and far too slow for thousands of objects, so engines work in two phases. The broad phase quickly discards pairs that are obviously far apart, using a spatial index. The narrow phase runs exact geometry tests on the few pairs that remain.
The geometry uses a handful of standard tools. The convex hull is the smallest convex shape containing a set of points, and collision tests are much simpler for convex shapes. Two line segments cross if and only if the endpoints of each lie on opposite sides of the other, which an orientation test (the sign of a cross product) decides without any division. Rotating a shape uses sines and cosines, or complex numbers. A k-d tree over the objects' centres answers "which objects are near this one" without looking at the rest.
--build
1. Rotation: to turn a point `(x, y)` by angle `a` about a centre `(cx, cy)`, compute `dx = x - cx`, `dy = y - cy`, then `x' = cx + dx*cos(a) - dy*sin(a)` and `y' = cy + dx*sin(a) + dy*cos(a)`. This is multiplication by the complex number `cos(a) + i*sin(a)` (see Rotations and complex numbers).
2. Convex hull by the monotone chain (see Convex hull): sort the points by x then y. Build the lower hull by scanning left to right, and while the last two kept points and the new one do not make a left turn (cross product not positive), drop the last kept point. Build the upper hull the same way scanning right to left. Join the two, giving the hull in counter-clockwise order.
3. Orientation: `orient(a, b, c) = (b - a) x (c - a)`, positive when `c` is left of the line from `a` to `b`, negative when right, zero when collinear.
4. Segment intersection (see Segment intersection): segments `p1p2` and `p3p4` cross when `orient(p1,p2,p3)` and `orient(p1,p2,p4)` have opposite signs and `orient(p3,p4,p1)` and `orient(p3,p4,p2)` also differ. If an orientation is zero, check whether that point lies within the other segment's bounding box (touching counts as a hit).
5. Narrow phase for two convex polygons: they collide if any edge of one crosses an edge of the other, or if one lies entirely inside the other, which is checked by testing one vertex against the other polygon (a vertex is inside a counter-clockwise convex polygon if it is left of or on every edge).
6. Broad phase with a k-d tree (see k-d tree): give each body a centre (mean of its vertices) and a radius (largest distance from the centre to a vertex). Build a tree over the centres. For each body, ask the tree for centres within `radius + largest radius`, and keep only pairs whose circles overlap.
7. Run the narrow phase on the surviving pairs. Visibility (what is hidden behind what) uses the same pieces: space partitioning trees decide which objects to draw, and hulls and segments clip and sort them.
--think
- Signal: many moving objects with "do these touch" questions, or any geometry that depends on left versus right. The two-phase pattern (cheap filter, exact test) applies everywhere: databases, physics, rendering.
- Use exact arithmetic where you can. Orientation tests on integers are exact. With floating point, nearly collinear points can give inconsistent signs and make hull or intersection code fail, so real libraries use robust predicates or an epsilon with care.
- Collision in physics engines also needs the contact point, depth and normal to push objects apart, and the separating axis theorem (for convex shapes, check the axes of the edge normals) answers yes or no more directly than edge tests.
- Spatial structures: k-d trees, uniform grids and bounding volume hierarchies each suit different distributions. A uniform grid is simplest when objects are similar in size. A sweep along one axis (sort by x and compare neighbours) is also popular.
- Mistakes: forgetting the containment case (a small shape inside a big one crosses no edge), a hull that keeps collinear points (use a non-strict test), and comparing a squared distance with an unsquared radius.
--cost
Hull O(n log n) for the sort. Segment test O(1). A broad phase with a k-d tree costs O(n log n) to build and about O(log n + k) per query for k results, instead of O(n) per object for brute force.
--java
static double[] rotate(double[] p, double a, double cx, double cy) {      // rotate by angle a about (cx, cy)
    double dx = p[0] - cx, dy = p[1] - cy;
    return new double[]{cx + dx * Math.cos(a) - dy * Math.sin(a), cy + dx * Math.sin(a) + dy * Math.cos(a)};
}

static double orient(double[] a, double[] b, double[] c) { return (b[0] - a[0]) * (c[1] - a[1]) - (b[1] - a[1]) * (c[0] - a[0]); }

static List<double[]> hull(List<double[]> pts) {                           // monotone chain, counter-clockwise
    List<double[]> p = new ArrayList<>(pts);
    p.sort((a, b) -> a[0] != b[0] ? Double.compare(a[0], b[0]) : Double.compare(a[1], b[1]));
    if (p.size() < 3) return p;
    List<double[]> h = new ArrayList<>();
    for (int pass = 0; pass < 2; pass++) {
        int start = h.size();
        for (double[] q : p) {
            while (h.size() >= start + 2 && orient(h.get(h.size() - 2), h.get(h.size() - 1), q) <= 0) h.remove(h.size() - 1);
            h.add(q);
        }
        h.remove(h.size() - 1);                                            // the last point starts the other half
        Collections.reverse(p);
    }
    return h;
}

static boolean onSeg(double[] a, double[] b, double[] c) {
    return Math.min(a[0], b[0]) <= c[0] && c[0] <= Math.max(a[0], b[0]) && Math.min(a[1], b[1]) <= c[1] && c[1] <= Math.max(a[1], b[1]);
}

static boolean segInter(double[] p1, double[] p2, double[] p3, double[] p4) {
    double o1 = orient(p1, p2, p3), o2 = orient(p1, p2, p4), o3 = orient(p3, p4, p1), o4 = orient(p3, p4, p2);
    if (((o1 > 0) != (o2 > 0)) && o1 != 0 && o2 != 0 && ((o3 > 0) != (o4 > 0)) && o3 != 0 && o4 != 0) return true;   // proper crossing
    return (o1 == 0 && onSeg(p1, p2, p3)) || (o2 == 0 && onSeg(p1, p2, p4)) || (o3 == 0 && onSeg(p3, p4, p1)) || (o4 == 0 && onSeg(p3, p4, p2));
}

static boolean insideConvex(List<double[]> poly, double[] q) {            // counter-clockwise convex polygon
    for (int i = 0; i < poly.size(); i++) if (orient(poly.get(i), poly.get((i + 1) % poly.size()), q) < 0) return false;
    return true;
}

static boolean collide(List<double[]> A, List<double[]> B) {              // narrow phase for two convex polygons
    for (int i = 0; i < A.size(); i++) for (int j = 0; j < B.size(); j++)
        if (segInter(A.get(i), A.get((i + 1) % A.size()), B.get(j), B.get((j + 1) % B.size()))) return true;
    return insideConvex(B, A.get(0)) || insideConvex(A, B.get(0));        // one shape inside the other
}

static class KD {                                                          // k-d tree over points: radius queries
    final double[][] pts; final int[] left, right; final int root;
    KD(double[][] pts) {
        this.pts = pts; left = new int[pts.length]; right = new int[pts.length];
        List<Integer> ids = new ArrayList<>();
        for (int i = 0; i < pts.length; i++) ids.add(i);
        root = build(ids, 0);
    }
    private int build(List<Integer> ids, int depth) {
        if (ids.isEmpty()) return -1;
        final int axis = depth % 2;
        ids.sort(Comparator.comparingDouble(i -> pts[i][axis]));
        int mid = ids.size() / 2, node = ids.get(mid);
        left[node] = build(new ArrayList<>(ids.subList(0, mid)), depth + 1);
        right[node] = build(new ArrayList<>(ids.subList(mid + 1, ids.size())), depth + 1);
        return node;
    }
    void within(int node, int depth, double[] q, double r, List<Integer> out) {
        if (node < 0) return;
        if (Math.hypot(pts[node][0] - q[0], pts[node][1] - q[1]) <= r) out.add(node);
        double diff = q[depth % 2] - pts[node][depth % 2];
        if (diff - r <= 0) within(left[node], depth + 1, q, r, out);       // the circle reaches the left side
        if (diff + r >= 0) within(right[node], depth + 1, q, r, out);      // the circle reaches the right side
    }
}

static List<int[]> collisions(List<List<double[]>> bodies) {              // broad phase with a k-d tree, then narrow phase
    int n = bodies.size();
    double[][] c = new double[n][2]; double[] rad = new double[n]; double maxR = 0;
    for (int i = 0; i < n; i++) {
        for (double[] v : bodies.get(i)) { c[i][0] += v[0] / bodies.get(i).size(); c[i][1] += v[1] / bodies.get(i).size(); }
        for (double[] v : bodies.get(i)) rad[i] = Math.max(rad[i], Math.hypot(v[0] - c[i][0], v[1] - c[i][1]));
        maxR = Math.max(maxR, rad[i]);
    }
    KD tree = new KD(c);
    List<int[]> out = new ArrayList<>();
    for (int i = 0; i < n; i++) {
        List<Integer> near = new ArrayList<>();
        tree.within(tree.root, 0, c[i], rad[i] + maxR, near);
        for (int j : near)
            if (j > i && Math.hypot(c[i][0] - c[j][0], c[i][1] - c[j][1]) <= rad[i] + rad[j] && collide(bodies.get(i), bodies.get(j))) out.add(new int[]{i, j});
    }
    out.sort((a, b) -> a[0] != b[0] ? a[0] - b[0] : a[1] - b[1]);
    return out;
}
--py
import math

def rotate(p, a, cx=0.0, cy=0.0):                    # rotate by angle a about (cx, cy)
    dx, dy = p[0] - cx, p[1] - cy
    return (cx + dx * math.cos(a) - dy * math.sin(a), cy + dx * math.sin(a) + dy * math.cos(a))

def orient(a, b, c):
    return (b[0] - a[0]) * (c[1] - a[1]) - (b[1] - a[1]) * (c[0] - a[0])

def hull(pts):                                       # monotone chain, counter-clockwise
    p = sorted(set(pts))
    if len(p) < 3:
        return p
    h = []
    for seq in (p, p[::-1]):                         # lower hull, then upper hull
        start = len(h)
        for q in seq:
            while len(h) >= start + 2 and orient(h[-2], h[-1], q) <= 0:
                h.pop()
            h.append(q)
        h.pop()                                      # the last point starts the other half
    return h

def on_seg(a, b, c):
    return min(a[0], b[0]) <= c[0] <= max(a[0], b[0]) and min(a[1], b[1]) <= c[1] <= max(a[1], b[1])

def seg_inter(p1, p2, p3, p4):
    o1, o2, o3, o4 = orient(p1, p2, p3), orient(p1, p2, p4), orient(p3, p4, p1), orient(p3, p4, p2)
    if o1 * o2 < 0 and o3 * o4 < 0:
        return True                                  # proper crossing
    return (o1 == 0 and on_seg(p1, p2, p3)) or (o2 == 0 and on_seg(p1, p2, p4)) or \
           (o3 == 0 and on_seg(p3, p4, p1)) or (o4 == 0 and on_seg(p3, p4, p2))

def inside_convex(poly, q):                          # counter-clockwise convex polygon
    return all(orient(poly[i], poly[(i + 1) % len(poly)], q) >= 0 for i in range(len(poly)))

def collide(A, B):                                   # narrow phase for two convex polygons
    for i in range(len(A)):
        for j in range(len(B)):
            if seg_inter(A[i], A[(i + 1) % len(A)], B[j], B[(j + 1) % len(B)]):
                return True
    return inside_convex(B, A[0]) or inside_convex(A, B[0])      # one shape inside the other

class KD:                                            # k-d tree over points: radius queries
    def __init__(self, pts):
        self.pts = pts
        self.root = self._build(list(range(len(pts))), 0)

    def _build(self, ids, depth):                    # node = (id, left, right)
        if not ids:
            return None
        ids.sort(key=lambda i: self.pts[i][depth % 2])
        mid = len(ids) // 2
        return (ids[mid], self._build(ids[:mid], depth + 1), self._build(ids[mid + 1:], depth + 1))

    def within(self, q, r):
        out = []

        def go(node, depth):
            if node is None:
                return
            i, left, right = node
            if math.dist(self.pts[i], q) <= r:
                out.append(i)
            diff = q[depth % 2] - self.pts[i][depth % 2]
            if diff - r <= 0: go(left, depth + 1)    # the circle reaches the left side
            if diff + r >= 0: go(right, depth + 1)   # the circle reaches the right side

        go(self.root, 0)
        return out

def collisions(bodies):                              # broad phase with a k-d tree, then narrow phase
    centres = [(sum(v[0] for v in b) / len(b), sum(v[1] for v in b) / len(b)) for b in bodies]
    rad = [max(math.dist(c, v) for v in b) for c, b in zip(centres, bodies)]
    tree, max_r, out = KD(centres), max(rad), []
    for i, b in enumerate(bodies):
        for j in tree.within(centres[i], rad[i] + max_r):
            if j > i and math.dist(centres[i], centres[j]) <= rad[i] + rad[j] and collide(b, bodies[j]):
                out.append((i, j))
    return sorted(out)
--cpp
typedef pair<double, double> P2;

P2 rotatePt(P2 p, double a, double cx, double cy) {                       // rotate by angle a about (cx, cy)
    double dx = p.first - cx, dy = p.second - cy;
    return {cx + dx * cos(a) - dy * sin(a), cy + dx * sin(a) + dy * cos(a)};
}

double orient(P2 a, P2 b, P2 c) { return (b.first - a.first) * (c.second - a.second) - (b.second - a.second) * (c.first - a.first); }

vector<P2> hull(vector<P2> p) {                                            // monotone chain, counter-clockwise
    sort(p.begin(), p.end());
    p.erase(unique(p.begin(), p.end()), p.end());
    if (p.size() < 3) return p;
    vector<P2> h;
    for (int pass = 0; pass < 2; pass++) {                                 // lower hull, then upper hull
        size_t start = h.size();
        for (P2 q : p) {
            while (h.size() >= start + 2 && orient(h[h.size() - 2], h.back(), q) <= 0) h.pop_back();
            h.push_back(q);
        }
        h.pop_back();                                                      // the last point starts the other half
        reverse(p.begin(), p.end());
    }
    return h;
}

bool onSeg(P2 a, P2 b, P2 c) { return min(a.first, b.first) <= c.first && c.first <= max(a.first, b.first) && min(a.second, b.second) <= c.second && c.second <= max(a.second, b.second); }

bool segInter(P2 p1, P2 p2, P2 p3, P2 p4) {
    double o1 = orient(p1, p2, p3), o2 = orient(p1, p2, p4), o3 = orient(p3, p4, p1), o4 = orient(p3, p4, p2);
    if (o1 * o2 < 0 && o3 * o4 < 0) return true;                           // proper crossing
    return (o1 == 0 && onSeg(p1, p2, p3)) || (o2 == 0 && onSeg(p1, p2, p4)) || (o3 == 0 && onSeg(p3, p4, p1)) || (o4 == 0 && onSeg(p3, p4, p2));
}

bool insideConvex(const vector<P2>& poly, P2 q) {                          // counter-clockwise convex polygon
    for (size_t i = 0; i < poly.size(); i++) if (orient(poly[i], poly[(i + 1) % poly.size()], q) < 0) return false;
    return true;
}

bool collide(const vector<P2>& A, const vector<P2>& B) {                  // narrow phase for two convex polygons
    for (size_t i = 0; i < A.size(); i++) for (size_t j = 0; j < B.size(); j++)
        if (segInter(A[i], A[(i + 1) % A.size()], B[j], B[(j + 1) % B.size()])) return true;
    return insideConvex(B, A[0]) || insideConvex(A, B[0]);                 // one shape inside the other
}

struct KD {                                                                // k-d tree over points: radius queries
    vector<P2> pts; vector<int> left, right; int root;
    explicit KD(const vector<P2>& p) : pts(p), left(p.size(), -1), right(p.size(), -1) {
        vector<int> ids(p.size());
        iota(ids.begin(), ids.end(), 0);
        root = build(ids, 0);
    }
    double coord(int i, int axis) const { return axis == 0 ? pts[i].first : pts[i].second; }
    int build(vector<int> ids, int depth) {
        if (ids.empty()) return -1;
        int axis = depth % 2;
        sort(ids.begin(), ids.end(), [&](int a, int b) { return coord(a, axis) < coord(b, axis); });
        int mid = ids.size() / 2, node = ids[mid];
        left[node] = build(vector<int>(ids.begin(), ids.begin() + mid), depth + 1);
        right[node] = build(vector<int>(ids.begin() + mid + 1, ids.end()), depth + 1);
        return node;
    }
    void within(int node, int depth, P2 q, double r, vector<int>& out) const {
        if (node < 0) return;
        if (hypot(pts[node].first - q.first, pts[node].second - q.second) <= r) out.push_back(node);
        double diff = (depth % 2 == 0 ? q.first : q.second) - coord(node, depth % 2);
        if (diff - r <= 0) within(left[node], depth + 1, q, r, out);       // the circle reaches the left side
        if (diff + r >= 0) within(right[node], depth + 1, q, r, out);      // the circle reaches the right side
    }
};

vector<pair<int, int>> collisions(const vector<vector<P2>>& bodies) {     // broad phase with a k-d tree, then narrow phase
    int n = bodies.size();
    vector<P2> c(n); vector<double> rad(n, 0); double maxR = 0;
    for (int i = 0; i < n; i++) {
        for (auto& v : bodies[i]) { c[i].first += v.first / bodies[i].size(); c[i].second += v.second / bodies[i].size(); }
        for (auto& v : bodies[i]) rad[i] = max(rad[i], hypot(v.first - c[i].first, v.second - c[i].second));
        maxR = max(maxR, rad[i]);
    }
    KD tree(c);
    vector<pair<int, int>> out;
    for (int i = 0; i < n; i++) {
        vector<int> near;
        tree.within(tree.root, 0, c[i], rad[i] + maxR, near);
        for (int j : near)
            if (j > i && hypot(c[i].first - c[j].first, c[i].second - c[j].second) <= rad[i] + rad[j] && collide(bodies[i], bodies[j])) out.push_back({i, j});
    }
    sort(out.begin(), out.end());
    return out;
}
--go
import (
	"math"
	"slices"
	"sort"
)

type pt [2]float64

func rotate(p pt, a, cx, cy float64) pt { // rotate by angle a about (cx, cy)
	dx, dy := p[0]-cx, p[1]-cy
	return pt{cx + dx*math.Cos(a) - dy*math.Sin(a), cy + dx*math.Sin(a) + dy*math.Cos(a)}
}

func orient(a, b, c pt) float64 { return (b[0]-a[0])*(c[1]-a[1]) - (b[1]-a[1])*(c[0]-a[0]) }

func hull(pts []pt) []pt { // monotone chain, counter-clockwise
	p := slices.Clone(pts)
	sort.Slice(p, func(a, b int) bool { return p[a][0] < p[b][0] || p[a][0] == p[b][0] && p[a][1] < p[b][1] })
	if len(p) < 3 {
		return p
	}
	var h []pt
	for pass := 0; pass < 2; pass++ {
		start := len(h)
		for _, q := range p {
			for len(h) >= start+2 && orient(h[len(h)-2], h[len(h)-1], q) <= 0 {
				h = h[:len(h)-1]
			}
			h = append(h, q)
		}
		h = h[:len(h)-1] // the last point starts the other half
		slices.Reverse(p)
	}
	return h
}

func onSeg(a, b, c pt) bool {
	return min(a[0], b[0]) <= c[0] && c[0] <= max(a[0], b[0]) && min(a[1], b[1]) <= c[1] && c[1] <= max(a[1], b[1])
}

func segInter(p1, p2, p3, p4 pt) bool {
	o1, o2, o3, o4 := orient(p1, p2, p3), orient(p1, p2, p4), orient(p3, p4, p1), orient(p3, p4, p2)
	if o1*o2 < 0 && o3*o4 < 0 { // proper crossing
		return true
	}
	return o1 == 0 && onSeg(p1, p2, p3) || o2 == 0 && onSeg(p1, p2, p4) || o3 == 0 && onSeg(p3, p4, p1) || o4 == 0 && onSeg(p3, p4, p2)
}

func insideConvex(poly []pt, q pt) bool { // counter-clockwise convex polygon
	for i := range poly {
		if orient(poly[i], poly[(i+1)%len(poly)], q) < 0 {
			return false
		}
	}
	return true
}

func collide(A, B []pt) bool { // narrow phase for two convex polygons
	for i := range A {
		for j := range B {
			if segInter(A[i], A[(i+1)%len(A)], B[j], B[(j+1)%len(B)]) {
				return true
			}
		}
	}
	return insideConvex(B, A[0]) || insideConvex(A, B[0]) // one shape inside the other
}

type KD struct { // k-d tree over points: radius queries
	pts         []pt
	left, right []int
	root        int
}

func newKD(pts []pt) *KD {
	k := &KD{pts: pts, left: make([]int, len(pts)), right: make([]int, len(pts))}
	ids := make([]int, len(pts))
	for i := range ids {
		ids[i] = i
	}
	k.root = k.build(ids, 0)
	return k
}

func (k *KD) build(ids []int, depth int) int {
	if len(ids) == 0 {
		return -1
	}
	axis := depth % 2
	sort.Slice(ids, func(a, b int) bool { return k.pts[ids[a]][axis] < k.pts[ids[b]][axis] })
	mid := len(ids) / 2
	node := ids[mid]
	k.left[node] = k.build(slices.Clone(ids[:mid]), depth+1)
	k.right[node] = k.build(slices.Clone(ids[mid+1:]), depth+1)
	return node
}

func (k *KD) within(node, depth int, q pt, r float64, out *[]int) {
	if node < 0 {
		return
	}
	if math.Hypot(k.pts[node][0]-q[0], k.pts[node][1]-q[1]) <= r {
		*out = append(*out, node)
	}
	diff := q[depth%2] - k.pts[node][depth%2]
	if diff-r <= 0 { // the circle reaches the left side
		k.within(k.left[node], depth+1, q, r, out)
	}
	if diff+r >= 0 { // the circle reaches the right side
		k.within(k.right[node], depth+1, q, r, out)
	}
}

func collisions(bodies [][]pt) [][2]int { // broad phase with a k-d tree, then narrow phase
	n := len(bodies)
	c, rad, maxR := make([]pt, n), make([]float64, n), 0.0
	for i, body := range bodies {
		for _, v := range body {
			c[i][0] += v[0] / float64(len(body))
			c[i][1] += v[1] / float64(len(body))
		}
		for _, v := range body {
			rad[i] = max(rad[i], math.Hypot(v[0]-c[i][0], v[1]-c[i][1]))
		}
		maxR = max(maxR, rad[i])
	}
	tree := newKD(c)
	var out [][2]int
	for i := 0; i < n; i++ {
		var near []int
		tree.within(tree.root, 0, c[i], rad[i]+maxR, &near)
		for _, j := range near {
			if j > i && math.Hypot(c[i][0]-c[j][0], c[i][1]-c[j][1]) <= rad[i]+rad[j] && collide(bodies[i], bodies[j]) {
				out = append(out, [2]int{i, j})
			}
		}
	}
	sort.Slice(out, func(a, b int) bool { return out[a][0] < out[b][0] || out[a][0] == out[b][0] && out[a][1] < out[b][1] })
	return out
}

### gis
--idea
A spatial database or GIS (geographic information system) stores places, roads and regions, and answers questions such as "which shops are inside this district?", "which roads cross this latitude?" or "what is within 2 km of me?". Scanning every record for every question does not scale, so the data is organised in a spatial index that narrows the search to a small area first.
The model combines three tools. A k-d tree over the points answers a rectangle (bounding box) query, "all points with `x` and `y` in this box", by pruning every part of the tree that lies outside it. Because a district is not a rectangle, the box is only a cheap first filter: the exact test is point in polygon, done with a ray: count how many times a ray from the point crosses the polygon's edges, and an odd count means inside. An interval tree answers "which stored ranges contain this value", for example which road segments span a given latitude. Real systems use the R-tree, which stores rectangles in a balanced tree like a B-tree, but the filter-then-refine pattern is the same.
--build
1. Points: a list of `(x, y)` places. Polygon: a list of vertices in order.
2. k-d tree (see k-d tree): build recursively. At depth `d` sort the ids by coordinate `d mod 2`, take the median as the node, and build the two halves as children.
3. Rectangle query: visit a node, and if its point lies in the box, report it. Go to the left child only if the box reaches the left side of the split (`box minimum <= node coordinate`), and to the right child only if it reaches the right side (`box maximum >= node coordinate`). Whole subtrees outside the box are skipped.
4. Point in polygon (see Point in polygon): for point `(px, py)` and each edge `(x1, y1) -> (x2, y2)`, if the edge straddles the horizontal line through the point (one end above `py`, the other not) and the crossing point lies to the right of `px`, flip an inside flag. Edges are treated as half-open at the top so that a vertex on the ray is not counted twice.
5. Places in a region: compute the polygon's bounding box, get the candidates with the k-d rectangle query, and keep those that pass the point in polygon test. The cheap filter removes most points before the exact test runs.
6. Interval tree (see Interval trees): sort the intervals by their low end and treat the sorted array as an implicit balanced tree whose middle element is the root. For each subtree store `maxHi`, the largest high end inside it.
7. Stabbing query for value `x`: at a subtree, if `maxHi < x` nothing there can contain `x`, so stop. Otherwise search the left half, report the middle interval if it contains `x`, and search the right half only if the middle interval starts at or before `x` (everything to the right starts even later).
--think
- Signal: queries by location or range over large data sets. Filter then refine is the general rule: a cheap, conservative test (bounding box) to discard most candidates, then the exact, costly one.
- Index choice: a k-d tree is static and simple (rebuild when data changes a lot). R-trees and quadtrees handle inserts, and databases also use geohashes (a string encoding of a location where shared prefixes mean nearness) so an ordinary B-tree index can serve spatial queries.
- Coordinates matter: latitude and longitude are angles on a sphere, so distances, areas and "nearest" need spherical formulas or a projection. Longitude wraps at 180 degrees, which breaks naive boxes that cross it.
- Polygons have holes and many vertices, so exact tests are expensive and worth minimising with the filter step. Edge cases (a point exactly on an edge, a vertex on the ray) need a consistent rule.
- Mistakes: pruning with strict inequalities on boundaries and losing points that lie exactly on the box edge, counting a ray through a vertex twice, and treating degrees of latitude and longitude as equal lengths.
--cost
k-d tree build O(n log n), rectangle query O(sqrt(n) + k) for k results in 2D. Point in polygon O(vertices). Interval stabbing O(log n + k).
--java
static class KD {                                                          // k-d tree for rectangle queries
    final double[][] pts; final int[] left, right; final int root;
    KD(double[][] pts) {
        this.pts = pts; left = new int[pts.length]; right = new int[pts.length];
        List<Integer> ids = new ArrayList<>();
        for (int i = 0; i < pts.length; i++) ids.add(i);
        root = build(ids, 0);
    }
    private int build(List<Integer> ids, int depth) {
        if (ids.isEmpty()) return -1;
        final int axis = depth % 2;
        ids.sort(Comparator.comparingDouble(i -> pts[i][axis]));
        int mid = ids.size() / 2, node = ids.get(mid);                       // the median splits the points
        left[node] = build(new ArrayList<>(ids.subList(0, mid)), depth + 1);
        right[node] = build(new ArrayList<>(ids.subList(mid + 1, ids.size())), depth + 1);
        return node;
    }
    void inBox(int node, int depth, double[] lo, double[] hi, List<Integer> out) {
        if (node < 0) return;
        double[] p = pts[node];
        if (p[0] >= lo[0] && p[0] <= hi[0] && p[1] >= lo[1] && p[1] <= hi[1]) out.add(node);
        int axis = depth % 2;
        if (lo[axis] <= p[axis]) inBox(left[node], depth + 1, lo, hi, out);    // the box reaches the left side
        if (hi[axis] >= p[axis]) inBox(right[node], depth + 1, lo, hi, out);   // the box reaches the right side
    }
}

static boolean pointInPolygon(double[][] poly, double px, double py) {      // ray casting: odd crossings = inside
    boolean inside = false;
    for (int i = 0, j = poly.length - 1; i < poly.length; j = i++) {
        double x1 = poly[j][0], y1 = poly[j][1], x2 = poly[i][0], y2 = poly[i][1];
        if ((y1 > py) != (y2 > py) && px < (x2 - x1) * (py - y1) / (y2 - y1) + x1) inside = !inside;   // half-open at the top
    }
    return inside;
}

static List<Integer> placesInRegion(double[][] pts, double[][] poly) {      // filter by bounding box, then refine
    double[] lo = {1e300, 1e300}, hi = {-1e300, -1e300};
    for (double[] v : poly) for (int a = 0; a < 2; a++) { lo[a] = Math.min(lo[a], v[a]); hi[a] = Math.max(hi[a], v[a]); }
    KD tree = new KD(pts);
    List<Integer> cand = new ArrayList<>(), out = new ArrayList<>();
    tree.inBox(tree.root, 0, lo, hi, cand);
    for (int id : cand) if (pointInPolygon(poly, pts[id][0], pts[id][1])) out.add(id);
    Collections.sort(out);
    return out;
}

static class IntervalTree {                                                 // implicit tree over intervals sorted by low end
    final double[][] iv; final double[] maxHi;
    IntervalTree(double[][] intervals) {
        iv = intervals.clone();
        Arrays.sort(iv, (a, b) -> Double.compare(a[0], b[0]));
        maxHi = new double[iv.length];
        fill(0, iv.length - 1);
    }
    private double fill(int l, int r) {
        if (l > r) return Double.NEGATIVE_INFINITY;
        int m = (l + r) / 2;
        return maxHi[m] = Math.max(iv[m][1], Math.max(fill(l, m - 1), fill(m + 1, r)));
    }
    void stab(double x, int l, int r, List<double[]> out) {                 // call with l = 0, r = n - 1
        if (l > r) return;
        int m = (l + r) / 2;
        if (maxHi[m] < x) return;                                           // nothing in this subtree reaches x
        stab(x, l, m - 1, out);
        if (iv[m][0] <= x && x <= iv[m][1]) out.add(iv[m]);
        if (iv[m][0] <= x) stab(x, m + 1, r, out);                          // the right half starts even later
    }
}
--py
import math

class KD:                                            # k-d tree for rectangle queries
    def __init__(self, pts):
        self.pts = pts
        self.root = self._build(list(range(len(pts))), 0)

    def _build(self, ids, depth):                    # node = (id, left, right)
        if not ids:
            return None
        ids.sort(key=lambda i: self.pts[i][depth % 2])
        mid = len(ids) // 2                          # the median splits the points
        return (ids[mid], self._build(ids[:mid], depth + 1), self._build(ids[mid + 1:], depth + 1))

    def in_box(self, lo, hi):
        out = []

        def go(node, depth):
            if node is None:
                return
            i, left, right = node
            p, axis = self.pts[i], depth % 2
            if lo[0] <= p[0] <= hi[0] and lo[1] <= p[1] <= hi[1]:
                out.append(i)
            if lo[axis] <= p[axis]: go(left, depth + 1)      # the box reaches the left side
            if hi[axis] >= p[axis]: go(right, depth + 1)     # the box reaches the right side

        go(self.root, 0)
        return out

def point_in_polygon(poly, px, py):                  # ray casting: odd crossings = inside
    inside = False
    for i in range(len(poly)):
        x1, y1 = poly[i - 1]
        x2, y2 = poly[i]
        if (y1 > py) != (y2 > py) and px < (x2 - x1) * (py - y1) / (y2 - y1) + x1:   # half-open at the top
            inside = not inside
    return inside

def places_in_region(pts, poly):                     # filter by bounding box, then refine
    lo = (min(v[0] for v in poly), min(v[1] for v in poly))
    hi = (max(v[0] for v in poly), max(v[1] for v in poly))
    cand = KD(pts).in_box(lo, hi)
    return sorted(i for i in cand if point_in_polygon(poly, *pts[i]))

class IntervalTree:                                  # implicit tree over intervals sorted by low end
    def __init__(self, intervals):
        self.iv = sorted(intervals)
        self.max_hi = [0.0] * len(self.iv)

        def fill(l, r):
            if l > r:
                return -math.inf
            m = (l + r) // 2
            self.max_hi[m] = max(self.iv[m][1], fill(l, m - 1), fill(m + 1, r))
            return self.max_hi[m]

        fill(0, len(self.iv) - 1)

    def stab(self, x):                               # all intervals containing x
        out = []

        def go(l, r):
            if l > r:
                return
            m = (l + r) // 2
            if self.max_hi[m] < x:
                return                               # nothing in this subtree reaches x
            go(l, m - 1)
            if self.iv[m][0] <= x <= self.iv[m][1]:
                out.append(self.iv[m])
            if self.iv[m][0] <= x:
                go(m + 1, r)                         # the right half starts even later

        go(0, len(self.iv) - 1)
        return out
--cpp
typedef pair<double, double> P2;

struct KD {                                                                // k-d tree for rectangle queries
    vector<P2> pts; vector<int> left, right; int root;
    explicit KD(const vector<P2>& p) : pts(p), left(p.size(), -1), right(p.size(), -1) {
        vector<int> ids(p.size());
        iota(ids.begin(), ids.end(), 0);
        root = build(ids, 0);
    }
    double coord(int i, int axis) const { return axis == 0 ? pts[i].first : pts[i].second; }
    int build(vector<int> ids, int depth) {
        if (ids.empty()) return -1;
        int axis = depth % 2;
        sort(ids.begin(), ids.end(), [&](int a, int b) { return coord(a, axis) < coord(b, axis); });
        int mid = ids.size() / 2, node = ids[mid];                         // the median splits the points
        left[node] = build(vector<int>(ids.begin(), ids.begin() + mid), depth + 1);
        right[node] = build(vector<int>(ids.begin() + mid + 1, ids.end()), depth + 1);
        return node;
    }
    void inBox(int node, int depth, P2 lo, P2 hi, vector<int>& out) const {
        if (node < 0) return;
        P2 p = pts[node];
        if (p.first >= lo.first && p.first <= hi.first && p.second >= lo.second && p.second <= hi.second) out.push_back(node);
        int axis = depth % 2;
        double l = axis == 0 ? lo.first : lo.second, h = axis == 0 ? hi.first : hi.second, c = coord(node, axis);
        if (l <= c) inBox(left[node], depth + 1, lo, hi, out);             // the box reaches the left side
        if (h >= c) inBox(right[node], depth + 1, lo, hi, out);            // the box reaches the right side
    }
};

bool pointInPolygon(const vector<P2>& poly, double px, double py) {        // ray casting: odd crossings = inside
    bool inside = false;
    for (size_t i = 0, j = poly.size() - 1; i < poly.size(); j = i++) {
        double x1 = poly[j].first, y1 = poly[j].second, x2 = poly[i].first, y2 = poly[i].second;
        if ((y1 > py) != (y2 > py) && px < (x2 - x1) * (py - y1) / (y2 - y1) + x1) inside = !inside;   // half-open at the top
    }
    return inside;
}

vector<int> placesInRegion(const vector<P2>& pts, const vector<P2>& poly) {   // filter by bounding box, then refine
    P2 lo = {1e300, 1e300}, hi = {-1e300, -1e300};
    for (auto& v : poly) { lo.first = min(lo.first, v.first); lo.second = min(lo.second, v.second); hi.first = max(hi.first, v.first); hi.second = max(hi.second, v.second); }
    KD tree(pts);
    vector<int> cand, out;
    tree.inBox(tree.root, 0, lo, hi, cand);
    for (int id : cand) if (pointInPolygon(poly, pts[id].first, pts[id].second)) out.push_back(id);
    sort(out.begin(), out.end());
    return out;
}

struct IntervalTree {                                                      // implicit tree over intervals sorted by low end
    vector<P2> iv; vector<double> maxHi;
    explicit IntervalTree(vector<P2> intervals) : iv(move(intervals)) {
        sort(iv.begin(), iv.end());
        maxHi.assign(iv.size(), 0);
        fill((int)0, (int)iv.size() - 1);
    }
    double fill(int l, int r) {
        if (l > r) return -1e300;
        int m = (l + r) / 2;
        return maxHi[m] = max({iv[m].second, fill(l, m - 1), fill(m + 1, r)});
    }
    void stab(double x, int l, int r, vector<P2>& out) const {              // call with l = 0, r = n - 1
        if (l > r) return;
        int m = (l + r) / 2;
        if (maxHi[m] < x) return;                                           // nothing in this subtree reaches x
        stab(x, l, m - 1, out);
        if (iv[m].first <= x && x <= iv[m].second) out.push_back(iv[m]);
        if (iv[m].first <= x) stab(x, m + 1, r, out);                       // the right half starts even later
    }
};
--go
import (
	"math"
	"slices"
	"sort"
)

type pt [2]float64

type KD struct { // k-d tree for rectangle queries
	pts         []pt
	left, right []int
	root        int
}

func newKD(pts []pt) *KD {
	k := &KD{pts: pts, left: make([]int, len(pts)), right: make([]int, len(pts))}
	ids := make([]int, len(pts))
	for i := range ids {
		ids[i] = i
	}
	k.root = k.build(ids, 0)
	return k
}

func (k *KD) build(ids []int, depth int) int {
	if len(ids) == 0 {
		return -1
	}
	axis := depth % 2
	sort.Slice(ids, func(a, b int) bool { return k.pts[ids[a]][axis] < k.pts[ids[b]][axis] })
	mid := len(ids) / 2 // the median splits the points
	node := ids[mid]
	k.left[node] = k.build(slices.Clone(ids[:mid]), depth+1)
	k.right[node] = k.build(slices.Clone(ids[mid+1:]), depth+1)
	return node
}

func (k *KD) inBox(node, depth int, lo, hi pt, out *[]int) {
	if node < 0 {
		return
	}
	p := k.pts[node]
	if p[0] >= lo[0] && p[0] <= hi[0] && p[1] >= lo[1] && p[1] <= hi[1] {
		*out = append(*out, node)
	}
	axis := depth % 2
	if lo[axis] <= p[axis] { // the box reaches the left side
		k.inBox(k.left[node], depth+1, lo, hi, out)
	}
	if hi[axis] >= p[axis] { // the box reaches the right side
		k.inBox(k.right[node], depth+1, lo, hi, out)
	}
}

func pointInPolygon(poly []pt, px, py float64) bool { // ray casting: odd crossings = inside
	inside := false
	for i, j := 0, len(poly)-1; i < len(poly); j, i = i, i+1 {
		x1, y1, x2, y2 := poly[j][0], poly[j][1], poly[i][0], poly[i][1]
		if (y1 > py) != (y2 > py) && px < (x2-x1)*(py-y1)/(y2-y1)+x1 { // half-open at the top
			inside = !inside
		}
	}
	return inside
}

func placesInRegion(pts, poly []pt) []int { // filter by bounding box, then refine
	lo, hi := pt{math.Inf(1), math.Inf(1)}, pt{math.Inf(-1), math.Inf(-1)}
	for _, v := range poly {
		for a := 0; a < 2; a++ {
			lo[a], hi[a] = min(lo[a], v[a]), max(hi[a], v[a])
		}
	}
	tree := newKD(pts)
	var cand, out []int
	tree.inBox(tree.root, 0, lo, hi, &cand)
	for _, id := range cand {
		if pointInPolygon(poly, pts[id][0], pts[id][1]) {
			out = append(out, id)
		}
	}
	sort.Ints(out)
	return out
}

type IntervalTree struct { // implicit tree over intervals sorted by low end
	iv    [][2]float64
	maxHi []float64
}

func newIntervalTree(intervals [][2]float64) *IntervalTree {
	t := &IntervalTree{iv: slices.Clone(intervals), maxHi: make([]float64, len(intervals))}
	sort.Slice(t.iv, func(a, b int) bool { return t.iv[a][0] < t.iv[b][0] })
	t.fill(0, len(t.iv)-1)
	return t
}

func (t *IntervalTree) fill(l, r int) float64 {
	if l > r {
		return math.Inf(-1)
	}
	m := (l + r) / 2
	t.maxHi[m] = max(t.iv[m][1], t.fill(l, m-1), t.fill(m+1, r))
	return t.maxHi[m]
}

func (t *IntervalTree) stab(x float64, l, r int, out *[][2]float64) { // call with l = 0, r = n - 1
	if l > r {
		return
	}
	m := (l + r) / 2
	if t.maxHi[m] < x { // nothing in this subtree reaches x
		return
	}
	t.stab(x, l, m-1, out)
	if t.iv[m][0] <= x && x <= t.iv[m][1] {
		*out = append(*out, t.iv[m])
	}
	if t.iv[m][0] <= x { // the right half starts even later
		t.stab(x, m+1, r, out)
	}
}
### ridematch
--idea
A ride-hailing or delivery platform must, every few seconds, decide which driver serves which rider, and in what order a driver should visit several stops. These are two classic optimisation problems. The first is an assignment problem: given the cost (pickup time) of every driver and rider pair, match them one to one so that the total cost is smallest. Matching each rider to the nearest free driver one by one (a greedy rule) can leave later riders with terrible options. The Hungarian algorithm finds the true minimum total.
The second is the travelling salesman problem (TSP): visit a set of stops and return, in the shortest total distance. For a handful of stops, dynamic programming over subsets solves it exactly. The assignment problem is a special case of min-cost max-flow (send one unit from each rider to a driver through the edges with their costs), and flow is the model that scales to extras like several seats per car. Real platforms batch requests over a short window to match them together, then use heuristics for longer routes.
--build
1. Cost matrix: `cost[i][j]` = the cost of rider `i` being served by driver `j` (for example, pickup time). Suppose there are `n` riders and `m >= n` drivers.
2. Hungarian algorithm with potentials (see Hungarian algorithm): keep a number `u[i]` for each row and `v[j]` for each column, called potentials, such that `u[i] + v[j] <= cost[i][j]` always holds. An edge is "tight" when it holds with equality. The aim is a perfect matching using only tight edges, which is then optimal.
3. Add the rows one at a time. For row `i`, grow a search tree over columns: maintain `minv[j]`, the smallest slack `cost - u - v` that reaches column `j`, and `way[j]`, which previous column gave it. Repeatedly choose the unvisited column with the smallest slack `delta`.
4. Adjust the potentials by `delta`: raise `u` for rows in the tree and lower `v` for columns in the tree (and lower `minv` of the others). This makes at least one new edge tight without breaking the rule. If the chosen column is free, stop. Otherwise continue from the row currently matched to it.
5. Follow `way` back to flip the matching along the augmenting path. After all rows are added, `-v[0]` is the minimal total cost, and the array `p` gives each column's row.
6. Route planning with TSP DP (see Traveling salesman DP): `dp[mask][last]` = shortest length of a path that starts at the depot (stop 0), visits exactly the stops in the bitmask `mask`, and ends at `last`. Start with `dp[{0}][0] = 0`. Extend by one unvisited stop: `dp[mask | {nxt}][nxt] = min(dp[mask][last] + dist[last][nxt])`. The answer is the minimum over `last` of `dp[all][last] + dist[last][0]`.
7. For larger problems the platform formulates a Min-cost max-flow: riders on one side, drivers on the other, edge costs as pickup times and capacities as seats, and solves it with successive shortest paths.
--think
- Signal: pair up two sets at minimum total cost (Hungarian, or flow), or order stops (TSP). Many other problems reduce to these: task assignment, scheduling workers to jobs, tracking objects between video frames.
- Greedy does not work for assignment: the nearest-first rule can be arbitrarily worse than optimal. The Hungarian algorithm runs in O(n^3), which is fast enough for a few thousand riders.
- TSP is NP-hard: the subset DP is exact but O(2^n * n^2), good up to about 20 stops. Beyond that use heuristics (nearest neighbour then 2-opt improvements) or solvers (OR-tools), and accept near-optimal routes.
- Objective design matters more than the algorithm: minimise total pickup time, or the worst wait, or revenue, and add fairness and driver availability. Costs come from a routing engine and live traffic, which need to be fast (see Maps and GPS navigation).
- Mistakes: forgetting that the Hungarian algorithm above needs rows not more than columns, using the wrong sign for maximisation (negate the costs), and forgetting the return leg in the TSP.
--cost
Hungarian O(n^2 * m). TSP DP O(2^n * n^2) time and O(2^n * n) memory.
--java
static final int INF = 1_000_000_000;

// cost is n x m with n <= m. Returns the minimum total cost; match[i] = column assigned to row i.
static int hungarian(int[][] cost, int[] match) {
    int n = cost.length, m = cost[0].length;
    int[] u = new int[n + 1], v = new int[m + 1], p = new int[m + 1], way = new int[m + 1];   // potentials, matching, path
    for (int i = 1; i <= n; i++) {
        p[0] = i;
        int j0 = 0;
        int[] minv = new int[m + 1];
        Arrays.fill(minv, INF);
        boolean[] used = new boolean[m + 1];
        do {
            used[j0] = true;
            int i0 = p[j0], delta = INF, j1 = 0;
            for (int j = 1; j <= m; j++) if (!used[j]) {
                int cur = cost[i0 - 1][j - 1] - u[i0] - v[j];                 // slack of the edge
                if (cur < minv[j]) { minv[j] = cur; way[j] = j0; }
                if (minv[j] < delta) { delta = minv[j]; j1 = j; }
            }
            for (int j = 0; j <= m; j++) {
                if (used[j]) { u[p[j]] += delta; v[j] -= delta; } else minv[j] -= delta;   // adjust the potentials
            }
            j0 = j1;
        } while (p[j0] != 0);
        do { int j1 = way[j0]; p[j0] = p[j1]; j0 = j1; } while (j0 != 0);       // flip the matching along the path
    }
    for (int j = 1; j <= m; j++) if (p[j] != 0) match[p[j] - 1] = j - 1;
    return -v[0];
}

static int tsp(int[][] dist) {                                              // shortest tour from stop 0 through all stops
    int n = dist.length, full = (1 << n) - 1;
    int[][] dp = new int[1 << n][n];
    for (int[] row : dp) Arrays.fill(row, INF);
    dp[1][0] = 0;
    for (int mask = 1; mask <= full; mask++) for (int last = 0; last < n; last++) {
        if (dp[mask][last] >= INF || (mask >> last & 1) == 0) continue;
        for (int nxt = 0; nxt < n; nxt++)
            if ((mask >> nxt & 1) == 0) dp[mask | 1 << nxt][nxt] = Math.min(dp[mask | 1 << nxt][nxt], dp[mask][last] + dist[last][nxt]);
    }
    int best = INF;
    for (int last = 0; last < n; last++) if (dp[full][last] < INF) best = Math.min(best, dp[full][last] + dist[last][0]);
    return best;
}
--py
INF = float("inf")

def hungarian(cost):                                 # cost is n x m with n <= m. Returns (total, match[i] = column)
    n, m = len(cost), len(cost[0])
    u, v, p, way = [0] * (n + 1), [0] * (m + 1), [0] * (m + 1), [0] * (m + 1)    # potentials, matching, path
    for i in range(1, n + 1):
        p[0], j0 = i, 0
        minv, used = [INF] * (m + 1), [False] * (m + 1)
        while True:
            used[j0] = True
            i0, delta, j1 = p[j0], INF, 0
            for j in range(1, m + 1):
                if not used[j]:
                    cur = cost[i0 - 1][j - 1] - u[i0] - v[j]            # slack of the edge
                    if cur < minv[j]:
                        minv[j], way[j] = cur, j0
                    if minv[j] < delta:
                        delta, j1 = minv[j], j
            for j in range(m + 1):                                       # adjust the potentials
                if used[j]:
                    u[p[j]] += delta; v[j] -= delta
                else:
                    minv[j] -= delta
            j0 = j1
            if p[j0] == 0:
                break
        while j0:                                                        # flip the matching along the path
            j1 = way[j0]
            p[j0] = p[j1]
            j0 = j1
    match = [0] * n
    for j in range(1, m + 1):
        if p[j]:
            match[p[j] - 1] = j - 1
    return -v[0], match

def tsp(dist):                                       # shortest tour from stop 0 through all stops
    n = len(dist)
    full = (1 << n) - 1
    dp = [[INF] * n for _ in range(1 << n)]
    dp[1][0] = 0
    for mask in range(1, full + 1):
        for last in range(n):
            if dp[mask][last] == INF or not mask >> last & 1:
                continue
            for nxt in range(n):
                if not mask >> nxt & 1:
                    new = mask | 1 << nxt
                    dp[new][nxt] = min(dp[new][nxt], dp[mask][last] + dist[last][nxt])
    return min(dp[full][last] + dist[last][0] for last in range(n) if dp[full][last] < INF)
--cpp
const int INF = 1000000000;

// cost is n x m with n <= m. Returns the minimum total cost; match[i] = column assigned to row i.
int hungarian(const vector<vector<int>>& cost, vector<int>& match) {
    int n = cost.size(), m = cost[0].size();
    vector<int> u(n + 1), v(m + 1), p(m + 1), way(m + 1);                // potentials, matching, path
    for (int i = 1; i <= n; i++) {
        p[0] = i;
        int j0 = 0;
        vector<int> minv(m + 1, INF);
        vector<char> used(m + 1, 0);
        do {
            used[j0] = 1;
            int i0 = p[j0], delta = INF, j1 = 0;
            for (int j = 1; j <= m; j++) if (!used[j]) {
                int cur = cost[i0 - 1][j - 1] - u[i0] - v[j];            // slack of the edge
                if (cur < minv[j]) { minv[j] = cur; way[j] = j0; }
                if (minv[j] < delta) { delta = minv[j]; j1 = j; }
            }
            for (int j = 0; j <= m; j++) {
                if (used[j]) { u[p[j]] += delta; v[j] -= delta; } else minv[j] -= delta;   // adjust the potentials
            }
            j0 = j1;
        } while (p[j0] != 0);
        do { int j1 = way[j0]; p[j0] = p[j1]; j0 = j1; } while (j0);    // flip the matching along the path
    }
    match.assign(n, 0);
    for (int j = 1; j <= m; j++) if (p[j]) match[p[j] - 1] = j - 1;
    return -v[0];
}

int tsp(const vector<vector<int>>& dist) {                             // shortest tour from stop 0 through all stops
    int n = dist.size(), full = (1 << n) - 1;
    vector<vector<int>> dp(1 << n, vector<int>(n, INF));
    dp[1][0] = 0;
    for (int mask = 1; mask <= full; mask++) for (int last = 0; last < n; last++) {
        if (dp[mask][last] >= INF || !(mask >> last & 1)) continue;
        for (int nxt = 0; nxt < n; nxt++)
            if (!(mask >> nxt & 1)) dp[mask | 1 << nxt][nxt] = min(dp[mask | 1 << nxt][nxt], dp[mask][last] + dist[last][nxt]);
    }
    int best = INF;
    for (int last = 0; last < n; last++) if (dp[full][last] < INF) best = min(best, dp[full][last] + dist[last][0]);
    return best;
}
--go
import "slices"

const INF = 1_000_000_000

// cost is n x m with n <= m. Returns the minimum total cost; match[i] = column assigned to row i.
func hungarian(cost [][]int) (int, []int) {
	n, m := len(cost), len(cost[0])
	u, v := make([]int, n+1), make([]int, m+1)   // potentials
	p, way := make([]int, m+1), make([]int, m+1) // matching, path
	for i := 1; i <= n; i++ {
		p[0] = i
		j0 := 0
		minv := make([]int, m+1)
		for j := range minv {
			minv[j] = INF
		}
		used := make([]bool, m+1)
		for {
			used[j0] = true
			i0, delta, j1 := p[j0], INF, 0
			for j := 1; j <= m; j++ {
				if used[j] {
					continue
				}
				if cur := cost[i0-1][j-1] - u[i0] - v[j]; cur < minv[j] { // slack of the edge
					minv[j], way[j] = cur, j0
				}
				if minv[j] < delta {
					delta, j1 = minv[j], j
				}
			}
			for j := 0; j <= m; j++ { // adjust the potentials
				if used[j] {
					u[p[j]] += delta
					v[j] -= delta
				} else {
					minv[j] -= delta
				}
			}
			if j0 = j1; p[j0] == 0 {
				break
			}
		}
		for j0 != 0 { // flip the matching along the path
			j1 := way[j0]
			p[j0] = p[j1]
			j0 = j1
		}
	}
	match := make([]int, n)
	for j := 1; j <= m; j++ {
		if p[j] != 0 {
			match[p[j]-1] = j - 1
		}
	}
	return -v[0], match
}

func tsp(dist [][]int) int { // shortest tour from stop 0 through all stops
	n := len(dist)
	full := 1<<n - 1
	dp := make([][]int, 1<<n)
	for mask := range dp {
		dp[mask] = slices.Repeat([]int{INF}, n)
	}
	dp[1][0] = 0
	for mask := 1; mask <= full; mask++ {
		for last := 0; last < n; last++ {
			if dp[mask][last] >= INF || mask>>last&1 == 0 {
				continue
			}
			for nxt := 0; nxt < n; nxt++ {
				if mask>>nxt&1 == 0 {
					dp[mask|1<<nxt][nxt] = min(dp[mask|1<<nxt][nxt], dp[mask][last]+dist[last][nxt])
				}
			}
		}
	}
	best := INF
	for last := 0; last < n; last++ {
		if dp[full][last] < INF {
			best = min(best, dp[full][last]+dist[last][0])
		}
	}
	return best
}

### timetable
--idea
A school or university must assign every exam to a time slot so that no two exams that share a student are scheduled at the same time. Draw each exam as a dot and join two dots with a line when some student takes both. Giving each dot a colour (a time slot) so that joined dots always differ is exactly graph colouring, and the fewest colours needed is the minimum number of time slots.
Finding the minimum is NP-hard, so there is no known fast exact method for large graphs. For realistic sizes, backtracking with good ordering works: colour the most constrained exams first (those that conflict with the most others), try the colours one by one, and undo the choice as soon as it leads to a dead end. A simple trick cuts repeated work: never introduce colour number `c + 1` before colour `c` has been used, since swapping the names of colours gives the same solution. Real timetabling adds more constraints (rooms with limited seats, students with back-to-back exams, teacher availability), and the room assignment part is a flow or matching problem.
--build
1. Graph: `n` exams, and `adj[i]` the set of exams in conflict with exam `i` (they share a student). Build it from the enrolment lists.
2. Order the exams by decreasing number of conflicts (degree). Hard exams are decided first, so contradictions appear early and prune the search.
3. `solve(position)`: if every exam is coloured, success. Take the exam at this position in the order.
4. Try each colour `c` from 0 up to `used` (the number of colours in use so far, so that at most one new colour is opened), as long as it is below the limit `k`. A colour is allowed if no neighbour already has it.
5. Colour the exam, recurse to the next position, and if that fails, remove the colour (backtrack, see Backtracking) and try the next one.
6. If no colour works, return failure to the caller, which then tries its next option.
7. Fewest slots: call the solver with `k = 1, 2, 3, ...` until it succeeds. The first success gives the minimum, the chromatic number. A lower bound is the size of the largest group of mutually conflicting exams (a clique).
8. A greedy colouring (give each exam the lowest colour its neighbours do not use) is instant but may use more colours than needed. It is a good starting upper bound, and a good fallback for huge instances.
--think
- Signal: "assign labels so that related items differ": exam slots, register allocation in a compiler (see Compilers and parsers), frequency assignment for radio towers, map colouring, Sudoku. All are colouring, the constraint graph is what changes.
- Ordering heuristics matter more than clever code. The most constrained first rule (DSATUR chooses by the number of different colours already seen among neighbours) often solves big instances instantly.
- Know the limits: any graph needs at most `maxDegree + 1` colours (greedy proves it), bipartite graphs need 2, and planar graphs need at most 4. Deciding whether 3 colours suffice is already NP-complete.
- Real instances are solved with tools: SAT solvers, integer programming, or local search methods such as Simulated annealing, which are better at improving a good solution than proving it optimal.
- Mistakes: not checking the colours of all neighbours (only earlier ones), searching with symmetric colour permutations (huge wasted work, the `used + 1` rule fixes it), and using recursion depth equal to the number of vertices on very large inputs.
--cost
Exponential in the worst case. With good ordering and pruning it is often fast for hundreds of exams. Greedy colouring is O(V + E).
--java
// adj.get(i) = exams that conflict with i. Returns the slot of each exam using at most k slots, or null.
static int[] colorExams(List<List<Integer>> adj, int k) {
    int n = adj.size();
    Integer[] order = new Integer[n];
    for (int i = 0; i < n; i++) order[i] = i;
    Arrays.sort(order, (a, b) -> adj.get(b).size() - adj.get(a).size());      // most conflicts first
    int[] color = new int[n];
    Arrays.fill(color, -1);
    return place(0, 0, order, adj, color, k) ? color : null;
}

private static boolean place(int pos, int used, Integer[] order, List<List<Integer>> adj, int[] color, int k) {
    if (pos == order.length) return true;
    int v = order[pos];
    for (int c = 0; c <= Math.min(used, k - 1); c++) {                         // at most one new colour at a time
        boolean ok = true;
        for (int u : adj.get(v)) if (color[u] == c) { ok = false; break; }
        if (!ok) continue;
        color[v] = c;
        if (place(pos + 1, Math.max(used, c + 1), order, adj, color, k)) return true;
        color[v] = -1;                                                         // backtrack
    }
    return false;
}

static int minSlots(List<List<Integer>> adj) {
    for (int k = 1; ; k++) if (colorExams(adj, k) != null) return k;
}
--py
def color_exams(adj, k):                             # adj[i] = exams that conflict with i; returns slots or None
    n = len(adj)
    order = sorted(range(n), key=lambda i: -len(adj[i]))   # most conflicts first
    color = [-1] * n

    def place(pos, used):
        if pos == n:
            return True
        v = order[pos]
        for c in range(min(used, k - 1) + 1):        # at most one new colour at a time
            if all(color[u] != c for u in adj[v]):
                color[v] = c
                if place(pos + 1, max(used, c + 1)):
                    return True
                color[v] = -1                        # backtrack
        return False

    return color if place(0, 0) else None

def min_slots(adj):
    k = 1
    while color_exams(adj, k) is None:
        k += 1
    return k
--cpp
// adj[i] = exams that conflict with i. Returns the slot of each exam using at most k slots, or an empty vector.
vector<int> colorExams(const vector<vector<int>>& adj, int k) {
    int n = adj.size();
    vector<int> order(n), color(n, -1);
    iota(order.begin(), order.end(), 0);
    sort(order.begin(), order.end(), [&](int a, int b) { return adj[a].size() > adj[b].size(); });   // most conflicts first
    function<bool(int, int)> place = [&](int pos, int used) {
        if (pos == n) return true;
        int v = order[pos];
        for (int c = 0; c <= min(used, k - 1); c++) {                      // at most one new colour at a time
            bool ok = true;
            for (int u : adj[v]) if (color[u] == c) { ok = false; break; }
            if (!ok) continue;
            color[v] = c;
            if (place(pos + 1, max(used, c + 1))) return true;
            color[v] = -1;                                                 // backtrack
        }
        return false;
    };
    return place(0, 0) ? color : vector<int>();
}

int minSlots(const vector<vector<int>>& adj) {
    for (int k = 1;; k++) if (!colorExams(adj, k).empty()) return k;
}
--go
import "sort"

// adj[i] = exams that conflict with i. Returns the slot of each exam using at most k slots, or nil.
func colorExams(adj [][]int, k int) []int {
	n := len(adj)
	order := make([]int, n)
	color := make([]int, n)
	for i := range order {
		order[i], color[i] = i, -1
	}
	sort.SliceStable(order, func(a, b int) bool { return len(adj[order[a]]) > len(adj[order[b]]) }) // most conflicts first
	var place func(pos, used int) bool
	place = func(pos, used int) bool {
		if pos == n {
			return true
		}
		v := order[pos]
		for c := 0; c <= min(used, k-1); c++ { // at most one new colour at a time
			ok := true
			for _, u := range adj[v] {
				if color[u] == c {
					ok = false
					break
				}
			}
			if !ok {
				continue
			}
			color[v] = c
			if place(pos+1, max(used, c+1)) {
				return true
			}
			color[v] = -1 // backtrack
		}
		return false
	}
	if place(0, 0) {
		return color
	}
	return nil
}

func minSlots(adj [][]int) int {
	for k := 1; ; k++ {
		if colorExams(adj, k) != nil {
			return k
		}
	}
}

### calendar
--idea
A calendar app answers two questions all the time: when are all these people free, and does this new meeting clash with anything? Both are interval problems. Each meeting is an interval `[start, end)` on a timeline, and the answers come from sweeping across the timeline in time order.
To find common free time, merge everyone's busy intervals. A sweep line does this neatly: list every start as a `+1` event and every end as a `-1` event, sort the events by time, and walk through them while keeping a count of how many people are busy. Whenever the count is 0 the time is free for everyone, and the stretches where it stays 0 are the free slots. To check a new meeting quickly against a large calendar, keep the booked meetings in a sorted structure so that only the neighbours of the new start time need to be examined, instead of every meeting. An interval tree generalises this to overlapping meetings and "everything that overlaps this window".
--build
1. Times are numbers (minutes from midnight). A meeting is `(start, end)` with `start < end`, and meetings that only touch at a boundary (one ends exactly when the next starts) do not overlap.
2. Free slots across people (see Sweep line and Interval scheduling): for each busy interval, clip it to the working day `[dayStart, dayEnd]` and ignore it if empty. Emit two events: `(start, +1)` and `(end, -1)`.
3. Sort all events by `(time, delta)`. At the same time, ends (`-1`) come before starts (`+1`), so back-to-back meetings leave no false gap and no false overlap.
4. Walk through the events with `count = 0` and `freeStart = dayStart`. When `count == 0` and the next event is a start at time `t`, the stretch `freeStart .. t` is free: record it if `t - freeStart >= minLen`. Apply the event to `count`. When `count` returns to 0 after an end at `t`, set `freeStart = t`.
5. After the last event, the stretch `freeStart .. dayEnd` is free too: record it if long enough.
6. Fast conflict check (see Ordered sets and maps): keep booked meetings in a map sorted by start time. To test a new meeting `[s, e)`, find the booked meeting with the largest start `<= s` (floor): it must end at or before `s`. Then find the first meeting with start `>= s` (ceiling): it must start at or after `e`. If both hold, there is no conflict, and you insert the new meeting. This is two O(log n) lookups, assuming the existing meetings do not overlap each other.
7. Real calendars add recurring events (generate the occurrences lazily), time zones and daylight saving, buffers between meetings, room resources, and ranking of the candidate slots by preference.
--think
- Signal: "find a gap", "do these overlap", "how many rooms are needed" (the maximum count during the sweep is the number of rooms), merging overlapping ranges. Events sorted with a sweep is the standard approach.
- The tie rule at equal times is the classic bug. Decide whether touching intervals overlap (here they do not), and then order the events accordingly.
- A sorted map is enough when the booked intervals never overlap, as in one person's calendar. If overlaps are allowed (an interval tree, or the sweep) you must look at more than just the two neighbours.
- Time zones: store meetings in UTC and convert only for display. Daylight saving days have 23 or 25 hours, so minutes from midnight is not safe across them.
- Mistakes: not clipping busy intervals to the working day, sorting by time only (ties then give wrong gaps), and using inclusive ends so that back-to-back meetings clash.
--cost
Free slots: O(m log m) for m meetings, dominated by the sort. Conflict check and insert: O(log n) with a sorted map.
--java
// busy: one list of {start, end} per person. Returns the free slots {start, end} of at least minLen within the day.
static List<int[]> freeSlots(List<List<int[]>> busy, int dayStart, int dayEnd, int minLen) {
    List<int[]> events = new ArrayList<>();                                  // {time, delta}
    for (List<int[]> person : busy) for (int[] iv : person) {
        int s = Math.max(iv[0], dayStart), e = Math.min(iv[1], dayEnd);      // clip to the working day
        if (s < e) { events.add(new int[]{s, 1}); events.add(new int[]{e, -1}); }
    }
    events.sort((a, b) -> a[0] != b[0] ? a[0] - b[0] : a[1] - b[1]);         // ends before starts at equal times
    List<int[]> out = new ArrayList<>();
    int count = 0, freeStart = dayStart;
    for (int[] ev : events) {
        if (count == 0 && ev[1] == 1 && ev[0] - freeStart >= minLen) out.add(new int[]{freeStart, ev[0]});
        count += ev[1];
        if (count == 0) freeStart = ev[0];
    }
    if (dayEnd - freeStart >= minLen) out.add(new int[]{freeStart, dayEnd});
    return out;
}

static class Booked {
    final TreeMap<Integer, Integer> meetings = new TreeMap<>();               // start -> end, sorted by start
    boolean canBook(int s, int e) {
        Map.Entry<Integer, Integer> before = meetings.floorEntry(s);          // the meeting starting at or before s
        if (before != null && before.getValue() > s) return false;
        Map.Entry<Integer, Integer> after = meetings.ceilingEntry(s);         // the first meeting starting at or after s
        return after == null || after.getKey() >= e;
    }
    boolean book(int s, int e) { if (!canBook(s, e)) return false; meetings.put(s, e); return true; }
}
--py
import bisect

def free_slots(busy, day_start, day_end, min_len):
    """busy: one list of (start, end) per person. Returns free slots of at least min_len within the day."""
    events = []                                      # (time, delta)
    for person in busy:
        for s, e in person:
            s, e = max(s, day_start), min(e, day_end)        # clip to the working day
            if s < e:
                events += [(s, 1), (e, -1)]
    events.sort()                                    # ends (-1) sort before starts (+1) at equal times
    out, count, free_start = [], 0, day_start
    for t, delta in events:
        if count == 0 and delta == 1 and t - free_start >= min_len:
            out.append((free_start, t))
        count += delta
        if count == 0:
            free_start = t
    if day_end - free_start >= min_len:
        out.append((free_start, day_end))
    return out

class Booked:
    def __init__(self):
        self.starts, self.ends = [], []              # parallel lists sorted by start

    def can_book(self, s, e):
        i = bisect.bisect_right(self.starts, s)      # meetings starting at or before s are before index i
        if i > 0 and self.ends[i - 1] > s:
            return False                             # the previous meeting runs past s
        j = bisect.bisect_left(self.starts, s)       # first meeting starting at or after s
        return j == len(self.starts) or self.starts[j] >= e

    def book(self, s, e):
        if not self.can_book(s, e):
            return False
        i = bisect.bisect_left(self.starts, s)
        self.starts.insert(i, s); self.ends.insert(i, e)
        return True
--cpp
// busy: one list of {start, end} per person. Returns the free slots of at least minLen within the day.
vector<pair<int, int>> freeSlots(const vector<vector<pair<int, int>>>& busy, int dayStart, int dayEnd, int minLen) {
    vector<pair<int, int>> events;                                         // {time, delta}
    for (auto& person : busy) for (auto& iv : person) {
        int s = max(iv.first, dayStart), e = min(iv.second, dayEnd);       // clip to the working day
        if (s < e) { events.push_back({s, 1}); events.push_back({e, -1}); }
    }
    sort(events.begin(), events.end());                                    // ends (-1) sort before starts (+1) at equal times
    vector<pair<int, int>> out;
    int count = 0, freeStart = dayStart;
    for (auto& ev : events) {
        if (count == 0 && ev.second == 1 && ev.first - freeStart >= minLen) out.push_back({freeStart, ev.first});
        count += ev.second;
        if (count == 0) freeStart = ev.first;
    }
    if (dayEnd - freeStart >= minLen) out.push_back({freeStart, dayEnd});
    return out;
}

struct Booked {
    map<int, int> meetings;                                                // start -> end, sorted by start
    bool canBook(int s, int e) const {
        auto after = meetings.lower_bound(s);                              // first meeting starting at or after s
        if (after != meetings.end() && after->first < e) return false;
        if (after != meetings.begin()) {
            auto before = prev(after);                                     // the last meeting starting before s
            if (before->second > s) return false;
        }
        return true;
    }
    bool book(int s, int e) { if (!canBook(s, e)) return false; meetings[s] = e; return true; }
};
--go
import (
	"slices"
	"sort"
)

// busy: one list of {start, end} per person. Returns the free slots {start, end} of at least minLen within the day.
func freeSlots(busy [][][2]int, dayStart, dayEnd, minLen int) [][2]int {
	var events [][2]int // {time, delta}
	for _, person := range busy {
		for _, iv := range person {
			s, e := max(iv[0], dayStart), min(iv[1], dayEnd) // clip to the working day
			if s < e {
				events = append(events, [2]int{s, 1}, [2]int{e, -1})
			}
		}
	}
	sort.Slice(events, func(a, b int) bool { // ends before starts at equal times
		return events[a][0] < events[b][0] || events[a][0] == events[b][0] && events[a][1] < events[b][1]
	})
	var out [][2]int
	count, freeStart := 0, dayStart
	for _, ev := range events {
		if count == 0 && ev[1] == 1 && ev[0]-freeStart >= minLen {
			out = append(out, [2]int{freeStart, ev[0]})
		}
		if count += ev[1]; count == 0 {
			freeStart = ev[0]
		}
	}
	if dayEnd-freeStart >= minLen {
		out = append(out, [2]int{freeStart, dayEnd})
	}
	return out
}

type Booked struct{ meetings [][2]int } // {start, end}, kept sorted by start

func (b *Booked) canBook(s, e int) bool {
	i := sort.Search(len(b.meetings), func(i int) bool { return b.meetings[i][0] > s }) // first meeting starting after s
	if i > 0 && b.meetings[i-1][1] > s {                                                // the one before runs into s
		return false
	}
	return i == len(b.meetings) || b.meetings[i][0] >= e // the next one must start by e
}

func (b *Booked) book(s, e int) bool {
	if !b.canBook(s, e) {
		return false
	}
	i := sort.Search(len(b.meetings), func(i int) bool { return b.meetings[i][0] > s })
	b.meetings = slices.Insert(b.meetings, i, [2]int{s, e})
	return true
}

### social
--idea
A social network is a graph: people are nodes and friendships are edges. Two features come straight from graph search. "People you may know" suggests people who are friends of your friends but not yet your friends, ranked by how many friends you have in common. "Communities" are groups of people connected by chains of friendships, the connected components of the graph.
Both use breadth-first search (BFS), which explores outward in layers: first the person, then their friends (distance 1), then friends of friends (distance 2). People at distance exactly 2 are the suggestions, and counting how many different friends lead to each of them gives the number of mutual friends. A heap keeps the best few. Components come from starting a BFS at every person not yet visited: each BFS finds one whole component. At the scale of a real network the graph is split across many machines, and many searches are batched together (multi-source BFS) instead of run one at a time.
--build
1. Graph (see Graph representation): `adj[u]` is the list of `u`'s friends. Friendship is mutual, so every edge appears in both lists.
2. Suggestions for `u` (the second layer of BFS, see BFS): put `u`'s friends in a set `friends`. For each friend `f`, for each friend `g` of `f`: if `g` is not `u` and not in `friends`, add 1 to `count[g]`. After this, `count[g]` is the number of mutual friends of `u` and `g`.
3. Rank the candidates: keep the `k` with the highest count using a min-heap of size `k` (see Top-k with a heap). Break ties by smaller id so the output is the same every run. Return them from best to worst.
4. Components (see Connected components): keep `comp[v] = -1` for unvisited people. For each person `s` still unvisited, run a BFS from `s` with a queue, marking every person reached with the new component number. The count of BFS runs is the number of components.
5. Distances from several people at once: put all of them in the queue at distance 0 and run one BFS. Every person gets the distance to the nearest of them (see Multi-source BFS). This is how "closest to any of my friends' friends" or "distance to the nearest seed account" is computed in one pass.
6. At scale, popular people with millions of friends make the second layer huge, so systems cap the number of friends explored per person, sample, or precompute suggestions offline. Other signals (shared groups, contact lists, interactions) are added to the ranking.
--think
- Signal: relationships and "how connected are these", "groups", "within two steps". BFS answers "fewest hops". Use Union-find (DSU) instead of BFS when edges arrive one at a time and you only need to ask "are these two connected".
- Degrees of separation: the famous "six degrees" claim is a BFS fact about the shape of such graphs: they are small worlds. A BFS from one person reaches almost everyone within a handful of layers, but the layer sizes explode, which is why you stop at 2.
- Counting mutual friends in the way shown costs the sum over friends of their degrees, which is large for celebrities. Intersecting sorted friend lists, or using bitsets for small groups, are faster ways to compare two specific people.
- Directed graphs (followers) change the meaning: "people followed by people I follow" uses the out-edges, and the number of strongly connected components replaces the connected components.
- Mistakes: suggesting the person to themselves or to their current friends, double counting when an edge is stored once, and marking nodes visited when popped instead of when pushed (the queue then holds duplicates).
--cost
Suggestions for one person cost O(sum of the degrees of their friends) plus the heap. Components cost O(V + E) for the whole graph.
--java
static List<Integer> suggestions(List<List<Integer>> adj, int u, int k) {
    Set<Integer> friends = new HashSet<>(adj.get(u));
    Map<Integer, Integer> count = new HashMap<>();                          // candidate -> number of mutual friends
    for (int f : adj.get(u)) for (int g : adj.get(f))
        if (g != u && !friends.contains(g)) count.merge(g, 1, Integer::sum);
    PriorityQueue<int[]> heap = new PriorityQueue<>((a, b) -> a[0] != b[0] ? a[0] - b[0] : b[1] - a[1]);   // worst on top
    for (Map.Entry<Integer, Integer> e : count.entrySet()) { heap.add(new int[]{e.getValue(), e.getKey()}); if (heap.size() > k) heap.poll(); }
    LinkedList<Integer> out = new LinkedList<>();
    while (!heap.isEmpty()) out.addFirst(heap.poll()[1]);
    return out;
}

static int[] components(List<List<Integer>> adj) {                          // component number of every person
    int n = adj.size();
    int[] comp = new int[n];
    Arrays.fill(comp, -1);
    int id = 0;
    for (int s = 0; s < n; s++) {
        if (comp[s] >= 0) continue;
        Deque<Integer> q = new ArrayDeque<>();
        q.add(s); comp[s] = id;
        while (!q.isEmpty()) {                                              // one BFS finds one whole component
            int u = q.poll();
            for (int v : adj.get(u)) if (comp[v] < 0) { comp[v] = id; q.add(v); }
        }
        id++;
    }
    return comp;
}

static int[] distancesFrom(List<List<Integer>> adj, int[] sources) {         // multi-source BFS
    int[] dist = new int[adj.size()];
    Arrays.fill(dist, -1);
    Deque<Integer> q = new ArrayDeque<>();
    for (int s : sources) { dist[s] = 0; q.add(s); }
    while (!q.isEmpty()) {
        int u = q.poll();
        for (int v : adj.get(u)) if (dist[v] < 0) { dist[v] = dist[u] + 1; q.add(v); }
    }
    return dist;
}
--py
import heapq
from collections import deque

def suggestions(adj, u, k):
    friends = set(adj[u])
    count = {}                                       # candidate -> number of mutual friends
    for f in adj[u]:
        for g in adj[f]:
            if g != u and g not in friends:
                count[g] = count.get(g, 0) + 1
    best = heapq.nlargest(k, ((c, -g) for g, c in count.items()))    # most mutual friends, ties to the smaller id
    return [-neg for _, neg in best]

def components(adj):                                 # component number of every person
    comp = [-1] * len(adj)
    cid = 0
    for s in range(len(adj)):
