        if comp[s] >= 0:
            continue
        comp[s] = cid
        q = deque([s])
        while q:                                     # one BFS finds one whole component
            u = q.popleft()
            for v in adj[u]:
                if comp[v] < 0:
                    comp[v] = cid
                    q.append(v)
        cid += 1
    return comp

def distances_from(adj, sources):                    # multi-source BFS
    dist = [-1] * len(adj)
    q = deque(sources)
    for s in sources:
        dist[s] = 0
    while q:
        u = q.popleft()
        for v in adj[u]:
            if dist[v] < 0:
                dist[v] = dist[u] + 1
                q.append(v)
    return dist
--cpp
vector<int> suggestions(const vector<vector<int>>& adj, int u, int k) {
    set<int> friends(adj[u].begin(), adj[u].end());
    map<int, int> count;                                                     // candidate -> number of mutual friends
    for (int f : adj[u]) for (int g : adj[f]) if (g != u && !friends.count(g)) count[g]++;
    typedef pair<int, int> E;                                                // {mutual friends, -id}: the worst on top
    priority_queue<E, vector<E>, greater<E>> heap;
    for (auto& kv : count) { heap.push({kv.second, -kv.first}); if ((int)heap.size() > k) heap.pop(); }
    vector<int> out;
    while (!heap.empty()) { out.insert(out.begin(), -heap.top().second); heap.pop(); }
    return out;
}

vector<int> components(const vector<vector<int>>& adj) {                     // component number of every person
    int n = adj.size(), id = 0;
    vector<int> comp(n, -1);
    for (int s = 0; s < n; s++) {
        if (comp[s] >= 0) continue;
        queue<int> q;
        q.push(s); comp[s] = id;
        while (!q.empty()) {                                                 // one BFS finds one whole component
            int u = q.front(); q.pop();
            for (int v : adj[u]) if (comp[v] < 0) { comp[v] = id; q.push(v); }
        }
        id++;
    }
    return comp;
}

vector<int> distancesFrom(const vector<vector<int>>& adj, const vector<int>& sources) {   // multi-source BFS
    vector<int> dist(adj.size(), -1);
    queue<int> q;
    for (int s : sources) { dist[s] = 0; q.push(s); }
    while (!q.empty()) {
        int u = q.front(); q.pop();
        for (int v : adj[u]) if (dist[v] < 0) { dist[v] = dist[u] + 1; q.push(v); }
    }
    return dist;
}
--go
import "sort"

func suggestions(adj [][]int, u, k int) []int { // people you may know, most mutual friends first
	friends := map[int]bool{}
	for _, f := range adj[u] {
		friends[f] = true
	}
	count := map[int]int{} // candidate -> number of mutual friends
	for _, f := range adj[u] {
		for _, g := range adj[f] {
			if g != u && !friends[g] {
				count[g]++
			}
		}
	}
	var cand []int
	for g := range count {
		cand = append(cand, g)
	}
	sort.Slice(cand, func(a, b int) bool { // more mutual friends first, ties by id
		return count[cand[a]] > count[cand[b]] || count[cand[a]] == count[cand[b]] && cand[a] < cand[b]
	})
	return cand[:min(k, len(cand))]
}

func components(adj [][]int) []int { // component number of every person
	comp := make([]int, len(adj))
	for i := range comp {
		comp[i] = -1
	}
	id := 0
	for s := range adj {
		if comp[s] >= 0 {
			continue
		}
		q := []int{s}
		comp[s] = id
		for len(q) > 0 { // one BFS finds one whole component
			u := q[0]
			q = q[1:]
			for _, v := range adj[u] {
				if comp[v] < 0 {
					comp[v] = id
					q = append(q, v)
				}
			}
		}
		id++
	}
	return comp
}

func distancesFrom(adj [][]int, sources []int) []int { // multi-source BFS
	dist := make([]int, len(adj))
	for i := range dist {
		dist[i] = -1
	}
	var q []int
	for _, s := range sources {
		dist[s] = 0
		q = append(q, s)
	}
	for len(q) > 0 {
		u := q[0]
		q = q[1:]
		for _, v := range adj[u] {
			if dist[v] < 0 {
				dist[v] = dist[u] + 1
				q = append(q, v)
			}
		}
	}
	return dist
}

### orderbook
--idea
An exchange keeps an order book for each traded instrument: the list of offers to buy (bids) and to sell (asks) that have not been matched yet. A limit order says "buy up to 100 units at no more than price 50". When a new order arrives, the engine checks whether it can trade against the other side right away. A buy order trades with the lowest-priced sell orders as long as their price is at or below its limit. Whatever is left unmatched rests in the book, waiting for a counterparty.
The matching rule is price-time priority: the best price goes first, and among equal prices the order that arrived earlier goes first. The data structure has to give the best order on each side instantly and let new orders be added in log time. That is a priority queue: a max-heap on price for bids and a min-heap for asks, with an arrival counter breaking ties. Cancelling an order from the middle of a heap is awkward, so engines mark it cancelled and discard it when it reaches the top (lazy deletion). An ordered map keyed by price (see Ordered sets and maps), with a queue of orders at each price, is the alternative that also makes market depth (total volume at each price) easy to read.
--build
1. Orders: each has an `id`, a side (buy or sell), a `price` and a quantity `qty`. Keep `orders[id] = (side, price, qty)` for the live ones.
2. Two heaps (see Heaps and priority queues): `bids` ordered by `(-price, seq)` so the highest price comes first, and `asks` ordered by `(price, seq)` so the lowest comes first. `seq` is a counter that grows with every order, so older orders win ties.
3. Best price: look at the top of a heap. If the top entry belongs to an order that was cancelled or is fully filled (it is no longer in `orders`), pop it and look again (lazy deletion).
4. Add a buy order: while it has quantity left and the best ask exists with `askPrice <= price`, trade `min(qty, askQty)` at the resting order's price (the maker's price). Record the trade, and reduce both quantities. A resting order that reaches 0 is removed.
5. If the incoming order still has quantity, push it into the `bids` heap with the next `seq` and store it in `orders`. A sell order is the mirror image against the bids.
6. Cancel: delete the id from `orders`. Its heap entry is discarded later when it reaches the top.
7. Multiset view (see Multisets): the depth at each price can be kept as a map from price to total quantity, so that "how much is available at the best price" is one lookup. Real engines also support market orders, immediate-or-cancel and other order types, and run each instrument's book on a single thread so that the order of events is exactly defined.
--think
- Signal: "always take the best of a changing collection", with insert, remove-best and cancel. Priority queues fit, and so do schedulers and event simulations. The extra twist here is fairness rules (price-time) that must be exact, since they have legal weight.
- Heap versus ordered map: the heap is simpler and fast for the common operations, but cannot reach into the middle (cancel, amend, depth). A map of price levels with a FIFO queue per level supports all of these in O(log levels), and it is what many real engines use.
- Trades happen at the resting order's price, so an aggressive buyer can get a better price than their limit. This is called price improvement and is a normal outcome of the rule.
- Determinism is crucial: given the same sequence of orders, the engine must produce exactly the same trades, so no randomness, no unordered maps in the matching path, and careful tie-breaking.
- Mistakes: matching at the incoming order's price instead of the resting price, forgetting to skip cancelled entries when reading the best price, comparing prices with floating point (use integers, such as cents or ticks), and letting a sequence number overflow.
--cost
Each add or match step is O(log n) for n resting orders. A single order can produce many trades, one per resting order it sweeps.
--java
static class OrderBook {
    // heap entries: {sortKey, seq, id}. Orders: id -> {side (1 buy, 2 sell), price, qty}
    final PriorityQueue<long[]> bids = new PriorityQueue<>((a, b) -> a[0] != b[0] ? Long.compare(a[0], b[0]) : Long.compare(a[1], b[1]));
    final PriorityQueue<long[]> asks = new PriorityQueue<>((a, b) -> a[0] != b[0] ? Long.compare(a[0], b[0]) : Long.compare(a[1], b[1]));
    final Map<Integer, int[]> orders = new HashMap<>();
    long seq = 0;

    private long[] best(PriorityQueue<long[]> heap) {                       // top live entry; drop cancelled ones
        while (!heap.isEmpty() && !orders.containsKey((int) heap.peek()[2])) heap.poll();
        return heap.peek();
    }

    // returns the trades as {takerId, makerId, price, qty}
    List<int[]> add(int id, int side, int price, int qty) {
        List<int[]> trades = new ArrayList<>();
        PriorityQueue<long[]> other = side == 1 ? asks : bids;
        while (qty > 0) {
            long[] top = best(other);
            if (top == null) break;
            int[] maker = orders.get((int) top[2]);
            if (side == 1 ? maker[1] > price : maker[1] < price) break;      // the best resting price does not cross
            int q = Math.min(qty, maker[2]);
            trades.add(new int[]{id, (int) top[2], maker[1], q});            // trade at the resting order's price
            qty -= q; maker[2] -= q;
            if (maker[2] == 0) { orders.remove((int) top[2]); other.poll(); }
        }
        if (qty > 0) {                                                       // the rest waits in the book
            orders.put(id, new int[]{side, price, qty});
            (side == 1 ? bids : asks).add(new long[]{side == 1 ? -price : price, seq++, id});
        }
        return trades;
    }

    void cancel(int id) { orders.remove(id); }                              // lazy: the heap entry is dropped later
    Integer bestBid() { long[] t = best(bids); return t == null ? null : orders.get((int) t[2])[1]; }
    Integer bestAsk() { long[] t = best(asks); return t == null ? null : orders.get((int) t[2])[1]; }
}
--py
import heapq

class OrderBook:
    def __init__(self):
        self.bids, self.asks = [], []                # heap entries: (sort key, seq, id)
        self.orders = {}                             # id -> [side, price, qty] for live orders
        self.seq = 0

    def _best(self, heap):                           # top live entry; drop cancelled ones
        while heap and heap[0][2] not in self.orders:
            heapq.heappop(heap)
        return heap[0] if heap else None

    def add(self, oid, side, price, qty):            # side: "buy" or "sell". Returns trades (taker, maker, price, qty)
        trades = []
        other = self.asks if side == "buy" else self.bids
        while qty > 0:
            top = self._best(other)
            if top is None:
                break
            maker = self.orders[top[2]]
            if (maker[1] > price) if side == "buy" else (maker[1] < price):
                break                                # the best resting price does not cross
            q = min(qty, maker[2])
            trades.append((oid, top[2], maker[1], q))        # trade at the resting order's price
            qty -= q; maker[2] -= q
            if maker[2] == 0:
                del self.orders[top[2]]
                heapq.heappop(other)
        if qty > 0:                                  # the rest waits in the book
            self.orders[oid] = [side, price, qty]
            heapq.heappush(self.bids if side == "buy" else self.asks,
                           (-price if side == "buy" else price, self.seq, oid))
            self.seq += 1
        return trades

    def cancel(self, oid):
        self.orders.pop(oid, None)                   # lazy: the heap entry is dropped later

    def best_bid(self):
        t = self._best(self.bids)
        return self.orders[t[2]][1] if t else None

    def best_ask(self):
        t = self._best(self.asks)
        return self.orders[t[2]][1] if t else None
--cpp
struct OrderBook {
    typedef tuple<long long, long long, int> Entry;                          // {sort key, seq, id}
    priority_queue<Entry, vector<Entry>, greater<Entry>> bids, asks;         // smallest key first
    map<int, array<int, 3>> orders;                                          // id -> {side (1 buy, 2 sell), price, qty}
    long long seq = 0;

    const Entry* best(priority_queue<Entry, vector<Entry>, greater<Entry>>& heap) {   // top live entry; drop cancelled ones
        while (!heap.empty() && !orders.count(get<2>(heap.top()))) heap.pop();
        return heap.empty() ? nullptr : &heap.top();
    }

    // returns the trades as {takerId, makerId, price, qty}
    vector<array<int, 4>> add(int id, int side, int price, int qty) {
        vector<array<int, 4>> trades;
        auto& other = side == 1 ? asks : bids;
        while (qty > 0) {
            const Entry* top = best(other);
            if (!top) break;
            int makerId = get<2>(*top);
            auto& maker = orders[makerId];
            if (side == 1 ? maker[1] > price : maker[1] < price) break;      // the best resting price does not cross
            int q = min(qty, maker[2]);
            trades.push_back({id, makerId, maker[1], q});                    // trade at the resting order's price
            qty -= q; maker[2] -= q;
            if (maker[2] == 0) { orders.erase(makerId); other.pop(); }
        }
        if (qty > 0) {                                                       // the rest waits in the book
            orders[id] = {side, price, qty};
            (side == 1 ? bids : asks).push({side == 1 ? -(long long)price : price, seq++, id});
        }
        return trades;
    }

    void cancel(int id) { orders.erase(id); }                               // lazy: the heap entry is dropped later
    int bestBid() { auto t = best(bids); return t ? orders[get<2>(*t)][1] : -1; }
    int bestAsk() { auto t = best(asks); return t ? orders[get<2>(*t)][1] : -1; }
};
--go
import "container/heap"

type order struct{ side, price, qty int } // side: 1 buy, 2 sell

type book [][3]int64 // heap entries {sortKey, seq, id}: a min-heap, so price time priority

func (b book) Len() int { return len(b) }
func (b book) Less(i, j int) bool {
	return b[i][0] < b[j][0] || b[i][0] == b[j][0] && b[i][1] < b[j][1]
}
func (b book) Swap(i, j int) { b[i], b[j] = b[j], b[i] }
func (b *book) Push(x any)   { *b = append(*b, x.([3]int64)) }
func (b *book) Pop() any     { o := *b; x := o[len(o)-1]; *b = o[:len(o)-1]; return x }

type OrderBook struct {
	bids, asks book
	orders     map[int]*order // live orders by id
	seq        int64
}

func newOrderBook() *OrderBook { return &OrderBook{orders: map[int]*order{}} }

func (ob *OrderBook) best(h *book) ([3]int64, bool) { // top live entry; drop cancelled ones
	for h.Len() > 0 {
		if _, live := ob.orders[int((*h)[0][2])]; live {
			return (*h)[0], true
		}
		heap.Pop(h)
	}
	return [3]int64{}, false
}

// returns the trades as {takerId, makerId, price, qty}
func (ob *OrderBook) add(id, side, price, qty int) [][4]int {
	var trades [][4]int
	other := &ob.asks
	if side == 2 {
		other = &ob.bids
	}
	for qty > 0 {
		top, ok := ob.best(other)
		if !ok {
			break
		}
		maker := ob.orders[int(top[2])]
		if side == 1 && maker.price > price || side == 2 && maker.price < price { // the best resting price does not cross
			break
		}
		q := min(qty, maker.qty)
		trades = append(trades, [4]int{id, int(top[2]), maker.price, q}) // trade at the resting order's price
		qty -= q
		maker.qty -= q
		if maker.qty == 0 {
			delete(ob.orders, int(top[2]))
			heap.Pop(other)
		}
	}
	if qty > 0 { // the rest waits in the book
		ob.orders[id] = &order{side, price, qty}
		key, mine := int64(price), &ob.asks
		if side == 1 {
			key, mine = -key, &ob.bids // best bid = highest price = smallest negated key
		}
		heap.Push(mine, [3]int64{key, ob.seq, int64(id)})
		ob.seq++
	}
	return trades
}

func (ob *OrderBook) cancel(id int) { delete(ob.orders, id) } // lazy: the heap entry is dropped later

func (ob *OrderBook) bestBid() (int, bool) {
	t, ok := ob.best(&ob.bids)
	if !ok {
		return 0, false
	}
	return ob.orders[int(t[2])].price, true
}

func (ob *OrderBook) bestAsk() (int, bool) {
	t, ok := ob.best(&ob.asks)
	if !ok {
		return 0, false
	}
	return ob.orders[int(t[2])].price, true
}

### riskmc
--idea
Many financial questions have no neat formula: what is an option worth, how much could a portfolio lose in a bad month? An option's payoff depends on the future price of an asset, which is uncertain. Two numerical methods give the answer anyway. Monte Carlo simulation generates thousands of possible futures at random and averages the payoff. A lattice (binomial tree) models the price as moving up or down by fixed factors at each step, and works backwards from the final payoffs with dynamic programming.
The model for the price is a random walk: each small step multiplies the price by a random factor. In a Markov chain, the future depends only on the present state, and that is exactly what makes backward induction on the lattice possible. A European call option pays `max(S_T - K, 0)` at time `T`, where `S_T` is the final price and `K` the strike. Its fair value today is the expected payoff in a "risk-neutral" world, discounted back by the interest rate. Both methods estimate that expectation: Monte Carlo by sampling, the lattice by exact summation over a simplified model. The famous Black-Scholes formula gives the exact value for this simple case, which is used as a check.
--build
1. Inputs: current price `S`, strike `K`, interest rate `r`, volatility `sigma` (how wildly the price moves, per year), and time to expiry `T` in years.
2. Random walk (see Random walks): split `[0, T]` into `steps` pieces of length `dt = T / steps`. At each step, `S *= exp((r - sigma^2 / 2) * dt + sigma * sqrt(dt) * Z)` where `Z` is a standard normal random number. This is geometric Brownian motion, the standard model, in which price changes are random percentages.
3. Monte Carlo (see Randomization): simulate `n` full paths, compute each final payoff `max(S_T - K, 0)`, average them, and discount: `price = exp(-r * T) * average`. The error shrinks like `1 / sqrt(n)`: 100 times more paths for 10 times more accuracy.
4. Binomial lattice (Cox-Ross-Rubinstein): with `n` steps set `u = exp(sigma * sqrt(dt))`, `d = 1 / u` and the risk-neutral up probability `p = (exp(r * dt) - d) / (u - d)`. After `n` steps the price is `S * u^j * d^(n-j)` for `j` up moves, and the payoff there is `max(price - K, 0)`.
5. Work backwards (probability DP, see Probability DP and Markov chains): the value at a node is the discounted expectation of the two nodes it can move to, `value = exp(-r*dt) * (p * up + (1 - p) * down)`. Repeat from step `n - 1` down to 0. The single value at the start is the price. Only a single array of size `n + 1` is needed, updated in place.
6. Compare with the closed form, and watch both converge as `n` grows.
7. Real risk engines simulate many correlated assets, fit the volatility to market data, and use variance reduction (antithetic samples, control variates) to cut the error without more paths. They also use the same simulations to estimate risk measures such as value at risk.
--think
- Signal: an expectation over many random outcomes that has no closed form, or has path-dependent payoffs. Monte Carlo handles almost any payoff and any number of dimensions. A lattice or finite-difference method is more accurate in few dimensions and handles early exercise (American options) naturally, by taking the maximum of "exercise now" and "wait" at each node.
- Monte Carlo error is statistical, so report a standard error with the answer. Use a fixed seed in tests, and enough paths that the check is stable.
- The lattice is exactly the DP view: the states are the price levels at each step, the transitions have probabilities, and the answer is built from the end. It is why the same ideas appear in games and speech (see Speech recognition and Viterbi decoding).
- Model risk dominates in practice: real prices have jumps and changing volatility. A precise answer from a wrong model is still wrong, so check the assumptions before trusting the digits.
- Mistakes: forgetting to discount, using the real-world drift instead of the risk-free rate `r` for pricing, a negative probability `p` when `dt` is too large for the chosen `sigma`, and sharing one random stream across things that should be independent.
--cost
Monte Carlo: O(n * steps) for n paths. Lattice: O(steps^2) time and O(steps) memory.
--java
static double monteCarloCall(double S, double K, double r, double sigma, double T, int paths, int steps, Random rnd) {
    double dt = T / steps, drift = (r - sigma * sigma / 2) * dt, vol = sigma * Math.sqrt(dt), sum = 0;
    for (int i = 0; i < paths; i++) {
        double price = S;
        for (int s = 0; s < steps; s++) price *= Math.exp(drift + vol * rnd.nextGaussian());   // one random-walk step
        sum += Math.max(price - K, 0);                                       // payoff of this path
    }
    return Math.exp(-r * T) * sum / paths;                                   // average, discounted
}

static double binomialCall(double S, double K, double r, double sigma, double T, int n) {
    double dt = T / n, u = Math.exp(sigma * Math.sqrt(dt)), d = 1 / u;
    double p = (Math.exp(r * dt) - d) / (u - d), disc = Math.exp(-r * dt);   // risk-neutral up probability
    double[] v = new double[n + 1];
    for (int j = 0; j <= n; j++) v[j] = Math.max(S * Math.pow(u, j) * Math.pow(d, n - j) - K, 0);   // payoffs at expiry
    for (int step = n - 1; step >= 0; step--)                                // work backwards
        for (int j = 0; j <= step; j++) v[j] = disc * (p * v[j + 1] + (1 - p) * v[j]);
    return v[0];
}
--py
import math, random

def monte_carlo_call(S, K, r, sigma, T, paths=100000, steps=5):
    dt = T / steps
    drift, vol, total = (r - sigma ** 2 / 2) * dt, sigma * math.sqrt(dt), 0.0
    for _ in range(paths):
        price = S
        for _ in range(steps):
            price *= math.exp(drift + vol * random.gauss(0, 1))      # one random-walk step
        total += max(price - K, 0)                                   # payoff of this path
    return math.exp(-r * T) * total / paths                          # average, discounted

def binomial_call(S, K, r, sigma, T, n=500):
    dt = T / n
    u = math.exp(sigma * math.sqrt(dt)); d = 1 / u
    p = (math.exp(r * dt) - d) / (u - d)             # risk-neutral up probability
    disc = math.exp(-r * dt)
    v = [max(S * u ** j * d ** (n - j) - K, 0) for j in range(n + 1)]    # payoffs at expiry
    for step in range(n - 1, -1, -1):                # work backwards
        v = [disc * (p * v[j + 1] + (1 - p) * v[j]) for j in range(step + 1)]
    return v[0]
--cpp
double monteCarloCall(double S, double K, double r, double sigma, double T, int paths, int steps, mt19937& rng) {
    normal_distribution<double> Z(0.0, 1.0);
    double dt = T / steps, drift = (r - sigma * sigma / 2) * dt, vol = sigma * sqrt(dt), sum = 0;
    for (int i = 0; i < paths; i++) {
        double price = S;
        for (int s = 0; s < steps; s++) price *= exp(drift + vol * Z(rng));   // one random-walk step
        sum += max(price - K, 0.0);                                           // payoff of this path
    }
    return exp(-r * T) * sum / paths;                                         // average, discounted
}

double binomialCall(double S, double K, double r, double sigma, double T, int n) {
    double dt = T / n, u = exp(sigma * sqrt(dt)), d = 1 / u;
    double p = (exp(r * dt) - d) / (u - d), disc = exp(-r * dt);              // risk-neutral up probability
    vector<double> v(n + 1);
    for (int j = 0; j <= n; j++) v[j] = max(S * pow(u, j) * pow(d, n - j) - K, 0.0);   // payoffs at expiry
    for (int step = n - 1; step >= 0; step--)                                 // work backwards
        for (int j = 0; j <= step; j++) v[j] = disc * (p * v[j + 1] + (1 - p) * v[j]);
    return v[0];
}
--go
import (
	"math"
	"math/rand"
)

func monteCarloCall(S, K, r, sigma, T float64, paths, steps int, rnd *rand.Rand) float64 {
	dt := T / float64(steps)
	drift, vol, sum := (r-sigma*sigma/2)*dt, sigma*math.Sqrt(dt), 0.0
	for i := 0; i < paths; i++ {
		price := S
		for s := 0; s < steps; s++ {
			price *= math.Exp(drift + vol*rnd.NormFloat64()) // one random-walk step
		}
		sum += math.Max(price-K, 0) // payoff of this path
	}
	return math.Exp(-r*T) * sum / float64(paths) // average, discounted
}

func binomialCall(S, K, r, sigma, T float64, n int) float64 {
	dt := T / float64(n)
	u := math.Exp(sigma * math.Sqrt(dt))
	d := 1 / u
	p, disc := (math.Exp(r*dt)-d)/(u-d), math.Exp(-r*dt) // risk-neutral up probability
	v := make([]float64, n+1)
	for j := range v { // payoffs at expiry
		v[j] = math.Max(S*math.Pow(u, float64(j))*math.Pow(d, float64(n-j))-K, 0)
	}
	for step := n - 1; step >= 0; step-- { // work backwards
		for j := 0; j <= step; j++ {
			v[j] = disc * (p*v[j+1] + (1-p)*v[j])
		}
	}
	return v[0]
}

### filesync
--idea
File sync tools (rsync, Dropbox) and BitTorrent move big files efficiently. When you edit a large file and sync it, only the changed parts should travel. The trouble is that an insertion shifts everything after it, so comparing the two versions block by block at fixed positions finds nothing in common. The rsync algorithm solves this with a rolling hash: a fingerprint of a window of bytes that can be updated in constant time as the window slides one byte, so every offset of the new file can be checked cheaply against the blocks of the old file.
The receiver, who has the old file, splits it into blocks and sends a short fingerprint of each. The sender slides a window along the new file. When the window's rolling hash matches an old block's hash (and a stronger hash confirms it, to rule out accidental matches), the sender emits a reference "copy block 7" instead of the bytes, and jumps ahead a whole block. Otherwise it emits one literal byte and slides on. BitTorrent solves a different problem: many peers each hold different pieces of a file. Each keeps a bitset of the pieces it has, and requests the rarest missing piece first, which keeps every piece available in the swarm.
--build
1. Rolling hash (see Rabin-Karp and String hashing): treat a window of `B` bytes as digits of a number in base `BASE` modulo `M`: `h = (b0 * BASE^(B-1) + ... + b_(B-1)) mod M`. Sliding the window by one byte: `h = ((h - out * BASE^(B-1)) * BASE + in) mod M`, where `out` is the byte leaving and `in` the byte entering. The cost is constant, whatever `B` is.
2. Signatures from the old file: for each block of `B` bytes, store the weak (rolling) hash and a strong hash (here a 64-bit FNV mix; real tools use MD5 or BLAKE). Put them in a Hash table from the weak hash to the list of `(strong hash, block index)`.
3. Delta from the new file: compute the rolling hash of the window at position `i = 0`. Loop: if the weak hash is in the table and the strong hash of the window matches an entry, output `copy(index)`, jump `i += B` and recompute the hash from scratch. Otherwise append `new[i]` to the pending literal bytes and slide the window by one with the rolling update.
4. When fewer than `B` bytes remain, emit the rest as literals. The output is a list of operations: copies of old blocks and runs of literal bytes.
5. Apply the delta on the old file: for `copy(index)` append block `index` of the old file, for a literal append its bytes. The result equals the new file exactly.
6. Piece selection in a swarm (see Bitsets): `mine` is a bitset of the pieces you have, and each peer has one. For every piece you lack that at least one peer has, count the peers holding it (a population count over the bitsets). Request the piece with the smallest positive count, the rarest first.
7. Real tools add compression, many files, verifying each piece against a hash list (a Merkle tree for BitTorrent v2), and choking rules that decide whom to upload to.
--think
- Signal: updating a remote copy of large data when most of it is unchanged, or any "find shared chunks at arbitrary shifts" problem. The rolling hash is the key trick: a checksum that updates in O(1) when the window moves. The same idea finds duplicate chunks in backups (content-defined chunking splits where the rolling hash hits a pattern, so insertions do not shift all chunk boundaries).
- Two hashes because of cost versus safety: the weak one is cheap and runs at every offset but has collisions, the strong one runs only on weak matches and makes a wrong match negligible.
- Block size is a trade-off: small blocks find more matches but make larger signatures and more operations, large blocks are cheap but miss small shared pieces.
- Rarest first keeps the whole swarm healthy: if everyone downloads the most common piece, the rare pieces disappear when their holders leave. It also spreads uploads evenly.
- Mistakes: a rolling update that forgets to take the modulo of a negative number, not recomputing the hash after a jump, trusting the weak hash alone, and an off-by-one in the window range at the end of the file.
--cost
Signatures O(old size). Delta O(new size) expected, since each byte is hashed once and strong hashes run only on weak matches. Rarest first costs O(pieces * peers) per decision with a population count per piece.
--java
static final long BASE = 131, MOD = 1_000_000_007L;

static long strong(String s) {                                              // 64-bit FNV-1a: the strong hash
    long h = 0xcbf29ce484222325L;
    for (int i = 0; i < s.length(); i++) { h ^= s.charAt(i); h *= 0x100000001b3L; }
    return h;
}

static long weak(String s) {                                                // rolling hash of a whole window
    long h = 0;
    for (int i = 0; i < s.length(); i++) h = (h * BASE + s.charAt(i)) % MOD;
    return h;
}

static Map<Long, List<long[]>> signatures(String old, int B) {              // weak hash -> list of {strong hash, block index}
    Map<Long, List<long[]>> table = new HashMap<>();
    for (int i = 0; i + B <= old.length(); i += B) {
        String block = old.substring(i, i + B);
        table.computeIfAbsent(weak(block), k -> new ArrayList<>()).add(new long[]{strong(block), i / B});
    }
    return table;
}

// ops: {index, ""} copies block `index` (index >= 0); {-1, text} is a literal run
static List<Object[]> delta(String neu, Map<Long, List<long[]>> table, int B) {
    List<Object[]> ops = new ArrayList<>();
    StringBuilder lit = new StringBuilder();
    long pw = 1;
    for (int i = 0; i < B - 1; i++) pw = pw * BASE % MOD;                   // BASE^(B-1)
    int i = 0;
    long h = neu.length() >= B ? weak(neu.substring(0, B)) : 0;
    while (i + B <= neu.length()) {
        long found = -1;
        List<long[]> cand = table.get(h);
        if (cand != null) { long sh = strong(neu.substring(i, i + B)); for (long[] c : cand) if (c[0] == sh) { found = c[1]; break; } }
        if (found >= 0) {                                                   // the window equals an old block
            if (lit.length() > 0) { ops.add(new Object[]{-1, lit.toString()}); lit.setLength(0); }
            ops.add(new Object[]{(int) found, ""});
            i += B;
            if (i + B <= neu.length()) h = weak(neu.substring(i, i + B));
        } else {                                                            // slide by one byte
            lit.append(neu.charAt(i));
            if (i + B < neu.length()) h = (((h - neu.charAt(i) * pw % MOD + MOD) % MOD) * BASE + neu.charAt(i + B)) % MOD;
            i++;
        }
    }
    lit.append(neu.substring(i));
    if (lit.length() > 0) ops.add(new Object[]{-1, lit.toString()});
    return ops;
}

static String applyDelta(String old, List<Object[]> ops, int B) {
    StringBuilder out = new StringBuilder();
    for (Object[] op : ops) {
        int idx = (int) op[0];
        if (idx >= 0) out.append(old, idx * B, idx * B + B); else out.append((String) op[1]);
    }
    return out.toString();
}

static int rarestFirst(long mine, long[] peers, int n) {                    // bitsets of pieces; returns a piece or -1
    int bestPiece = -1, bestCount = Integer.MAX_VALUE;
    for (int piece = 0; piece < n; piece++) {
        if ((mine >> piece & 1) == 1) continue;
        int c = 0;
        for (long p : peers) c += (int) (p >> piece & 1);
        if (c > 0 && c < bestCount) { bestCount = c; bestPiece = piece; }
    }
    return bestPiece;
}
--py
BASE, MOD = 131, 1_000_000_007

def strong(s):                                       # 64-bit FNV-1a: the strong hash
    h = 0xcbf29ce484222325
    for ch in s:
        h = ((h ^ ord(ch)) * 0x100000001b3) & 0xFFFFFFFFFFFFFFFF
    return h

def weak(s):                                         # rolling hash of a whole window
    h = 0
    for ch in s:
        h = (h * BASE + ord(ch)) % MOD
    return h

def signatures(old, B):                              # weak hash -> list of (strong hash, block index)
    table = {}
    for i in range(0, len(old) - B + 1, B):
        block = old[i:i + B]
        table.setdefault(weak(block), []).append((strong(block), i // B))
    return table

def delta(new, table, B):
    """Returns ops: ("copy", block index) or ("lit", text)."""
    ops, lit, i = [], [], 0
    pw = pow(BASE, B - 1, MOD)                       # BASE^(B-1)
    h = weak(new[:B]) if len(new) >= B else 0
    while i + B <= len(new):
        found = None
        for sh, idx in table.get(h, []):
            if sh == strong(new[i:i + B]):
                found = idx
                break
        if found is not None:                        # the window equals an old block
            if lit:
                ops.append(("lit", "".join(lit))); lit = []
            ops.append(("copy", found))
            i += B
            if i + B <= len(new):
                h = weak(new[i:i + B])
        else:                                        # slide by one byte
            lit.append(new[i])
            if i + B < len(new):
                h = ((h - ord(new[i]) * pw) * BASE + ord(new[i + B])) % MOD
            i += 1
    lit.append(new[i:])
    if "".join(lit):
        ops.append(("lit", "".join(lit)))
    return ops

def apply_delta(old, ops, B):
    return "".join(old[v * B:(v + 1) * B] if kind == "copy" else v for kind, v in ops)

def rarest_first(mine, peers, n):                    # bitsets of pieces; returns a piece or None
    best = None
    for piece in range(n):
        if mine >> piece & 1:
            continue
        c = sum(p >> piece & 1 for p in peers)
        if c > 0 and (best is None or c < best[0]):
            best = (c, piece)
    return best[1] if best else None
--cpp
const long long BASE = 131, MOD = 1000000007;

unsigned long long strongHash(const string& s) {                            // 64-bit FNV-1a: the strong hash
    unsigned long long h = 0xcbf29ce484222325ULL;
    for (unsigned char c : s) { h ^= c; h *= 0x100000001b3ULL; }
    return h;
}

long long weakHash(const string& s) {                                       // rolling hash of a whole window
    long long h = 0;
    for (unsigned char c : s) h = (h * BASE + c) % MOD;
    return h;
}

map<long long, vector<pair<unsigned long long, int>>> signatures(const string& old, int B) {   // weak hash -> {strong hash, block index}
    map<long long, vector<pair<unsigned long long, int>>> table;
    for (size_t i = 0; i + B <= old.size(); i += B) {
        string block = old.substr(i, B);
        table[weakHash(block)].push_back({strongHash(block), (int)(i / B)});
    }
    return table;
}

// ops: {index, ""} copies block `index` (index >= 0); {-1, text} is a literal run
vector<pair<int, string>> delta(const string& neu, const map<long long, vector<pair<unsigned long long, int>>>& table, int B) {
    vector<pair<int, string>> ops;
    string lit;
    long long pw = 1;
    for (int k = 0; k < B - 1; k++) pw = pw * BASE % MOD;                   // BASE^(B-1)
    size_t i = 0;
    long long h = neu.size() >= (size_t)B ? weakHash(neu.substr(0, B)) : 0;
    while (i + B <= neu.size()) {
        int found = -1;
        auto it = table.find(h);
        if (it != table.end()) { unsigned long long sh = strongHash(neu.substr(i, B)); for (auto& c : it->second) if (c.first == sh) { found = c.second; break; } }
        if (found >= 0) {                                                   // the window equals an old block
            if (!lit.empty()) { ops.push_back({-1, lit}); lit.clear(); }
            ops.push_back({found, ""});
            i += B;
            if (i + B <= neu.size()) h = weakHash(neu.substr(i, B));
        } else {                                                            // slide by one byte
            lit += neu[i];
            if (i + B < neu.size()) h = (((h - (unsigned char)neu[i] * pw % MOD + MOD) % MOD) * BASE + (unsigned char)neu[i + B]) % MOD;
            i++;
        }
    }
    lit += neu.substr(i);
    if (!lit.empty()) ops.push_back({-1, lit});
    return ops;
}

string applyDelta(const string& old, const vector<pair<int, string>>& ops, int B) {
    string out;
    for (auto& op : ops) out += op.first >= 0 ? old.substr((size_t)op.first * B, B) : op.second;
    return out;
}

int rarestFirst(unsigned long long mine, const vector<unsigned long long>& peers, int n) {   // bitsets of pieces; returns a piece or -1
    int bestPiece = -1, bestCount = INT_MAX;
    for (int piece = 0; piece < n; piece++) {
        if (mine >> piece & 1) continue;
        int c = 0;
        for (auto p : peers) c += p >> piece & 1;
        if (c > 0 && c < bestCount) { bestCount = c; bestPiece = piece; }
    }
    return bestPiece;
}
--go
import "strings"

const (
	BASE = 131
	MOD  = 1_000_000_007
)

func strong(s string) uint64 { // 64-bit FNV-1a: the strong hash
	h := uint64(0xcbf29ce484222325)
	for i := 0; i < len(s); i++ {
		h ^= uint64(s[i])
		h *= 0x100000001b3
	}
	return h
}

func weak(s string) uint64 { // polynomial hash of a whole window
	h := uint64(0)
	for i := 0; i < len(s); i++ {
		h = (h*BASE + uint64(s[i])) % MOD
	}
	return h
}

type sig struct {
	strong uint64
	index  int
}

func signatures(old string, B int) map[uint64][]sig { // weak hash -> {strong hash, block index}
	table := map[uint64][]sig{}
	for i := 0; i+B <= len(old); i += B {
		block := old[i : i+B]
		table[weak(block)] = append(table[weak(block)], sig{strong(block), i / B})
	}
	return table
}

type op struct { // index >= 0 copies old block `index`; index -1 is a literal run
	index int
	text  string
}

func delta(neu string, table map[uint64][]sig, B int) []op {
	var ops []op
	var lit strings.Builder
	pw := uint64(1)
	for i := 0; i < B-1; i++ {
		pw = pw * BASE % MOD // BASE^(B-1)
	}
	i, h := 0, uint64(0)
	if len(neu) >= B {
		h = weak(neu[:B])
	}
	for i+B <= len(neu) {
		found := -1
		if cand, ok := table[h]; ok {
			sh := strong(neu[i : i+B])
			for _, c := range cand {
				if c.strong == sh {
					found = c.index
					break
				}
			}
		}
		if found >= 0 { // the window equals an old block
			if lit.Len() > 0 {
				ops = append(ops, op{-1, lit.String()})
				lit.Reset()
			}
			ops = append(ops, op{found, ""})
			i += B
			if i+B <= len(neu) {
				h = weak(neu[i : i+B])
			}
		} else { // slide by one byte: drop neu[i], add neu[i+B]
			lit.WriteByte(neu[i])
			if i+B < len(neu) {
				h = ((h+MOD-uint64(neu[i])*pw%MOD)%MOD*BASE + uint64(neu[i+B])) % MOD
			}
			i++
		}
	}
	lit.WriteString(neu[i:])
	if lit.Len() > 0 {
		ops = append(ops, op{-1, lit.String()})
	}
	return ops
}

func applyDelta(old string, ops []op, B int) string {
	var out strings.Builder
	for _, o := range ops {
		if o.index >= 0 {
			out.WriteString(old[o.index*B : o.index*B+B])
		} else {
			out.WriteString(o.text)
		}
	}
	return out.String()
}

func rarestFirst(mine uint64, peers []uint64, n int) int { // bitsets of pieces; returns a piece or -1
	bestPiece, bestCount := -1, 1<<30
	for piece := 0; piece < n; piece++ {
		if mine>>piece&1 == 1 {
			continue
		}
		c := 0
		for _, p := range peers {
			c += int(p >> piece & 1)
		}
		if c > 0 && c < bestCount {
			bestCount, bestPiece = c, piece
		}
	}
	return bestPiece
}
